# Hinglaj Devi — takeaways

*Source: "JÜRGEN SCHAFLECHNER" as the title page prints him — "Schaflechner, Jurgen, 1981–"[text-layer?]
[Jürgen] in the CIP block (p. 4, folio not stated) and "Schaflechner, Jürgen" at p. 335 —
"Hinglaj Devi / Identity, Change, and Solidification at a / Hindu Temple in Pakistan" as the title
page prints it (p. 3, folio not stated; the slashes are the page's own line breaks and the spacing
is decorative, not part of the title). Imprint as printed, and it contradicts itself on the same
page: "© Oxford University Press 2018" and a CIP description line reading "New York : Oxford
University Press, 2018" against an LCC print line reading 2017 (p. 4, folio not stated) — both
years are recorded and neither is preferred. 364 pages, transcribed `text_layer` on 14 September 2026; provenance in
`out/ocr/schaflechner_hinglaj_devi/`. Page references are PDF page indices; **this book prints no
recoverable folio — 0 folio numbers and 3,073 "folio not stated" across 19 chunks — so every
reference is (p. N, folio not stated)**. Reviewed: no.*

## What this book is

A single-shrine monograph, and the shrine is **shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir**. The book is a
study of what the author calls "the solidification of tradition" at one Goddess temple in the desert
of Balochistan, built out of five years of fieldwork and a large vernacular textual corpus: "this
challenge will be taken up using approaches from current political and anthropological theory applied
to the data from my intensive fieldwork and textual research between 2009 and 2015" (p. 14, folio not
stated). **The title as the book's own pages print it is not recoverable from these extracts:
(not stated).** The imprint wording that is printed is the CIP block at p. 4 (folio not stated):
"Published in the United States of America by Oxford University Press 198 Madison Avenue, New York,
NY 10016, United States of America."; "Names: Schaflechner, Jurgen, 1981– author."; "Description: New
York : Oxford University Press, 2018. | Includes bibliographical references and index."; "LCCN
2017028646 (print) | LCCN 2017052020 (ebook)"; "ISBN 9780190850531 (updf) | ISBN 9780190850548 (epub)
| ISBN 9780190850555 (online content) | ISBN 9780190850524 (hardback : alk. paper)";
"Classification: LCC BL1243.79.P183 (ebook) | LCC BL1243.79.P183 H568 2017 (print) | DDC
294.5/350954915—dc23". **Two imprint years therefore stand side by side and are not reconciled: the
description line says 2018 and the LCC print line says 2017** (p. 4, folio not stated). **The
author's forename is also printed two ways — "Jurgen" in the CIP block (p. 4, folio not stated) and
"Schaflechner, Jürgen" in the Works Cited (p. 335, folio not stated).** Dedication: "Für Max als
Willkommen und Versprechen. / Für Nora als Dank und zum Abschied." (p. 5, folio not stated). Parts of
three chapters appeared earlier, in *Muslim Wanderers in South Asia* (ed. Boivin and Delage, London:
Routledge, 2015), in *The Ambivalence of Denial: Danger and Appeal of Rituals* (ed. Hüsken and Simon,
Wiesbaden: Harrassowitz), and in *Zeitschrift für Indologie und Südasienstudien* vol. 32 (Bremen:
Hempen Verlag, 2016) (p. 8, folio not stated). The contents (p. 6, folio not stated) are printed
**with no page numbers at all**: "Acknowledgments"; "Conventions for Transliteration, Transcription,
and Sources"; "Introduction"; "1 The Struggle Over Truth"; "2 Hinglaj in Perspective"; "3 Historical
Representations and Recent Changes"; "4 (Un)necessary Hardships in 'Getting There'"; "5 Change and
Perseverance"; "6 Solidifying Hinglaj: Striving for a Uniform Tradition"; "Conclusion"; "Works
Cited"; "Index". Two map leaves carry captions and no map text: "FIGURE 0.1 Map of Pakistan. Maps
created by Nils Harm, Department of Geography, South Asia Institute, University of Heidelberg" (p. 11,
folio not stated) and "FIGURE 0.2 Map of Sindh and south Balochistan" (p. 12, folio not stated).

**What the book is trying to show about the shrine.** Schaflechner's opening question is about a
field, not a site — "WHAT IS PAKISTANI Hinduism? Or, even more generally, is there anything that can
be called Pakistani Hinduism at all? Not much has been published to date about the numerous Hindu
communities that exist in today's Islamic Republic of Pakistan." (p. 13, folio not stated) — and his
answer runs through one shrine: "notwithstanding that this study is centered on the rituals and
narratives of one particular site located somewhere in the far-off desert of Balochistan, its recent
developments reveal much about the Hindu communities of Pakistan." (p. 24, folio not stated) His
explicit argument is that the tradition at Hinglaj is being made solid *now*, in front of him, by
nameable actors: "During the course of this process, certain practices at Hinglaj became labeled as
the 'real,' 'true,' or 'proper' ways to engage with the Goddess, whereas others were termed as 'fake'
or 'notional.' In this book, I call these intricate dynamics of inclusion and exclusion 'a
solidification of tradition,' and one of my major goals is to utilize this concept for my case study."
(p. 29, folio not stated) The cause he assigns is material: "the current struggle over the prevailing,
or hegemonic, interpretation of the shrine is directly linked to its significant infrastructural
changes" (p. 30, folio not stated), and in the Conclusion he names the two events flatly — "two
elements stand out as particularly important: the institutionalization of the pilgrimage by the
Hinglaj Sheva Mandali (HSM) in 1986 and the completion of the Makran Coastal Highway (MCH) at the
beginning of the new millennium." (p. 319, folio not stated) **He is careful about what he is not
claiming**: "I do not claim, therefore, the absence of a homogeneous Hindu tradition at the shrine but
rather show how the production of precisely such a unified identity has repeatedly been attempted by
many actors." (p. 37, folio not stated) And he counts himself in: "the present study plays its own
role in the solidification of the Hinglaj tradition and thus also produces—intentionally and
unintentionally—another truth-claim." (pp. 48–49, folio not stated)

**Reliability, and what the book says its own evidence will not bear.** This is an unusually
self-limiting book and the limits should travel with every fact taken from it. It is "limited to
providing a snapshot of current developments around Hinglaj" (p. 201, folio not stated). Its
twentieth-century evidence rests on one man's memory: "Dewani himself visited Hinglaj for the first
time when he was a child, more than forty years ago, in 1971… In fact, I could not find anyone else
who had seen the place before 1971, and so most of my inquiries about the history of the valley during
the twentieth century came, one way or another, back to Dewani's experience." (p. 276, folio not
stated) On the shrine's deep past: "details of this old journey, let alone one continuous way of
engaging with the Goddess through the centuries, cannot decisively be established." (p. 321, folio not
stated) On the Sanskrit attestations: "Sanskrit sources, however, are of limited use in dating Hinglaj
to a particular era… A direct link between the mentioning of the name in Sanskrit sources and the site
in Balochistan today is difficult to prove." (p. 52, folio not stated) On the colonial travellers he
mines: "all these authors operated as colonial masters under the guidance of locals or religious
experts who influenced their writing." (p. 237, folio not stated) He repeatedly records his own source
failures: "Unfortunately, I was not able to obtain a copy of this text at the Royal Asiatic Society in
2009." (p. 98, folio not stated); "Unfortunately, I lack the expertise to locate the Sanskrit text by
Jareca in a particular time period." (p. 100, folio not stated); "Unfortunately, there is little in the
precolonial literature regarding the actual practices of walking to the Hinglaj shrine." (p. 112, folio
not stated); "I have, however, not been able to locate the text passages in any of the Skanda Purāṇa
editions available to me." (p. 99, folio not stated); and of the claim that the Hinglaj narrative
belongs to the Skanda Purāṇa, "I have not been able to verify this claim." (p. 65, folio not stated)
**A user of this book for an archive entry should quote the hedge with the fact.**

### shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir — Shaktipeeth Shri Hinglaj Mata Mandir

**This is the subject of the entire book**, and it is the densest single treatment of any site in
this corpus. Nineteen of the nineteen chunks carry material for it. The book's title page reads
"Hinglaj Devi / Identity, Change, and Solidification at a / Hindu Temple in Pakistan" by "JÜRGEN
SCHAFLECHNER" (p. 3, folio not stated), "© Oxford University Press 2018" (p. 4, folio not stated).
The Library of Congress heading, which is not the author's own wording, reads: "Subjects: LCSH:
Hinglaj Devi (Temple : Hinglaj, Pakistan) | Hindu pilgrims and pilgrimages—Pakistan—Hinglaj. |
BISAC: RELIGION / Hinduism / Rituals & Practice. | SOCIAL SCIENCE / Anthropology / Cultural."
(p. 4, folio not stated). The fieldwork base is "my intensive fieldwork and textual research between
2009 and 2015" (p. 14, folio not stated), restated as "the fieldwork for this book (2009–2015)"
(p. 49, folio not stated).

**Names, as the book prints them, gathered across all nineteen chunks and not normalised.** In the
author's English: `Hinglaj`, `Hinglaj Devi`, `Hinglaj Mata`, `Mata Hinglaj`, `the Hinglaj shrine`,
`the Hinglaj temple`, `the temple of Hinglaj in Balochistan`, `her Balochistan temple`, `the
Balochistan shrine`, `the desert shrine`, `the desert Goddess`, `the Goddess's abode`, `the Mother's
abode`, `the aloof shrine of the Goddess`, `the formerly aloof Balochistan temple`, `the valley of
Hinglaj`, `the Hinglaj Valley`, `the sacred valley`, `Hinglaj/Nani`, `the valley of Hinglaj/Nani`,
`the original [mūl] Hinglaj in Pakistan` (pp. 13, 23–29, 103, 115, 136, 145, 150, 167, 172, 175,
180–181, 221–223, 244, 251–268, 276–297, 306–326, folios not stated). In transliteration from the
Sanskrit and the vernaculars: `Hiṅgulā`, `Hiṅgulāja`, `Hiṅgulāṭa`, `Hiṃgulā`, `Hiṅglāja`,
`Hiṅghalāja`, `Hiṃg'lāj`, `Hiṅglāj`, `Hiṃlāj`, `hiṅgula`, and undiacriticked `Hingulata` and
`Hingula` (pp. 52–57, 98, 136, 144, folios not stated). In Urdu, from a leaflet: `hinglāj mātā`
(p. 100, folio not stated). In Masson's 1842 English: `Hinglátz` (p. 91, folio not stated). On the
site's own English signboard: "Mata Mandir" and "Mata Hinghlaj [sic] Mandir" (p. 284, folio not
stated) — **the `[sic]` is the book's own.** Under Muslim traditions the site and the Goddess are
`Bibi Nani`, `Nani Hinglaj`, `Nani` (pp. 24, 89–91, 108, folios not stated), and in Hart's quoted
legend the Goddess is `Shree Mata` (p. 114, folio not stated). **The archive row's own name,
`Shaktipeeth Shri Hinglaj Mata Mandir`, matches none of these exactly** — see Doubts.

- **Saint / deity**
  - **The archive row's `principal_figure` cell is empty and the book supplies a candidate the row
    does not carry.** Schaflechner's subject is "the Goddess Hinglaj" / "Hinglaj Devi" (pp. 29–30,
    folios not stated), also "Hinglaj Mata", "Mata Hinglaj", "the Mother", "the Devi", "maiyā", "the
    Mahashakti Hinglaj" (pp. 64, 68, 70, 150, 449 n/a — pp. 64–70, 150, folios not stated).
    **Whether that name should be written into the row's `principal_figure` column is a human
    decision and is not made here.**
  - **She is a clan and caste Goddess (Hin. `kul'devī`) for named communities.** "members of the
    Brahmakshatriya, Charan, Kapri, and Lohana communities in particular have produced ritual
    manuals, mytho-historical narratives, travelogues, video documentations, institutions, and other
    material that has influenced the course of events at Hinglaj lately" (p. 58, folio not stated);
    "the Brahmakshatriya branch in particular explains its genealogy through the intervention of the
    Goddess in Balochistan" (p. 59, folio not stated); "Hinglaj is the clan Goddess (Hin. kul'devī)
    of the Dewanis, a Sindhi Lohana family from Las Bela" (p. 274, folio not stated); "Many of these
    Lasi Lohana communities worship the Goddess Hinglaj as their clan Goddess" (p. 82, folio not
    stated). In the years before the HSM, "certain castes and communities, like the
    Brahmakshatriyas, the Charans, and the Kapris, used to visit the shrine of Hinglaj and worship
    the Goddess as patron or clan deity. Other local communities living in Sindh and in Balochistan,
    both Hindu and (Zikri) Muslim, also claim an ancient connection with the desert shrine."
    (p. 320, folio not stated)
  - **The Charan bond and its two obligations.** "The Charan in particular claim to have a special
    bond with Hinglaj in Balochistan, and the pilgrimage to her shrine at some point in one's life is
    mandatory for many of them (Westphal and Westphal-Hellbusch 1974, 311)." (p. 69, folio not
    stated) "Charans often say that when a boy is born into the community, a bell rings at the
    Hinglaj temple in Balochistan. Furthermore, it is the responsibility of every Charan male to
    visit the Goddess at Hinglaj before his 60th birthday; otherwise a rock will tumble down into the
    sacred valley, eventually burying the shrine." (p. 70, folio not stated) "For the male members of
    the Charan jāti, for example, the journey to the shrine ought to be undertaken once in their
    lifetime." (p. 168, folio not stated) She is their `ādiśakti`: "the primordial energy that has
    proliferated through their community for many generations (Westphal and Westphal-Hellbusch 1976;
    Samaur 1999; Kamphorst 2008)" (p. 172, folio not stated).
  - **Contested inside the Charani Sagati.** "according to Charan narratives, Hinglaj herself was
    once a Charani (Samaur 1999, 503). In fact, she is said to have been the first of countless
    incarnations of the Goddess in this world." (p. 70, folio not stated) Kamphorst, quoted: "it is
    the Mahashakti Hinglaj herself who is credited with the heroic deeds of all her avatārīs.
    (Kamphorst 2008, 244)" (p. 70, folio not stated). Against that: "the notion that Hinglaj is
    associated with the circle of Charani Sagati is also contested among Charans today. While some
    are proud to include Hinglaj into this circle of incarnations… others are of the opinion that
    Hinglaj is the ādiśakti, the primordial divine power that can only ever partially incarnate into
    this mundane world." (p. 71, folio not stated) **The book resolves neither position.**
  - **A "historical" Hinglaj, in three incompatible accounts the book keeps side by side.** Samaur:
    her origins lie in the "gaur'viyā cāraṇ" branch from Thatta, "her father's name was Haridas", and
    that Thatta connection made the saṃnyāsīs of the Charan and the Udasin community hold "that the
    temple in Thatta was as important as the main shrine in Balochistan (Samaur 1999, 503)" (p. 71,
    folio not stated). P. P. Payaka, in reproduction: "Hala in Sindh was called Kohala in the former
    times, and the Goddess Hinglaj was once the queen of the Kohala or Kohana area. In this account,
    Hinglaj is portrayed as a Charani and the leader of the Tumbel Charan, whom she guided from Sindh
    to the Makran. P. P. Payaka also mentions that she remained a virgin all her life and was well
    versed in the religious texts (Westphal and Westphal-Hellbusch 1974, 315)." (p. 71, folio not
    stated) Kamphorst: "the woman's name was 'Charani Deval'", dated to the eighth century (p. 71,
    folio not stated). **Three accounts, no reconciliation.**
  - **The Kapri relation, and initiation by emergence from the cave.** "The Encyclopedia of Religion
    and Ethics only mentions that the Kapris are followers of the Goddess Ashapura and that they
    frequently visit the shrine of Hinglaj in Balochistan (Hastings and Selbie 2003, 716)." (p. 75,
    folio not stated) "For the Kapri community, anyone who emerges from Hinglaj's cave in Balochistan
    is automatically initiated into the group and can assume the title 'Kapri.'" (p. 75, folio not
    stated) Depavat: "everyone who comes out of the Goddess's womb (Hin. garbha) also attains the
    title 'kāpaḍiyā' (Depavat 2010, 147)"; Kamphorst: "In this way all travellers become religious
    brothers and assume the title Kapadiya" (Kamphorst 2008, 238) (p. 75, folio not stated).
  - **The Goddess as vegetarian, with two standing counter-positions the book does not adjudicate.**
    "Today, the Goddess Hinglaj is generally understood as a vegetarian deity who only accepts
    non-animal offerings from her worshippers." (p. 285, folio not stated) Against this: "Some
    colonial sources, however, also show that until recently, it was customary for many people to eat
    the sacrificed animals at the Kali shrine located just some meters before the temple of Hinglaj
    (Goldsmid 1863, 207)." (p. 285, folio not stated) And against *that*: "Some Sindhi Hindu authors,
    however, claim this to be a tradition alien to Hinglaj, brought to the shrine by foreigners
    (Khatri 1973, 10)." (p. 285, folio not stated) **Three positions, printed in that order; the
    author resolves none of them.** The vegetarian emphasis is dated: "After 2001, when the shrine
    rose in importance as a unifying signifier for the entire Pakistani-Hindu community, the critical
    role of vegetarianism became increasingly emphasized at Hinglaj by the HSM." (p. 285, folio not
    stated)
  - **Venerated as a Muslim pīr.** "It further explains how the veneration of Hinglaj as the pīr
    'Bibi Nani' within certain Muslim traditions allows her to exert considerable power within
    modern-day Sindhi nationalist discourses." (p. 24, folio not stated) **`Bibi Nani` is printed
    inside double quotation marks and without diacritics there**; the book gives no shrine, tomb or
    ʿurs under that name: **(not stated)**. Present-day Zikris "see Hinglaj as a Balochi 'pīr' (Urd.
    saint) who is a part of their distinct culture and who has the power to fulfill wishes." (p. 90,
    folio not stated) The Zikris "consider Hinglaj to be the grave of one of Prophet Mohammad's
    family members" (p. 325, folio not stated); **the family member is not named**: **(not stated)**.
  - **The name's etymology, twice.** "the etymology of the name Hinglaj, which relates to the
    Sanskrit word 'hiṅgula' (also hiṅguli, hiṅgulu), meaning 'vermillion' or 'cinnabar.'" (p. 57,
    folio not stated); "It is said that as a pious, married Hindu woman, Sati wore vermillion (Hin.
    'siṃdūr') in her hair, and, hence, after the upper part of her head fell in Balochistan, the
    place became known as Hiṅgula, which eventually was changed to Hinglaj (Billimoria 1944, 93)."
    (p. 57, folio not stated) A second etymological lead: "the term hiṅgu (Skt.; Hin. also, hīṃg or
    hiṃgu) also means asafoetida, a plant used frequently in cooking and found in the desert area
    around Hinglaj" (p. 98, folio not stated).

- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **Where the book puts it, and what it never gives.** "the temple of Hinglaj in Balochistan"
    (p. 13, folio not stated); "the Hinglaj shrine and its ritual journeys in Balochistan" (p. 23,
    folio not stated); "one particular site located somewhere in the far-off desert of Balochistan"
    (p. 24, folio not stated); "the Goddess's remote abode in the desert of Balochistan" (p. 25,
    folio not stated); "Hinglaj Valley" (p. 25, folio not stated); "the entry to the Hingol National
    Park" (p. 152, folio not stated); "the Hingol National Park, in which the shrine is located"
    (p. 295, folio not stated) — **the last is the one sentence in the book that states the park
    containment as a fact about the shrine, and it corroborates the archive row's own
    `location_short`.** **No district, no tehsil, no road address and no coordinates appear anywhere
    in the book**: **(not stated)**. The LC heading places the temple at "Hinglaj, Pakistan" and
    gives no province (p. 4, folio not stated).
  - **Remoteness as the historical condition of everything else.** "For centuries, the shrine of
    Hinglaj was famous for being extremely difficult to reach. Its location in the arid desert of
    Balochistan, far away from any urban settlements, made the journey exceptionally demanding. Many
    reports document the deaths of pilgrims either coming to or returning from the shrine." (p. 29,
    folio not stated) **No report is named, no pilgrim is named and no number of deaths is given**:
    **(not stated)**. "The elusiveness of the location was also confirmed by the members of the 4x4
    Off-Road Club of Karachi, whom I met in 2010. The group had been going to the Hingol Valley
    repeatedly and only found the shrine after a few years of searching." (p. 100, folio not stated)
    "as little as thirty years ago, only a handful of people actually knew the exact location of the
    shrine" (p. 82, folio not stated). Before the 1980s "the shrine's character was marked by this
    reputation for danger—perpetuated in literature, movies, and oral traditions" (p. 97, folio not
    stated); the site "formerly earned most of its 'symbolic capital' (Bourdieu 1998) from its
    distant desert location and resulting restricted mobility" (p. 86, folio not stated).
  - **The temple's fabric, described in the author's own voice — the fullest architectural passage
    in the book.** "A couple of hundred meters after the Kali Mata shrine, the confining walls of the
    Hinglaj Mata temple on the southern side of the valley spring into view. The walls are built on a
    stone platform leading into a natural cave that was apparently carved out of the mountain by a
    river ages ago. Only the northern and eastern walls are made of stone bricks and attach to the
    naturally formed hollow producing the foundations of the temple" (p. 223, folio not stated). "The
    cave forms a natural arch about 9 meters high at its peak and about 64 meters in length." (p. 225,
    folio not stated) "The current stone walls inside the cave's confines support no roof since the
    overhanging arch of the cave forms a natural shelter from sun and rain." (p. 225, folio not
    stated)
  - **The age of the enclosure is explicitly undetermined, and the author lays out the whole
    evidence.** The earliest photograph available is from "Marc Aurel Stein's 1943 account", which
    shows "that the basic stone structure had already been built in the mid-twentieth century (Stein
    1943, 196)" (p. 225, folio not stated). "the earliest colonial sources on the Hinglaj
    Valley—Wilford (1851), Hart (1839), and Goldsmid (1863)—do not mention any stable stone
    structure; Hart and Goldsmid instead describe a 'mud edifice' (Hart 1839, 95; Goldsmid 1863,
    208)" (p. 225, folio not stated). "Later, in his travelogue from a 1961 journey, Nabi Bux Khan
    Baloch mentions a stone structure, estimating the age of the building to be somewhere between 50
    to 60 years (Khan Baloch 1970, 132). These accounts suggest that the confining walls must be
    roughly a century old." (p. 225, folio not stated) "There is neither a foundation stone nor is
    any inscription visible today that could help to determine the building's exact age. To date, I
    have been unable to find any sources from between the years 1861 and 1943 mentioning the temple,
    and no one from the HSM or any other local people were able to determine the age of the walls.
    Thus, the exact age of the enclosure remains unknown." (p. 225, folio not stated) A tradition he
    will not vouch for: "I often heard that the walls were built gradually over time with stones from
    the valley by pilgrims and mendicants. The veracity of this claim, however, cannot be
    determined." (p. 225, folio not stated) **No architect, no patron and no foundation date
    anywhere in the book**: **(not stated)**.
  - **Masson's 1842 description, the earliest physical account the book quotes** (p. 91, folio not
    stated): "The sacred locality is called Hinglátz. It is understood to be consecrated to Parbatí,
    the goddess of nature, the universal mother, &c., or Diana, the moon, &c. By Máhommedans, by whom
    it is alike revered, the shrine is considered as one of Bíbí Nánī, the lady Nání, or the motherly
    lady. It is possible they have preserved the ancient name NANAIA, that of the goddess of the old
    Persians, and the Bactrians, and now so well known to us by coins. There is a small mat or temple
    at Hinglátz, but the chief attractions appear to be natural objects, as a kand, or reservoir of
    water; a well, of unfathomable depth, above the mat; and the semblance the mural disposition of
    the rock presents, in a certain spot, to that of a fortress. There are also said to be figures of
    the sun and the moon hewn on the rock, in an accessible site. (Masson 1977, 390f.)" The author
    adds: "This is the first explicit textual mention of the name 'Bibi Nani' available to me and is
    one of the primary references to a Muslim influence at the shrine within colonial literature."
    (p. 91, folio not stated)
  - **Datable building works, all attributed, none with a named architect or patron.** A concrete
    platform supporting "the northern (front) portion of the temple" at the time of fieldwork
    (p. 225, folio not stated); on the eastern side "a set of stairs constructed around 1998 to
    replace a simple stone heap" leading to "the inner courtyard of the temple where larger groups
    can gather for the pūjā or bhajans" (p. 225, folio not stated); "The HSM demolished the divider
    between the two rooms around 1998 in order to create a wider space to accommodate the crowds"
    (p. 225, folio not stated); "During the HSM's renovations in the late 1990s, the walls were
    cleaned and the inside of the temple was completely covered with tiles" (p. 225, folio not
    stated); "At the end of the 1990s, an additional room was built opposite the entrance for the
    residing pujārī, Gopal Maharaj. During the melā, this place is used for storing drinking water."
    (p. 225, folio not stated). **The renovation is dated four ways across the book and the fourth
    disagrees**: "Around the end of the 1990s, the Mandali began renovations in the temple" (p. 227,
    folio not stated); "in the late 1990s, the HSM directed the visitor's gaze to a narrow area of
    the stone cluster" (p. 229, folio not stated); "the HSM's refurbishing work and the subsequent
    tiling of the sanctum at the end of the 1990s" (pp. 235–236, folio not stated); and **"The HSM's
    construction work in the early 1990s"** (p. 248, folio not stated). See Doubts.
  - **What the renovation destroyed.** "Older photographs and videotapes show that pilgrims who
    successfully reached the shrine would write their names and addresses on the walls. Inside the
    temple, many names were visible written in Gujarati, Nastaliq, English, and Bengali." (p. 225,
    folio not stated) "Unfortunately, this reconstruction work also obliterated many traces of the
    temple's history, complicating a historical survey." (p. 225, folio not stated) "In the past,
    this inner space was separated into two rooms: one where music was played and the other where
    food was stored (Hart 1839, 95; Goldsmid 1863, 208; Abro 1991, 62)." (p. 225, folio not stated)
  - **Who paid.** "While many buildings inside the Hinglaj Valley were financed by the provincial
    government of Balochistan, according to the HSM, the reconstruction work at the temple was
    supported mainly by donations from the Hindu community." (p. 226, folio not stated) Planning "is
    done in communication with other actors, such as the Balochistan government, which represents an
    important source of financial means for construction work at the temple" (p. 245, folio not
    stated). "In recent years, the HSM gradually improved the infrastructure around the temple by
    constructing shelters, water tanks, and roads in and near the valley. Such projects were made
    possible by financial support from private national and international Hindu donors together with
    grants from the Balochistan government." (p. 323, folio not stated) **No sums, no dates, no named
    donor and no named government department**: **(not stated)**. **No waqf, no Auqaf and no
    endowment of any kind is named anywhere in the book** (p. 84, folio not stated, where only the
    Balochistan provincial government appears as funder): **(not stated)**.
  - **The image of the Goddess, measured.** "The image of Hinglaj is located on the far west side of
    the temple, where the arch of the natural cave inside which the temple is nestled reaches down to
    meet the ground. Now considered to be the main image of the Goddess, the representation rests on
    an oblong stone of approximately 1 meter by 1.5 meters sitting on the far west side of a wide
    platform about 1 meter off the ground. The platform itself is around 5.5 to 7.5 meters wide, but
    it is irregular instead of rectangular in shape" (p. 226, folio not stated). "The platform was
    built around the base of a huge rock, or maybe a mud hill, of unknown age (Hart 1839, 95)."
    (p. 226, folio not stated) Figure caption: "FIGURE 5.6 Hinglaj Devi, 2012" (p. 226, folio not
    stated). Elsewhere the platform "was originally built on the foundations of a small rock or mud
    hill (Hin. miṭṭī kā ṭīlā), the age of which is uncertain" (p. 247, folio not stated).
  - **The image is an oblong stone, not a carved statue, and its contact with the cave wall is what
    grounds the claim of self-creation.** "the oblong stone upon which Hinglaj/Nani's image rests is
    partly covered with tiles, and only several stone bumps are visible atop it" (p. 229, folio not
    stated). "The Goddess's image seems to rest on an extension of the cave wall, under which there
    is a small channel" (p. 227, folio not stated). "It is this alleged connection of the oblong
    stone with the cave wall that has endowed the image with the characteristic of svayaṃbhū"
    (p. 227, folio not stated). In the sanctum description of Chapter 5: "Inside the temple, the
    sanctum, an oblong stone with some raised mounds atop it, along with a small rift in the mountain
    wall called the garbha (Hin. womb), are also considered to be svayaṃbhū." (p. 200, folio not
    stated)
  - **Two globular stones are the present focus, and their faces are a modern addition.**
    "Representations of eyes and noses have also been attached to the two round stones today to
    bestow the rocks with human faces. Over the course of time, pilgrims have also brought women's
    ornaments, crowns, and umbrellas to decorate the image." (p. 229, folio not stated) **No date is
    given for when the eyes and noses were attached**: **(not stated)**. The whole cluster is read as
    "an anthropomorphic figure lying flat with its head toward the east and its legs toward the west"
    (p. 231, folio not stated), formed of "several connected stones or piṃḍ (Hin. lump, a ball,
    stones that are worshipped as images of the Goddess)" (p. 231, folio not stated). "In recent
    decades, these stones have been covered with layer after layer of vermilion paste (Hin. siṃdūr)"
    (p. 232, folio not stated), and "the figure's alleged body parts from the head downward are
    always covered with red and orange cloth, and thus its full shape is no longer visible" (p. 232,
    folio not stated). The Sanskrit names support an anthropomorphic reading: "The names 'Koṭṭarī,'
    'Koṭṭavī,' and 'Koṭṭari' refer to a naked woman in Sanskrit, which again would hint at the
    possibility of an anthropomorphic figure—that of a naked woman (maybe giving birth) seen in the
    stone clusters (Skt. piṇḍī) and worshipped as the shrine's sanctum sanctorum." (p. 98, folio not
    stated)
  - **The tiling of the oblong stone, the single most consequential alteration.** "The single most
    important change made to the Goddess's image was certainly the tiling of the oblong stone, which
    led to the subsequent backgrounding of the visual impression of a Muslim gravestone." (p. 261,
    folio not stated) "While Hart, Goldsmid, and Stein describe the sanctum as a rectangular stone
    resembling the form of a Muslim grave with some 'diminutive domes' (Goldsmid 1863, 208), on top,
    the tiling of the oblong stone shifted the attention from the structure as a whole to the stone
    heaps on top, now emphasized as comprising the Goddess's image." (p. 261, folio not stated) **No
    date for the tiling**: **(not stated)**. The conclusion restates it: "One of the most significant
    measures undertaken by the temple committee was the renovation of the Hinglaj temple structure
    and, in particular, the remodeling of the Goddess's image." (pp. 324–325, folio not stated)
    "While older pictures of this sanctum sanctorum show that the inner part of the temple resembled
    the shape of a saintly figure's grave, hence potentially supporting Zikri Muslim religious
    imaginaries at the shrine, the HSM countered this impression with the tiling of the temple's
    inside." (pp. 324–325, folio not stated) **The pictures are neither dated nor sourced there**:
    **(not stated)**.
  - **The Muslim reading of the same stone, recorded by Stein and carried with its damage flag.**
    "Muhammadan pilgrims who also visit Hinglaj take the stone block, which a carved railing bearing
    various sacred emblems surrounds, as marking the grave of a saintly wife of `cAli`[text-layer?]
    [ʿAli], miraculously translated to this spot." (p. 235, folio not stated, quoting Stein 1943,
    202). The author generalises: there are "two possible modes of interpreting the image that
    existed prior to the HSM's institutionalization of the shrine: one interpretation sees the site
    as the gravestone of a saintly figure in Islam (interchangeably the grandmother, nanny, or wife
    of Ali), and the other interpretation sees it as the (lying) representation of the Hindu Devi or
    the Goddess Nani" (p. 235, folio not stated); the community is "the (Zikri) Muslim history of the
    shrine, associated with the grave of a saintly figure" (p. 236, folio not stated), "the local
    (Zikri) Muslim religious imaginary" (p. 246, folio not stated). **No Zikri custodian, no Zikri
    ritual and no Zikri date is given**: **(not stated)**.
  - **Two inscriptions, which must not be merged, and neither is dated.** "Particularly regrettable
    is the partial obstruction of a Hindi inscription on the back of the oblong stone … some of the
    words from the inscription were partially buried under concrete and tiles. Today, only the name
    of the donor, who apparently helped construct the image's foundation, is visible." (p. 227, folio
    not stated) **The donor's name itself is not given**: **(not stated)**, and "if there was any
    date on this inscription, it is no longer visible" (p. 227, folio not stated). "it can only be
    surmised that the present inscription—and therefore parts of the structure around the
    image—must have appeared between 1943 and 1961 when Khan Baloch's party visited the shrine"
    (p. 227, folio not stated). A second, different inscription transliterated by Ishtiaq Ansari
    "must have been produced after Khan Baloch's visit in 1961" (p. 227, folio not stated). The two
    texts, in note 25: "The text reads: 'I have taken refuge in the fold of the Mother Shri Hinglaj.
    You keep my sins safe, my [second] birth was successful. Landlord Dhasaram Kemchand Patel's
    deceased wife's parents, Guru, Gosain, and the seven generations should prosper!' (Ansari 2001,
    333). The current text on the back of the image reads: 'Following the order of Shri Matavala
    Baba, Jagat Janani ordered to build this lion's throne for the Mother Hinglaj.' As already
    mentioned above, unfortunately, this is all that is decipherable today." (p. 264, folio not
    stated — Chapter 5 note 25). The author states plainly what would settle the question: "an
    archeological survey of the site would be necessary to uncover the exact age of the structure
    around the image" (p. 227, folio not stated). "Stein does not mention any inscription in his
    account (Stein 1943)" (p. 227, folio not stated).
  - **Two engraved footprints survive beside the inscription.** Khan Baloch "says that they had found
    the inscription directly in front of two engraved footsteps of a holy man … the two footprints he
    mentions are still clearly visible directly in front of the writing" (p. 227, folio not stated).
    **The holy man is not named**: **(not stated)**.
  - **The temple interior and its two zones.** "The platform holding the oblong stone structure and
    the image of Hinglaj separates the inside of the temple into two parts: the western, elevated
    part is mostly used for pūjā and ār'tī, and the lower, eastern part is for housing bigger groups
    of pilgrims during the festivals." (p. 228, folio not stated)
  - **The garbha tunnel, with measurements and a staged, partly man-made history.** Two doors "each
    around 90 centimeters high … lead into the tunnel underneath the platform" and "form the entrance
    and exit to the Goddess's garbha (Hin. womb) mentioned above, a U-shaped tunnel forming a
    semicircle underneath the image and opening up in the temple yard" (p. 228, folio not stated). "At
    its tallest, this tunnel is no more than 90 centimeters high, and at certain points, it is barely
    large enough for a person to fit through." (p. 247, folio not stated) Fabric: "one can clearly
    distinguish between the manmade work—however old it might be—and the natural cleavage in the
    stone of the mountain walls" (p. 249, folio not stated); the right-hand wall is "a stone and brick
    wall that, according to the resident pujārī, was built to stabilize the mud heap" (p. 249, folio
    not stated); the low roof is "supported by wooden crossbeams" at both ends (p. 249, folio not
    stated); "This crevice in the rock appears to be the oldest part of the tunnel, which is located
    directly underneath, or very close to the oblong stone on which the image of the Goddess rests"
    (p. 249, folio not stated). The author infers "an old exit from the tunnel" where the natural roof
    gives way to masonry (p. 249, folio not stated). "No member of the HSM I spoke with knew the age
    of the masonry or of the construction around the tunnel, and some were reluctant to talk about
    the organization's involvement in the process." (p. 248, folio not stated) Dating: "Goldsmid
    found a semicircular tunnel already in place by the time of his visit to the shrine and thus …
    the 'U' shape of the tunnel can be dated to at least the mid-nineteenth century" (p. 250, folio
    not stated); for Hart's time "it is very likely that in the mid-nineteenth century, the tunnel
    opened up on the northern side" (p. 250, folio not stated). And: "at one point, the natural rift
    from the mountain wall simply ends, and the man-made masonry begins. Thus, it is likely that in
    some undefined past, the tunnel was much shorter and was not in its current 'U' shape." (p. 251,
    folio not stated); "In the middle of the twentieth century, the allegedly natural tunnel looping
    beneath the image of the Goddess had already been supported and stabilized with man-made means."
    (p. 251, folio not stated); the stabilisation "could have happened at the same time as the
    construction of the outer stone wall or at some other point in the eighty years between
    Goldsmid's and Stein's visits" (p. 251, folio not stated); "From videotapes made around the end
    of the 1980s, one can see that the HSM later extended the tunnel on both sides to create the full
    'U' shape under the platform." (p. 251, folio not stated) **No exact date, no builder and no cost
    for any of these works**: **(not stated)**. The author's own fieldwork and the photography
    embargo: "I myself crawled through the tunnel over a dozen times, and one time I took pictures
    with my camera... The volunteers at the shrine allowed me to take photographs, but I had to
    promise not to publish them. However, Abro (1991) has published pictures of the tunnel." (p. 265,
    folio not stated — Chapter 5 note 29); "At no point today does the tunnel open to a cave where a
    man could stand up straight. If Goldsmid's account is accurate, then this would again show the
    massive changes the tunnel has undergone since the nineteenth century." (p. 265, folio not
    stated — note 31).
  - **Two incompatible glosses of `garbha`, one page apart, kept side by side.** p. 200 (folio not
    stated): "a small rift in the mountain wall called the garbha (Hin. womb)"; p. 201 (folio not
    stated): "the garbha, the tunnel underneath the sanctum". The same p. 200 also speaks separately
    of "the tunnel underneath the mūrti". **The book does not resolve which the garbha is.**
  - **The dhūnī / yajña kuṃḍ and its two removals.** "For a long time, this dhūnī or yajña kuṃḍ
    (Hin.), into which ritual offerings to the Goddess are given, was in the center of the oblong
    stone until the 1990s when it was shifted to the front of the image." (p. 233, folio not stated)
    "Recently, a large yajña kuṃḍ has been built in the yard in front of the temple." (p. 233, folio
    not stated) **No completion date for the outdoor kuṃḍ**: **(not stated)**.
  - **A śiva liṅga removed from the temple by the HSM.** "This liṅga was removed by the HSM in the
    1990s and thrown into the Sharan Kund (Hin. refuge-pond), which, before it dried up, was directly
    in front of the Hinglaj temple." (p. 238, folio not stated) Hart's companions called the conical
    stone "Sadasheo" (Hart 1839, 96) (p. 238, folio not stated); Abro's Sindhi form is "sadā śivo"
    (p. 241, folio not stated), and the book says the two are "most likely the same" (p. 241, folio
    not stated). **Three removal windows are given for what may be one or two objects — "some later
    time" after the 1940s, "the 1990s", "the mid-1990s", and a fourth window "sometime between 1943
    and the early 1990s" for "the globular stone that was so evident in Stein's 1943 photograph"
    (pp. 238, 241, 242, folios not stated). Do not resolve; see Doubts.**
  - **Sharan Kund, a ritual station that ceased inside the author's own fieldwork.** "Sharan Kund,
    for example, was once a pool lying directly in front of the temple of Hinglaj (see Fig. 5.17). It
    was then the last place for a purification bath before the pilgrims entered into the garbha of
    the Goddess (Lakhavat 2011, 53; Kaviya 2006, 3)." (p. 251, folio not stated) "Devotees would
    submerge three times under the fresh water together with their chosen śaraṇ'bhāi/bahan (Barhath
    2006, 31) before they would be permitted to enter the Goddess's abode." (p. 251, folio not
    stated) "over the past few years, this pond gradually filled up with debris and by 2009 was
    barely visible" (p. 251, folio not stated); "while in 2009 it was still obvious that a large pool
    had once been at this place, by 2011, the area was covered over with concrete, making evident the
    HSM's lack of any intention to dig it up again" (p. 251, folio not stated). Figure caption:
    "FIGURE 5.17 Sharan Kund in front of Hinglaj in 1943 (Stein 1943)" (p. 252, folio not stated).
    **Other names in the literature: "Kanyar Kun" (Jajot 2011, 56) and "Hinglaj Kund" (Barhath 2006,
    31)** (p. 264, folio not stated — note 27). "The foreman responsible for the construction work in
    the valley mentioned that he had redug the kuṃḍ several times until the HSM decided it was too
    much effort to maintain the pond." (p. 251, folio not stated) **The foreman is not named**:
    **(not stated)**.
  - **The other named features of the valley, as the book names them.** "the Goddess's tunnel (Hin.
    garbha)" (p. 180, folio not stated); "one of the pilgrim shelters" (p. 180, folio not stated);
    "Anil Kund, a pond atop one of the hill chains in Chaurasi" (pp. 180–181, folio not stated);
    "the ponds that lay a little way inside the valley" and "Til Kund" (p. 181, folio not stated);
    "the River Aghor" (p. 173, folio not stated) / "the Aghor River" (p. 175, folio not stated);
    "Brahma Kund... named after Brahma" (p. 255, folio not stated); Kali Kund and Kir Kund
    (pp. 254–255, folio not stated); "the Kali and Hinglaj temple" inside the main part of the valley
    (p. 165, folio not stated) and "the Kali Mata temple, the main location for the bali" (p. 296,
    folio not stated); "the shrine of Kali Mata" (p. 284, folio not stated). Also "the Hinglaj
    Valley—with its springs, ponds, and caves" (p. 200, folio not stated). **The Kali temple inside
    the valley is not any of the archive's Kali rows — see Doubts.**
  - **New structures added inside the valley, year by year.** (i) "Some years ago, Gujarati Hindus
    from Karachi began constructing a small booth for Melari (Meladi) Mata in the heart of the valley.
    By 2013, that booth had become a small concrete temple, tiled in a manner similar to Hinglaj and
    housing a statue of the Goddess about one and a half meters tall." (p. 271, folio not stated)
    (ii) "Around the beginning of the millennium, another group of visitors had a vision of Momai
    Mata a short distance up the mountains on the way to Chaurasi, where they later constructed a
    wooden edifice that has been expanded over the years." (p. 271, folio not stated) (iii) A Ganesha
    temple built over an installed statue at the paṛāv outside the valley (pp. 279–280, folio not
    stated), photographed as "The inside of the constructed Ganesha temple, 2010" (p. 281, folio not
    stated). "almost every year, pilgrims make additions or revisions to the different temples,
    statues, and ritual places" (p. 271, folio not stated). "Some of these, like the temple of Melari
    Mata, have financial backing from certain communities allowing them to become established
    religious sites, while others, more flimsy, ultimately vanish beneath the valley's rare but
    fierce rainfall." (p. 271, folio not stated) **No architect, no patron and no cost for any of
    them**: **(not stated)**.
  - **The valley's boundary, dated.** "Today, the point at which the Hinglaj Valley begins is clearly
    marked by a wall built by the HSM in 1996." (p. 217, folio not stated) Also "these shelters they
    [the HSM] have built there" (p. 290, folio not stated); "In those days, the path to Hinglaj
    temple was not yet extended and people had to climb over stones and through bushes to reach it.
    One of the easier paths led directly past the Kali Mata temple, the main location for the bali."
    (p. 296, folio not stated)
  - **Building work dated to 2012, with a discovery.** "In 2012, when the Mandali had started
    building a new road to the shrine and the front courtyard of the temple was being cemented to
    accommodate the crowds at the annual melās, workers found a new cave toward the east of the
    temple that had been buried under the sand. Gopal proudly showed it to me and wondered about the
    depth of the cave, which was not ascertainable at that time." (p. 265, folio not stated — note
    30).
  - **Ecological decline inside the valley, reported by visitors and endorsed by the author.**
    "Before the Hingol River was flowing, but now it stopped. The water at Ashapura, the water at
    Kali Mata . . . everything dried out due to the crowds." (p. 188, folio not stated) The cause
    given: "You know it is now a picnic place. Due to the coming and going, the littering, the usage
    of water for the children . . . all of these things caused the water to dry up." (p. 188, folio
    not stated) The author's summary: "the crowds are also responsible for major ecological changes
    inside the valley. The masses hinder the 'real' pilgrims from their spiritual experience and are
    even responsible for the drying up of sacred ponds." (p. 189, folio not stated)
  - **A restricted precinct at the shrine, from Hart.** "A similar place is mentioned by Hart, who
    describes an area which he has been told is named 'sath durwazu' (probably from Sin. 'seven
    doors') and which contains the private rooms of the Goddess. To spit on or defile this area is
    considered a serious transgression (Hart 1839, 91)." (p. 263, folio not stated — Chapter 5 note
    6).
  - **A feature the author could not verify.** "If one follows the work of Ansari (2001), then a
    large agnī kuṇḍ (Sin. fire place) was also located right behind what is assumed to be the face of
    the Goddess. Unfortunately, Ansari's maps of the area tend to be imprecise at times, and since I
    have not found another account confirming this, the information cannot be verified." (p. 264,
    folio not stated — note 26).
  - **Custodianship before 1986: the Zikris and the Dost Mohammad family.** "The Zikris, in fact,
    were in charge of the shrine for many years, until in 1986 when the organization was taken over
    by the HSM, the current Hindu temple committee. Before the 1980s, Dost Mohammad was the main
    caretaker of Hinglaj, and his descendants still work today for the HSM as cooks, guides, and
    drivers." (p. 90, folio not stated) "The family of Dost Mohammad, who had been the caretakers of
    the shrine for many generations, could not recall a time when a female was in charge at Hinglaj."
    (p. 115, folio not stated) "Until his death in the late 1990s, Dost Mohammad guided the pilgrims
    through this steep and challenging landscape, explaining the various sites along the way
    (Jethamalani 1994, 9)." (p. 255, folio not stated) The successor is named in note 33: "At the
    time of my visit in 2012, the tour to Chaurasi was still a family tradition and was guided by his
    son, Hussain." (p. 265, folio not stated — note 33). The 2010 group was guided at the shrine
    "under the guidance of a descendant of Dost Mohammad, whose family had been in charge of the
    shrine until the HSM took over the organization in 1986" (p. 181, folio not stated). **Dost
    Mohammad is given no given name beyond "Dost Mohammad", no office title, no birth year, no exact
    death year and no lineage; Hussain is given no dates**: **(not stated)**. The index has
    "Mohammad, Dost, 83, 106, 207, 241" (p. 353, folio not stated), **an index locator run and
    nothing more**.
  - **The temple committee: the Hinglaj Sheva Mandali (HSM), with a full foundation date.** "The
    Hinglaj Sheva Mandali (Urd. Hinglāj Śevā Manḍalī; in the following also HSM) was established in
    order to promote an annual pilgrimage to the shrine. It was created on January 5, 1986, in Bela
    (Las Bela district), Balochistan, and continues to be the main organization serving the temple
    and its pilgrims (Jethamalani 1994, 9)." (p. 82, folio not stated) Its purpose was "to promote
    Hinglaj as an important place of worship for the whole Hindu barādarī (Urd. community, clan) in
    Pakistan, as early advertisements for the yātrā demonstrate." (p. 82, folio not stated) **Founder
    named, once:** "The establishment of an annual gathering at Hinglaj was the idea of one person,
    Vasrup Chand Tulsi, who helped found the HSM for the express purpose of publicizing and
    organizing the pilgrimage (Jethamalani 1994, 8–9)." (p. 83, folio not stated) **No dates, caste,
    office or place of origin for him**: **(not stated)**; the index files the same man twice, as
    "Tulsi, Vasrup Chand, 76" and "Tusli, Vasrup Chand, 259" (p. 362, folio not stated) — see Doubts.
  - **The committee's composition, seat and forms of name.** "The temple committee at Hinglaj, the
    HSM has since its inception consisted mainly of Lohanas from the district of Las Bela." (p. 82,
    folio not stated) "The Las Bela Lohana community, which forms the majority of the Hinglaj temple
    committee, the Hinglaj Sheva Mandali, exerts special influence on the narratives and practices at
    the shrine today." (p. 80, folio not stated) It "consists mainly of members of the Lohana caste"
    (p. 292, folio not stated); its tradition is "the tradition of the HSM—that is, of the Lasi
    Lohana" (p. 289, folio not stated); "The Lohana members of the HSM stand at the forefront of this
    movement, claiming direct access to the Goddess's tradition through their long-term relationship
    with Hinglaj." (p. 324, folio not stated) It is "the Hinglaj Sheva Mandali in Karachi" and, in a
    pilgrim's account, "the people of the Mandali Trust who took us to get the darśan at Hinglaj"
    (pp. 173, 175, folio not stated), thereafter "the HSM" (p. 176, folio not stated) and "the
    Mandali" (pp. 265, 267, 290, 292, 295, 296, folio not stated). **Two printed spellings of the
    name stand two pages apart** — body text `Hinglāj Śevā Manḍalī (HSM)` with full diacritics
    (p. 30, folio not stated) against endnote `Hinglaj Sheva Mandali` bare (p. 28, folio not stated);
    and one damaged occurrence, "the Hingaj Sheva Mandai (HSM)" `[text-layer?]` (p. 197, folio not
    stated). Chapter 1's note 1 explains the transliteration: "This name is transliterated from the
    Urdu script, which uses śevā instead of the more commonly used sevā, and will from here onward
    not be given in its transliteration. I will also often use the shortened form, 'HSM.'" (p. 50,
    folio not stated) **Its legal form beyond "Trust", its officers and its membership number**:
    **(not stated)**.
  - **The committee's reach, instruments and power.** "Their ability to disseminate information
    inside and outside the valley, links with international devotees, and connections in the
    provincial and the federal government together have endowed the temple committee with the power
    to perpetuate its own version of the shrine's history and rituals." (p. 324, folio not stated)
    **Neither government is named and no official or department is named**: **(not stated)**. "As the
    main organizational body in charge, the HSM has for many years been overseeing the renovation of
    temples, the building of bridges, and the construction of community places inside the valley."
    (p. 324, folio not stated) Its instruments are "the work of trained volunteers, the erection of
    signs, and the construction of temples" (p. 314, folio not stated), which the author calls "acts
    of governmentality" oriented toward the "education" of the community (p. 314, folio not stated).
    "Although its members were never directly involved in composing and publishing texts, blogs, or
    ritual manuals, their experiences are often quoted by writers in Sindhi, Hindi, and Urdu and
    frequently serve as a point of reference on recent developments and organizational issues (Ratnu
    2007; Lakhavat 2011; Jethamalani 1994)." (p. 267, folio not stated) "the HSM not only has local
    influence in regulating the flow of information, ritual practices, and mobility inside the valley
    but indirectly also has a say in publications about the shrine having a broader reach." (p. 267,
    folio not stated) Its register "is marked by adjectives such as 'proper' and 'real'" (p. 324,
    folio not stated); "Many practices and narratives are often not directly forbidden, but are
    organized in a hierarchical order in which the HSM's own interpretation is planted squarely at
    the top." (p. 324, folio not stated) The one voice quoted directly is unnamed: "As one HSM
    associate put it, 'all arrive, but it is a difference of the class one is traveling in.'"
    (p. 324, folio not stated) The author's verdict: the HSM's acts "ultimately solidify the hegemony
    of a Lasi Lohana version of Hinduism at Hinglaj, which today is represented as the 'real' or the
    'actual' tradition of the Goddess." (p. 314, folio not stated) He names the process twice over:
    the committee's "actions in concealing tangible traces of Zikri Muslim religious markers are not
    only parts in a process of 'Hinduization' but also represent steps in a process of (Lasi)
    'Lohanaization' as they literally set in stone a particular parochial interpretation of Hinglaj
    and the beliefs and practices of her followers." (p. 325, folio not stated)
  - **Why the Zikri reading does not prevail.** "it is the HSM, as the main executor of reconstruction
    work at the temple, that constrains the contribution of the shrine's (Zikri) Muslim past by
    backgrounding—literally covering up—the oblong stone" (pp. 261–262, folio not stated). "Due to
    their lack of control over the renovations, the Zikri community has had few chances to establish
    their interpretation of the image at the shrine. A fact that certainly also has to do with the
    lack of educational, infrastructural, and organizational means among the Makrani Zikri
    community." (p. 262, folio not stated) "The lack of any current mytho-historic narratives at the
    shrine told from a Zikri perspective relates to an increasing 'Hinduization' inside the valley
    through which any kind of (Zikri) Muslim symbol is increasingly marginalized." (p. 90, folio not
    stated) "Zikri Muslim pilgrims still visit the shrine today" (p. 90, folio not stated).
  - **The resident priest, named.** "In 2009, Gopal Maharaj, the only permanent priest (Hin. pujārī)
    at the Hinglaj temple" (p. 46, folio not stated); "Maharaj, who gained his position as a pujārī
    through appointment by the local temple committee (the HSM) in 2008" (pp. 46–47, folio not
    stated); "Since Maharaj is the only permanent pujārī at the shrine and thus an authority for many
    visitors, his words and his interpretation of the shrine's mythology have a strong impact on
    views held by the pilgrims." (p. 47, folio not stated) He is "a very fine guide and conversation
    partner" whom the author "had met during almost all of my visits to Hinglaj" (p. 201, folio not
    stated), "the local priest Gopal Maharaj" in a 2012 figure caption (p. 237, folio not stated),
    and "Gopal Maharaj, the local priest at Hinglaj" (p. 252, folio not stated). **His previous post
    is given in his own words:** "This was at the time when I used to be the pujārī at the Makli
    temple in Thatta, before I came here." (p. 253, folio not stated) **No birth date, death date,
    lineage, caste or predecessor**: **(not stated)**; whether he and "the resident pujārī" cited on
    the tunnel wall (p. 249, folio not stated) are the same man is **(not stated)**. The index has
    "Maharaj, Gopal, 35–37, 186, 212, 223f, 235n30, 238–239, 244" (p. 352, folio not stated), **a
    locator run and nothing more**. Visitors "must ask the pujārī or the volunteer workers at the
    shrine" to open the tunnel doors (p. 248, folio not stated).
  - **The state's three interventions.** The Makran Coastal Highway: the valley "undoubtedly gained
    prominence from the construction of the MCH" (pp. 260–261, folio not stated) and received "wide
    international attention ... after the construction of the MCH" (p. 262, folio not stated). A dam:
    "the planned construction of a dam in the Hingol, which would have led to the flooding of access
    roads leading to the shrine. Here, the WAPDA's spatial conception—economic and not sacred—would
    have significantly altered the practices and eventually also the religious imaginary at Hinglaj."
    (p. 262, folio not stated) The HSM's argument against it: "these sites were not like common
    temples and could not simply be relocated"; the spokesperson added "This is a site of pilgrimage
    which is place specific." (p. 217, folio not stated) The author's gloss: "For the Hindu community,
    due to the temple's svayaṃbhū character and its connection to the landscape, it could not just be
    relocated." (p. 217, folio not stated) Third: "political support for the HSM by the Balochistan
    provincial government" (p. 262, folio not stated). **No dates for the MCH, the dam proposal or
    the political support**: **(not stated)**.
  - **A bridge that moved the festival date.** "The building of a bridge over the River Hingol also
    modified the spatial practices and definitively influenced the choosing of a date for the main
    melā in the Hindu calendar." (p. 262, folio not stated) **The old date, the new date and the
    bridge's completion year**: all **(not stated)**.
  - **The road, and the dates the book gives for it.** "Before the construction of the Makran Coastal
    Highway (MCH)—the highway that coincidentally linked Hinglaj to the outside world—the journey to
    the Goddess's remote abode in the desert of Balochistan frequently presented a lethally dangerous
    undertaking for her devotees" (p. 25, folio not stated). "The construction of a highway at the
    beginning of the new millennium (which happened to connect the isolated shrine with urban
    Pakistan) further added to these changes. The temple's location, although far from any urban
    environment, was now comfortably reachable by car, and as a result, after 2001, the site quickly
    developed into a stage for one of the biggest annual Hindu festivals in Pakistan." (p. 30, folio
    not stated) The road "from Liari to Gabd at the Iranian border" was completed "in 2003 (Hasan
    2009, 161)", while "the first part, linking the Hingol valley with Karachi, was completed and in
    use by 2001" (pp. 83, 85, folio not stated). Elsewhere: "the completion of the Makran Coastal
    Highway (MCH) at the beginning of the new millennium" (p. 319, folio not stated) against "The
    construction of the MCH in 2001 helped raise awareness of the shrine's existence among thousands."
    (pp. 321–322, folio not stated), and "the construction of the MCH around fifteen years later"
    than 1986 (p. 321, folio not stated). The road was "originally intended to boost economic
    development of the Balochistan coast" (p. 322, folio not stated). **See Doubts: the book gives
    2001, 2003 and "the beginning of the new millennium", and nowhere states that 2001 is the MCH's
    own completion year.** Its effects: "not only the modernization of transportation to the shrine,
    but in contrast also a renaissance of the walking practice to Hinglaj" (p. 26, folio not stated);
    it "unified the different routes into the valley, causing many pilgrimage stops to slip into
    disuse"; stops away from the road "such as the `guru-śiṣya paṛāv`" were abandoned; bus
    connections and tour operators "not only increased the number of visitors to the shrine but also
    influenced the practices en route" (p. 322, folio not stated). The author names the double effect
    "This `coincidentia oppositorum`, that is, the triggering of a unification as well as an
    elaboration of the pilgrimage" (p. 322, folio not stated). Pilgrims "praised the government for
    building a road to the Hinglaj shrine" (p. 180, folio not stated); **the government is not named
    and the road is given no completion date there**: **(not stated)**.
  - **The shrine sits in a legally restricted area.** "the legal ambiguity of the journey to a
    restricted area of Balochistan" (p. 179, folio not stated); "the status of visas for Balochistan
    is legally ambiguous" (p. 184, folio not stated). Groups "must report at police stations
    throughout the journey" and are "repeatedly checked on their way to and from Hinglaj" (p. 184,
    folio not stated). "Due to the fact that there is currently no solid legal base for the journey
    into Balochistan, the possibility of completing the transborder pilgrimage is highly tenuous and
    can quickly change in response to political circumstances between the two countries." (p. 192,
    folio not stated) **The book is 2018 and the fieldwork 2009–2015; "at the time of writing" is
    used twice for visa rules (p. 169, folio not stated) and is never dated — see Doubts.**
  - **The shrine sits on an old trade route.** "It is of interest that one of these trading routes
    led to Persia via the state of Las Bela and Makran (Cook 2010, 141). According to local
    narratives, this route passed close to the Hinglaj shrine, which supports Kamphorst's claim that
    most Goddess shrines linked to Hinglaj are found along trading routes of the western subcontinent
    (Kamphorst 2008, 253)." (p. 81, folio not stated)
  - **Two rumours about the shrine, which the author states plainly are false.** "Some devotees in
    Delhi even claimed that some years ago, the Taliban attacked the Hinglaj temple, which the
    devotees assert was once encompassed in an almost completely closed cave. Blasting away the rock
    resulted in the temple's current shape. Another rumor asserts that in 2006, three pilgrims from
    Rajkot, in Gujarat, were kidnapped and killed on their way to the temple. Both stories turned out
    to be sheer fabrications." (p. 170, folio not stated) He immediately adds that he does not
    thereby "downplay the lingering problems of the Hindu community in Pakistan or ignore the
    willingness of radical elements to use violence against non-Muslim minorities (Schaflechner 2014,
    2016a, 2016b, 2017)" (p. 170, folio not stated). A security assessment: "Even though an official
    of the Islamabad-based Jinnah Institute branded Hinglaj in 2012 as a 'high risk for catastrophe'
    due to a possibility of extremist attacks, it is one of the few places in Pakistan that offers a
    relatively secure public space for a massive Hindu festival to occur." (p. 50, folio not stated)
    **The official is not named and no attack is reported**: **(not stated)**.
  - **No saint, no tomb, no silsila, no ʿurs, no sajjada nashin, no mutawalli, no custodial lineage
    beyond the Dost Mohammad family, no endowment and no revenue total is given for this site
    anywhere in the book**: all **(not stated)**.

- **Practices & Events**
  - **The festival calendar, as a bare count and then itemised.** "Due to the regularity of events at
    the shrine—four major festivals annually—practices and the resulting changes follow a certain
    rhythm." (p. 197, folio not stated) The four are counted by the author: "I count four main
    festivals at the shrine: both `cīds`[text-layer?] [ʿīds], muḥarram, and the main phālgun/caitra
    melā. Festivals like Holi or Diwali do not play any significant role since they are not public
    holidays in Pakistan." (p. 277, folio not stated) Named separately as occasions on which the
    shrine is "widely frequented": "`baqra cīd`[text-layer?] [baqra ʿīd]" (p. 272, folio not stated),
    "`mīlād-al-nabī` (the birthday of Prophet Mohammad)" and "the day of `cāśūrah`[text-layer?]
    [ʿāśūrah] (mourning of muḥarram and the commemoration of the fasting of Moses)" (p. 272, folio
    not stated). Note 22 adds: "people also gather in smaller numbers at `cīd ul fit̤r`[text-layer?]
    [ʿīd ul fiṭr], `baqra cīd`[text-layer?] [baqra ʿīd], and `muḥarram`. This is mainly due to the
    public holidays in Pakistan." (p. 317, folio not stated, note 22) **Three of the four are Islamic
    holidays**, which the author states without comment.
  - **The main melā.** "most pilgrimage groups choose to set out in April, just before the big melā
    (Hin. festival) that falls in the month of phālgun or caitra in the Hindu calendar so that they
    can reach the valley in time for the festival activities" (p. 154, folio not stated). It is also
    "the April melā" (pp. 157, 197, folio not stated), "the Hinglaj melā" (p. 165, folio not stated),
    "the phālgun/caitra festivals" / "the April festival" / "the April celebrations" / "the spring
    gatherings" (pp. 291, 293, folio not stated), "The `phālgun`/`caitra` gathering in April"
    (p. 313, folio not stated), "the main festival at the shrine in April" (p. 323, folio not stated)
    and "the biggest festival of the year" (p. 317, folio not stated, note 22). **No date in any era
    is given for the melā beyond the month names, and the book never states the relation between the
    lunar month-names and April**: **(not stated)**. Its programme: "In recent years, the melā has
    officially begun with the visit to Chandrakup on the first day and ended with a collective havan
    (Hin. ceremony), in front of the Hinglaj temple on the third day (see Fig. 6.3)." (p. 281, folio
    not stated) Maintenance cycle: "in the days before the April melā, the Hingaj Sheva Mandai (HSM)"
    `[text-layer?]` "is busy refurbishing the place by repairing roads, painting images, and so
    forth. After the festival, there is much to clean, repair, and reconstruct." (p. 197, folio not
    stated) The festival's recency is stated twice: the politics of exclusion appear "in the
    practices during the—relatively recently established—annual festivals at Hinglaj Devi" (p. 30,
    folio not stated).
  - **Who comes.** "Attracting thousands of people from all castes, classes, and regions, this new
    accessibility caused views from all kinds of traditions to come together" (p. 30, folio not
    stated); the festival draws "vast crowds… from a wide range of castes, languages, ethnicities,
    and nationalities" (p. 27, folio not stated). "The Kohlis, together with other scheduled castes,
    such as the Meghvar or the Bhil, form the biggest part of the visitors at the Hinglaj festival,
    which also reflects the overall population of Hindus in Sindh, and more than 90 percent originate
    from these communities." (p. 154, folio not stated) The `mīlād-al-nabī` and `cāśūrah`
    [text-layer?] [ʿāśūrah] visitors come "mainly from urban areas like Karachi and Hyderabad"
    (p. 272, folio not stated); the `baqra cīd`[text-layer?] [baqra ʿīd] visitors "from Karachi and
    interior Sindh" (p. 272, folio not stated). "The shrine of Hinglaj is one of the main religious
    centers for the Devipujak community of Karachi." (p. 311, folio not stated)
  - **Visitor numbers — every figure the book prints, with its attribution.** Historical: "Henry
    Pottinger, who worked as a spy for the British Crown, writes that annual pilgrims to Hinglaj at
    the beginning of the nineteenth century numbered in the thousands (Pottinger 1816, 297)."
    (p. 113, folio not stated) "This observation is supported by the writings of Captain Hart, who
    estimated the number of pilgrims per year to be between five and six thousand (Hart 1839, 104)."
    (p. 113, folio not stated) "The memoirs of Seth Hotchand, a Sindhi Hindu businessman, also
    confirm that the temple was fairly popular around this time; he writes that in 1805–1806, some
    members of his family traveled to Hinglaj along with 2,000 other pilgrims (Hotchand 1982, 48)."
    (pp. 113–114, folio not stated) A colonial-revenue inference: note 18 gives the Gazetteer's tax
    band (1–5 rupees) and annual revenue (around 600 rupees) and says this "would imply a rough
    average of two hundred visitors to the shrine yearly via the Sonmiani route" (p. 147, folio not
    stated) — **the author's estimate from a revenue figure, never a counted number, and it covers
    one route only.** Modern: "Within just a couple of decades, the local environment has had to
    adapt to soaring numbers of visitors—from merely a few thousand a year to over forty thousand on
    a single weekend in the spring." (p. 261, folio not stated) "over forty thousand people visited
    the shrine" in April 2010, sourced "according to HSM's ticket sale records" (p. 84, folio not
    stated). On `baqra cīd`[text-layer?] [baqra ʿīd] "the HSM roughly estimates the figure to be
    between six thousand and nine thousand visitors on this public holiday (although, because this
    estimate is not based on selling tickets, it can remain only a speculation)" (p. 272, folio not
    stated). The bhanḍārā "feeds—according to the HSM—around forty thousand people on the main days
    of the April festival" (p. 291, folio not stated). "the organization claimed to have sold forty
    thousand tickets" (p. 317, folio not stated, note 15), with the euro conversion "about 85,000
    Euros (2012)" supplied by the author. Visitor numbers are "estimated mostly by the number of
    tickets sold" (p. 292, folio not stated). A ratio, not a count: "Of 1,000 Pakistanis who go to
    Hinglaj only 50 do it properly. They are the only Hinglajis [here]." (p. 171, folio not stated)
    A scarcity period: "Due to the scarcity of visitors—at least between 1965 and the end of the
    1980s—and due to the wide variation in the communities that visited Hinglaj, names and practices
    could not be rigidly attached to certain locations in the valley" (p. 256, folio not stated); and
    "Vernacular and colonial reports confirm that the journey was well frequented before Partition, a
    practice that, as locals confirm, apparently lasted until the 1965 war. However, there appears to
    have been a period of stagnancy from around the mid-1960s until the mid-1980s—a time when the
    shrine was scarcely visited." (p. 152, folio not stated) For the present: "the temple of Hinglaj
    attracts thousands of visitors from all over the country on Hindu and public holidays" (p. 266,
    folio not stated). The difficulty of counting is stated: "Due to the comparatively wide area over
    which the temples and holy springs are spread, it can be difficult to guess the number of
    devotees at the shrine at any given time" (p. 272, folio not stated). **No absolute annual
    visitor total is given anywhere in the book**: **(not stated)**.
  - **The entry fee, its year and its abolition, given twice.** "the entry fee of 250 rupees (in
    2012) in particular provoked resentment and protest among visitors, ultimately prompting a
    response by the HSM to abolish the ticket system in 2013" (p. 290, folio not stated); "The
    250-rupee fee was only imposed during the April celebrations and was not necessary at any other
    time of the year" (p. 291, folio not stated). Rumours about "the proceeds the HSM had received
    before 2013" persisted after abolition (p. 292, folio not stated). The ticketing system is
    elsewhere said to have been "aborted in 2013" (p. 166, folio not stated). **Its start date is
    given two incompatible ways — "in 2008, the Mandali introduced a new ticketing system requiring
    the purchase of an ochre-colored, plastic wristband called the bhag'vā paṭṭī (Hin. ochre
    cloth/ribbon) in place of the former paper tickets" (p. 219, folio not stated) against "Before
    2009, there had been another ticketing system in place" and a rearticulation "between 2009 and
    2013" (p. 209, folio not stated). See Doubts.**
  - **Access control at the shrine: the saffron band and the checkpoints.** The ticketing system
    "forced visitors wanting to enter the main part of the valley, where the Kali and Hinglaj temple
    are located, to wear a plastic wristband" (p. 165, folio not stated); visitors had to wear it "if
    they wished to cross into the western part of the valley where the Kali and Hinglaj temples are
    located", with "the display of the paṭṭī at two separate checkpoints" (p. 219, folio not stated).
    "the HSM countered this attempt at subversion by providing the bhag'vā paṭṭī for a fee, which
    paying pilgrims were required to wear visibly in order to gain access to the inner part of the
    valley" (p. 290, folio not stated); "the HSM engaged many young men who worked shifts during the
    day and night checking these wristbands to ensure that no one without it snuck by" (pp. 290–291,
    folio not stated), some of them "feeling powerful with their official identification cards and
    uniforms" (p. 291, folio not stated). **The band's own price is given only as "for a fee"**:
    **(not stated)**.
  - **A second, separate levy on the route, paid to the police.** "During the 2012 melā, every
    pilgrim had to pay an additional 100 rupees at a checkpoint when approaching Chandrakup, one of
    the main stops on the way to Hinglaj. This money, however, was to be paid to the police and not
    to the HSM." (p. 291, folio not stated) The author was told "it must be paid for the pilgrims'
    protection. Yet the officer did not tell me who it was the pilgrims had to be protected from."
    (p. 291, folio not stated) **The officer, the police unit and the checkpoint's exact location are
    not named**: **(not stated)**, and **the book says nothing about the fate of this levy after
    2013**: **(not stated)**.
  - **The community kitchen, water and electricity.** "the expenses for the bhanḍārā (Urd. the
    community kitchen) that feeds—according to the HSM—around forty thousand people on the main days
    of the April festival" (p. 291, folio not stated). Sceptics "claimed the HSM receives significant
    sums in the form of donations to provide food during the melā" (p. 291, folio not stated), and
    "many pilgrims told me that Hindu politicians and the `Minority Minster`[text-layer?] [Minority
    Minister] of Sindh donate food for the bhanḍārā annually, and so the HSM does not pay for the
    majority of the food provided by the community kitchen" (p. 291, folio not stated). "The
    organization confirmed its receipt of donations but nonetheless verbally played down the amount
    of food actually donated." (p. 291, folio not stated) **No named politician, no donation sum and
    no year**: **(not stated)**. Water: "the costs of organizing large amounts of water brought from
    the nearby Hingol River to the valley by tankers. Since the natural stream inside the valley does
    not carry enough water for the huge crowds that have frequented the festival in recent years, the
    vehicles organized by the HSM must constantly replenish the water supply to cover the need."
    (p. 291, folio not stated) Electricity: "an increase in petrol prices; petrol is used for the
    generators that provide the festival with electricity." (p. 291, folio not stated) During the
    non-April festivals "the organization did not charge any money for the running of the generator
    or the food" (pp. 291–292, folio not stated). **No tanker count, no volume, no generator count
    and no cost**: **(not stated)**.
  - **The central ritual: the crawl through the garbha.** "These narratives connect to the central
    ritual practice at the shrine in which pilgrims crawl through a small tunnel, signifying the womb
    of the Goddess from which they are born again. Before a pilgrim is permitted to enter this
    tunnel, he or she has to follow a rigid pattern of purification and worship." (p. 57, folio not
    stated) **The purification sequence is not described there**: **(not stated)**. Fully stated in
    the conclusion: "one of the oldest practices at the shrine and, in fact, the climax of the
    journey now as well as then, is the rebirthing ritual in which the devotee enters a tunnel
    underneath the image of the Goddess. Believers claim the Hinglaj `mūrti` to be `svayaṃbhū`, that
    is, generated of the mountainous stones through the will of the Goddess." (p. 321, folio not
    stated) The passage "becomes a performative act of rebirth out of the womb of the Goddess,
    sometimes even followed with mimicry of cries and gestures of newborn babies" and "has the power
    to cleanse Hinglaj's followers of all their prior sins" (p. 321, folio not stated). "every
    visitor to Hinglaj is reborn from the womb of the Goddess and thus gains the ātmā of a newborn
