    child" (p. 178, folio not stated). The pilgrims "entered the Goddess's garbha and circumambulated
    the mūrti through a tunnel in the mountain" (p. 181, folio not stated).
  - **The śaraṇ bhāī/bahan.** Pilgrims choose at Kanda Devi "their śaraṇ bhāī/bahan—the partner with
    whom they will enter the Mother's garbha" (p. 159, folio not stated); Kshatriya is reported to
    write that at the Hingol each pilgrim must choose a "śaraṇ'bhāī" (Hin. "brother taking refuge in
    the Mother") "together with whom he or she will pass through the tunnel underneath Hinglaj's
    mūrti" (p. 216, folio not stated).
  - **Purification and the order of entry, as the two mid-century novels describe it.** Abadhut:
    "Early the next morning, the pilgrims proceed to the shrine of Hinglaj, where they bathe and put
    on fresh clothes to enter the womb of the Mother (Abadhut 1964, 209). At the shrine, they find
    only a small altar (bedī) on which a lamp is burning (Abadhut 1964, 209). The place is decorated
    with oleander flowers, incense, and candles (Abadhut 1964, 209)." (p. 131, folio not stated)
    "Rupalal tells Abadhut that, as the mahaṃt (Hin. actually, 'superior of a monastery') of the
    group, Abadhut will be the first to enter the cave. In the light of the candles, Abadhut sees a
    narrow passage leading around the altar (Abadhut 1964, 210). After entering the tunnel, he has a
    vision of the Goddess who laughingly gazes at him, and only a few minutes later, he exits the
    passage on the other side." (pp. 131–132, folios not stated) "One pilgrim after another is led
    into the passage." (p. 132, folio not stated) Shastri: after crossing the stream "they reach the
    sanctum sanctorum, the author describes a cave around fifty feet high containing a vedī (Hin. a
    place for sacrifices). On the vedī a lamp is burning." (p. 142, folio not stated) "the pilgrims
    are ready for the darśan of the Goddess, for which they crawl on all fours through a small door
    on one side of the vedī leading to a tunnel into the mountain. The semicircular tunnel leads to
    another door on the other side of the platform." (p. 142, folio not stated) A timing prohibition:
    "the group must wait for one more day because, according to tradition, they are forbidden to
    cross the stream toward the cave during the day (Shastri 1977, 53)" (p. 142, folio not stated);
    before entering, "they take a bath, put on fresh clothes, and cross the stream" (p. 142, folio not
    stated), having first been ordered by the aguā to fast (p. 142, folio not stated). The ritual
    close: "the chaṛīdār spreads all the offerings on the vedī and starts the pūjā" (p. 142, folio not
    stated); on exiting the tunnel "Aghori Baba, who had followed them, garlands him with a mālā from
    the vedī, and the ritual is complete" (p. 142, folio not stated). **`bedī` (p. 134) against `vedī`
    (p. 142) — see Doubts.** **"around fifty feet high" is the only measurement given in the novels'
    accounts; no other dimension, no building date, no architect, no patron**: **(not stated)**.
  - **Rites of passage performed at the shrine.** "the upanayana saṃskāra or the guru dakṣiṇā, which
    are traditionally performed in and around Hinglaj (cf. Motyani 2011, 282; Khatri 1973, 24)"
    (p. 275, folio not stated). **Who performs them, and at which station in the valley**: **(not
    stated)**. And at the river: Lasi Lohana communities "associate with the shrine in Balochistan
    through certain rites of passage—such as the wearing of the janeū (Hin.), the brahmanical thread
    (Burton 1851, 315) that is bestowed on the young at the River Hingol. These rituals have gained
    increasing importance over the past years." (p. 82, folio not stated)
  - **The place of the temple in the ritual sequence.** In Jethamalani's 1994 list the Hinglaj temple
    is the third asthān of the third paṛāv — "Hinglaj temple, where the yagya (Urd.; from Skt.
    `<yajña>` `[text-layer?]` 'sacrifice'), havan (Urd.), and the darśan (Urd.) of Hinglaj are
    performed (Jethamalani 1994, 12)" (p. 203, folio not stated). **Entry is conditioned on
    Chandrakup:** "many accounts even say that it is not possible to enter the Goddess's valley
    without having made offerings there (Shastri 1977, 36)" (p. 210, folio not stated); "many
    pilgrims today believe that Hinglaj's temple may only be entered after visiting Chandrakup and
    making proper offerings" (p. 212, folio not stated). **And it was conditioned on the Kali
    sacrifice:** "Many texts show that after the sacrifice, pilgrims were not allowed to proceed
    immediately to the Hinglaj shrine, located just a few hundred meters away. Instead, they were
    required to return to Ashapura paṛāv, where the group would wait until nightfall; only then could
    they proceed to Hinglaj (Jethamalani 1994, 8)." (p. 222, folio not stated)
  - **The ban on animal sacrifice: dated 2004, stated four times, and never simply effective.** "This
    led to an official ban by the temple organization as recently as 2004." (p. 47, folio not stated)
    "The HSM officially banned animal sacrifice inside the valley in 2004" (p. 294, folio not
    stated); "In 2004 the HSM decided to ban this practice from the area." (p. 295, folio not
    stated); "the Mandali finally decided in 2004 to ban animal sacrifice in the valley" (p. 296,
    folio not stated); "With its prohibition in 2004, bali has become a contentious issue which
    produces two antagonistic camps." (p. 313, folio not stated) **The banning body is called "the
    temple organization" at p. 47 and is not there named as the HSM**: **(not stated)**; **the month
    and the deciding body's resolution are not given**: **(not stated)**. The author's account of its
    cause: "the history of the ban shows how organizational difficulties caused the ritual practice
    inside the valley to stop. … Only after the ban was put into effect was the prohibition
    increasingly reinterpreted as a `religions`[text-layer?] [religious] decision and became an
    important part of the tradition." (p. 47, folio not stated); "The main authority at Hinglaj, the
    HSM, did not enforce the ban due to their traditional and historic rejection of bali, but rather
    started it because of organizational and political reasons." (pp. 306, 313, folio not stated);
    "Although the ban on bali at Hinglaj may have originally been triggered by a host of
    organizational and infrastructural problems relating to the practice, through effective
    dissemination of its message, the temple committee has created an understanding of the
    prohibition as animated largely by the concept of 'actual' Hindu behavior." (p. 27, folio not
    stated); and in the conclusion, "Functional yet ultimately contingent events prompted the ban
    inside the valley. But once the temple committee adopted and enforced the prohibition, this
    debarment was retroactively established as a typical (read: natural) characteristic of worship at
    the shrine, clashing with other interpretations of the practice, such as the Devipujak-Vagri's
    cosmology." (p. 325, folio not stated) **The "functional yet ultimately contingent events" are
    not described there**: **(not stated)**. Its reach: the prohibition "swiftly became a defining
    characteristic of the melās within the valley, foremost the celebration at the time of `baqra
    cīd`[text-layer?] [baqra ʿīd]" (p. 306, folio not stated), and many pilgrims now go "specifically
    because animal sacrifice is (technically) banned there" (pp. 314–315, folio not stated) — **the
    parenthesis is the author's own.** A justification heard from a visitor: "Well, this is a nature
    reservoir, it is not allowed to kill animals here." (p. 295, folio not stated), against "the
    regulations of the Hingol National Park, in which the shrine is located" (p. 295, folio not
    stated); **the park's regulations are not quoted, no ordinance is named and no date is given**:
    **(not stated)**. The substitute rite: "the recent introduction of a substitute practice—a mere
    symbolic offering of a goat to Kali by cutting into the animal's ear, allowing a few drops of
    blood to spill, and then setting it free inside the valley" (pp. 311–312, folio not stated).
    **Who introduced it and in what year**: **(not stated)**. **What continued anyway:** a rooster
    sacrifice witnessed at the Kali Mata temple in 2010 "and also in later years", a goat offered by
    the Vagris "only the night before" (p. 294, folio not stated), and "members of the HSM secretly
    continued sacrificing goats in the mountains for the Goddess Kali" after the ban, "done in the
    name of all visitors to prevent misfortune" (p. 296, folio not stated). **An entry must not state
    flatly that sacrifice ceased at Hinglaj in 2004 — see Doubts.**
  - **The obligatory Kali offering, for one community.** "At every visit, however, each member of the
    Devipujak community is obliged to give `bali` (in the form of a rooster or a male goat) to the
    Goddess Kali, or else risk her wrath. For the Vagris, Kali is the guard (Hin. `caukīdār`) of the
    Goddess Hinglaj, and thus they must appease Kali before making their way on to Hinglaj. For the
    Devipujaks, without Kali's blessing, the whole `darśan` at Hinglaj is in vain." (p. 311, folio not
    stated) A disagreement inside that community about frequency: "Bhao Tulsi, for example, went to
    the shrine exactly once in his life, in the mid-1970s with a group of devotees from Karachi. Bhao
    has never returned there since; in his opinion, the journey to Hinglaj should only be made once
    in the lifetime." (p. 311, folio not stated) Against this, "the young men in the Devipujak
    community view the shrine differently. All of them have been to Hinglaj, and most have been
    several times; even Lali's son has repeatedly visited the shrine." (p. 311, folio not stated)
    **Bhao Tulsi's exact year and Lali's son's name**: **(not stated)**.
  - **Welfare organisations at the festival.** "During these festivals, many Hindu welfare
    organizations are also active inside the valley, advertising their philanthropic work for the
    community. Together with their humanitarian goals, these organizations often also propagate a
    certain idea of 'proper' Hindu behavior... Some organizations, like the Pakistan Hindu Seva Trust,
    distribute leaflets with prayers and use the festivals as a means of coming into regular contact
    with a network of Hindus from all over Pakistan." (p. 313, folio not stated)
  - **The journey: times, distances and stops.** "According to Briggs, the journey took twenty-four
    days (Briggs 1938, 107); however, according to Hotchand, it took two and a half months (Hotchand
    1982, 48); and Jethamalani goes as far as to say that the whole trip took six months
    (Jethamalani 1994, 7)." (p. 117, folio not stated) The author's explanation: "the time frame
    varies substantially due to the route and the modes of transportation chosen by the pilgrims"
    (p. 117, folio not stated). Distances: Shridevi's group covered "around 600 kilometers" on foot
    from "a village near Umerkot in Sindh" (p. 152, folio not stated); the Narayanpur road route from
    Karachi is "about 240 kilometers long" (p. 158, folio not stated). **Three incompatible tallies
    of ritual stops:** "Chhabria names thirteen stops and twenty-five places of worship (Chhabria
    2000, 56–57), while Jareca mentions thirteen stops and twenty-four places of worship (Jareca n.d.,
    104–106). Kshatriya documents eighteen stops and twenty-four pūjā places (Kshatriya 2005, 76)."
    (p. 263, folio not stated — note 3). A sea route existed but is barely documented: "Notwithstanding
    the fact that there existed a sea route to Hinglaj, not much has been passed down concerning this
    mode of transportation. Most accounts found today instead discuss the walking practice,
    emphasizing its danger and difficulties." (p. 146, folio not stated) The gathering point, mid
    twentieth century: "Abadhut, a Bengali ascetic, reaches the main gathering place for the
    pilgrimage to Hinglaj, the Nagnath akhāṛā in Karachi, in the month āṣāṛh, 1946 CE. It is from this
    point on that members of the local Nath community guide pilgrims once or twice a year to the
    shrine of Hinglaj Mata (Abadhut 1964, 1)." (p. 127, folio not stated) — **"once or twice a year"
    is the only frequency the book gives for organised pilgrimage in that period.** "People from all
    parts of India—such as Gujarat, Rajasthan, or Bengal—meet there to await the beginning of the
    journey that will carry them into the desert of Balochistan. After a month, enough people have
    gathered to comprise a pilgrimage party, and cameleers are arranged from the area around the
    Hinglaj shrine to guide the group (Abadhut 1964, 4)." (p. 127, folio not stated) — **the
    cameleers are recruited from around the shrine itself, a local economic tie the book does not
    quantify:** **(not stated)**. A stop after the shrine: "After the darśan, the group sets out to
    Akash Ganga (ākāś gaṃgā), another pilgrimage stop where they are to pluck a twig from a certain
    kind of tree (Abadhut 1964, 213)." (p. 132, folio not stated); the novel ends "as Abadhut boards
    a ship to Koteshwara in Gujarat, the pilgrimage's last stop (Abadhut 1964, 222)." (p. 132, folio
    not stated) "The narrative ends with the darśan at Akashganga, another tīrtha near Hinglaj."
    (p. 142, folio not stated)
  - **A purity mark taken on the way, still given but altered.** The practice "reflects concerns of
    ritual purity on the way to Hinglaj. To a certain extent, this ritual is still carried out today,
    though in a milder form. I was told that only two or three people came to the temple in the past
    few years to receive the mark. However, due to hygiene concerns, they did not have it burnt onto
    their forearm but asked instead for the mark of a cold stamp." (p. 117, folio not stated) **The
    mark's name, who administers it and what it is made of are not stated in that passage**: **(not
    stated)**; only "beginning of the twentieth century onward" survives as its dating there.
  - **The Hajj comparison — the book's most-repeated pilgrim datum.** "The association of the Hinglaj
    pilgrimage with the Hajj is frequently heard at the shrine. The phrase 'this is our hajj' ('yah
    hajj hai hamārā') is by far the most common answer to the question about why people have the
    incentive to travel (whether they drive or walk) to the shrine." (p. 155, folio not stated) "the
    epithet 'Hinglaji,' equivalent to the title 'Hajji,' is bestowed upon people who have successfully
    accomplished the journey. Today, however, this title is mainly found in India, where it still
    possesses a degree of aura and 'symbolic capital' (Bourdieu 1998, 47–52). In Pakistan, due to the
    easy accessibility of the shrine, the epithet has lost its value and is rarely used." (p. 155,
    folio not stated) "Many were (and still are in some cases) addressed as 'Hinglajis,' an honorific
    title for those who performed all the necessary rituals on the way." (p. 321, folio not stated)
    And from the Devipujaks: "they describe Hinglaj as their Hajj. For the community, it is the most
    important place in Hindu cosmology and ought to be visited by all Hindus once in their lifetime."
    (p. 311, folio not stated)
  - **The vocabulary of the journey.** "In Pakistan, the term yātrā is used mostly by Hindu pilgrims.
    The Urdu equivalent, ziyārat, is rarely applied in conjunction with Hinglaj." (p. 146, folio not
    stated) Merit: "For the Goddess's followers, the accurate performance of these practices also
    determines how much spiritual merit (Hin. puṇya) they eventually will receive from the divinity.
    For some, as I will show later, a visit at the shrine is very significant, having the power to
    influence this life and the afterlife." (p. 30, folio not stated) Austerity: "certain motifs, such
    as austerity (Hin., Skt. tapasyā), remain important themes within the whole Hinglaj genre even in
    modern times, while others have been lost in the contemporary era" (p. 25, folio not stated); the
    MCH's infrastructure has "led to a revitalization of walking patterns and to new interpretations
    of the concept of austerity (Hin., Skt. tapasyā)" (pp. 25–26, folios not stated).
  - **Indian devotees, the border and the proxy shrines.** "While Hinglaj devotees in Pakistan today
    can easily experience her shrine, due to the challenges and costs of obtaining a visa, her
    worshippers in India must often still rely on texts, videos, or the accounts of the few who have
    had the chance to cross the border for the ritual journey." (p. 26, folio not stated) Those who do
    get across "enjoy a special status within their communities" (p. 26, folio not stated). "The
    restricted mobility over the border caused by visa and financial limitations keeps the idea of a
    certain 'special status' attached to the ritual journey vivid" (p. 191, folio not stated).
    Partition is the governing constraint: the mandatory Charan pilgrimage "became problematic with
    the separation of the subcontinent in 1947, which also resulted in new dynamics within the ritual
    expression of Charan communities… each year, several groups from India manage to obtain visas and
    travel to Pakistan to visit the shrine." (p. 69, folio not stated) "There are many such proxy
    shrines for Hinglaj in India. Some of them were established long before the separation of the
    subcontinent, and others were the result of the rigid visa policy put in place afterward (for a
    list of Hinglaj temples in India, see Lakhavat 2011)." (p. 167, folio not stated) One Gujarat
    organisation "was in the process of constructing a tunnel leading to the temple's Hinglaj statue
    in order to simulate the cave setting of the original temple in Balochistan" (p. 168, folio not
    stated).
  - **Media at the shrine.** During the 2010 group's night pūjā "two news teams—one from the local
    Sindhi television station and another from the national Dawn station—arrived and interviewed
    Dayaram along with some HSM officials (Schaflechner 2011, 29:30)" (p. 181, folio not stated). "All
    of those interviewed emphasized the importance of the place and urged the governments of both
    India and Pakistan to loosen the visa regulations for Hinglaj" (p. 181, folio not stated). **The
    Sindhi station is not named**: **(not stated)**. "Indian groups at the shrine are frequently
    treated with respect and curiosity and get regular attention from local and national television
    channels broadcasting all over the country." (p. 270, folio not stated)
  - **The 2010 Gujarat pilgrimage, and the three-way disagreement about the sanctum.** "In 2010, I
    documented the Hinglaj pilgrimage by a group of devotees from Gujarat (Schaflechner 2011). The
    party was headed by Dayaram Kapri, who also served as the spiritual guide. In Pakistan, the tour
    had been organized by the HSM, and Virsimal Dewani, the main organizer in charge, accompanied the
    group. Local and national news picked up the story of the Indian pilgrims, and so two television
    teams came to the temple and interviewed Dayaram, Dewani, and Gopal Maharaj, the resident priest
    at the temple." (p. 48, folio not stated) "When the men, one after the other, were asked during
    the interviews what the temple's main sanctum sanctorum shows, all three gave conflicting answers.
    One said that the image stands for the womb of the Goddess, while another claimed that the upper
    part of the Goddess's head has fallen there. The third described a story in which it became
    evident that, in fact, it is the face of the Goddess Durga together with her main army officer
    that is found at Hinglaj." (p. 48, folio not stated) **The book does not say which man gave which
    answer**: **(not stated)**. The aftermath: "Dewani was not happy with the presentation on the
    television, as their interviews, he said, were edited in a way that made them appear ignorant and
    simply uninformed about the image of the Goddess. During the discussion, all three quoted their
    sources of information, but without finding an agreement, Dewani suddenly turned to me and said:
    'You come from a university, you will find out what the image really stands for!'" (p. 48, folio
    not stated) **No source any of the three quoted is named**: **(not stated)**.
  - **Two deaths in the valley's ponds, neither dated.** "It was the second day of `cīd`[text-layer?]
    [ʿīd] and the valley was full of people. I was at Til Kund then, when I heard that a child had
    drowned right here in Sharan Kund. People pulled the child out of the pond and brought it to
    Karachi, but it was too late—the child died." (p. 253, folio not stated, Gopal Maharaj).
    Separately, at Til Kund, "the pujārī mentioned that some years earlier, a mother and her four
    children drowned in the pond" (p. 254, folio not stated). **Neither event is given a year**:
    **(not stated)**; the first is placed only as "This was only a few years ago" (p. 253, folio not
    stated).
  - **An injury in 2012, used as evidence in the sacrifice quarrel.** "Incidents like the injury of
    one of the walking pilgrims in 2012 along the road (see Chapter 4) support the Devipujak's belief
    that the banning of sacrifice has brought misfortune to the `melā`." (p. 312, folio not stated)
    **The pilgrim is not named and the injury is not described**: **(not stated)**.
  - **Antiquity of the pilgrimage, and the author's refusal to date the shrine.** "From the sources
    available, it is evident that an elaborate pilgrimage system to the shrine has existed at least
    since the fourteenth century." (p. 145, folio not stated) "On the basis of these documents, it
    appears that pilgrims visited Hinglaj regularly at least from the beginning of the fourteenth
    century onward." (p. 113, folio not stated) "some form of ritual journey to Hinglaj has existed
    since around the fourteenth century (Westphal and Westphal-Hellbusch 1976, 114)" (p. 266, folio
    not stated). "Although the precise age of the shrine will remain unknown until proper
    archeological research is conducted at the site, it is safe to say that Hinglaj has attracted
    pilgrims from all over the subcontinent for several centuries." (p. 266, folio not stated) "But
    since the desert shrine cannot be properly dated without any archeological survey, the existence
    of a continuous religious practice at the shrine can also only be speculated upon." (p. 96, folio
    not stated) "Although my research indicates that the foundational parts of the sanctum are indeed
    of undefined age, I do not infer that this representation of the Goddess was not subject to change
    as well." (p. 200, folio not stated) **The attested pilgrimages the book lists, with its dates
    exactly as printed:** Hamir Gohil (Gehlote) "in the year 1301 CE", found in James Tod (Westphal
    and Westphal-Hellbusch 1976, 114) (p. 113, folio not stated); "an aristocrat from Rajasthan, Raja
    Bij Singh, went to Hinglaj in the year 1499 CE" (Chhabria 2000, 31) (p. 112, folio not stated);
    "another trip from the year 1508 CE in which two more nobles, Raja Todarmal and Raja Bharimal,
    are named" (Chhabria 2000, 31) (p. 112, folio not stated) against "Ansari mentions a journey of
    Raja Todarmal Brahmakhatri and Raja Bharimal Khatri, who arrived at Thatta in the year 1558 CE
    (Ansari 2001, 22). Both authors apparently refer to the same group of travelers but give
    different dates for their arrival." (p. 112, folio not stated) — **the book itself flags this
    contradiction**; "the journeys of Raja Madhu Singh (in 1792) and Raja Jagat Singh (in 1801)"
    (p. 113, folio not stated). "Inscriptions in Thatta, a city located on the eastern route to
    Hinglaj, suggest that a network of ritual journeys has been in place since the fourteenth
    century." (p. 320, folio not stated) — **the author immediately limits this**: such references
    "leave significant gaps, which make it impossible to conclusively speak about a continuous
    transmission of practices before the middle of the nineteenth century" (p. 320, folio not stated),
    and **no inscription is quoted, dated individually or located within Thatta**: **(not stated)**.
    A fourteenth-century report the author could not verify: "In conversations with a priest at
    Koteshwara, Gujarat, I was also told that the earliest record of a pilgrim going to Hinglaj is
    dated to the fourteenth century. As proof, he named the tomb of a saint close to Koteshwara on
    which the date of his journey was marked. Unfortunately, I did not have the opportunity to verify
    this information." (p. 147, folio not stated) **The saint is not named and the date is not
    given**: **(not stated)**. "This fact in Wilford's article furnishes additional evidence that the
    yātrā was frequented in the eighteenth century." (p. 147, folio not stated)
  - **The two datable modern changes, given repeatedly and consistently.** "This situation changed
    rapidly after 1986 when a temple committee, the Hinglāj Śevā Manḍalī (HSM), took over the
    organization of the pilgrimage and started to deal with these different histories." (p. 30, folio
    not stated) "the reinstitutionalization in 1986 and the construction of the MCH in 2001" (p. 156,
    folio not stated); "the centuries prior to the pilgrimage's institutionalization in the mid-1980s"
    (p. 186, folio not stated) against the HSM takeover "in 1986" (p. 181, folio not stated);
    "Starting with its (re-) institutionalization in 1986" (p. 266, folio not stated); "Since its
    inception in 1986, the Mandali has promoted Hinglaj as a religious space for the entire Hindu
    community (Urd. `barādarī`)" (p. 314, folio not stated); "the institutionalization of the
    pilgrimage by the Hinglaj Sheva Mandali (HSM) in 1986" (p. 319, folio not stated). "The
    pilgrimage's institutionalization in the mid-1980s was followed by the completion of the Makran
    Coastal Highway (MCH) in the beginning of the millennium." (p. 195, folio not stated) The whole
    transformation, dated by the author: "in just three decades, the Hinglaj `yātrā` evolved from a
    highly strenuous bodily experience and a once-in-a-lifetime undertaking to an annual sedentary
    festival that now represents an established event within the Pakistani-Hindu calendar" (p. 323,
    folio not stated); cf. "the past thirty years" (p. 320, folio not stated) and "about thirty years
    ago" (p. 323, folio not stated).
  - **An Urdu advertisement for the yātrā, quoted in the original.** "mulk bhar kī hindū barādarī
    xvātīn o mardon ko hinglāj mātā kī yātrā kī dacvat[text-layer?] [daʿvat] detī hai" (Urd.). From a
    1987 announcement." (p. 100, folio not stated) The book reads it as community-making: "Their
    efforts to make the place appealing to all Hindu communities of Pakistan is found in the repeated
    invocation of a Pakistani-Hindu community (Urd. 'mulk bhar kī hindū barādarī') on the announcement
    leaflets. This is one of the few instances where such a community is evoked in the texts I
    studied." (p. 101, folio not stated)
  - **Publishing about the shrine, counted and dated.** "In the 1980s and 1990s, only two books about
    Hinglaj were published. In the decade after 2001, five works about the Goddess were published,
    three of them in 2001, the same year as the completion of the first part of the MCH." (p. 101,
    folio not stated) The languages: the struggle is "also conspicuous in publications about the
    shrine appearing in Urdu, Sindhi, Hindi, and Gujarati." (p. 30, folio not stated); "more recent
    writings about the shrine in Gujarati, Hindi, Sindhi, and Urdu have had a significant influence on
    the practices and myths at Hinglaj. Such texts were often composed by devotees who worship the
    Goddess as their clan or caste deity (Hin. `kul'devī`), and therefore, they promote a particular
    understanding of the tradition." (pp. 57–58, folio not stated) Recent translation activity: "Texts
    in Sindhi, Hindi, Gujarati, and Sanskrit were translated into Urdu and thus became accessible
    across caste and community boundaries." (p. 323, folio not stated) **No translator, title,
    publisher or date**: **(not stated)**. A 2010 devotee on the same process: "The real [xālis] books
    on the Goddess were not translated yet and we could not understand Hindi or Gujarati… So people
    started to translate the proper [original in English] texts into Sindhi and Urdu, and people were
    encouraged to go to Hinglaj." (p. 31, folio not stated) Two works are named as the makers of the
    modern image: "the Bengali novel `Marutīrtha Hiṃlāj` (The Desert Pilgrimage to Hinglaj [1957]) by
    Abadhut (Dulalchandra Mukhopadhyay), together with its 1959 movie adaptation helped to make the
    journey known to a broader audience." (p. 320, folio not stated); "The 1959 film, in particular,
    cast Hinglaj into popular culture and helped foster the pilgrimage's extremely arduous image."
    (p. 126, folio not stated); and "Devadatta Shastri's 1977 Hindi novel, Āgneya Tīrtha Hiṅglāj (The
    Fiery Pilgrimage to Hinglaj)" (p. 25, folio not stated), of which "only the final four pages of
    the text describe events at Hinglaj" (p. 137, folio not stated). The author flags a doubt: "it is
    not completely clear if the similar literary account in Hindi by Devadatta Shastri (1977) proves a
    coherent pilgrimage practice at that time or, in fact, has been copied from the Bengali novel."
    (p. 320, folio not stated) How devotees read such books: "devotees often judge textual and visual
    productions describing the journey according to their 'authenticity' rather than their literary
    sophistication"; "devotees often treat texts on Hinglaj as guidebooks rather than as literary
    prose." (p. 125, folio not stated) Ratnu's verdict on the film: "there is nothing worth mentioning,
    only the cinematography was prominent. In the whole film the [Hinglaj] mountains are not shown and
    it was not filmed at the proper place" (Ratnu 2007, 8) (p. 125, folio not stated) — **the
    bracketed `[Hinglaj]` is Schaflechner's own insertion.**

- **Legends & Miracles**
  - **The dismemberment of Sati, narrated at length as the myth "frequently heard at the shrine
    today"** (p. 55, folio not stated): the Goddess incarnates as Sati, daughter of king Daksha;
    marries Shiva against her father's will; Daksha performs "the aśvamedha (Skt.)" and invites every
    god "except Shiva"; Sati "jumped into the sacrificial fire"; Shiva kills Daksha, then dances "the
    tāṇḍava (Skt.), a frantic dance that threatened to destroy the whole world"; "the God Vishnu took
    his discus and cut Sati's body into many parts that fell out of Shiva's arms and scattered all
    over the subcontinent", and each landing place "became known as śākta pīṭha (Skt.), or seats of
    the Goddess's power." **A variant is recorded at the shrine:** "although Vishnu's intervention is
    sometimes omitted; in this version, the wild motion of Shiva's dance alone caused the body's
    dismemberment." (p. 55, folio not stated) The conclusion says the renovation foregrounds exactly
    this narrative: "the story of the Goddess Sati's dismembering and the dissemination of her body
    parts over the South Asian continent" (p. 325, folio not stated).
  - **Which part of Sati fell at Hinglaj — three answers, kept side by side.** (i) The brahmarandhra,
    "upper part of the head", per the Pīṭhanirṇaya (p. 55, folio not stated); glossed by Apte as "an
    aperture on the crown of the head through which the soul is said to escape on its leaving the
    body" (Apte 1992) (p. 98, folio not stated); at the image, "one of the globular stones on the
    eastern side is Sati's skull, which came to rest there after Vishnu cut her body into several
    pieces" (p. 229, folio not stated). (ii) The navel, per the Caṇḍīmaṅgala: Mukundarama "instead
    states that the navel rather than the Devi's brahmarandhra has fallen at Hinglaj (Sircar 1973,
    33)" (pp. 230–231, folio not stated); "since the womb (garbha) is directly underneath the sanctum
    it must have been the navel that has fallen in Hinglaj" (p. 231, folio not stated); and at the
    shrine today a minority argues "that since the womb (Hin. garbha), in the form of a tunnel into
    the mountain, is found under the image at Hinglaj, above it can only be a part of her belly"
    (p. 57, folio not stated). (iii) The womb: "One pilgrim mentioned that the island is the place
    where the Goddess's brahmarandhra is found, while at Hinglaj it is actually her womb (Hin. kokh)
    that fell to earth. He also added that this is why pilgrims can be reborn at Hinglaj and that he
    never saw anybody being born from a woman's head." (p. 260, folio not stated) **The author states
    which prevails:** "it is clear that the imaginary of the fallen skull is the prevailing one"
    (p. 231, folio not stated).
  - **The Hingol legend — the shrine's own foundation story, paraphrased by the author from three
    written sources** ("Abro 1991; Khatri 1973; Chhabria 2000", p. 229, folio not stated). "The demon
    Vichitra had two sons. One of them was called Hingol and the other was named Sindur." (p. 229,
    folio not stated) Sindur loots China; the people of China, "worshippers of Shiva", petition him;
    Shiva promises that "within three days, Ganesha would come and relieve them from their
    afflictions"; "After three days, `Ganesh`[text-layer?] [Ganesha] came indeed, and killed the
    tyrant Sindur." (pp. 229–230, folio not stated) Hingol "retreated to a mountain, raised his hands
    toward the sky, and stood on one toe for thousands of years"; Brahma grants that "he could not be
    killed in war, by any weapon, or by any human being and also not under the sky nor on the earth"
    (pp. 229–230, folio not stated). Parvati appears as a beautiful woman, is pursued from the
    Himalaya to the River Indus and into Sindhudesh, and "at the place where the shrine is located
    today, the Goddess absorbed him into her body" (p. 230, folio not stated). **The naming of the
    place, in the Goddess's own words as the book gives them.** Hingol's last wish: "You should remain
    here at this very place under my name and fulfill all wishes that people bring to you!"; the
    Goddess answers "From now on I will be called Hinglaj Devi, and this place should be my place of
    worship. I will stay here forever with Hingol." "Since this day, both of their faces are found
    inside the cave." (p. 230, folio not stated) **The author's own placing of it:** "This story,
    which is clearly a reference to the legend of Narasimha in the Bhāgavata Purāṇa, however, is
    rarely heard at the shrine today. Devotees often simply state that the other face is Shiva's or
    Rama's." (p. 230, folio not stated); "no particular myth had, as of the end of my research, fully
    solidified around this enigmatic part of the statue" (p. 230, folio not stated).
  - **The second face in the effigy, told two ways by the same priest.** "In 2009, Gopal Maharaj, the
    only permanent priest (Hin. pujārī) at the Hinglaj temple, explained that the puzzling second
    face found in the Goddess's effigy represents the demon Hingol, a local tyrant killed by the Devi
    in mythological times. Later, in 2012, the priest changed his explanation, insisting instead that
    the second face is that of a vīr (Hin. hero/army commander) of the Goddess Durga, who killed the
    demon Mahishasura." (p. 46, folio not stated) **Both years are cleanly printed.** "Maharaj, who
    gained his position as a pujārī through appointment by the local temple committee (the HSM) in
    2008, revises the story and connects the shrine to a broader Hindu discourse." (pp. 46–47, folio
    not stated) "In it, he sets aside the tyrant Hingol, who is a part of the local tradition, and
    changes him into the demon Mahishasura, a figure of the famous Devī Māhātmaya." (p. 47, folio not
    stated) Today, "the other round stone is often interpreted as the face of one of Durga's vīrs who
    helped her to kill the demon Mahishasura." (p. 229, folio not stated)
  - **Rama's pilgrimage — the charter narrative of the whole route.** The sin: the sage Kumbhodar
    refuses Rama's food because he is a "Brahmicide"; "by slaying the demon Ravana, Rama made himself
    guilty of 'brahm hatyā'... and thus committed one of the worst sins possible. To cleanse himself
    of this sin, the sage advises Rama to make a pilgrimage to Hinglaj Mata, the only place that can
    purify him." (p. 120, folio not stated) "The sage Vashishta advised Rama to go on the pilgrimage
    to Hinglaj, which was the only place where he could be purified of his horrible offense." (p. 76,
    folio not stated) The defeat: at the "Ungakhera Bherum Luk" pass, "the Goddess's army, which
    guards the entry to her empire, intercepts the group. A fight breaks out in which many of Rama's
    men die, and after twelve years of battle, the king from Ayodhya and his army must retreat."
    (pp. 120–121, folios not stated) The condition: "she responds that he must return to his first
    stop, Ram Bagh, and make the journey again as a simple pilgrim. So Rama leaves his entourage, his
    army, and vehicles behind and sets out to walk to the shrine, accompanied only by his closest
    friends." (p. 121, folio not stated) The promise that founds later pilgrimage: "Rama's men are
    devastated at not being allowed to accompany their lord to the Goddess's abode. The Devi thus
    promises them that their descendants will, at some point in time, all come back to do the
    pilgrimage." (p. 121, folio not stated) The water miracles: Hanuman's stomping "produces only a
    dry riverbed"; Lakshmana's arrow "only succeeds in detaching one of the hills"; "In agony, Sita
    places her palm down on the soil and thereby makes five wells appear, from which the group
    drinks." (p. 121, folio not stated) The purification and its monument: "the Devi grants him
    purification of his sin. To mark his completed yātrā, he carves the symbols of the sun and moon
    on the mountain opposite the temple." (p. 121, folio not stated) The same carvings are shown to
    pilgrims at the shrine: "the Aghori Baba shows the group the symbols of the sun and the moon,
    which, as he explains, were carved into the mountain by Ramachandra on his journey to Hinglaj
    (Abadhut 1964, 212)." (p. 132, folio not stated); "the Aghori Baba shows them the signs of the
    sun and moon inscribed on the mountains opposite the temple and explains that these were created
    by Ramachandra himself when he came to Hinglaj to cleanse himself of his sin of killing a
    Brahmin." (p. 142, folio not stated); "the imprint of a sun and a moon on the side of the
    mountain, supposedly etched there by Rama after he completed his journey to Hinglaj to receive
    purification for the sin of killing a Brahmin" — "The place is said to be unreachable by mortals
    and indeed looks quite inaccessible from the bottom of the valley, though it is possible to climb
    up there." (p. 257, folio not stated). **The Brahmin is not named**: **(not stated)**. **The
    textual source the book could not find:** "Jethamalani writes that Rama's pilgrimage to Hinglaj is
    found in the `Sat Koṭī Ānand Rāmā’ian` `[text-layer?]` [Sat Koṭī Ānand Rāmāʾian] (Urd.), a text
    taken from the fifth chapter of the `Yātrā Kānḍ` (Urd.), an addition to the canonized corpus of
    Valmiki's Rāmāyaṇa (Jethamalani 1994, 37). Unfortunately, I have not been able to locate this text
    anywhere." (p. 120, folio not stated); the paraphrase is assembled "from the bits and pieces I
    have gathered from a few different texts (cf. Jethamalani 1994, 37–38; Hart 1839, 79–80; Jareca
    n.d., 110–111)" (p. 120, folio not stated). A second unverified source: "Hart also claims that the
    account of his journey can be found in the `Hiṃgulā Purāṇ` (Hart 1839, 79), a fact that I cannot
    confirm, at least not with the version available to me." (p. 121, folio not stated) Hart's causal
    claim, reported without endorsement: "Hart even posits that the pilgrimage's attractiveness is
    based mainly on the legend that Rama himself visited the shrine." (p. 121, folio not stated) What
    survives today: "Notwithstanding the fact that parts of this story are now widely forgotten, its
    core—the walking pilgrimage by the king of Ayodhya and his entourage—are still often remembered by
    visitors to the Hinglaj Mata shrine." (p. 119, folio not stated); "What remains among visitors of
    the shrine today is the notion that Rama, an important god-king, had to come to Hinglaj as a
    simple pilgrim to rid himself of the sin of killing Ravana—another proof for the importance of the
    journey's kinesthetic experience (see also Cunningham 1975, 309)." (p. 122, folio not stated)
    "no figure was ever as present in the representations of the traditional pilgrimage to Hinglaj as
    Rama, the seventh incarnation of the God Vishnu." (p. 119, folio not stated)
  - **The ship turned to stone at "Goorab-i-Sung"**, quoted from Hart at length: "A wealthy Shet named
    Kowaljee freighted a vessel from Hindoostan to carry himself and a large party to Hinglaj… when
    the Goddess, justly indignant at his arrogance and presumption, transformed the boat and every
    soul on board into stone. (Hart 1839, 91)" (p. 114, folio not stated). The book reads it as a
    sanction on route choice, not only as a wonder: "the 'usual' way is considered to be the one over
    land and not the more comfortable sea route" (p. 118, folio not stated).
  - **The Brahmakshatriya charter: the Goddess grants and withholds at Hinglaj.** Dadhichi tells the
    five princes that "the five princes must go to the shrine of Hinglaj to be initiated into her
    tradition. So the princes fast, become initiated into the mantra of the Goddess Hinglaj, and then
    set out on the pilgrimage to the Goddess's abode." (p. 64, folio not stated) They "eventually
    ascend the mountain at Hinglaj, where they fast and meditate for another twelve years." (p. 64,
    folio not stated) She refuses to undo Parashurama's curse — "How could the words of the mighty
    Parashurama become a lie? But I will help you and do what is possible" — then "instructs the
    princes in worshipping her as their clan Goddess [Hin. kul'devī] and adds that he who enters her
    cave will gain the dharma of an ascetic. She furthermore advises them to always worship the
    Saraswat Brahmins." She promises that their descendants "would be prosperous and fortunate in the
    future" even after they forget the art of weaponry (p. 64, folio not stated). The author's gloss:
    "The pilgrimage of Hinglaj also plays a significant role within this plot: it is here where the
    divine and the mundane meet and where the humans can exercise some agency over their fate,
    eventually even altering the course of the curse." (p. 66, folio not stated) "The Goddess Hinglaj
    serves here as the community's creator and as the guarantor of 'Kshatriyadom.'" (p. 68, folio not
    stated)
  - **Mekan Dada and the milk miracle.** The Goddess, "disguised as a normal shepherd, wearing a
    Balochi dress and accompanied by a herd of goats she had created through her mantra power", cannot
    fill Mekan's cup, "so that a hundred thousand liters came out, but still she could not fill the
    glass" (pp. 77–78, folios not stated); she then grants him the guruship of all who come to her
    place (p. 78, folio not stated). His own statement of the ritual programme, which is the fullest
    inventory of the site's features in that chapter: "I will go to Chandrakup, I will climb up to
    Anil Kund, I will do the pūjā at your temple, I will put siṃdūr [Hin. vermilion] on the place
    where Rama sat down, and I will see the moon and the sun carved into stone there! I will receive
    all the knowledge and wisdom from this place and only then will I go home." (p. 78, folio not
    stated) — **a cave, Anil Kund, a temple with pūjā, "the place where Rama sat down", and a carved
    moon and sun. No measurements, no building date, no architect: (not stated).**
  - **The Charan signs.** A bell rings at the Hinglaj temple in Balochistan whenever a Charan boy is
    born — "Charans often say" (p. 70, folio not stated). A rock will bury the shrine if a Charan man
    fails to come before his 60th birthday (p. 70, folio not stated), restated in the Barhath epigraph:
    "From the moment a Charan is born, Mother Hinglaj waits for him to come for the darśan; if he
    leaves the world otherwise [without having visited the shrine], then a stone falls from the
    Mother's mountain." (BARHATH 2006, 9) (p. 172, folio not stated). The Goddess "grants Charan men
    the ability to write and compose stories and poems. She is their muse, and they imagine her sitting
    on a poet's tongue, quickening his creativity." (p. 70, folio not stated) The Devi "promised to
    incarnate as a Charan woman periodically in order to bring change and prosperity to the community"
    (p. 70, folio not stated). Returning alive was itself the miracle: "Homecomers were seen as a
    divine gift since, as the Charans say, the Goddess herself had blessed such pilgrims and allowed
    them to return safely from the brink of death." (p. 172, folio not stated); "those returning alive
    from the dangerous pilgrimage were often celebrated as gifts from the Goddess herself, who
    protected them on their way" (p. 321, folio not stated). Karni Mata's link: the Goddess appeared to
    Karni's mother "While Karni's father was undertaking the pilgrimage to Hinglaj" (p. 73, folio not
    stated).
  - **How the shrine came to be where it is, per Kamphorst.** "the Tumbel spread the Goddess's fame in
    Las Bela, and so Hinglaj decided to settle in a cave close to the Hingol River, where she has been
    worshipped by the Charan and other communities since as early as the ninth century (Kamphorst 2008,
    237)." (p. 71, folio not stated)
  - **The Muslim legends of the site, from three colonial reports.** "Colonel Wilford writes that the
    local Muslim community living around the temple believed that Eve fell from heaven, landed in one
    of the ponds inside the valley, and later settled there and became known as Bibi Nani or as Hinglaj
    (Wilford 1851, 259; Abro 1991, 66)." (p. 89, folio not stated) "Hart mentions that Muslims in the
    area claimed that Hazrat Ali, the son-in-law of the Prophet Mohammad, split a rock in two with his
    sword as he was on his way into the valley (Hart 1839, 94)." (pp. 89–90, folio not stated) "And one
    of Aurel Stein's informants claimed that Hinglaj, or Bibi Nani, is considered to be the wife of Ali
    (Stein 1943, 202)." (p. 90, folio not stated) "Today, such myths have virtually vanished from
    Hinglaj, and even though many recent Hindu texts note this element of Muslim worship at the shrine,
    no particular details are given." (p. 90, folio not stated) The Eve story is repeated as the Zikri
    claim: "The (Zikri) Muslim tradition at the shrine claims that this is the place where Eve fell
    from paradise and later became known as Hinglaj (Abro 1991, 66)." (p. 255, folio not stated) The
    Muslim miracle-claim on the stone itself: the grave of a saintly wife of `cAli`[text-layer?] [ʿAli],
    "miraculously translated to this spot" (p. 235, folio not stated, quoting Stein 1943, 202).
  - **Holdich on the shared shrine** (p. 92, folio not stated): "Here Hindoo and Muhammadan pilgrims
    alike resort, each claiming the divine protection of the presiding goddess or of the departed saint,
    according to the tenets of their faith; and neither recognising that the object of their veneration
    is probably the same goddess who was known to the Chaldeans under the same old-world name (Nana) a
    thousand years before the time of Abraham. (Holdich 1904, 45)" Holdich "describes her influence as
    extending 'from the Euphrates to the Ganges' (Holdich 1904, 45)" (p. 92, folio not stated).
  - **The Muslim woman priest of the Goddess, reported and then doubted by the author.** "According to
    Briggs, there exists a legend that a Muslim woman was the priest of the Goddess in Hinglaj in those
    days. By ritually applying the ṭīkā (Hin. ornamental or sectarian mark) on the forehead of the
    (Hindu) pilgrims, she turned all of them into Muslims. Thus, on their way back, the pilgrims had to
    be reconverted to their original faith—hence the branding (Briggs 1938, 106–110)." (p. 115, folio
    not stated) Schaflechner's verdict: "Due to the fact that no similar account is mentioned in any
    other sources, it is difficult to confirm Briggs' suggestion… Thus, the story of converting pilgrims
    into Muslims may simply be another in the huge corpus of unverifiable legends surrounding the aloof
    shrine of the Goddess." (p. 115, folio not stated)
  - **The Goddess has left the temple — two versions.** "He claimed that the Goddess actually no longer
    resides at the shrine because she has become annoyed with the crowd herself. He added that if one
    wanted to meet her, one must climb up to Chaurasi, a two-and-a-half-hour ascent into the mountains,
    to a freshwater pond called Anil Kund where she had taken refuge." (p. 188, folio not stated) — the
    speaker is "Another visitor from Karachi". "visitors at Hinglaj often claim that Anil Kund has now
    become the 'real' tīrtha, the present-day abode of the Goddess who retreated high up into the
    mountains to escape the tiresome noise and masses of people now overrunning her temple in the
    valley" (p. 255, folio not stated). And the island version: "the Goddess moves between the Hinglaj
    Valley and the island, where she resides for the largest part of the day, for seven 'pruhurs' (also
    from Hin. pahar, a three-hour period) (Hart 1839, 98)" (p. 260, folio not stated); the few who know
    of Astola "portray it as the 'actual' seat of the Goddess who nowadays only rarely resides at
    Hinglaj" (p. 260, folio not stated).
  - **Anil Kund carries the Ganges.** "at the Anil pond, the waters of the River Ganges are said to be
    flowing" (pp. 146–147, folios not stated). Fully: "Once Shiva saved the earth from destruction by
    taming the waters of the River Ganges with the help of his long locks of hair (Wilkins 2003, 467).
    He also used to come to the Hinglaj mountains for the solitude necessary for his meditation, and so
    some of the water from the Ganges also found its way from his hair into the hollow at Anil Kund."
    (p. 255, folio not stated) — attributed to "the local tradition". "all the holy rivers, including
    the Ganges, the Sarasvati, the Kaveri, and others, assembled together to meet and worship her.
    Altogether, nine hundred rivers came together here to form Anil Kund... In other accounts, the
    number of rivers convening at Anil Kund is eighty-four thousand—thus the alternate name, Chaurasi
    (Hin. `<caurāsī>`[text-layer?] [caurāsī] eighty-four)." (p. 256, folio not stated) — attributed to
    "Pilgrims from Gujarat". **The two counts are not reconciled.**
  - **The Goddess drops the boulder as a concession — with a rival origin for the same stone.** "In
    ancient times, munis and r̥ṣis (Hin. sages, wise men) would meditate in the northern mountain range.
    For this reason, common people could no longer visit Chaurasi since they did not dare disturb the
    sages. As a result, the pilgrims asked the Goddess to arrange some other way for them to receive the
    merit" (p. 259, folio not stated) — attributed to Gopal Maharaj. Against it: "Another story says
    that Guru Gorakhnath himself took the stone from somewhere around Anil Kund and brought it to this
    place (Jethamalani 1994, 8)." (p. 259, folio not stated)
  - **The Goddess closes the pond at a bereaved mother's demand**, told by Gopal Maharaj as his own
    eyewitness account: "After only fifteen days there was so much rain that a huge landslide filled the
    pond... it was only since a mother cried in front of the Goddess, because she had lost her child,
    that the pond disappeared." (p. 253, folio not stated). He explicitly rejects rival accounts: "People
    talk a lot about what has happened, but none of their stories are true" (p. 253, folio not stated).
  - **The Goddess's dream instruction, which ended a line of the author's research.** "A few days later,
    Gopal Maharaj … approached me and told me that I must stop my research on the shape of the image and
    the garbha, the tunnel underneath the sanctum. Upon asking why, he told me that the Goddess had come
    to him in a dream and instructed him to tell me not to conduct any further inquiries on the topic
    because my work was disturbing her sleep." (p. 201, folio not stated) "This divine intervention put
    an end to my research into the sanctum sanctorum." (p. 201, folio not stated) The author is placed
    inside the same idiom elsewhere: his research is "the will and the game (Hin. līlā) of the Goddess
    who had decided to make herself better known with the help of a European PhD student" (p. 173, folio
    not stated), and note 8 gives an informant's untranslated statement: "'Ham ne to kuch nahīṃ kiyā. Jo
    āp ko mil'tā hai, yah sab mātā ki kr̥pā hai. Yah xud cāh'tī hai ki āp kā kām saphal ho jāe.'"
    (p. 193, folio not stated).
  - **The temple roof as self-generated**, attributed to Gopal Maharaj in a Dawn interview: "Every
    temple in the world is constructed with bricks and stones, but if you look at the roof of this
    place, you will realize that it has naturally generated. No one constructed it." (p. 200, folio not
    stated) The Hindi/Urdu original is given untranslated in note 2, with a month and year: "Duniyā kā
    har mandir banāyā jātā hai, īṃṭoṃ se patth'roṃ se, agar āp is kī chat dekheṃ yah qud'ratī banāyā
    huā hai, kisī ne banāyā nahīṃ hai" (Gopal Maharaj December 2010). See Schaflechner (2011, 29:00)."
    (p. 263, folio not stated — note 2). **The interview is dated only by that note.**
  - **Svayaṃbhū as the shrine's governing claim, and its economic stake.** "the concept of svayaṃbhū not
    only relates to certain objects but also can be linked to whole areas, as in the case of the Hinglaj
    Valley, the area surrounding Chandrakup volcano, or even the whole desert, considered the home of
    the Devi." (p. 200, folio not stated) "the HSM and visitors at Hinglaj often downplay any
    construction work at the temple, especially if it affects the image. Since the place is said to have
    evolved on its own, any kind of man-made alteration in and around it effectively undermines the idea
    of its authenticity. Alterations bearing directly upon the shape of the Goddess's image inside the
    temple, in particular, are extremely contentious." (p. 200, folio not stated) The stake is named as
    economic as well as symbolic: "the notion of svayaṃbhū, the symbolic and even financial capital of
    which should not be underestimated." (p. 200, folio not stated) Against it the author sets the
    evidence of change: "it is evident from travelogues and photographs that over the course of the past
    one hundred years, various devotees visiting the valley must have appropriated the shrine and the
    image, all according to their own religious conceptions." (p. 200, folio not stated) The
    self-generation idea is placed in a wider frame: "Sircar writes that the earliest such sthāns were
    understood as natural representations of the gods and goddesses. Pools and water springs were
    associated with the reproductive organs of the Devi, and hill chains were imagined as her breasts
    (cited in Erndl 1993, 35)." (p. 200, folio not stated)
  - **The garbha's purifying power**, as pilgrims' belief: "it was the power of the Mother's garbha (Hin.
    womb, lap)—a tunnel underneath the image of Hinglaj that is said to possess purifying qualities"
    (p. 157, folio not stated). The vision inside, in Abadhut only: the I-narrator "describes his
    experience in the tunnel leading around the bedī as a vision of the Mother Goddess. This reported
    image basically resembles her common iconographic form (Abadhut 1964, 210)." (p. 134, folio not
    stated) Shastri by contrast "goes inside but writes nothing in his account about the 'indescribable'
    experience", note 44 quoting "Anirvacanīy yah anubhav!" (Shastri 1977, 54) (pp. 142, 149, folios not
    stated). The silence is imposed: "The Aghori Baba receives Abadhut and advises him never in his whole
    life to speak of what he has seen in the tunnel (Abadhut 1964, 210)." (p. 132, folio not stated)
    **Pilgrims vanishing in the tunnel, and the author's disbelief:** "There are many legends about
    pilgrims disappearing in the tunnel due to such cavities, which allegedly lead deeper into the
    mountain. I strongly doubt that these holes were once big enough for a human being to fit through. No
    one I spoke with had actually witnessed such an incident, but the idea seems to persist among
    pilgrims at the shrine" (p. 265, folio not stated — note 30).
  - **The Goddess as protector on the road, in pilgrims' own words.** "some pilgrims recounted their
    dangerous way out of Karachi and proudly added how the power of the Goddess had guided their path
    through sensitive neighborhoods and how she protected the rather large group from being targeted."
    (p. 159, folio not stated) "There are many wild animals on the way, but the Mother always helps, and
    never has anyone been hurt. Nothing has ever happened to us. This is because we have the power of the
    triśūl, the chaṛī, and the jhoṃk, the flame that burns all the time in the rath. This flame is like
    our friend; it is sent by the Mother." (p. 164, folio not stated) The cart itself: "They say that
    people who are tired from walking need only to hold on to the cart, and they immediately receive
    renewed strength for the journey." (p. 164, folio not stated); "The rath becomes 'charged' (from a
    2012 interview; original in English) with divine energy, they say, which, once it reaches the valley,
    can also be transferred to the bypassing people." (p. 165, folio not stated); and in a pilgrim's
    words, March 2012: "So many people think that this cart is miraculous and do their prayers there. They
    think it is their cart." (p. 166, folio not stated) The stick: each chaṛī "had the power to protect the
    group on their dangerous journey, provided they properly worshipped it on the way" (p. 207, folio not
    stated).
  - **A wish granted for offspring**, in Giresh's words: "I came since I wanted my sister to have a child.
    I had already come twice, once on a bus and then on a motorbike, but my Mother told me that I must come
    on foot. Then [the Goddess] heard my wish and my sister had a child. This is why I am now walking to
    her shrine." (p. 153, folio not stated)
  - **The Goddess as the agent of the visa, and bureaucracy as her līlā.** "She is the one who influences
    the employees working in the Pakistani embassy to issue visas, or who causes airport security personnel
    to get distracted at the right time." (p. 173, folio not stated) Rajendra at the X-ray machine: "in the
    very moment when my bag went into the `X-`/`ray`[text-layer?] [X-ray] machine, someone came to meet the
    officer in charge and started a conversation. My bag came out and there was no trouble." (p. 176, folio
    not stated) "Devotees often interpret the uncertainties and frustrations involved in the process as
    signs of the Devi's 'game' (Hin. līlā)." (p. 171, folio not stated) The author's characterisation:
    "Rajendra's description is full of miracles, and he found many small signs of divine intervention on the
    way." (p. 176, folio not stated)
  - **Two dream commands transferring Hinglaj's power to Indian sites.** Anoop Dan "claimed some decades ago
    that he had a dream in which the Goddess appeared to him saying that since her shrine in Balochistan was
    no longer reachable, the site of Temra Rai should be visited by Charans in its place." (p. 167, folio not
    stated) In Gujarat, a devotee "announced that the Goddess had appeared to him in a dream and advised him
    of the temple's remote desert location, after which he founded a temple committee to run it."
    (pp. 167–168, folios not stated) And a third, from Delhi, quoted from the temple's head and reported by
    the author as a representative stereotype rather than endorsed: "My guru had a dream of the Goddess
    Hinglaj in which she told him to build her temple here in India, because there is no proper worship
    [pūjā arcanā] in Pakistan. [The Goddess told him] that barbarians [mlecch] rule the country, and that
    terrorists have taken over her temple. Her followers do not dare to go to her abode anymore and her
    worship has ceased. Due to that reason my Guru gave me the order to build this temple here [in Delhi]."
    (p. 170, folio not stated) Returned Hinglajis "often established Hinglaj temples in their cities and
    villages, and in a few cases even made the Goddess appear by herself locally (Skt. svayambhū) as a
    result of their devotion" (p. 172, folio not stated).
  - **The Goddess takes her own sacrifice if denied one** — attributed to the Devipujak-Vagris, not asserted
    by the author. "every time a festival is organized at Hinglaj and the Mother is deterred from her
    sacrifice, she causes accidents and disease among the pilgrims, thereby taking her own sacrifice ('mātā
    xud bali letī')" (p. 294, folio not stated). "To support their point, the Vagris described many incidents
    in which people had horrible accidents or fell sick during the melā in Hinglaj." (p. 294, folio not
    stated) **No incident is dated or named**: **(not stated)**. In a 2012 Devipujak's words: "Since the
    Hinglaj Sheva Mandali has prohibited animal sacrifice, every year there are some accidents... In one way
    or another, the Mother takes her gift [`bheṃṭ`]." (p. 312, folio not stated) The sanction is general: "If
    the community ceases to do so, I was frequently told, the Goddess would herself take the life of a member
    of the community. If it came to this, they explained, the Goddess would cause accidents, or bring disease
    upon the community." (p. 310, folio not stated); note 29 gives the community's own words: "`Ham agar na
    deṃ, to hamāre meṃ se koi ek jāegā. mātā bali xud leṃgī!`" (p. 317, folio not stated, note 29); and the
    threat at its widest: "She will even destroy the whole universe [`sarva naṣṭ kar degī pure brahmāṃḍ ko`]."
    (p. 312, folio not stated, with note 32 "From an interview in 2012.", p. 318). Note 31 records one
    interviewee's reaction to the 2012 injury: "He felt validated and interpreted the situation as the wrath
    of the Goddess who was robbed of her sacrifice by the temple organization. He also mentioned some other
    cases that came to his mind when people had slipped or gotten lost in the valley." (p. 318, folio not
    stated, note 31). **Kali as gatekeeper, in a 2010 testimony:** "In 2010, one member of the community told
    me: 'Before our forefathers came here on camels. At that time Kali was really here [`svayaṃ thī`]. People
    needed to give the sacrifice here; it was necessary . . . . If you went to Hinglaj without giving a
    sacrifice to Kali she came after you and slapped you.'" (p. 317, folio not stated, note 30). And a
    substitution accepted: "I would take a coconut with me. So, I started to offer coconuts to Kali Mata and
    I said: 'Mother, accept this now instead.'" (p. 305, folio not stated).
  - **The origin of blood sacrifice, attributed to the community and glossed by the author against a text.**
    "For the community in Karachi, this tradition dates back to mythological times when the Devi was forced
    to consume the blood of demons during a battle in order to counteract their continuous reproduction. In
    my conversations with them, the Devipujak never directly referred to the `Devī Māhātmaya`, but their
    version of the episode closely parallels the story of the Goddess's struggle with the demon Raktabija
    (Devadatta 2003, 130–135). According to Lali Bhua, it was because of this fight that the Goddess
    developed a taste for blood as sacrifice from her worshippers (Hin. `xūn caṛhānā`)." (p. 310, folio not
    stated) The author's inference is his own: "it seems it is not simply the consumption of blood the Vagri
    Goddess is interested in, but the general life energy of her subjects" (p. 310, folio not stated).
  - **Route legends the book attaches to the Hinglaj pilgrimage and to nothing else.** The two worshipped
    stones of the guru and the student, told by the aguā on the mountain before Chandrakup: a guru asked his
    student for water; "Because the student was a guru-bhakta, a devout follower of the master, he gave all of
    his drinking water away until there was none left for himself"; the guru refused him water in turn, the
    student died, the guru's bottle "shattered into pieces on the ground, spilling all the water into the
    sand", and the guru died beside him. "Since that time, these two stones have been worshipped in memory of
    this incident." (p. 140, folio not stated; note 40 adds "A similar story is also found in Abadhut
    (Abadhut 1964, 56)", p. 148, folio not stated). Camala Devi's punishment — "the Goddess would punish any
    pilgrim who dared to do so with leprosy", attributed to "the belief of devotees" via Wilford 1851, 257
    (pp. 203–204, folios not stated). The whale's bone — "believed by pilgrims to be a whale's bone dug into
    the earth by some holy man to stop the surf from reaching beyond this point", via Wilford 1851, 252
    (p. 203, folio not stated). The camels' swelling feet, attributed to Abro: "Another legend says that
    camels are not allowed to cross into the valley since their feet would swell from the presence of the
    Goddess (Abro 1991, 58)." (p. 221, folio not stated) The Goddess Ashapura welcoming pilgrims, attributed
    to Dayaram Kapri: "The Goddess Ashapura used to sit there to welcome the pilgrims. Since at this point,
    the devotees' wish to accomplish the yātrā was finally fulfilled and the shrine of Hinglaj was near, the
    Goddess took the form of Ashapura to congratulate them. In memoriam of the days when the Goddess still
    made herself visible in this way to the pilgrims, a statue of Ashapura was erected there by a Gujarati
    businessman." (p. 220, folio not stated) **The businessman is not named**: **(not stated)**. The
    blossoming twig as the Goddess's consent to a chosen śaraṇ'bhāī (Kshatriya 2005, 77–78) (p. 216, folio not
    stated). The snake as a form of the Goddess: at the Jagnath temple "On the inner temple wall is an image
    of a snake, a representation of one of the Goddess's many forms." (p. 207, folio not stated) Aghori Baba's
    tenure as a called vocation: "Following the order of the Goddess, Aghori Baba had stayed behind, equipped
    only with a `cilamand`[text-layer?] [cilam and] some gāṁjā." (p. 142, folio not stated)
  - **Wilford's informants on the rite before the temple.** "Loca-mátá `the mother of the mankind`
    [text-layer?] [the mother of mankind] is the chief deity, and before her temple, they strip naked, and
    rolling themselves upon the rough pavement they, like madmen, call out `"Ai-mátá! Ai-mátá! cleanse us
    from our impurities.`[text-layer?] [closing quotation mark absent as printed]" (pp. 234–235, folio not
    stated, citing Wilford 1851, 258f.)
  - **Hinglaj as a person in legend.** In the Gorakhnath story the site's name is used of a woman who cooks
    and waits: "all the food that Hinglaj had prepared in anticipation of his arrival" (p. 206, folio not
    stated). "Tradition has it that Hinglaj was very fond of the Nath community, especially of Guru
    Gorakhnath. So the Goddess waited day after day in Thatta for Gorakhnath to come to meet her, and, in
    anticipation of" — **the chunk breaks off mid-sentence at p. 205 and the sentence resumes in the next
    chunk at p. 206** (p. 205, folio not stated).
  - **Ghosts and possession, excluded by devotees rather than narrated by the book.** The 2010 interviewee:
    "People back then were afraid of ghosts, evil spirits, and such things at Hinglaj. They did not
    understand." (p. 31, folio not stated) The author's reading: "Ghost possession and the handling of evil
    spirits (both common occurrences among many Hindu communities in Pakistan) are excluded by this devotee
    as 'improper' parts of the tradition" (p. 31, folio not stated).
  - **The BVP's dated ritual promise, the only calendrical observance attached to the site in the Sanskrit
    material.** The Brahmavaivarta Purāṇa "mentions Hinglaj (under the name Hiṅgulā), stating that a devotee
    reaches the end of the rebirth circle by seeing the idol of Durga at Hinglaj on the eighth day after the
    new moon in the month āśvin (Skt.)." (p. 54, folio not stated) The verse is printed in Chapter 2's note
    4: "Hiṅgulāyāṃ tathā 'ṣṭamyāmiṣe māsi site śubhe/śrīdurgāpratimāṃ dr̥ṣṭvā karoti janmakhaṇḍanam//
    (śrīkr̥ṣṇajanmakhaṇḍa, 76.21)" (p. 98, folio not stated). Schaflechner rejects the standing translation:
    "Sen translates this as the 'pilgrimage of vermillion,' hence literally translating 'hiṅgula' as
    vermillion (Sen 1974, 383). Aside from its semantically questionable content, in my opinion, the
    translation is inaccurate as the rest of the chapter in the BVP clearly describes the merits of visiting
    certain holy places." (p. 98, folio not stated) **This is a textual claim, not a festival kept at the
    shrine today.**
  - **Carcikā's wandering** — born of Shiva's sweat in the fight with Andhaka, she "roamed the earth only to
    eventually settle at 'the best of all places—the Hingulata mountain'" (p. 53, folio not stated).

- **Significance**
  - **The book's own claim for the shrine's rank, in the author's voice.** The conclusion offers "a final
    look at the dramatic relationship between identity, change, and solidification for Pakistan's largest
    religious minority at its most important place of worship today." (p. 27, folio not stated) "HINGLAJ
    DEVI IN Balochistan has come to be one of the most important centers for Hindu spiritual practice in
    Pakistan over the past three decades." (p. 29, folio not stated) — **the case here is the text layer's
    rendering of small capitals and is not reliable evidence of the printed case; see Doubts.** "Today, many
    Pakistani Hindus share the opinion that Hinglaj occupies an outstanding position among the country's
    religious sites." (p. 270, folio not stated) It is "generally considered the largest active tīrtha sthān
    in Pakistan" (p. 279, folio not stated), and "for most people, the shrine is considered the oldest in the
    entire Hindu tradition, so ancient and important that Hindus even come from India to visit it" (p. 270,
    folio not stated) — **the author reports these as opinions held, not as his own datings.** "Hinglaj is
    not simply a particular, unique locality anymore; it stands in a synecdochical relationship to Hinduism
    as such" (p. 287, folio not stated). "Because of the shrine's central role, however, its examination
    offers meaningful insights into group formation and the Hindu body's relationship to the nation state."
    (p. 325, folio not stated) "this cacophony of voices—which conspicuously emerged after the shrine gained
    its central position for Pakistani Hinduism" (p. 187, folio not stated).
  - **Primacy among the śākta pīṭhas, and the two grounds devotees give.** "In the eyes of many Pakistani
    (and Indian) Hindus, Hinglaj is the oldest and thus the greatest of all śākta pīṭhas (Skt. seats of
    power) in the world." (p. 51, folio not stated) "The term pīṭha (Skt. seat, altar) specifically describes
    holy sites relating to Goddess worship all over South Asia" (p. 52, folio not stated). (i) "Pakistani
    devotees today claim that this primary position is because Hinglaj would be a 'xālis sthān' (Hin.), a
    'real' pilgrimage place, linked, for example, to its characteristic of being svayaṃbhū (Hin.; Skt.
    `svayambhū )`[text-layer?] [svayambhū], self-generated (this will be discussed in detail in Chapter 5)."
    (p. 52, folio not stated) (ii) "Others legitimize this claim by noting that Hinglaj (as Hiṅgulā,
    Hiṅgulāja, Hiṅgulāṭa) has been mentioned in various Sanskrit sources and thereby, is part of the real
    deal." (p. 52, folio not stated) The economy of citation: "In many such sources, the site in Balochistan
    appears as one of the śākta pīṭhas, also associated with the famous legend of the Goddess Sati found in
    the Mahābhārata. This mentioning in the Sanskrit corpus serves as 'symbolic capital' for the temple in
    Balochistan, frequently wielded by devotees in conversations about the shrine's legitimacy." (p. 52,
    folio not stated) The two modern grounds: "Hinglaj's ancient heritage (which Hindus and non-Hindus alike
    place within the same time frame as that of the Mohenjo-Daro civilization) and its mention in Sanskrit
    scriptures (cf. Motyani 2011, Ansari 2001)"; "these two labels—its age and its ties to
    Sanskrit—distinguish the place from other Hindu sites in Pakistan and endow it with the aura of a 'real'
    tīrtha sthān" (p. 270, folio not stated). **Which Sanskrit scriptures, and which passages: (not
    stated)** there.
  - **The Sanskrit attestation, source by source, with the datings the book gives and refuses to settle.**
    The Hevajra Tantra, Sādhanamāla and Kālikā Purāṇa, "the oldest known texts dealing with these pīṭhas",
    "do not include Hinglaj in their respective lists of four (Datta 2009, 157)" (p. 52, folio not stated).
    `Kulārṇava Tantra`: "Hiṅgulā" is found there and, "when allegedly quoting the Rudrayāmala—names eighteen
    pīṭhas, with 'Hiṅgulā' being third on the list (Sircar 1973, 18)" (p. 52, folio not stated); dated
    "earlier than 1000 CE" by P. V. Kane (Banerji 2007, 149) (p. 52, folio not stated), "between the eleventh
    and the fifteenth centuries" by Brooks (Brooks 2002, 347), and "between the thirteenth and the fourteenth
    centuries" by White (White 2003, 23) (p. 53, folio not stated). `Rudrayāmala`: composed "before 1052 CE"
    per Sircar (Sircar 1973, 17n4) but "not earlier than the thirteenth or fourteenth century" per White,
    during what he calls the "Tantric revival" (White 2003, 23); and decisively, "the currently available
    text of the Rudrayāmala Tantra itself does, in fact, mention only ten such pīṭhas as the principal holy
    sites of the Goddess. The name 'Hiṅgulā' is not one of them." (p. 53, folio not stated) `Kubjikā Tantra`:
    mentions Hinglaj "as the fifth of summa summarum forty-two pīṭhas (Sircar 1973, 19)", "usually supposed
    to be a fairly early work" per Sircar, "a late work" (twelfth to fourteenth centuries) per Bharati
    (Bharati 1965, 66n25), undated by Banerji (Banerji 2007, 146) (p. 53, folio not stated). The author's
    verdict: "tantric texts, therefore, prove fairly inaccurate for the attempt to date the occurrence of the
    name Hinglaj." (p. 53, folio not stated)
  - **Vāmana Purāṇa (VāmP), the possible earliest mention — and the author's refusal of the identification.**
    "At VāmP chapter 44, verse 47, it reads that Carcikā later roamed the earth only to eventually settle at
    'the best of all places—the Hingulata mountain' (Gupta 1968, 393)." (p. 53, folio not stated) Agrawala
    "assumes (a) that this line is, in fact, a reference to the mountain range in Balochistan and (b) that
    Hiṅgulāja is the 'Indianization' of a Nani cult at the same place (Agrawala 1964, 137)" (p. 53, folio not
    stated). **Schaflechner refuses (a):** "I cannot share Agrawala's conviction about the location of the
    shrine… a conclusive link between the text and the site in Balochistan as the location of the Hingula
    mountain is not given because the VāmP does not elaborate on where the aforesaid mountain is to be
    found." (pp. 53–54, folio not stated) Counter-evidence: "Brighenti points out the fact that another text,
    the early Pali Buddhist Kuṇālajātaka, locates the Hingula mountain in the Himalaya instead of toward the
    western side of the subcontinent (Brighenti 2016, 44)." (p. 54, folio not stated) VāmP datings: ninth to
    sixteenth centuries per scholars Rocher quotes, "most probably" the ninth per Hazara (Rocher 1986,
    240–241); "the first half of the seventh century" per G. P. Bhatt, "connecting it to the empires of
    Harṣavardhana and Mahendravarma Pallava" (Bhatt 2013, xiv); "late" per Mylius (Mylius 1988, 143) (p. 54,
    folio not stated). Verdict: "many scholars agree that the VāmP could indeed be of an early origin, thus
    making this possibly one of the first mentioning of the name Hiṅgulā." (p. 54, folio not stated)
  - **Brahmavaivarta Purāṇa (BVP).** Dating: the name was known to "Al Biruni (973–1048) and thus must be
    older than the eleventh century (Brown 1974, 23f.)", but "the extent to which this name also refers to
    the canonical text of the BVP as we find it today is questionable (Brown 1974, 24)"; Mylius calls it
    "fairly late" (Mylius 1988, 142); per Farquhar the relevant `Kr̥ṣṇajanmakhaṇḍa` "is an interpolation into
    the BVP", and "The general part about the pīṭhas must also be understood as a later interpolation,
    inserted not earlier than the fourteenth or fifteenth century (Sircar 1973, 6n3)." (p. 54, folio not
    stated)
  - **Devī Bhāgavata Purāṇa (DBh) — absent from the older list, present in a later one.** "The given list of
    108 names and places of worship in section VII, chapter 30, pp. 53–102 does not include the name Hinglaj.
    Only a later list in section VII, chapter 38, pp. 5–30 identifies Hiṅgulā as one of the śākta tīrthas.
    Even this list, however, is said to be a medieval interpolation (Sircar 1973, 107)." (pp. 54–55, folio not
    stated) Datings: core "likely from the eleventh or twelfth century" with the final version needing "four
    to five more centuries" (Brown 1992, 3); "between the eighth and twelfth centuries" per Beane, who
    "generally tends to date texts earlier than do other scholars" (Beane 1977, 62); "completed no later than
    1200 AD" per Hazara (p. 54, folio not stated). **The two page ranges are in an order the book does not
    explain — see Doubts.**
  - **Pīṭhanirṇaya (PN) — the text on which the shrine's primacy rests.** "In the Pīṭhanirṇaya (PN), the
    number of fifty-one śākta pīṭhas was first associated with the body parts of the Goddess Sati (Sircar
    1973, 4). In this list, Hinglaj is mentioned first, and thus the text is most frequently quoted as proof
    of the primary position of Hinglaj among all the śākta seats." (p. 55, folio not stated) What it supplies:
