    "the name of the pīṭha (Hiṅgulā, Hiṅgulāṭa) as well as the names of the residing Goddess there (Koṭṭarī,
    Koṭṭavī, Koṭṭarīśā), the associated form of Shiva (Bhīmalocana), and most significantly, the part of
    Sati's body—the brahmarandhra (Skt. 'upper part of the head')—that is said to have fallen there (Sircar
    1973, 85)." (p. 55, folio not stated) Its date: "Sircar concludes that the PN must have been composed
    between the years 1690 and 1720 (Sircar 1973, 24). Thus, it cannot be definitely proven that claims to
    Hinglaj's primacy among the śākta pīṭha due to its association with the Goddess's brahmarandhra are older
    than the given time frame of the PN." (p. 56, folio not stated) **`1690` and `1720` are both cleanly
    printed.** The text is elsewhere dated "(seventeenth to eighteenth century)" (p. 229, folio not stated).
  - **Tantracūḍāmaṇi — the source today's Hinglaj literature cites, which the book disqualifies.** "The claim
    that the upper part of the Goddess's head fell at Hinglaj is frequently cited as evidence of the site's
    importance, with literature about Hinglaj mentioning the Tantracūḍāmaṇi as the source of this claim
    (Lakhavat 2011, 23; Sharma 2011, 78; Samaur 1999, 503)." (p. 56, folio not stated) Banerji ascribes the
    work, "also known by the name 'Śāktakrama,'" to Purnananda Giri, "dating it to around the year 1571
    (Banerji 2007, 191)", possibly drawn from Chakravarti 1963, 67 (p. 56, folio not stated). "If these dates
    are correct, the association of Hinglaj with the Goddess's brahmarandhra could be as late as the sixteenth
    century. The Tantracūḍāmaṇi seems to have disappeared today; only Sircar mentions an existing copy of this
    manuscript in the library of the Royal Asiatic Society in Calcutta" (p. 56, folio not stated) — and Sircar
    also says that list "has little to do with the text of the Pīṭhanirṇaya" (Sircar 1973, 4). Verdict: "The
    reference to the site in an older Tantracūḍāmaṇi, often mentioned in today's literature about Hinglaj,
    cannot be seen as conclusive." (p. 57, folio not stated)
  - **The eleventh-century limit on the name, from Khajuraho.** "Support for the assumption that the name did
    not arise before the eleventh century is found in Desai's study on the image of Hiṅghalāja in Khajuraho.
    Following linguistic traces, and with the help of H. C. Bhayani, Desai concludes that the word Hiṅghalāja
    could not have originated earlier than the eleventh century (Desai 2013, 113f.)." (p. 98, folio not
    stated) Against that, "Sircar admits to defects in the text that prevent the name 'Hiṅglāja' from being
    fully determinable (Sircar 1973, 33n2)" (p. 98, folio not stated).
  - **The chapter's summary verdict on dating, which must be carried whole.** "the first mention of the name
    Hinglaj in the Sanskrit literature can only be estimated to have occurred between the ninth and fifteenth
    centuries. The association with the Goddess Sati's brahmarandhra, however, and the notion of Hinglaj as
    the primary pīṭha first appeared in the Pīṭhanirṇaya, which, according to Sircar, dates to sometime
    between 1690 and 1720 (Sircar 1973, 24)." (p. 57, folio not stated) **A second, differently bounded
    summary is given later and must be kept beside it:** "Although the name Hinglaj and its variations are
    well documented in a variety of Sanskrit sources, due to the difficulty of dating these texts, it is
    impossible to find a precise time in which it first appeared in the scriptures. Thus, it can only be
    assumed that the earliest mention of her must have occurred sometime between the tenth and the fifteenth
    centuries." (p. 96, folio not stated); and in the conclusion, "the mentioning of Hinglaj (as `Hiṅgulā`,
    `Hiṅgulāja`, `Hiṅgulāṭa`) in Sanskrit sources between the tenth and the fifteenth centuries" (p. 320,
    folio not stated). **Ninth-to-fifteenth against tenth-to-fifteenth — see Doubts.**
  - **Competing estimates of the cult's age, none endorsed.** Tambs-Lyche "could have been a part of a cult
    that originally began in the fifth century", on Colonel Tod, "but also acknowledges that Tod's dating
    tends to be too early"; with the Devī Māhātmaya reaching Rajasthan "in the sixth or seventh century", "a
    rough estimate between the fifth and seventh century could be an accurate guess (Tambs-Lyche 1999, 30n7)"
    (pp. 71–72, folios not stated). The author's own summary: the cult "was already active between the
    seventh and ninth centuries, possibly incorporating rituals that might have already existed at the shrine
    of Hinglaj/Nani in Balochistan. Unfortunately, no definite proof is available." (p. 74, folio not stated)
    "Following research on the Charan community, however, Kamphorst estimates that the shrine must have had
    some religious importance since at least the ninth century." (pp. 96–97, folio not stated) "According to
    Tambs-Lyche, Hinglaj could even have been linked to a cult already in existence as early as the fifth
    century. Such estimates, however, are merely based on oral histories and hence cannot be conclusively
    verified." (p. 97, folio not stated) **No date here may enter an entry as settled.**
  - **Hinglaj as the anchor of a regional Goddess map.** "the Goddess Hinglaj is understood by some as the
    first incarnation, the ādiśakti, and thus the center for many variations of Goddess worship in western
    India (Tambs-Lyche 2004, 64)"; "these various local incarnations became anchored to the shrine of Hinglaj
    and further interpreted in its light. With the establishment of these links to the place in Balochistan, a
    continuous history was created that is reiterated and perpetuated today in the stories and the poetry of
    the bards." (p. 74, folio not stated) Comparatively, White "emphasizes the similarities between Hinglaj
    and Kamakhya, another important śākta pīṭha in eastern India, and even calls them 'geographical and
    mythological mirror[s]' (White 1996, 196)" (pp. 211–212, folios not stated). Endnote 1 of Chapter 2 adds:
    "Ibrahim Shah mentions three śākta pīṭha in Pakistan, yet he does not present or evaluate the sources upon
    which he bases this assertion. However, I agree with him that Hinglaj is the most frequented of these
    three (Shah 2008, 26). Singh and Singh (2010) name two other places of Goddess worship in Pakistan
    mentioned in the Matsya Purāṇa." (p. 97, folio not stated) **The other two Pakistani śākta pīṭhas and the
    two Matsya Purāṇa sites are not named**: **(not stated)**.
  - **The melā's rank, and why remoteness makes it possible.** "one of the biggest—for many even the
    biggest—festival for Hindu communities in Pakistan" (p. 271, folio not stated). "the location of the shrine
    in a remote desert region, outside urban settlements, is certainly a significant reason for the temple's
    increasing public visibility… related to the small number of public spaces available for Hindus in
    Pakistan" (p. 271, folio not stated). "The remote location of Hinglaj helps the community to celebrate in
    an undisturbed environment, unlike public Hindu celebrations elsewhere which can sometimes lead to
    resentment, or even violence." (p. 271, folio not stated) "Due to such insecurities, the shrine of Hinglaj
    represents a safe haven for undisturbed Hindu celebrations. Conveniently located close to a highway, yet
    far enough from urban centers and possible threats, Hinglaj provides a remote stage for public displays of
    Hindu faith and draws worshippers from all corners of the country." (p. 319, folio not stated) "the site's
    power to attract various castes, classes, and ethnicities also stems from its unique geographical
    position, which is on the one hand sufficiently distant and on the other close enough to urban settlements
    in Pakistan to house a free and unfettered Hindu religious practice." (p. 24, folio not stated) The valley
    is treated as "a confined Hindu religious site in the Islamic Republic" (p. 202, folio not stated).
  - **The shrine as the book's theoretical object.** "hegemony plays a crucial part in the solidification of
    tradition at the shrine of Hinglaj, where the struggle of opposing truth-claims is sedated through the
    institutionalization of one community and their particular practices and narratives." (p. 42, folio not
    stated) "this book does not merely aim at describing the wide range of voices engaging with Hinglaj and
    her Balochistan temple but rather looks at the struggles over the 'true' narratives about the Goddess and
    her ritual practices." (p. 43, folio not stated) "These two examples show how a struggle for one history
    or, in other words, the struggle over 'truth' at the Hinglaj shrine is fairly conspicuous today." (p. 47,
    folio not stated) "The valley of Hinglaj is a point of convergence for a wide array of (Pakistani) Hindu
    concepts and truths and, as I argued before, also a location where 'ideals of Hinduness' are performed."
    (p. 296, folio not stated) "The place describes and prescribes narratives and religious practices that also
    gain influence outside the valley." (p. 296, folio not stated) Why no single tradition formed: "Its remote
    location is also partially responsible for the fact that no single prevailing narrative evolved over time.
    The scarcity of pilgrims over the years, the comparatively small amount of written documentation, and the
    various languages (and scripts) involved in the recording of travelogues, oral accounts, or rituals at the
    shrine hindered a coherent single tradition from emerging." (p. 29, folio not stated) "Many different
    communities documented different kinds of mandatory rituals, routes, and places on the way. In short,
    various types of 'true,' 'actual,' and 'proper' practices and beliefs arose." (p. 30, folio not stated)
    "Devotees generally understand the place as a source of ancient and uniform wisdom with clear boundaries
    regarding proper and improper practices." (p. 30, folio not stated) "Many opinions I found about the shrine
    and the residing Goddess made it clear that for devotees, there is a 'real' or 'authentic' Hinglaj, which
    simply has not been correctly understood by others." (p. 30, folio not stated) "Due to numerous recent
    adjustments and changes at the temple and its festival, however, prevailing and widely uncontested
    narratives have not yet fully emerged." (p. 324, folio not stated)
  - **The Sindh connection and Sindhi nationalism.** "the Goddess Hinglaj is also called locally among (Zikri)
    Muslim and Hindu communities in Las Bela and the Makran. This name raises the possibility of a link between
    Hinglaj/Bibi Nani and the Chaldean Goddess Nana" (p. 91, folio not stated). "The valley of Hinglaj is also
    located in this area and served the ancient Sindhis as a place of contemplation. This is why—according to
    Abro—Hinglaj can also be called a 'thought evolution site' (Abro 1991, 25; Sin. avāilī vīcār vastyūn)."
    (p. 94, folio not stated) The argument the site is made to carry: "This approach will show both how Hindu
    cultural practices flourish in Pakistan today and also how they need a certain environment—such as
    Hinglaj—to thrive." (p. 23, folio not stated) Why Indian interest matters: "Because many are accustomed to
    looking to India in terms of religious authority, this valorization of the shrine is refreshing for the
    community" (p. 270, folio not stated), the dependence being caused by "a lack of translations of Hindu
    religious texts into Urdu (`nastaclīq`[text-layer?] [nastaʿlīq]), Punjabi (also śāh mukhī), and Sindhi
    (nasx) as well as the government's reluctance to offer religious education for Hindus in many schools"
    (p. 270, folio not stated). "This influx of pilgrims from India raises awareness about the shrine in a
    wider Pakistani media landscape, increases its appeal to Pakistani Hindus and, at the same time,
    introduces various religious practices from all over India at the shrine." (p. 270, folio not stated)
  - **The emergence of a local actor.** "the recent emergence of the Lasi-Lohana caste, a local community
    based around the shrine, as a new and powerful actor in the process of writing and rewriting the history
    of Hinglaj" (p. 24, folio not stated). The Chapter 6 preview states the mechanism: the plural religious
    landscape "has led not to a democratization of interpretation of the shrine's various traditions but
    rather to an imposition of a particular interpretation favored by the local Lasi Lohana community. Thanks
    to the vast institutional network attached to the temple committee—largely composed of Lasi Lohana group
    members—Lohana practices at the temple have been framed and disseminated in a way that suggests their
    association with broader notions of progress and education. This narrative has allowed the temple
    committee to appeal to Hindus from a wide variety of backgrounds and perpetuate its own understanding of
    appropriate and 'correct' practices at the shrine." (p. 27, folio not stated) **The chunk does not say
    whether "the temple committee" there is the HSM — see Doubts.**
  - **Recent change at the site, stated at the outset and dated only loosely.** "Due to the shrine's new
    accessibility paired with its recent institutionalization, many formerly disconnected practices and
    narratives started to meet on a regular basis." (p. 24, folio not stated) "an overview of the recent
    infrastructural and other developments at and around the shrine and their effect on each of these
    historical narratives" (p. 24, folio not stated). "the chapter demonstrates how both man-made and natural
    material alterations in the past, such as renovations or landslides, led to new narratives and ritual
    practices at the shrine." (p. 27, folio not stated) **No renovation and no landslide is dated there**:
    **(not stated)**. "Today, the tradition of Hinglaj finds itself at an intricate crossroads. On the one
    hand, it is rooted in an ancient history; on the other hand, the site has seen some of its most
    disruptive changes within only a few decades." (p. 195, folio not stated) "the prestige and exclusivism
    surrounding the pilgrimage to Hinglaj, having all but disappeared in Pakistan following the construction
    of the MCH, is still very much alive in India" (p. 26, folio not stated). "After the construction of the
    Makran Coastal Highway, Hinglaj attracted a large and diverse following with a variety of different ideas
    on how to practice faith at the shrine." (p. 297, folio not stated) "The establishment of annual festivals
    at Hinglaj, for example, offers the means for a continuous interpellation of certain characteristics as
    the tradition's natural elements." (p. 324, folio not stated) "Before the 1980s, the shrine's character
    was marked by this reputation for danger… But in recent years, the shrine has become increasingly
    accessible for anyone in Pakistan, and its reputation is therefore changing." (p. 97, folio not stated)

### chandragup-baba-chandragup — Chandragup (Baba Chandragup)

**The second-densest row in this book.** The book spells the site `Chandrakup` with a *k* throughout,
against the archive's `Chandragup` with a *g*; it also prints `Chandragup`, `Kup`, `Caṃdrakūp Bābā`,
`Caṃdrakūp Mahārāj`, `Baba Chandrakup`, `the mud volcano`, `Chandrakup volcano`, `Chandrakup mud
volcano`, and in a Sindhi quotation `Chandarkūp` (pp. 128, 130, 133, 181, 200, 203, 210, 264, folios
not stated). **The archive row's `principal_figure`, "Shiva (associated)", is the archive's, not the
book's, except where the book is quoted saying so below.** See Doubts for the spelling gap.

- **Shrine / site**
  - **The name and its two etymologies, as printed.** "The mud volcano Chandrakup (from Hin.
    caṃdrakūp 'the moon well') (sometimes shortened to 'Kup'), also called Chandragup (from Sin.
    chandra gūp 'the moon's mud') (cf. Motyani 2011, 281; Stein 1943, 202)" (p. 210, folio not
    stated). **The archive row's name, "Chandragup (Baba Chandragup)", matches the second form.**
  - **Its place on the route, measured.** "one of the stops en route to Hinglaj most often described"
    (p. 210, folio not stated); "The stop is located to the south of the MCH, at a distance of around
    84 kilometers from the last major paṛāv, the village of Liari (Chhabria 2000, 60)." (p. 211, folio
    not stated) "Chandrakup is not connected to the main road, and therefore pilgrims must walk a few
    kilometers from the highway through the desert." (p. 211, folio not stated) In the older route it
    lies between Sonmiani and the River Aghor: from Sonmiani the party proceeds "westward in the
    direction of the mud volcano Chandrakup" (p. 140, folio not stated); "the westbound path toward
    Chandrakup proceeds mostly according to the location of wells in the area" (p. 140, folio not
    stated); it is "several kilometers" from the well where the dead watchman is found (p. 141, folio
    not stated). On the modern walking route, "Only one stop, Kanda Devi station (Hin. paṛāv), a few
    kilometers after Chandrakup, is unique to the group's route" (p. 159, folio not stated), after
    which "the community reached the Hingol River" (p. 160, folio not stated). In Jethamalani's 1994
    list it is the second of the two asthāns of the Khari Nadi paṛāv: "Chandrakup, a mud volcano that
    is worshipped as an embodiment of Shiva" (p. 203, folio not stated).
  - **Three volcanoes, one of them ritual; and the counts disagree between sources.** "Three mud
    volcanoes are located in close proximity to one another, the highest of which plays a central role
    in religious practices related to the pilgrimage" (p. 210, folio not stated). Figure caption:
    "FIGURE 5.2 Pilgrims descending Chandrakup. View toward the south crater, 2012" (p. 210, folio not
    stated). Note 10: "Hart writes that there are a total of eighteen such mud volcanoes in the area:
    seven close to Chandrakup and another eleven at other locations in Makran (Hart 1840, 145)."
    (p. 263, folio not stated) Note 12: "During my fieldwork, three volcanoes were visible in close
    proximity, but I have only witnessed ritual practices at the highest one, located in the north.
    Hart mentions that he saw four mud volcanoes in this area, but he also notes that offerings were
    only performed at the northern-most crater (Hart 1839, 89). Snead also noted four, which he
    observed to be in different stages of development (Snead 1964, 549)." (p. 263, folio not stated)
  - **Its size, as the book gives it.** "Over the years, the geothermal activity of the mud volcano
    has produced an accumulation of residue11 reaching a current height of almost 150 meters, making
    Kup one of the world's largest volcanoes of its kind." (p. 211, folio not stated) **The `residue11`
    is a note callout glued to the word, not a numeral — see Doubts.**
  - **Its physical state, reported and observed.** "From Hart's account, it is evident that the north
    and south craters at Chandrakup were both once active (Hart 1839, 87–91). After an earthquake in
    1945, the craters ejected an enormous amount of mud, and local witnesses observed a large fireball
    in the area, most likely due to the ignition of a tremendous amount of gas released in the quakes
    (Sondhi, cited in Delisle et al. 2002, 96)." (p. 211, folio not stated) "During my own visits, I
    always found the southern volcano to be dormant, but the northern volcano always seemed to be in
    an active state, though the intensity of its emissions varied." (p. 211, folio not stated) The
    author corrects the novels: "Abadhut's account of this trope of self-sacrifice is curious, as it
    portrays Chandrakup as an actual volcano filled with deadly lava (molten rock) able to kill
    immediately on contact. The geothermal activities at Chandrakup, however, make suicide there a
    difficult endeavor. Because Chandrakup's activity is caused by the rising of methane gas, its
    surface is neither hot nor smoke-emitting (Chapter 5). In addition, the mud at Chandrakup's
    surface often turns fairly solid (Schaflechner 2011, 21:40)" (p. 133, folio not stated). The
    chemistry, cited to a geologist: "According to the geologist A. W. Stiffe, the liquid part of this
    matter consists of chlorine, sulfuric acid, lime, soda, and potash (Stiffe, cited in Snead 1964,
    555)." (p. 263, folio not stated — note 11). A criticism of a prior survey: "Delisle et al. have
    done extensive research at the site. In their paper, however, they mistake the north and south
    craters repeatedly, which leads to confusion for the reader." (p. 264, folio not stated — note 13)
  - **Who is worshipped there is explicitly unsettled.** "There is no prevailing notion about which
    deity is actually worshipped at the rim of the volcano. While Abadhut does not mention any
    particular god, but talks generally about 'Caṃdrakūp Bābā' or 'Caṃdrakūp Mahārāj,' other accounts
    emphasize that Chandrakup is associated with Rama." (p. 214, folio not stated) Against that,
    "Pilgrims nowadays often explain simply that the volcano is the seat of Shiva, who must be visited
    and appeased with offerings so as to gain permission to meet his wife in her private abode.
    Jethamalani also maintains that the God Shiva is worshipped there (Jethamalani 1994, 44). Motyani,
    too, writes that Shiva is worshipped at Chandrakup and reinforces the explanation that the bubbles
    that rise from the crater are manifestations of the god in the form of a liṅga (Skt.; Motyani
    2011, 281)." (p. 215, folio not stated) **The archive row's `principal_figure`, "Shiva
    (associated)", is consistent with this page; the book states the association as current pilgrim
    explanation and as two named authors' claims, not as its own.** Elsewhere: "at the mud volcano
    Chandrakup, stories of Rama or Shiva are recalled" (pp. 146–147, folios not stated); Jasol's
    contrary description, "There is a śivakuṇḍ on this mountain, out of which lava is erupting" (Jasol
    2008, 46) (p. 148, folio not stated); Kshatriya's, "the slope of the volcano itself has the form of
    Shiva's cilam (Kshatriya 2005, 88)" (p. 264, folio not stated — note 15); and in Sindhi,
    "Chandarkūp srī rāmachandar sān mansūb āhe" (Sin.; Ansari 2001, 296)" (p. 264, folio not stated —
    note 17), **printed with no gloss**: **(not stated)**.
  - **Named among the areas held to be self-generated:** "the concept of svayaṃbhū … can be linked to
    whole areas, as in the case of the Hinglaj Valley, the area surrounding Chandrakup volcano, or even
    the whole desert" (p. 200, folio not stated).
  - **The author's negative finding on alchemy.** "Although Chandrakup itself is not among White's
    often cited mercury wells that, when combined with sulfur, serve as an alchemical symbol for the
    mystical union of Shiva and the Devi (White 1996, 194), he describes the volcano as the 'principal
    feature' of the pilgrimage (White 1996, 205)." (p. 212, folio not stated) "In general, White draws
    many interesting conclusions in his work, but I have not encountered any traces of his alchemical
    hypotheses at Chandrakup or the Hinglaj shrine today." (p. 212, folio not stated)
  - **No temple, no image, no custodian, no priest, no district, no building, no ʿurs and no annual
    date of its own is given for this site anywhere in the book**: all **(not stated)**. The archive's
    "Baba Chandragup" personal form appears in the book only as Abadhut's `Caṃdrakūp Bābā` /
    `Caṃdrakūp Mahārāj` and as "Baba Chandrakup" (pp. 128, 214, folios not stated).

- **Practices & Events**
  - **Its function on the route is to grant or withhold permission to go on.** "the group sets off in
    the direction of Chandrakup, the mud volcano, where they will ask for permission to proceed to the
    shrine of Hinglaj Mata (Abadhut 1964, 47)" (p. 128, folio not stated). "many accounts even say that
    it is not possible to enter the Goddess's valley without having made offerings there (Shastri 1977,
    36)" (p. 210, folio not stated); "many pilgrims today believe that Hinglaj's temple may only be
    entered after visiting Chandrakup and making proper offerings" (p. 212, folio not stated); "after
    Thirumal's suicide, many pilgrims failed to perform the proper rituals at the volcano and thus
    traditionally would not be allowed to proceed to Hinglaj" (p. 134, folio not stated).
  - **The preparation rite.** "The aguā gathers the pilgrims around the chaṛī and orders them to fast
    and to stay awake through the night for the preparation of the roṭ, a special kind of cake that
    will be offered to Chandrakup the next day." (p. 141, folio not stated) "Before the group ascends
    the mud volcano, Rupalal instructs them on the proper proceedings at Chandrakup (Abadhut 1964,
    156–157). He says that to receive permission to continue their journey, they must worship
    Chandrakup and yell out their sins at the crater's edge. Only then will they be allowed to carry on
    with their journey. The pilgrims also bake a small cake (roṭ), which must be offered to the
    volcano." (p. 130, folio not stated) "After an emotional night in which many pilgrims worry acutely
    about their upcoming confessions at Chandrakup, the group begins their ascent (Abadhut 1964, 164)."
    (p. 130, folio not stated) Approach conditions: "The area before Chandrakup is quite arid, and
    there is nothing to protect the pilgrims from the blazing heat." (p. 129, folio not stated); "ahead
    lies one of the most demanding parts of their journey" (p. 129, folio not stated).
  - **The pūjā and the confession rite, in full.** "The next day, on the top of Chandrakup, the
    chaṛīdār performs the pūjā and tells the pilgrims to come forward one by one." (p. 141, folio not
    stated) "Once at the top and at the rim of the crater, the pilgrims must admit their sins, after
    which they should throw their offerings into the volcano. Before yelling out their sins, every
    pilgrim must state his or her name, caste, and village, as well as his or her father's,
    grandfather's, and great-grandfather's names (Shastri 1977, 42). The offerings consist of gāṁjā
    (Hin. marijuana), coconuts, and cilams." (p. 141, folio not stated) "On the top of the volcano,
    Rupalal offers the roṭ prepared by all the pilgrims together by throwing it into the crater
    (Abadhut 1964, 166). One after another, the pilgrims are called to confess their sins." (p. 130,
    folio not stated) — **the roṭ is prepared collectively and offered by the aguā, not by each
    pilgrim.** Further offerings: "Some pilgrims also offer pieces of bread. Snead mentions the
    offering of Ganges water, earthen pots, flowers, and red thread (Snead 1964, 553n9)." (p. 264,
    folio not stated — note 14)
  - **The sanction, as the sources give it.** "the offerings of those who are righteous, or of those
    who confess all their sins, will be accepted, and their offerings will sink immediately into the
    mud. But the contributions of the evildoers who do not confess will not sink down into the volcano,
    and the bubbling of the mud will stop. Such people will have stones thrown at them and then be sent
    away." (p. 141, folio not stated) Note 43 gives the Hindi: "`. . . pahāṛ se ḍhelā mārkar bhagā diyā
    jāyegā`" (Shastri 1977, 42) (p. 148, folio not stated). At its harshest: "According to the bubbling
    of the mud and the reaction of the wind, the chaṛīdār is able to tell if the pilgrim's sins are
    forgiven (Shastri 1977, 41). If anyone is unwilling to confess (or if his or her transgressions are
    too cruel to be forgiven), then the bubbles stop rising and the offering is not accepted (Lakhavat
    2011, 138). According to Abadhut and Shastri, in such situations, the yātrī is not allowed to
    proceed on the journey and must be left there in the desert to die (Abadhut 1964, 204; Shastri
    1977, 41)." (p. 213, folio not stated)
  - **The coconut augury, and two flatly opposed acceptance rules.** "According to tradition, the
    number of bubbles that rise, together with the intensity of the gas emission in response to the
    offering, defines the pilgrim's destiny." (p. 212, folio not stated) "Abadhut mentions that the
    offerings must submerge into the mud as a sign that Baba Chandrakup has accepted the oblation;
    however, this practice is not consistently followed. Even Hart indicates that at the time of his
    own journey, the offerings did not sink due to the thickness of the mud (Hart, cited in Hughes
    1877, 145)." (p. 213, folio not stated) The 2010 party: "the bus drove directly to a small hut on
    the Makran Coastal Highway where the HSM had organized `a off-road vehicle`[text-layer?] [an
    off-road vehicle] to pick up the pilgrims and transport them to Chandrakup mud volcano. There,
    everybody climbed to the top and, per Dayaram's instruction, threw a coconut into the crater
    (Schaflechner 2011, 23:00)." (p. 181, folio not stated) "Since the mud was fairly solid on that
    day, only a few coconuts submerged into the volcano. However, no one seemed bothered by that, and
    they all continued their journey. Dayaram later said that according to the tradition, people would
    not be allowed to continue if their offering was not accepted, but since the Goddess had already
    brought the pilgrims from a place so far away, she surely wanted to see them at her abode."
    (p. 181, folio not stated) **The inverse rule, from a named community:** "Certain Devipujak-Vagri
    pilgrims from Karachi have a contrasting interpretation: for them, the offering is accepted only
    when the coconut is thrown up again by the volcano. The explanation they offer for their belief is
    that Shiva, who resides in the volcano, must manifest in the form of a liṅga. This happens when a
    larger bubble emerges that takes the conical shape of the liṅga." (p. 264, folio not stated — note
    16). **Two communities' rules, not a correction of one by the other; both are kept. See Doubts.**
  - **The stick cooled in the volcano, on the modern walking route.** From the Liari paṛāv "They walked
    from this stop to Chandrakup, where the group 'cooled down' the chaṛī, the stick held by the
    group's leader, by immersing it in the volcano. This cooling down of the stick is associated with
    the heat that is generated in the process of the pilgrimage through bodily and mental exhaustion."
    (p. 160, folio not stated)
  - **Its place in the current festival canon, and its opening position in the melā.** "To this day,
    Chandrakup remains one of the principal places visited during the phālgun/caitra melā, and the stop
    is mentioned on the announcement flyers distributed by the HSM. The mud volcano's inclusion within
    the temple committee's current 'pilgrimage canon' has secured its position within the revitalized
    pilgrimage network." (p. 215, folio not stated) "In recent years, the melā has officially begun
    with the visit to Chandrakup on the first day and ended with a collective havan (Hin. ceremony), in
    front of the Hinglaj temple on the third day (see Fig. 6.3)." (p. 281, folio not stated)
  - **A police levy at its approach.** "During the 2012 melā, every pilgrim had to pay an additional
    100 rupees at a checkpoint when approaching Chandrakup, one of the main stops on the way to
    Hinglaj. This money, however, was to be paid to the police and not to the HSM." (p. 291, folio not
    stated) **The officer, the unit and the checkpoint's exact location are not named**: **(not
    stated)**.
  - **A bath and a gift on the legendary route.** On the Brahmakshatriya route the princes "proceed to
    Chandrakup, where they take a bath and present a gift of 60,000 cows to the local Brahmins",
    between "the five wells" and "Karavirak, in the middle of the Hingol River" (p. 64, folio not
    stated). Mekan Dada lists it first among the things he must do before going home: "I will go to
    Chandrakup, I will climb up to Anil Kund" (p. 78, folio not stated).

- **Legends & Miracles**
  - **The eighty-four Chandra-Kups**, quoted from Hughes, *The Country of Baluchistan, Its Geography,
    Topography, Ethnology and History*: "In the 'Sat Jug,' or golden age, Suda Shiwa, or Siva, and
    Parbatti, his wife, visited the Makli hills near Tatta… He arrived at Hinglaj, but could not find
    the Devi there. It is said that she took the shape of a fly, and so remained on the ball of ashes
    which Siva held in his hand. For twelve years he searched for her, but in vain, when, in a paroxysm
    of rage he dashed the ball to the ground, where it lay broken in eighty-four pieces. These
    eighty-four parts of the ball became the hills known as 'Chandra-Kups.' (Hughes 1877, 142)"
    (p. 214, folio not stated).
  - **The eighteen Koops**, quoted from Hart's 1839 travelogue: "'After the abduction of Seeta by
    Rawan,' [sic] among others engaged in endeavouring to discover the place of her concealment was
    Sedasheo (a form of Mahadeo.) For twelve years did he unceasingly prosecute the search without
    success… he dashed his 'Biboot' (cake made of the fine ashes of cow-dung, for rubbing on the neck
    and arms, or marking the forehead) on the ground. It split into eighteen pieces, and formed as many
    Koops. Seeta, in the form of Shree Mata instantly appeared… she ordained that previous to visiting
    her Temple, every pilgrim should pay his devotions at one of the 'Koops,' which Sedasheo named,
    after her husband 'Rama Chandra,' and his spirit is supposed to inhabit them. (Hart 1839, 88f.)"
    (pp. 214–215, folios not stated). **The `[sic]` is the book's own.** **Eighty-four against
    eighteen; the author does not reconcile them — see Doubts.** His comment: "While this story clearly
    associates the mud volcano with the story of Rama and Sita, today, stories involving the prince
    from Ayodhya and his wife are rarely found in relation to the volcano." (p. 215, folio not stated)
  - **The volcano withholds its bubbles from an unconfessed sin — reported in both novels.** In
    Abadhut: "one of the pilgrims fails to fully confess his sins, and the bubbles of the volcano
    immediately cease to appear. With this episode, the author leaves open the possibility of divine
    intervention." (p. 134, folio not stated); at length, "At one man's turn, however, the wind stops
    and the volcano's bubbles cease to rise. This is understood as a sign that the pilgrim has not
    confessed to all of his sins, and so Rupalal implores him to repent. The man initially hesitates
    but then admits that he impregnated a servant who was subsequently sent away from his house a few
    years ago (Abadhut 1964, 168). After that, the volcano's bubbles again begin to rise (Abadhut 1964,
    169)." (pp. 130–131, folios not stated) In Shastri: "When the time comes for a devotee from Barmer
    to make his offering, the bubbles and the wind suddenly stop, and his gifts do not submerge in the
    volcano's mud. Only after he yells out his sins is his offering accepted and sinks down into the
    mud (Shastri 1977, 45)." (p. 141, folio not stated)
  - **A possession and an exorcism at the crater rim**, attributed to Shastri's account: "another
    pilgrim from Gujarat becomes possessed by a spirit. As he speaks, the man's voice changes to that
    of a female. The female ghost blames the pilgrim for having taken her virginity and then murdering
    her afterward. In revenge for his acts, the jinn announces that she will now kill the Gujarati man
    by throwing him into the volcano. But the chaṛīdār manages to cast out the spirit with mantras and
    some of the volcano's mud, which he smears on the man's forehead." (p. 141, folio not stated)
    **The book uses "spirit", "female ghost" and "jinn" of the same being within four sentences — see
    Doubts.**
  - **The volcano answers Shastri.** "At the conclusion of his speech on the difficulty of acting
    morally, a huge eruption spews from the volcano, spitting out two coconuts. Pleased, the chaṛīdār
    taps Shastri on the shoulder and explains this strange incident to him as a sign of Chandrakup's
    deep satisfaction with Shastri's words (Shastri 1977, 47)." (p. 142, folio not stated)
  - **Sin turned to smoke, rain and cloud** — Abadhut on Chandrakup, quoted in note 28: "Then this very
    sin [which has been thrown into Chandrakup] turns into smoke and dissolves in the endless sky. It
    becomes a cloud and rains down on the whole world; in the form of water [this sin] falls down on
    peoples' heads" (Abadhut 1964, 147) (p. 148, folio not stated). "This impression of the smoking
    crater was later adopted in the visual representation of the same scene in the film Marutīrtha
    Hiṃlāj." (p. 148, folio not stated)
  - **The rite broken off by a suicide.** "Suddenly, Thirumal appears on the rim of the crater and with
    wild laughter jumps into the volcano, submerging beneath the mud (Abadhut 1964, 169). In horror,
    the whole group runs down the volcano's side, including those who have not yet confessed their sins
    (Abadhut 1964, 170). Exhausted and terrified, they proceed toward Hinglaj without returning to
    Chandrakup." (p. 131, folio not stated) Rupalal later "says that the group repeatedly disregarded
    the proper rules of the pilgrimage (Abadhut 1964, 204). Particularly in light of Thirumal's suicide
    at Chandrakup, which prevented a few pilgrims from confessing their sins, he feels the group should
    not have been allowed to proceed—let alone cross the River Aghor." (p. 131, folio not stated)
    Earlier, "Kunti's secret hope is that at Baba Chandrakup, Thirumal will be healed of his mental
    illness (Abadhut 1964, 48)." (p. 128, folio not stated) — **this is the only place in that chapter
    where the honorific `Baba` is attached to the site.**
  - **Chandrakup as a wishing well**, attributed to Brohi: "Brohi writes that Chandrakup today is used
    as a wishing well where pilgrims ask the residing deity for favors (Brohi 1998, 32–33)." (p. 212,
    folio not stated)
  - **A comparable bubbling augury, referred by Briggs to Chaurasi and not to Chandrakup**, quoted from
    Haji Abdula Nabi: the water "rises up at times with a bubbling noise, discolored like a river fresh
    swollen after rain, and carrying mud in suspension. The Hindu pilgrims, when this takes place,
    throw in betel-nuts, cloves, cardamoms and coconut. Should there be delay in the rising, pilgrims
    in the most abject manner will call on Mātā to give them a sight of herself, exhorting each other
    to reveal their sins and inwardly repent. (Briggs 1938, 108)" (p. 213, folio not stated).

- **Significance**
  - **Its literary function, in the author's argument.** "For Abadhut—as later for Shastri—the volcano
    is a suitable literary `mise en scéne`[text-layer?] [mise en scène]" (pp. 133–134, folios not
    stated); "Chandrakup therefore serves as one of the main stages for conveying the subtext of the
    story" (p. 134, folio not stated); "both authors use Chandrakup as a central literary
    `mise en scéne`[text-layer?] [mise en scène] in which the stories' main characters are dramatically
    developed" (p. 145, folio not stated).
  - **Index evidence only.** "Chandrakup, 55, 70, 119–125, 131–136, 168, 186–189, 196–203, 197f"
    (p. 342, folio not stated), with the related headwords "coconuts, offerings of, 189, 199, 200, 290"
    (p. 342, folio not stated) and "gas, emission of, 199" (p. 345, folio not stated). **These are
    evidence that the book discusses those topics at those printed pages, nothing more, and no locator
    has been converted into a `(p. N)`.**

### bhit-bhit-shah — Bhit (Bhit Shah)

**Mapped under the single-row person ruling of 18 September 2026**: Shah Abdul Latif Bhittai is this
row's `principal_figure`, and the book names him as a poet, a traveller, a figure of legend and a
nationalist emblem — never as the occupant of the shrine at Bhit Shah, except once (below). The
book's forms: `Shah Abdul Latif "Bhittai"`, `Shah Abdul Latif`, `Shah Latif`, `Latif`, `Bhittai`;
in the quoted Mangi text `Shah Abdul Lateef Bhitai` and `Bhitai`; in the index `Latif, Shah Abdul`
(pp. 90, 94–96, 101, 108, 154–155, 240, 327, 332, 351, folios not stated).

- **Saint**
  - **Three sets of life dates, and none agrees with the archive.** "the journey of the national poet
    Shah Abdul Latif 'Bhittai' (1690–1751)" (p. 90, folio not stated) and "the Sindhi national poet
    Shah Abdul Latif 'Bhittai' (1690–1751)" (p. 95, folio not stated); against "Shah Abdul Latif
    (1689–1753)" (p. 108, folio not stated) and "Sindhi poets such as Shah Abdul Latif (1689–1753)"
    (p. 240, folio not stated). **The archive row's `figure_died` is 1752 and matches neither.** All
    are recorded as printed and none is corrected; see Doubts.
  - **No silsila, no lineage, no ʿurs and no tomb description is given for him anywhere in the book**:
    **(not stated)**.
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **His shrine is named exactly once, and only to tie it to Hinglaj.** "Latif's journey connects the
    shrine of Hinglaj Devi with his own dargāh in Bhit Shah (Sindh), and many legends exist telling of
    the connection between the saint and the Goddess." (p. 108, folio not stated) **The archive row's
    name and location, "Bhit (Bhit Shah)… Bhit, Sindh", agree.** **No building, no date, no custodian,
    no architecture and no ʿurs for the dargāh**: **(not stated)**.
- **Practices & Events**
  - **His journey to Hinglaj, dated two ways.** "As the poet visited Hinglaj in the eighteenth century,
    this important role assigned to Bhittai also shifts Hinglaj into the limelight." (p. 95, folio not
    stated); "according to Sindhi history, Shah Abdul Latif (1689–1753) had visited Hinglaj with a
    group of Kanphata Yogis at the beginning of the eighteenth century." (p. 108, folio not stated) —
    **the claim is attributed ("according to Sindhi history"), not asserted.** "It is said that Latif
    went to Hinglaj accompanying a group of 'kāpṛī' on their way to the shrine in Balochistan (Agha
    2004, 446). Moreover, following the visit to the shrine, the poet continued to wander with the
    kāpṛīs for three more years, visiting other places like Dwarka (Dwarka district, Gujarat), Lakhpat
    (Kutch), Lahoot (Khuzdar), and Jaisalmer (Rajasthan) (Agha 2004, 427)." (p. 95, folio not stated)
    **The companions are "Kanphata Yogis" at p. 108 and "kāpṛī" at p. 95 — both as printed.**
  - **Where the journey is recorded.** "Latif's experiences are documented in one part of his magnum
    opus, the Risalo, in a section called Sur Rāmkalī (Agha 2004, 427)." (p. 95, folio not stated) "His
    experiences with a group of 'Kapri' are found in his magnum opus, the Risālo." (p. 108, folio not
    stated) The relevant place: "there are repeated references to the Kapris' slit ears in the section
    Sur Rāmkalī chapter 3, 4–8. (Agha 2004, 446)" (p. 101, folio not stated).
  - **He is cited by living pilgrims as their warrant for walking.** Putu, a Kohli pilgrim: "Long ago
    Shah Abdul Latif also came here with a group of Yogis. He came here to ask the Mother to help him.
    See, we are also Yogis when we come here. Those who want something from the Mother are all called
    Yogis or saṃnyāsīs." (pp. 154–155, folios not stated) The author restates it: "Since the Sindhi
    poet Latif also walked to Hinglaj, Putu saw it as his duty (Hin. farz) to go on this pilgrimage."
    (p. 155, folio not stated) "Putu had walked to Hinglaj from his home village in interior Sindh when
    I met him at the shrine. He and other men in the group claimed that one of the main reasons for
    their visit to Hinglaj was its praise in the poetry of Bhittai." (p. 101, folio not stated)
- **Legends & Miracles**
  - **The milk miracle and the flight to Thatta**, attributed to legend: "Legend has it that after
    making a long and difficult journey, Latif had reached the shrine of the Goddess and was offering
    the Mother some milk from his palm. In this moment, the image came alive, and the Mother drank from
    the hands of the saint. This evoked the envy of the Yogis, who then decided to kill him. Latif fled
    the scene and disappeared into the earth only to re-emerge at Thatta, some 350 kilometers away."
    (pp. 108–109, folio not stated)
- **Significance**
  - **A cornerstone of Sindhi nationalist discourse**, per Verkaaik's list of its elements: "Sindhi
    language, the poetry of [national poet] Shah Abdul Latif, the Indus, the peasant or hari, and above
    all, Sufism and mysticism" (Verkaaik 2010, 197) (p. 88, folio not stated); "Described by Verkaaik
    as one of the key elements of Sindhi nationalist discourse (Verkaaik 2010, 197)" (p. 95, folio not
    stated). "According to another publication by Verkaaik, it was in fact G. M. Syed who held primary
    responsibility for generating Bhittai's role as the most important poet of Sindh (Verkaaik 2004,
    33)." (p. 101, folio not stated)
  - **Abdul Qadir Mangi's estimate of him**, quoted from the introduction to *Legacy of Shah Latif*:
    "Our ancient civilization Mohen-jo-Daro was, indeed, great. So is our saint-poet Shah Abdul Lateef
    Bhitai in his art and thought. He is our identity even in this modern age. Every Sindhi, of
    whatever faith he may be, believes in the greatness of Bhitai. From a Kohli Bheel to a Syed, every
    one considers Bhitai his spiritual guide. Probably no poet of any other language in the world has
    attained so much popularity and fame in masses. (Kanasro 2007, 11)" (p. 95, folio not stated)
    Schaflechner's gloss: "Shah Latif is here described as the embodiment of Sindhi identity that
    'even' remains in modern times. The poet is exalted as being the idol of every Sindhi,
    transgressing every distinction of religion, class or caste." (p. 95, folio not stated)
  - **The contested reading of his Hinglaj journey.** "This section has been interpreted in
    various—often diametrically opposed—ways, thereby validating the worldviews of orthodox (Sindhi)
    Muslims and Sindhi nationalists alike. While Sindhi nationalists look upon Latif as the ideal
    Sindhi—the perfect example for the tolerant and 'syncretic' history of Sindh—other, more orthodox
    writers aim to explain away Latif's journey as 'youthful recklessness' (Agha 2004, 427) with the
    goal of distancing the poet from any possible Hindu influence." (p. 95, folio not stated) "In such
    orthodox discourses, Latif's eventual break with the group of ascetics is attributed to a
    religiously animated disagreement (Agha 2004, 427)." (p. 96, folio not stated) "And third, the
    poetry of Shah Abdul Latif presents Hinglaj as an important religious site for both Islam and
    Hinduism, thereby transcendent of petty religious boundaries." (p. 96, folio not stated) "Within
    Sindhi nationalist arguments, however, the extensive description of the Hinglaj shrine in Latif's
    work is proof of the tolerant inclination of 'the Sindhi' and his positive attitude toward other
    religions." (p. 96, folio not stated) "Latif's peaceful journey with Hindu nomads is thus
    paradigmatic for the liberal character of 'the Sindhi.'" (p. 96, folio not stated) Abro uses him as
    a baseline: Hinglaj "holds an importance for Sindhis aside from its mere mention in the work of the
    national poet Shah Abdul Latif (Abro 1991, 21)" (pp. 94–95, folio not stated).
  - **He is why another scholar took up Hinglaj.** "Khan Baloch became interested in the place through
    the work of Sindhi poets such as Shah Abdul Latif (1689–1753) and Shah Inayatullah (1655–1718)."
    (p. 240, folio not stated) **The book does not say what in his work concerns Hinglaj**: **(not
    stated)**.
  - **Bibliography and index evidence, recorded as such.** "Agha, Muhammad Y., trans. (1985) 2004.
    Shah jo Risalo. Karachi: Sindhica Academy." (p. 327, folio not stated); `Kanasro, Manzoor A. 2007:`
    [text-layer?] [`Kanasro, Manzoor A. 2007.`] "Legacy of Shah Latif. Karachi: Sindhica Academy."
    (p. 332, folio not stated); index `Latif, Shah Abdul, 84, 88–90, 98–99, 142–143, 226` (p. 351,
    folio not stated), `Risalo (Latif), 89` (p. 358, folio not stated), `Legacy of Shah Latif (Mangi),
    89` (p. 351, folio not stated) with the author headword `Mangi, Abdula Qadir, 89` (p. 352, folio not
    stated). **Locators only; none has been converted into a `(p. N)`.**

### shri-swaminarayan-mandir-karachi — Shri Swaminarayan Mandir, Karachi

The book's forms: "the Swaminarayan temple on M.A. Jinnah Road", "the Swaminarayan temple on M. A.
Jinnah Road", "the Swaminarayan temple in Karachi", "the Swami Narayana organization"; index
`Swaminarayan temple, 167, 194` (pp. 159, 180, 207, 297, 360, folios not stated). **The street match
with the archive row's `location_short` ("M. A. Jinnah Road, Karachi, Sindh, Pakistan") is exact,
except that the book once prints `M.A. Jinnah Road` without a space after the initials.**

- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **Nothing is said about the building itself.** No founder, no foundation date, no deity named at
    the site, no custodian, no trustee, no architecture, no measurement, no revenue: all **(not
    stated)**.
- **Practices & Events**
  - **It is the present assembly point for the Hinglaj pilgrimage in Karachi, stated twice.** The 2012
    group "intended to visit several temples in Karachi, including the Jagnath akhāṛā, which, until
    just a few decades ago was a main gathering spot for Hinglaj pilgrims. (Today, this role has been
    largely assumed by the Swaminarayan temple on M.A. Jinnah Road.)" (p. 159, folio not stated) "This
    practice of starting the pilgrimage at one of the akhāṛās has generally ceased, with the
    Swaminarayan temple on M. A. Jinnah Road now serving as the main gathering spot for Hinglaj
    pilgrims in Karachi." (p. 207, folio not stated) **No date is given for when the role shifted**:
    **(not stated)**.
  - **It houses Indian pilgrim parties.** The 2010 Gujarati group travelled "in an air-conditioned bus
    that, on the visitors' second day, whisked them from their accommodation in the Swaminarayan temple
    in Karachi to Thatta and afterward to the valley of Hinglaj" (p. 180, folio not stated); the group
    returned to Karachi to stay the night after the Thatta offerings (p. 181, folio not stated). **The
    book attaches no ritual and no custodian to it — it is a dharamshala function only.**
- **Saint**
  - **The founder is dated but never named.** "The work of reform movements such as the Swami Narayana
    organization, established already during the lifetime of its founder (1781–1830), and later the
    ISKCON society, helped to securely link ahiṃsā and its corollary in vegetarianism to the
    conventional depiction of Hinduism and even to the Indian nation state itself." (p. 297, folio not
    stated) **The book prints only "(1781–1830)"; the archive row's `principal_figure` is "Bhagwan
    Swaminarayan" and its `figure_died` cell is empty. The book does not name him, does not call him
    Swaminarayan in person, and gives no death place**: **(not stated)**. **Whether "(1781–1830)" may
    be carried into that row's empty `figure_died` cell is a human decision — see Doubts.** The index
    keeps the movement and the temple apart: `Swami Narayana organization, 282` is a separate headword
    from `Swaminarayan temple, 167, 194` (p. 360, folio not stated), **and the two must not be
    merged.**

### shrine-of-pir-mangho — Shrine of Pir Mangho

The book's forms: "the shrine of Magho Pir", "the dargāh (Urd.) of the Sufi saint Mango Pir", "the
shrine of Mango Pir in Karachi", Hart's "Peer Moogha", and the index headword `Mango Pir, 71, 198`
(pp. 78, 211, 263, 352, folios not stated). **Three spellings against the archive's `Pir Mangho`; a
later worker matching on string alone will miss it. See Doubts.**

- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **Location, as the book gives it.** "the shrine of Magho Pir, which is now located in the northern
    part of Karachi" (p. 78, folio not stated); "the dargāh (Urd.) of the Sufi saint Mango Pir, in
    northern Karachi" (p. 211, folio not stated); "the shrine of Mango Pir in Karachi" (p. 263, folio
    not stated). **The archive row places it at "Gadap Town, Karachi, Sindh"; the book says only
    "northern Karachi".**
  - **Its one physical feature: hot springs.** "Other examples of similar thermic phenomena also exist
    on this anticline, such as at the dargāh (Urd.) of the Sufi saint Mango Pir, in northern Karachi
    (Snead 1964, 549)." (p. 211, folio not stated) — **the shrine is named in this book only as a
    geological comparison with Chandrakup.** And in note 9: "knowing that it is said that Lalu Jasraj
    stayed at a hermitage near the hot springs makes the association possible (Hart 1839, 80)."
    (p. 263, folio not stated)
  - **No saint's name, no dates, no silsila, no custodian, no ʿurs, no ritual and no crocodiles are
    given anywhere in the book**: all **(not stated)**.
- **Legends & Miracles**
  - **A conjectured association with Lalu Jasraj, twice, and hedged both times.** "It seems rather
    likely that the shrine of Magho Pir, which is now located in the northern part of Karachi, was once
    associated with Lalu Jasraj (Burton 1851, 326)." (p. 78, folio not stated) Lalu Jasraj is, in the
    Kapri narratives, "Rama's aguā (Hin. guide)" and "the founder of the Kapris" (pp. 74, 78, folios
    not stated), and elsewhere "the chaṛīdār (Hin. stickbearer) of Rama and his entourage" (p. 209,
    folio not stated). Note 9: "Hart's description allows for an association of the mythological figure
    Lalu Jasraj with the shrine of Mango Pir in Karachi. Notwithstanding the fact that Hart calls the
    saint 'Peer Moogha,' knowing that it is said that Lalu Jasraj stayed at a hermitage near the hot
    springs makes the association possible (Hart 1839, 80)." (p. 263, folio not stated) **The
    association is the author's own and is hedged — "It seems rather likely", "allows for an
    association", "makes the association possible" — and is not asserted as fact.** The author is
    separately sceptical about the Lalu Jasraj cycle: "I came across this narrative mainly during my
    conversations with Dayaram Kapri from Gujarat… who claimed that this reference is found in the
    Hiṃgulā Purāṇ. Unfortunately, I was not able to locate the story there, and only a few of my other
    informants in Pakistan mentioned this myth about Liari, so it is questionable how widespread it
    is outside the Gujarati Kapri community." (p. 209, folio not stated)
  - **Index evidence only.** `Mango Pir, 71, 198` (p. 352, folio not stated); the headwords
    `methane gas, 198` (p. 353, folio not stated) and `Stiffe, A. W., 198n11` (p. 361, folio not
    stated) carry locators on the same printed page — **evidence only that the book discusses all three
    on that page, not that they are connected.**

### lal-shahbaz-qalandar — Lal Shahbaz Qalandar

The book's forms: `Lal Shahbaz Qalandar`, then `Lal Shahbaz`, `Shahbaz`, `Qalandar`; index
`Lal Shahbaz Qalandar, 148` and, separately, `Qalandar, Lal Shahbaz, 97–98, 179` (pp. 107, 108, 160,
191, 351, 357, folios not stated).

- **Saint**
  - **No death date, no dates of any kind and no silsila for him anywhere in the book**: **(not
    stated)**.
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **The shrine is named with a district that disagrees with the archive.** "One example is provided
    by the dargāh (Urd. shrine, tomb of a saint) of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar in Sehwan Sharif in the Dadu
    district." (p. 107, folio not stated) **`shrine_index.tsv` gives the row's location as "Sehwan
    Sharif, Jamshoro District, Sindh". Both are recorded; neither is corrected. See Doubts.**
  - **No tomb description, no building, no date, no custodian and no revenue**: **(not stated)**.
- **Practices & Events**
  - **Shared Hindu–Muslim use.** "Today, local and translocal Hindu and Muslim communities alike visit
    this shrine regularly, and both take part in the worship of the saint (Boivin 2012, 290)."
    (pp. 107–108, folio not stated)
  - **Two Hindu families in the ʿurs ritual.** "During the curs[text-layer?] [ʿurs] (Urd. celebration,
    wedding of the saint with God) of Lal Shahbaz, two Hindu families play a crucial role in the
    arrangement of the henna (Hin. mehaṁdī) ceremony at the shrine. According to the legend, it was
    Shahbaz himself who arranged this process and appointed, in addition to the local Muslim elite, two
    Hindu families to take part in it (Boivin 2012, 290)." (p. 108, folio not stated) **Neither family
    is named and no ʿurs date is given**: **(not stated)**. **This is the only ʿurs named anywhere in
    the book.**
  - **An annual walking pilgrimage to it, abandoned for Hinglaj in 2004.** "Before this, he walked
    annually to the `curs`[text-layer?] [ʿurs] of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar where, according to his own
    account, he had gone for eleven subsequent years. Raj frequently went together with members of the
    Christian and Hindu paṃcāyat from his neighborhood. Eventually he stopped going to Sehwan and
    instead started to go to Hinglaj." (pp. 160–161, folios not stated) In Raj Kumar's own words, from
    a 2012 interview: "We set out walking from Liari [on our way to Sehwan Sharif] and kept our luggage
    on a camel, [and also] rations [Engl.], water . . . It was my first year. We went with the
    Meghwarji—the Meghwarjis from next to our neighborhood. We were five guys. So I thought to myself:
    'Man, forget Qalandar! Again we are climbing the mountains for Noorani. The Mother's [Hinglaj] road
    is all straight. It is by road [Engl.].' I said: 'We have to go this way!' So the Qalandar was out
    [lit.: died] and I left from there [to Hinglaj].'" (p. 160, folio not stated) The route: "The foot
    journey from Liari to Sehwan meant a long and grueling trek through the Pab mountain chain, a
    relatively arduous and exhausting path in comparison to the route to Hinglaj along the highway."
    (p. 161, folio not stated) The author's reading: "Raj Kumar and his fellow travelers changed not
    only their route but even their final destination in 2004 due to the existence of an easier, 'by
    road' walking route to Hinglaj." (p. 161, folio not stated) And: "As confirmed by Raj Kumar, one of
    the walking pilgrims from Narayanpur, the easy road along the highway was an incentive to change
    his annual pilgrimage from Lal Shahbaz to Hinglaj Devi." (p. 191, folio not stated) **The
    untranslated original of the interview is printed in Chapter 4's note 2** (p. 192, folio not
    stated) — see Doubts, since the book states no referent for `qalaṃdar` or `nūrānī` there.
- **Significance**
  - **The head of a pilgrimage route into Balochistan.** "Lal Shahbaz is also the starting point for
    the pilgrimage to Lahoot la Makan, the abode of the saint Shah Noorani in the Khuzdar district in
    Balochistan." (p. 108, folio not stated) A 2010 encounter confirms the route is still used: "In
    2010, I met a Punjabi pilgrim in Lahoot la Makan who had just arrived from Sehwan Sharif and
    confirmed that this side of the pilgrimage route is still frequented; however, he mentioned that
    the longer path to Hinglaj is no longer used due to the dangerous political situation in the area."
    (p. 147, folio not stated)
  - **Index evidence only, and the two runs must not be merged.** `Lal Shahbaz Qalandar, 148` (p. 351,
    folio not stated) and `Qalandar, Lal Shahbaz, 97–98, 179` (p. 357, folio not stated), **with no
    cross-reference between them and no overlap in the runs.** See Doubts.

### sadh-belo-sadh-belo-island-temple — Sadh Belo (Sadh Belo Island Temple)

The book spells it `Sadh Bela`, against the archive's `Sadh Belo`; index `Sadh Bela, 129` (pp. 138,
359, folios not stated).

- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **Named with its island and its city, which is what makes the identification.** "he uses the time
    to go to Sadh Bela, a tīrtha, as he is told, in the city of Sukkur on an island in the River Indus"
    (p. 138, folio not stated). **The archive row reads "Sadh Belo (Sadh Belo Island Temple)", "Sadh
    Belo Island, near Sukkur/Rohri, Sindh, Pakistan" — the location matches; the terminal vowel does
    not.** **No building, no date, no architecture, no measurement, no revenue**: **(not stated)**.
  - **Its custodian, by office only.** "There the mahaṃt (Hin. superior of a monastery, abbot) of Sadh
    Bela grows very fond of Shastri and provides him with supplies and advice for his pilgrimage."
    (p. 138, folio not stated) **The mahaṃt is not named, no date is given, and the archive row's
    `principal_figure`, Baba Bankhandi Maharaj, is not mentioned anywhere in the book**: **(not
    stated)**.
- **Practices & Events**
  - **The one practice recorded is hospitality to a Hinglaj pilgrim.** "The abbot instructs him to keep
    many onions in case of a drinking-water shortage; he advises that the onion's juice would help
    stave off nausea and dizziness as well as provide liquid (Shastri 1977, 8)." (p. 138, folio not
    stated) Note 32 pairs the onion advice with Abadhut: "Abadhut (1964, 79)" (p. 148, folio not
    stated). **Everything this book carries into this row is a single visit, datable only to Shastri's
    journey, and an office title.**

### ramapir-temple-tando-allahyar — Ramapir Temple, Tando Allahyar

The book's forms: "the fourteenth-century saint Ramdevji, whose main temple is located in the Sindhi
city of Thando Allah Yar"; index headword `Ramdevji, 2` (pp. 13, 357, folios not stated). **The book
spells the town `Thando Allah Yar`, three words with an aspirated `Th-`, against the archive row's
`Tando Allahyar`, and spells the figure `Ramdevji` against the row's `Ramdev Pir (Ramapir)`. Quoted
as printed; see Doubts.**

- **Saint**
  - **A dating the archive row does not carry.** "Many—especially in the rural areas—follow the
    teachings of local Sufi pīrs (Urd. spiritual guide) or adhere to the teachings of the
    fourteenth-century saint Ramdevji, whose main temple is located in the Sindhi city of Thando Allah
    Yar." (p. 13, folio not stated) **"Fourteenth-century" is Schaflechner's dating; the row's
    `figure_died` cell is empty, so this book would be supplying it. Carry it forward as
    Schaflechner's, not as the archive's, and see Doubts.**
  - **This is the whole of what the book says about him.** No death year, no silsila or sampradaya, no
    shrine description, no ʿurs or mela, no custodian and no visitor figure: **(not stated)**.
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **Named only as "his main temple" in that one sentence** (p. 13, folio not stated). The index adds
    a single locator, `Ramdevji, 2` (p. 357, folio not stated). **The separate index headword `Ram Pir
    temple, 150` (p. 357, folio not stated) is deliberately left unattached to this row — the book does
    not locate that temple.** Separately, the book attests Ramapir devotion in Karachi: "the local Ram
    Pir temple" in the Narayanpur neighbourhood of Karachi (p. 163, folio not stated), **which is not
    this row's site** — see Doubts.

### shah-noorani-shrine-syed-bilawal-shah-noorani — Shah Noorani Shrine (Syed Bilawal Shah Noorani)

The book's forms: `Shah Noorani` for the saint; `Lahoot la Makan`, `Lahoot`, `Lāhūt`, and in an Urdu
book title `Lāhūṭ lā Makān` for the site (pp. 108, 147, 241, folios not stated). **The archive row's
`location_short` is "Lahoot Lamakan valley, Khuzdar District, Balochistan, Pakistan" — district and
valley agree exactly; the book writes the place as three words against the archive's two.**

- **Saint**
  - "the abode of the saint Shah Noorani in the Khuzdar district in Balochistan" (p. 108, folio not
    stated), "who is said to have lived in this valley" (p. 108, folio not stated). **No death date, no
    dates of any kind, no silsila, no lineage and no ʿurs**: **(not stated)**.
- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **The cave and the stone.** "The main attraction in Lahoot is the cave of the saint Shah Noorani,
    who is said to have lived in this valley. Inside the cave, one finds a curiously shaped stone that
    is allegedly the image of Ali's camel. Some authors claim that this image used to be worshipped as
    a śiva liṅga before the place became associated with the Sufi saint (Khatri 1973), and indeed, the
    shape of the stone would support this idea, as I could see during a visit in 2010." (p. 108, folio
    not stated) **The author's own site visit is dated 2010.**
  - **The site read as a converted Hindu place.** "Abro suggests that Lahoot was once an important
    place for Hindu worship (Abro 1991), which is consistent with the history of other Hindu sites in
    the area that were converted to Sufi centers over time (Boivin 2007)." (p. 108, folio not stated)
  - **Landscape parallel with Hinglaj.** "There are fascinating similarities between the two valleys.
    Both are small oases with natural springs (Urd. chaśmah) and caves in confined valleys." (p. 108,
    folio not stated)
  - **No tomb, no building date, no architect, no patron, no custodian and no revenue**: **(not
    stated)**.
- **Practices & Events**
  - **A new road, then a bombing, with a date and a toll.** "Interestingly, and similar to Hinglaj,
    Lahoot is currently experiencing a new wave of interest mainly due to a newly constructed road
    leading to the shrine thus making the journey through the mountainous area easier. However, a
    terrible bomb blast in November 2016, killing more than fifty people, has curbed the growing influx
    of visitors and has created a volatile situation for Balochistan's shrines." (p. 108, folio not
    stated) **No attacker is named**: **(not stated)**.
  - **Its place on a pilgrimage route, and the loss of the onward path.** "Lal Shahbaz is also the
    starting point for the pilgrimage to Lahoot la Makan" (p. 108, folio not stated). "This pilgrimage
    route was once connected to the Makran Coast and the abode of 'Nani Hinglaj'… Today, however, this
    path seems to have been widely forgotten." (p. 108, folio not stated) "A vestige of this now
    obsolete pilgrimage route can be found at the end of the Urdu book Lāhūṭ lā Makān by Nazamani,
    which provides a description of the way from Lahoot to Hinglaj (Nazamani 2009, 26)." (p. 108, folio
    not stated) In 2010 the author met a Punjabi pilgrim at Lahoot la Makan who had come from Sehwan
    Sharif and "confirmed that this side of the pilgrimage route is still frequented; however, he
    mentioned that the longer path to Hinglaj is no longer used due to the dangerous political
    situation in the area." (p. 147, folio not stated)
- **Legends & Miracles**
  - **Ali's camel in the cave**, given as allegation: "a curiously shaped stone that is allegedly the
    image of Ali's camel" (p. 108, folio not stated).

### Place-level attachments under ruling (a) of 21 September 2026

*Ruling (a): a bare toponym takes the id of **every** archive row in that place, joined on
`location_short`. Each bullet below converts a Pass 1 hedge that named the rows and withheld the
mapping pending the ruling; the hedge language has been deleted. **This material is place-level.
It is evidence that the book discusses that place at those pages, and it must never be read as the
book describing any one of the sites named on the bullet head.** Every id on a head is on one
physical line.*

- **Karachi** — **bhagnari-mandir**, **darya-lal-mandir-darya-lal-sankat-mochan-mandir**, **jhollay-lal-mandir**, **shree-ratneshwar-mahadev-temple-karachi**, **shri-laxmi-narayan-mandir-native-jetty-bridge**, **shri-panchmukhi-hanuman-mandir-karachi**, **shri-swaminarayan-mandir-karachi**, **shri-varun-dev-mandir**, **shrine-of-abdullah-shah-ghazi**, **shrine-of-pir-mangho**, **shrine-of-qalandar-baba-auliya** (11 rows, the ruled count)
  - Karachi is the busiest toponym in the book. It is a city of violence against Hindus (p. 18,
    folio not stated), the seat of a housing authority (p. 16, folio not stated), a magazine's base
    (p. 21, folio not stated), a march route (p. 23, folio not stated), a spelling example (p. 10,
    folio not stated), the port of departure and the interview city of the 4x4 Off-Road Club (pp. 100,
    101, 113, 115, folios not stated), the seat of the Nagnath akhāṛā and the Jagnath akhāṛā (pp. 127,
    138, 159, 207, 292–294, folios not stated), the HSM's own seat (p. 173, folio not stated), the
    staging city and the flight destination from Bombay for Indian pilgrims (pp. 175, 180, 181, 183,
    folios not stated), the home of the Devipujak-Vagri community and the engine of change on the
    pilgrimage route (pp. 306–312, 322, folios not stated), a source of ʿīd and muḥarram pilgrims and
    a place of public slaughter (pp. 291, 294, 304, folios not stated), the home of "one elderly HSM
    volunteer" (p. 238, folio not stated), the origin of the Melari Mata donors (p. 271, folio not
    stated) and the city to which the drowned child was taken (p. 253, folio not stated). It is the
    start of the road route to Hinglaj, "about 240 kilometers long" from Narayanpur (p. 158, folio not
    stated), and the reason the early route stations vanished: "The ongoing growth of Karachi and the
    corresponding urbanization of the areas where the early stops occurred caused all but a few of the
    resting places to completely disappear." (p. 121, folio not stated) The index headwords "Empress
    Market, Karachi, 292" and "Aram Bagh, 112" (pp. 344, 340, folios not stated) are locators only.
  - **Karachi sites the book names that have no archive row of their own** — the Nagnath akhāṛā, the
    Jagnath akhāṛā (Bhimpura), the Ram Bagh / Aram Bagh in Saddar town, the "local Ram Pir temple" in
    Narayanpur, and the unnamed Kali temple where the Sanjesh interview took place — **are not folded
    into any of the eleven ids above.** See Doubts.
- **Lahore** — **abul-faiz-qalander-ali-suharwardi**, **bibi-pak-daman**, **darbar-abul-muali-qadri**, **data-darbar**, **ganj-e-inayat-sarkar**, **ghazi-ilm-din-shaheed**, **gurdwara-babay-nanki**, **gurdwara-baoli-sahib-guru-arjan-dev-ji-lahore**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-jhalian-jhalian-dhilwan**, **gurdwara-chhevin-patshahi-mozang**, **gurdwara-chowmala-sahib**, **gurdwara-dera-sahib**, **gurdwara-guru-ram-das-ji**, **gurdwara-patshahi-chhevin-hadiara-lahore**, **gurdwara-shaheed-bhai-taru-singh**, **gurdwara-shaheed-ganj-singh-singhnian**, **jain-mandir-lahore**, **khawaja-feroz-ud-din-gharib-nawaz**, **krishna-mandir-ravi-road**, **loh-temple-lava-temple**, **madho-lal-hussain**, **mazar-e-iqbal**, **peer-makki**, **samadhi-of-maharaja-ranjit-singh**, **shah-jamal**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-chiragh**, **shrine-of-baba-shah-kamal**, **shrine-of-mauj-darya-bukhari**, **shrine-of-mian-mir**, **shrine-of-miran-hussain-zanjani-zanjani-sahib**, **shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri**, **tahir-bandagi-qadri**, **tomb-of-qutbuddin-aibak**, **valmik-mandir-naqi-road**, **wasif-ali-wasif** (35 rows, the ruled count)
  - Lahore is named three times and thinly: as a Punjabi city in the survey of Pakistani Hinduism
    (p. 28, folio not stated); as the home of a Nath-sect informant (p. 138, folio not stated); and as
    the index headword `Lahore, 129` (p. 351, folio not stated). **The book attaches no shrine, tomb,
    saint, ʿurs or custodian to Lahore anywhere.** The attachment is place-level and carries nothing
    about any of the thirty-five sites.
  - **`darbar-malik-ahmad-ayaz` is deliberately NOT on this head.** Its `location_short` does not
    contain the string "Lahore" — the survey text records "Shah Alam Market" and says "No city,
    district, tehsil or province is stated anywhere in the survey for the shrine itself." A join on
    `location_short` therefore does not reach it, and the ruled count of 35 is reached without it.
    Recorded so the omission is visible and a human can rule. See Doubts.
- **Peshawar** — **dargah-pir-ratan-nath-jee**, **gorakhnath-goraknath-temple**, **gurdwara-bhai-beba-singh**, **gurdwara-bhai-joga-singh**, **kali-bari-mandir**, **panj-tirath**, **rahman-baba-mausoleum-rehman-baba-shrine**, **shrine-of-akhund-darweza-baba**, **shrine-of-mian-umar-baba-chamkani**, **wadpagga-sharif** (10 rows, the ruled count)
  - Peshawar is named once, and only as the home of robbers inside Abadhut's novel (pp. 128–129,
    folios not stated). **It is not offered as a site, and nothing whatever about any of these ten
    rows is carried.** The attachment is mechanical under ruling (a) and is recorded as such.
- **Multan** — **prahladpuri-temple**, **shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya**, **shrine-of-hafiz-muhammad-jamal-multani**, **shrine-of-hazrat-shah-ali-akbar-shah-ali-akbar-shamsi**, **shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam**, **shrine-of-shah-shams-ud-din-sabzwari**, **shrine-of-shah-yusaf-gardez**, **shrine-of-syed-musa-pak** (8 rows, the ruled count)
  - Multan is named twice: once in the survey of where Pakistani Hindu communities live (p. 80, folio
    not stated), and once as a nineteenth-century distance reference inside a quotation from Charles
    King 1865, "the desert between Cutch and Multan" (p. 258, folio not stated). **No shrine is
    attached in either place.**
- **Islamabad** — **bari-imam**, **golra-sharif**, **gurdwara-sahib-saidpur-guru-nanak-dev-ji**, **ram-mandir-saidpur-ram-kund-mandir** (4 rows, the ruled count)
  - Islamabad is named once: "an official of the Islamabad-based Jinnah Institute branded Hinglaj in
    2012 as a 'high risk for catastrophe' due to a possibility of extremist attacks" (p. 50, folio not
    stated). **The Pass 1 notes did not flag Islamabad as a toponym case; it is added here because the
    toponym is present at a cited page and ruling (a) is a standing ruling.** Flagged under Doubts so
    a human can strike it if the reading is thought too thin.
- **Sukkur** — **gurdwara-dash-mesh-pita**, **gurdwara-pehli-patshahi-jind-pir-sukkur**, **kalka-cave-temple-asthan-of-kalka-devi**, **sadh-belo-sadh-belo-island-temple**, **sant-baba-asudaram-darbar-panno-aqil** (5 rows)
  - Sukkur is named as the city of the Sadh Bela island tīrtha, "in the city of Sukkur on an island in
    the River Indus" (p. 138, folio not stated), and as the index headword `Sukkur, 129` (p. 361,
    folio not stated). **Only `sadh-belo-sadh-belo-island-temple` carries site-specific material; the
    other four rows take the id at place level only.**
- **Rohri** — **gurdwara-pehli-patshahi-jind-pir-sukkur**, **sadh-belo-sadh-belo-island-temple** (2 rows)
  - Rohri is named once, in the survey of Hindu settlement (p. 94, folio not stated), with no shrine
    attached.
- **Shikarpur** — **gurdwara-sach-khand-sahib**, **khatwari-darbar-shikarpur**, **sant-bhagat-kanwar-ram-temple-chak** (3 rows)
  - Shikarpur is named for its merchants and moneylenders (p. 58, folio not stated), for merchant
    demographics (p. 98, folio not stated), in the survey of Hindu settlement (p. 80, folio not
    stated), and as the index headword `Shikarpur, 48` (p. 360, folio not stated). **No shrine is
    attached in any of them.**
- **Tharparkar** — **churrio-jabal-durga-mata-temple**, **gori-temple-gori-jo-mandar**, **nagarparkar-jain-temples-nagarparkar-cultural-landscape** (3 rows, as the Pass 1 hedge named them)
  - Tharparkar is named once — "Another incident followed in Tharparkar two days later" (p. 17, folio
    not stated) — and once as the index headword `Tharparkar, 5` (p. 362, folio not stated). **No
    shrine, tomb, saint, ʿurs or custodian is attached.** **`nagarparkar-jain-temples-nagarparkar-
    cultural-landscape` has "Nagarparkar, Sindh, Pakistan" in its `location_short` and not the string
    "Tharparkar"; it is carried here because the Pass 1 hedge named it as a Tharparkar-district row.
    See Doubts.**
- **Umerkot** — **shrine-of-makhdoom-abdul-rahim-girhori**, **umarkot-amarkot-shiv-mandir** (2 rows)
  - Named as "a village near Umerkot in Sindh", the home of a walking pilgrim group that covered
    "around 600 kilometers" on foot to Hinglaj (p. 152, folio not stated), and as the index headword
    `Umerkot, 140` (p. 362, folio not stated). **The book spells it `Umerkot`, the archive `Umarkot`
    in one row and `Umerkot District` in the other.**
- **Quetta** — **gurdwara-singh-sabha**, **gurdwara-sri-tilganji-sahib** (2 rows)
  - Named in the survey of Pakistani Hindu settlement (pp. 16, 83, 85, 202, folios not stated) and as
    the index headword `Quetta, 5, 78` (p. 357, folio not stated). **No shrine attached.**
- **Hyderabad** — **guru-gurpat-mandir-db-80-sirey-ghat** (1 row)
  - The book reports an attack on "a temple in Hyderabad" on 28 March 2014 **without naming the
    temple** (p. 17, folio not stated); names Hyderabad as a source of `mīlād-al-nabī` and `cāśūrah`
    [text-layer?] [ʿāśūrah] visitors to Hinglaj (p. 272, folio not stated) and of `cīd`[text-layer?]
    [ʿīd] and muḥarram pilgrims (p. 291, folio not stated); and indexes "Hyderabad, 5, 80, 141"
    (p. 348, folio not stated). **The unnamed attacked temple is not identified with this row — see
    Doubts.**
- **Larkana** — **swami-dharmdas-darbar-larkana-kennedy-market** (1 row)
  - The book reports an attack in March 2014 on "a Hindu temple in Larkana, a city in the north of
    Sindh", **with no name, no locality within the city and no denomination** (p. 17, folio not
    stated); index `Larkana, 5, 6` (p. 351, folio not stated). **The unnamed temple is not identified
    with this row — see Doubts.**
- **Hala** — **shrine-of-makhdoom-nooh-hala** (1 row)
  - Named once, inside a quoted account of the Goddess's origins: "Hala in Sindh was called Kohala in
    the former times, and the Goddess Hinglaj was once the queen of the Kohala or Kohana area."
    (p. 71, folio not stated) **Nothing in the book connects that shrine to Hinglaj and no such
    connection should be inferred.**
- **Sehwan Sharif / Sehwan** — **lal-shahbaz-qalandar**, **shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar** (2 rows)
  - Sehwan is the dargāh town of Lal Shahbaz Qalandar (p. 107, folio not stated), the destination Raj
    Kumar abandoned for Hinglaj (pp. 160–161, folios not stated), a landscape reference in "the
    mountains of Sehwan" (p. 113, folio not stated), the starting point of a 2010 pilgrim met at
    Lahoot la Makan (p. 147, folio not stated), and the index headword `Sehwan, 97, 104, 148–149`
    (p. 359, folio not stated). **`shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar` takes the id at place level only: its
    `location_short` reads "Laki (Lakhi), near Sehwan Sharif", i.e. near the town rather than in it,
    and the book never names Lakhi Shah Saddar. See Doubts.**
- **Lahoot / Lahoot la Makan / Khuzdar** — **shah-noorani-shrine-syed-bilawal-shah-noorani** (1 row)
  - Besides the site-specific material in the subsection above, the toponym also appears as a stop on
    Shah Abdul Latif's wandering, "Lahoot (Khuzdar)" (p. 95, folio not stated); inside Abro's book
    title `Hinglāj ai Lāhūt` (pp. 94, 241, folios not stated); inside Nazamani's title `Lāhūṭ lā
    Makān` (p. 108, folio not stated); and as the index headwords `Lahoot, 89, 98`, `Khuzdar, 98` and
