    `Lāhūt lā Makān (Nazamani), 98` (pp. 350, 351, folios not stated). **The book's `Lāhūt` /
    `Lahoot la Makan` against the archive's `Lahoot Lamakan`.**
- **Las Bela / Lasbela District and Hingol National Park** — **chandragup-baba-chandragup**, **shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir** (2 rows)
  - "the Tumbel spread the Goddess's fame in Las Bela" (p. 71, folio not stated); "a district close to
    Hinglaj" (p. 79, folio not stated); the HSM "has since its inception consisted mainly of Lohanas
    from the district of Las Bela" (p. 82, folio not stated); "the Dewanis, a Sindhi Lohana family
    from Las Bela" (p. 274, folio not stated); the HSM was "created on January 5, 1986, in Bela (Las
    Bela district), Balochistan" (p. 82, folio not stated); "the entry to the Hingol National Park"
    (p. 152, folio not stated); "the Hingol National Park, in which the shrine is located" (p. 295,
    folio not stated). **Both rows carry site-specific material in their own subsections above; the
    place-level line is recorded for completeness.**
- **Chandrakup as a bare place-name** — **chandragup-baba-chandragup** (1 row)
  - Where the book names Chandrakup with no rite attached — "the area after Chandrakup" on Goldsmid's
    route (p. 114, folio not stated), the police checkpoint "when approaching Chandrakup" (p. 291,
    folio not stated), and the index headword (p. 342, folio not stated) — the id is taken under
    ruling (a) as well as on the site-specific ground set out in that row's subsection.

*Province- and region-level names are **not** joined here. `Sindh`, `Balochistan`, `Punjab`, `the
Makran`, `Las Bela` as a region rather than a district, `Kutch`, `Gujarat`, `Rajasthan` and
`Saurashtra` occur throughout the book and each appears in dozens of rows' `location_short` strings;
the Pass 1 notes refuse them as "province-level and too broad for any row", and the ruled toponym
counts supplied to this run are city-level (lahore 35, karachi 11, peshawar 10, multan 8, islamabad
4). **Recorded under Doubts as a point for Rauf rather than decided here.** Toponyms with no archive
row at all — Thatta, Makli, Liari, Sonmiani, Ormara, Omara, Pasni, Gwadar, Bela town, Uthal,
Narayanpur, Vindar, the Hab River, the Hingol River, Astola Island, Koteshwara, Akash Ganga, and the
Indian and Iranian place-names — take no id under any reading and belong to the "Figures and sites
not in the archive" section, which this part does not own.*

### Person-level attachments under the many-row rule of 22 September 2026 and the single-row rule of 18 September 2026

*A person who is the principal figure of many rows takes all of them; a person who is a single row's
principal figure takes that row's id even where the book names them only as an author, a literary
reference or a figure inside a myth. Each bullet below converts a Pass 1 hedge that named the rows
and withheld the mapping; the hedge language, including the repeated "escalated to Rauf" wording
around the deity case, has been deleted. **The material is person-level and says nothing about the
sites named on the bullet head.** Every id on a head is on one physical line.*

- **Guru Nanak** — **gurdwara-babay-de-ber**, **gurdwara-balila-sahib-bal-lila-sahib**, **gurdwara-chakki-sahib**, **gurdwara-choa-sahib**, **gurdwara-darbar-sahib-kartarpur**, **gurdwara-khoohi-bhai-lalo-bhai-lalo-di-khooi**, **gurdwara-malji-sahib**, **gurdwara-panja-sahib**, **gurdwara-patti-sahib**, **gurdwara-pehli-patshahi-jind-pir-sukkur**, **gurdwara-rori-sahib**, **gurdwara-sach-khand-sahib**, **gurdwara-sacha-sauda**, **gurdwara-sahib-saidpur-guru-nanak-dev-ji**, **gurdwara-sri-kiara-sahib**, **gurdwara-sri-tilganji-sahib**, **gurdwara-tambo-sahib**, **gurudwara-janam-asthan-nankana-sahib** (18 rows, the ruled count)
  - **Footprints of Guru Nanak are shown in the Hinglaj valley.** "the engraved footsteps of Guru
    Nanak" (p. 256, folio not stated); "The hollowed-out footsteps of Guru Nanak connect Hinglaj to
    the Sikh and Nanak paṃth (Hin. sect) traditions" (p. 257, folio not stated), with Lakhavat's map
    argument at the same page and note 32's list (p. 265, folio not stated).
  - "Many Lohanas in Sindh follow the teachings of Nanak" (p. 80, folio not stated); "the practices of
    the Nanak paṃth" and "Many upper-caste Hindus in Sindh, especially from the Lohana community,
    refer to Nanak and his teachings (Thakur 1997, 141)" (p. 286, folio not stated). The book also
    names "the tikānā (Sin.), the temple containing the Guru Granth Sahib" (p. 286, folio not stated),
    **which corresponds to the archive's whole "Nanakpanthi / Udasi Darbar" category rather than to
    any one row** — category-level material, recorded here and flagged in Doubts.
  - Index: `Nanak, 73` and, separately, `Nanak, Guru, 242, 243`, plus `Nanak paṃth, 270` (p. 354,
    folio not stated). **Two headwords with disjoint runs and no cross-reference; they must not be
    concatenated. See Doubts.**
- **Shiva (Mahadev)** — **amb-temples-amb-sharif**, **bhagnari-mandir**, **chandragup-baba-chandragup**, **katas-raj-temples**, **shahwala-teja-singh-mandir**, **shiv-mandir-chiti-ghati**, **shree-ratneshwar-mahadev-temple-karachi**, **umarkot-amarkot-shiv-mandir** (8 rows, the ruled count)
  - Shiva runs through the whole book. In the Sati myth he marries Sati against Daksha's will, kills
    Daksha and dances the tāṇḍava (p. 55, folio not stated). As `Bhīmalocana` he is the form of Shiva
    associated with Hinglaj in the Pīṭhanirṇaya (p. 55, folio not stated). He is the deity pilgrims
    say resides in Chandrakup — "the volcano is the seat of Shiva, who must be visited and appeased
    with offerings so as to gain permission to meet his wife in her private abode"; "Chandrakup, a mud
    volcano that is worshipped as an embodiment of Shiva" (pp. 203, 215, folios not stated); the
    bubbles are "manifestations of the god in the form of a liṅga" (Motyani 2011, 281) (p. 215, folio
    not stated); the slope "has the form of Shiva's cilam" (Kshatriya 2005, 88) (p. 264, folio not
    stated). He tames the Ganges with his hair and meditates in the Hinglaj mountains, so that Ganges
    water reaches Anil Kund (p. 255, folio not stated). He is the former occupant of Til Kund (p. 254,
    folio not stated). A śiva liṅga stood before the Hinglaj platform until the HSM removed it in the
    1990s (p. 238, folio not stated), and the same stone is Hart's "Sadasheo" and Abro's "sadā śivo"
    (pp. 238, 241, folios not stated). He and Parvati travel to Hinglaj via the Makli hills in the
    volcano legends (pp. 206, 214, folios not stated). Devotees say the second face at Hinglaj "is
    Shiva's or Rama's" (p. 230, folio not stated). Gāṁjā and a cilam are smoked "to appease the God
    Shiva" (p. 139, folio not stated); "stories of Rama or Shiva are recalled" at Chandrakup
    (pp. 146–147, folios not stated); a walking stick is "shaped like Shiva's trident, the triśūla"
    (p. 160, folio not stated). Bhairava is glossed by a pilgrim as Shiva — "There is a Bhairava
    [Shiva] temple in my village" (p. 174, folio not stated). Index: `Shiva, 43–45, 65–66, 97,
    105–106, 130, 147–148, 192–193, 201–202, 241` (p. 360, folio not stated) and "branding iron of
    Shiva and Shakti, 102f" (p. 341, folio not stated). **`chandragup-baba-chandragup` is on this head
    as well as having its own subsection; that subsection rests on the named site and its rites, never
    on the word "Shiva".**
- **Guru Gorakhnath** — **gorakhnath-goraknath-temple**, **tilla-jogian** (2 rows, the ruled count)
  - **Gorakhnath is a named actor in the Hinglaj material.** "Tradition has it that Hinglaj was very
    fond of the Nath community, especially of Guru Gorakhnath. So the Goddess waited day after day in
    Thatta for Gorakhnath to come to meet her" (p. 205, folio not stated), and in the next chunk "all
    the food that Hinglaj had prepared in anticipation of his arrival" (p. 206, folio not stated).
    The limestone boon, attributed to Lakhavat: the piled khic'ṛī turned to limestone, and "anyone who
    returned from Hinglaj wearing a necklace of these limestone pebbles would be freed from the
    fetters of this world (Lakhavat 2011, 35)" (p. 206, folio not stated). In the valley: "the dhūnī
    of Guru Gorakhnath" (p. 256, folio not stated); "The fireplace of Goraknath dates back to the
    long-standing tradition of Nath Yogis who made the journey to Hinglaj (Briggs 1938)" (pp. 256–257,
    folio not stated); "two texts describe the pilgrimage of Guru Gorakhnath to Hinglaj: the Gorakh
    Caritra and the Gorakhpurāṇa (Lakhavat 2011, 80)" (p. 257, folio not stated); "Another story says
    that Guru Gorakhnath himself took the stone from somewhere around Anil Kund and brought it to this
    place (Jethamalani 1994, 8)." (p. 259, folio not stated); and note 32's "Guru Goraknath" (p. 265,
    folio not stated). Bibliography: "Briggs, George W. 1938. `Gorakhnāth` and the `Kānphata`
    [text-layer?] [reading not supplied] Yogis." (p. 328, folio not stated). Index: "Gorakhnath, Guru,
    192, 242", "Gorakhnath sect, 105–106", "Gorakh Caritra, 242–243", "Gorakhpurāṇa, 242–243" (p. 346,
    folio not stated). **The book spells him both `Gorakhnath` and `Goraknath`, as the archive row's
    own title does.** **The group-level Nath material — the "Nath saṃpradāy", the "sādhus of the Nath
    community", the "Nath Siddhas", the Kanphata community, the Nagnath and Jagnath akhāṛās, and Pir
    Ratan Nath — attaches to no row and is not folded in here; see Doubts.**
- **Valmiki (Bhagwan Valmik)** — **lal-kurti-temple-balmiki-mandir-rawalpindi**, **valmik-mandir-naqi-road**, **valmiki-swamiji-mandir-gracy-lines-rawalpindi** (3 rows)
  - Named as the author of the canonical Rāmāyaṇa: the `Yātrā Kānḍ` is "an addition to the canonized
    corpus of Valmiki's Rāmāyaṇa (Jethamalani 1994, 37)" (p. 120, folio not stated); "Valmiki's
    Sanskrit version of the Rāmāyaṇa" (p. 120, folio not stated); the bibliography entry "Goldman,
    Robert P., and Sally J. Sutherland. 1984. The Rāmāyaṇa of Vālmīki." (p. 330, folio not stated);
    index `Valmiki, 110` (p. 363, folio not stated). **The book nowhere identifies that Valmiki with
    the Valmiki venerated at those three shrines; the equation is not the book's. See Doubts.**
- **Kali** — **kalat-kali-temple**, **kali-bari-mandir**, **kalka-cave-temple-asthan-of-kalka-devi** (3 rows)
  - Kali is named throughout as the guard of Hinglaj and the recipient of the sacrifice the HSM
    banned: "For the Vagris, Kali is the guard (Hin. `caukīdār`) of the Goddess Hinglaj, and thus they
    must appease Kali before making their way on to Hinglaj." (p. 311, folio not stated) "some
    pilgrims offer black goats and others offer coconuts" at the Kali Mata asthān (p. 203, folio not
    stated). A rooster sacrifice was witnessed at the Kali Mata temple in 2010 "and also in later
    years" (p. 294, folio not stated); HSM members "secretly continued sacrificing goats in the
    mountains for the Goddess Kali" after the ban (p. 296, folio not stated). On Astola Island "the
    place is dedicated to the Goddess Kali, and goats are sacrificed at a particular spot on the
    island" with "a few ruins of a Kali temple on the island" (p. 260, folio not stated). The Karachi
    Kali Mata pujārī kept spirits: "He kept spirits [bhūt pā'lnā] for a long time. If he let one
    loose, then it might also catch you. He captured many." (p. 304, folio not stated), and replied
    "No, no, they won't catch you! People must be very frightened." (p. 304, folio not stated). Pond
    names: Kali Kund (pp. 254–255, folio not stated). Index: `Kali, 189, 245–246, 290, 297` (p. 350,
    folio not stated); `Kali Mata temple, 117, 209–210, 210f, 269, 278, 281` (p. 350, folio not
    stated). **None of the three archive rows is the Kali temple inside the Hinglaj valley, the Kali
    temple on Astola or the unnamed Karachi Kali temple; the attachment is person-level only and those
    three sites are new-row candidates. See Doubts.**
- **Krishna** — **krishna-mandir-kabari-bazar**, **krishna-mandir-ravi-road** (2 rows, the ruled count)
  - Named once in a list of deities Pakistani Hindu communities worship, "Shiva, Krishna, or Vishnu"
    (p. 107, folio not stated), and once in the index, `Krishna, 97` (p. 350, folio not stated). **No
    temple, rite or place is attached to him anywhere in the book**: **(not stated)**.
- **Rama** — **ram-mandir-saidpur-ram-kund-mandir** (1 row)
  - Rama dominates the book's account of the pilgrimage route, and the whole of that material is set
    out under `shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir` above: his brahm hatyā and the sentence of
    pilgrimage (p. 120, folio not stated); his defeat by the Goddess's army at the Ungakhera Bherum
    Luk pass and his twelve-year battle (pp. 120–121, folios not stated); the condition that he come
    as a simple pilgrim from Ram Bagh (p. 121, folio not stated); the Devi's promise to his excluded
    men (p. 121, folio not stated); the water miracles of Hanuman, Lakshmana and Sita (p. 121, folio
    not stated); the sun and moon he carves on the mountain opposite the temple (pp. 121, 132, 142,
    257, folios not stated); his ninety-six Brahmins accepting Hinglaj as their clan Goddess (p. 76,
    folio not stated); the Saraswat Brahmins' ṭīkā, which "according to legend—they had gotten from
    Ramacandra. Rama was in need of a priest and since he could not find any in Balochistan he simply
    applied a ṭīkā on a mlecch's (Hin. barbarian) forehead." (Burton 1851, 312) (p. 100, folio not
    stated); Lalu Jasraj as "the aguā of Ramacandra's party and the first Kapri" (p. 181, folio not
    stated); the pūjā places named for him on the route, "the resting place of Rama (Sin. rām
    jharoku)" (p. 256, folio not stated), "Khari Nadi, where people take a bath in the ocean and
    worship Rama" and "the Rama pūjā" (pp. 203, 204, folios not stated); and "the mytho-historic
    journey of Rama and his entourage" now largely destroyed by Karachi's growth (p. 322, folio not
    stated). **"no figure was ever as present in the representations of the traditional pilgrimage to
    Hinglaj as Rama, the seventh incarnation of the God Vishnu."** (p. 119, folio not stated) Index:
    `Rama, 60n28, 67, 69–70, 93, 108–113, 137, 186, 191, 242` (p. 357, folio not stated). **The book
    never connects Rama to Saidpur, Islamabad, Ram Kund or any temple of his; the attachment is
    person-level only.**
- **Durga (Mata)** — **churrio-jabal-durga-mata-temple** (1 row)
  - Durga is the goddess whose vīr appears in the Hinglaj effigy (pp. 46, 229, folios not stated); the
    idol seen at Hinglaj in the BVP's promise (p. 54, folio not stated); the face at Hinglaj in one of
    the three television answers (p. 48, folio not stated); Stein's "apparently worshipped as a
    manifestation of Durga" (p. 235, folio not stated); the Sanskrit `śrīdurgāpratimāṃ` of the BVP
    verse (p. 98, folio not stated); and a possible relation of Nani, "may be related to Nana or
    Durga" (p. 101, folio not stated). Index: "Durga, 35–37, 43–44, 90n55, 126, 216, 222" (p. 344,
    folio not stated). **Always at the Balochistan site or in a text, never at Churrio Hill,
    Nagarparkar, which the book never mentions.**
- **Hanuman (Panchmukhi)** — **shri-panchmukhi-hanuman-mandir-karachi** (1 row)
  - Named as "Rama's close ally" and as the maker of a dry riverbed on the Hinglaj route: "The Bahur
    River, for example, is said to have been created by Hanuman's attempt to get water for Sita."
    (pp. 119, 121, 122, folios not stated); and as the contested dedicatee of a paṛāv in the Hinglaj
    valley where pilgrims "even put a small Hanuman statue there!" (pp. 279–280, folio not stated).
    Index: "Hanuman, 69, 70, 110–112, 208, 263–264" (p. 346, folio not stated). **A different site in
    a different city from the archive row; the attachment is person-level only.**
- **Narasimha** — **prahladpuri-temple** (1 row)
  - Named twice, both times as a Purāṇic myth and not a temple: "the famous myth of Narasimha,
    canonized in the Bhāgavata Purāṇa" (p. 229, folio not stated) and "This story, which is clearly a
    reference to the legend of Narasimha in the Bhāgavata Purāṇa" (p. 230, folio not stated). Index:
    `Narasimha, 216–217` (p. 354, folio not stated). **The index also has a distinct person headword
    `Jasol, Narasimha, 116` (p. 349, folio not stated), which is not the same entry and must never be
    merged.**
- **Lava (Luv)** — **loh-temple-lava-temple** (1 row)
  - Named once, inside a Lohana caste-origin etymology: "Rama's son, called Lava, from whom the name
    Lohana ultimately developed" (p. 80, folio not stated), attributed to Thakur and to the author's
    own Sindhi interlocutors. Index: `Lava, 72` (p. 351, folio not stated). **No temple, tomb, image
    or place is attached to him**: **(not stated)**.
- **Varuna (Varun Dev)** — **shri-varun-dev-mandir** (1 row)
  - Named once, in the Lohana iron-fort legend attributed to Thakur: "Varuna built the Rathors an iron
    fort that saved them from the Raja's attack. Even though the fort disappeared twenty-one days
    after the attack, the Rathors were from then onward called Lohana, which the community translates
    as 'those of iron' (Thakur 1997, 56)." (p. 80, folio not stated) Index: `Varuna, 72` (p. 363,
    folio not stated). **No temple, rite or place is attached**: **(not stated)**.

*Two person-names in this book are **not** converted, because each names two different people rather
than one person heading two rows, so neither ruling reaches them: `Noorani` and `Shah Inayatullah`.
Both are set out under Doubts. Figures with no archive row at all — Sati, Vishnu, Brahma, Parvati,
Ganesha, Nani / Bibi Nani, Loca-mátá, Ashapura, Melari (Meladi) Mata, Momai Mata, Karni Mata, Avar
Mata, Khodiyar, Parashurama, Dadhichi, Jayasena, Mekan Dada, Lalu Jasraj, Hazrat Ali and Mohammad Ali
Jinnah — take no id and belong to the "Figures and sites not in the archive" section, which this part
does not own.*

### Further mappings identified in the cross-cutting material, not merged into the subsections above

*Recorded by the workers who held the non-archive extracts. Each id below was checked against `shrine_index.tsv`; the material behind it sits in the section the worker names. Left as a list rather than folded in, so that nothing is asserted twice and a human can see which mappings arrived late.*

*Everything below was parked in the Pass 1 "Other saints, sites and events" material as taking no id, in most cases
under the interim reading of convention (a) that was superseded by Rauf's ruling of 21 September 2026, or under an
interim deity convention superseded by the many-row-person ruling of 22 September 2026. Under the rulings now in force
each of these belongs in `## Shrines in the archive this book informs`, which another worker owns. **I have not mapped
any of them; they are listed here with the id and the page references for the coordinator.** Ids are given exactly as
`shrine_index.tsv` spells them. Where a name answers to more than one row, every candidate is named and none is
chosen.*

### 1. A site the Pass 1 notes repeatedly parked as a bare toponym, which is in fact an archive row

- **Chandrakup / the Chandrakup volcano → `chandragup-baba-chandragup`.** `shrine_index.tsv` row:
  "Chandragup (Baba Chandragup)", Hindu Temple, "Hingol National Park, Lasbela District, Balochistan, Pakistan",
  principal figure "Shiva (associated)". Schaflechner's Chandrakup is the mud volcano in the Hingol on the Hinglaj
  route, and the book gives it more ritual detail than almost any other stop: the roṭ cake and the offerings of
  coconuts, gāṁjā, cilams, betel nut, coins and dal (pp. 141, 212–213, folios not stated); the overnight jāg'raṇ, the
  fast and the confession of sins at the rim (p. 213, folio not stated); the bubble augury once read by the chaṛīdār
  and now by an HSM member or any travelling ritual expert (p. 212, folio not stated); Hart's nineteenth-century
  parallel (p. 212, folio not stated); the local economy of lifts, resold coconuts, tea and begging (pp. 211, 214,
  folios not stated); the police checkpoint on the approach (p. 291, folio not stated); the first-day position in the
  three-day festival (p. 281, folio not stated); its place in the 2010 itinerary at film timecode 23:00 (pp. 180–181,
  folios not stated); the refused White and Philostratus identifications (p. 212, folio not stated); the inconsistency
  of practice there (p. 212, folio not stated); Goldsmid's elderly pilgrim encountered "in the area after Chandrakup"
  (p. 114, folio not stated); and its position in Jethamalani's three-paṛāv list as asthān 1b (pp. 202–203, folios not
  stated). **The spelling differs — `Chandrakup` in the book, `Chandragup` in the archive — which is presumably why
  the Pass 1 worker did not join it.**

### 2. Place-level attachments under ruling (a) — the toponym takes every archive row in that place

*The join is on `location_short` and is mechanical via `toponym_map.txt`; the ruled counts named in the brief are
given where they exist, and the ids are listed where the set is small enough to be read off `shrine_index.tsv`
without risk.*

- **Karachi → the 11 Karachi rows (ruled count).** Karachi is the book's second city and appears throughout: the Nagnath
  and Jagnath akhāṛās in Bhimpura (pp. 127, 138, 142, 159, 206–207, 307, folios not stated); Narayanpur Colony and its
  Ram Pir and Santoshi Mata temples (pp. 157, 163, folios not stated); the Devipujak-Vagri `maṭh` (p. 307, folio not
  stated); the Hindu graveyard at Purana Golimar (p. 273, folio not stated); Jinnah's mazār and the 14 August marches
  (pp. 23, 273, folios not stated); Ram Bagh / Aram Bagh in Saddar town (p. 122, folio not stated); the Swaminarayan
  temple as the 2010 group's first stop (p. 180, folio not stated); an unnamed temple where an interview was recorded
  (p. 304, folio not stated); the Empress Market, Sunday Bazaar and Defence Housing Authority (pp. 16, 307, folios not
  stated); Defence Colony (p. 273, folio not stated); the city as the pilgrimage's assembly point and as the agent of
  the route's change (pp. 83–85, 121, 322, folios not stated); Dar Digest's base (p. 21, folio not stated); the 1992
  riots (p. 307, folio not stated); and the index run `Karachi, 12, 77–78, 83, 103, 107, 112, 117–133, 147, 168, 292,
  305` (p. 350, folio not stated).
- **Lahore → the 35 Lahore rows (ruled count).** Two mentions only, both thin: "Urdu class V, Punjab Textbooks Board,
  Lahore 2002" as a place of publication (p. 28, folio not stated), and "In 1940, on the way back from Kashmir to
  central India, the self-proclaimed wanderer Shastri meets a member of the Nath sect in Lahore who instructs him at
  length about the mysteries and the difficulties of the pilgrimage to Hinglaj." (p. 138, folio not stated) Index:
  `Lahore, 129` (p. 351, folio not stated). **The second mention is a real datum about the Hinglaj pilgrimage's reach
  into Lahore and should not be dropped for being short; the first is a publisher's imprint and the coordinator may
  wish to say so on the bullet.**
- **Multan → the 8 Multan rows (ruled count).** Two mentions: "Lohanpur in Multan" (Burton 1851, 314) (p. 80, folio not
  stated), and "the desert between Cutch and Multan" inside the Ctesias discussion (p. 258, folio not stated).
- **Peshawar → the 10 Peshawar rows (ruled count).** One mention: Peshawar as the home of the robbers in Shastri's
  account (pp. 128–129, folios not stated).
- **Quetta → `gurdwara-singh-sabha`, `gurdwara-sri-tilganji-sahib`.** "the persecuted Hazaras of Quetta" (p. 16, folio
  not stated); Quetta as the N 25's northern terminus and the older detour route (pp. 83, 85, 202, 207–208, folios not
  stated); index `Quetta, 5, 78` (p. 357, folio not stated).
- **Sukkur → `gurdwara-dash-mesh-pita`, `gurdwara-pehli-patshahi-jind-pir-sukkur`, `kalka-cave-temple-asthan-of-kalka-devi`, `sadh-belo-sadh-belo-island-temple`, `sant-baba-asudaram-darbar-panno-aqil`.** Index headword only: `Sukkur, 129` (p. 361, folio not stated). **The evidence is a single index locator on that PDF page and nothing more; the coordinator should weigh whether that is enough.**
- **Shikarpur → `khatwari-darbar-shikarpur`, `gurdwara-sach-khand-sahib`, `sant-bhagat-kanwar-ram-temple-chak`.** "a merchant and banker community influential in the city of Shikarpur (Sindh) in the nineteenth century (Markovits 2000, 60)" (p. 58, folio not stated); the Khatris "made up only 5 percent of the merchant population of Shikarpur" (p. 98, folio not stated); the 1881 census of Lohanas "from Shikarpur and Hyderabad" (p. 80, folio not stated); index `Shikarpur, 48` (p. 360, folio not stated).
- **Hyderabad (Sindh) → `guru-gurpat-mandir-db-80-sirey-ghat`.** The temple stormed on 28 March 2014 and the author's visit two days later (p. 17, folio not stated); the 1881/1891 census of Lohanas (p. 80, folio not stated); visitors to the shrine at `baqra cīd`[text-layer?] [baqra ʿīd] "from urban areas like Karachi or Hyderabad" (p. 294, folio not stated); PHS activity (p. 272, folio not stated); Malkani's departure from Hyderabad after Partition (p. 87, folio not stated).
- **Larkana → `swami-dharmdas-darbar-larkana-kennedy-market`.** The March 2014 temple burning: "In March 2014, an attack on a Hindu temple in Larkana, a city in the north of Sindh, paralyzed the entire Hindu community of Pakistan." (p. 17, folio not stated) Index: `Larkana, 5, 6` (p. 351, folio not stated). **The burned temple is not named, so this is a place-level attachment only.**
- **Tharparkar / Nagarparkar → `churrio-jabal-durga-mata-temple`, `gori-temple-gori-jo-mandar`, and (for Nagarparkar) `nagarparkar-jain-temples-nagarparkar-cultural-landscape`.** "Another incident followed in Tharparkar two days later." (p. 17, folio not stated) Index: `Tharparkar, 5` (p. 362, folio not stated). **Only `churrio-jabal-durga-mata-temple` and `gori-temple-gori-jo-mandar` carry "Tharparkar" in `location_short`; the Nagarparkar row carries "Nagarparkar" but not "Tharparkar", so the coordinator should decide whether the join reaches it.**
- **Umerkot / Umarkot → `umarkot-amarkot-shiv-mandir`, `shrine-of-makhdoom-abdul-rahim-girhori`.** "a village near Umerkot in Sindh", the home of Shridevi's walking group (p. 152, folio not stated); index `Umerkot, 140` (p. 362, folio not stated). **The archive spells the place `Umerkot` in one row and `Umarkot` in the other; the join must cover both.**
- **Hingol National Park / Lasbela District → `chandragup-baba-chandragup` and `shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir`.** "only a few kilometers away from the entry to the Hingol National Park" (p. 152, folio not stated); index `Hingol National Park, 140, 279–280` (p. 348, folio not stated). **Both archive rows sit inside this toponym.**
- **Sehwan / Sehwan Sharif → `lal-shahbaz-qalandar`, `shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar`.** "In 2010, I met a Punjabi pilgrim in Lahoot la Makan who had just arrived from Sehwan Sharif" (p. 147, folio not stated); the old route "over the mountains of Sehwan" taken to avoid the Sonmiani tax (p. 113, folio not stated); Raj Kumar's earlier Karachi–Sehwan foot route (p. 160, folio not stated); Sehwan Sharif twice as a place of publication and once in a works-cited title (pp. 328, 333, 336, folios not stated); index `Sehwan, 97, 104, 148–149` (p. 359, folio not stated). **`shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar`'s `location_short` reads "Laki (Lakhi), near Sehwan Sharif, Jamshoro, Sindh, Pakistan", so the toponym join reaches it as well as `lal-shahbaz-qalandar`.** See also item 4 below for the Qalandar quotation.
- **Khuzdar → `shah-noorani-shrine-syed-bilawal-shah-noorani`.** "Lahoot (Khuzdar)" named as a stop on Latif's wandering (p. 95, folio not stated); index `Khuzdar, 98` (p. 350, folio not stated).
- **Kashmir — flagged, not asserted.** "on the way back from Kashmir to central India" (p. 138, folio not stated). The archive's only Kashmir row is `sharada-peeth`, whose `location_short` is "Sharda, Neelum Valley (Azad Kashmir), Pakistan". **The book says "Kashmir" without qualification and the join to "Azad Kashmir" is not mechanical; the coordinator should decide.**

### 3. Places outside the Hinglaj network that carry a shrine in the book and answer to an archive row

- **Lahoot / Lahoot la Makan / `Lāhūt Lā Makān` / `Hinglāj ai Lāhūt` → `shah-noorani-shrine-syed-bilawal-shah-noorani`.**
  `shrine_index.tsv` row: "Shah Noorani Shrine (Syed Bilawal Shah Noorani)", Muslim Shrine, "Lahoot Lamakan valley,
  Khuzdar District, Balochistan, Pakistan". The book's material: **"a newly constructed road leading to the shrine"**
  at Lahoot, given as the second case of the book's own road-and-change argument (p. 108, folio not stated); "In 2010, I
  met a Punjabi pilgrim in Lahoot la Makan who had just arrived from Sehwan Sharif" (p. 147, folio not stated); "Lahoot
  (Khuzdar)" as a stop on Latif's post-Hinglaj wandering (Agha 2004, 427) (p. 95, folio not stated); Abro's 1991 book
  title `Hinglāj ai Lāhūt` (p. 94, folio not stated) and its index entry "Hinglāj ai Lāhūt (Abro), 87, 227, 240"
  (p. 348, folio not stated); the works-cited entry "Nazamani, Mahmud. 2009. Lāhūt Lā Makān. Unknown: Private
  Publication." (p. 334, folio not stated) and its index entry `Lāhūt lā Makān (Nazamani), 98` (p. 351, folio not
  stated); index `Lahoot, 89, 98` (p. 351, folio not stated). **The road sentence at p. 108 is a real, usable fact about
  that shrine and the Pass 1 notes parked it as a bare toponym.**

### 4. A name that could answer to two rows, and is left unchosen

- **"Noorani", in Raj Kumar's quoted speech about the Karachi–Sehwan foot route: "Again we are climbing the mountains
  for Noorani." (p. 160, folio not stated)** — candidates: `shah-noorani-shrine-syed-bilawal-shah-noorani` (Lahoot
  Lamakan valley, Khuzdar District, Balochistan) and `shrine-of-pir-chhatal-shah-noorani` (near Naulung, Moola gorge,
  Gandava area, Jhal Magsi District, Balochistan). **The "mountains" and the Sehwan walking route fit the first, but the
  book says nothing that settles it; both are named and neither is chosen.** The same speech carries the Qalandar
  quotation: "So I thought to myself: 'Man, forget Qalandar!… The Mother's [Hinglaj] road is all straight.'" (p. 160,
  folio not stated) — **a pilgrim choosing Hinglaj over Sehwan, which is evidence for `lal-shahbaz-qalandar` under the
  single-row person rule as well as under the toponym ruling.**
- **"Shah Inayatullah (1655–1718)", named as a Sindhi poet in the same sentence as Shah Abdul Latif, as one of the two
  poets through whose work Khan Baloch came to Hinglaj (p. 240, folio not stated)** — candidates:
  `dargah-roza-sufi-shah-inayat-shaheed` (Sufi Shah Inayat Shaheed, Jhok Sharif, `figure_died` 1718) and
  `shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri` (Shah Inayat Qadiri, Mozang Chungi, Lahore, `figure_died` c. 1728). **The archive's
  death year 1718 matches the book's printed 1718 exactly for the first, but the book gives no shrine, no place and no
  order, so the match is not made here. Both are named.**

### 5. Persons who are an archive row's principal figure, named by Schaflechner in passing or as authors

*Ruled 18 September 2026 for single-row persons, and 22 September 2026 for persons heading many rows. Every name below
was checked against the `principal_figure` column of all 169 rows.*

- **Shah Abdul Latif (Bhitai), and the Risalo → `bhit-bhit-shah`** (principal figure "Shah Abdul Latif Bhittai",
  `figure_died` 1752). He is the third leg of the book's Sindhi-nationalist argument: "the poetry of Shah Abdul Latif
  presents Hinglaj as an important religious site for both Islam and Hinduism" (p. 96, folio not stated); his journey
  is the proof offered that "Muslims and Hindus have both been worshipping at the shrine for many centuries" (p. 90,
  folio not stated); "there is, for example, a cave immediately behind the Mata Bhawani temple in Thatta that is
  rumored to be the place where Latif miraculously reappeared after he was forced to flee from Hinglaj" (p. 108, folio
  not stated); his onward wandering is listed as "Dwarka (Dwarka district, Gujarat), Lakhpat (Kutch), Lahoot (Khuzdar),
  and Jaisalmer (Rajasthan) (Agha 2004, 427)" (p. 95, folio not stated); the Kapri material is sourced to "the section
  Sur Rāmkalī chapter 3, 4–8. (Agha 2004, 446)" (p. 101, folio not stated); he is one of the two poets through whom Khan
  Baloch reached Hinglaj (p. 240, folio not stated); and the book quotes "Every Sindhi, of whatever faith he may be,
  believes in the greatness of Bhitai." (Kanasro 2007, 11) (p. 95, folio not stated). Index: `Risalo (Latif), 89` and
  `Legacy of Shah Latif (Mangi), 89` (pp. 358, 351, folio not stated). **This is the largest misfile in the book: the
  Pass 1 notes parked Latif's Hinglaj journey under "Other" and it is a real body of material for
  `bhit-bhit-shah`.**
- **Guru Nanak → all 18 gurdwara rows whose `principal_figure` is Guru Nanak** (the many-row rule of 22 September 2026;
  the count matches the brief's "Guru Nanak heads a bullet with all 18 gurdwara rows"). The book's material: "Many
  Lohanas in Sindh follow the teachings of Nanak, somewhat obscuring their religious affiliation for the colonial
  officers." (p. 80, folio not stated); "Cook notes the importance of Sikhism to both of these groups as further
  evidence of the intermingling of the Khatris and Lohanas (Cook 2010, 144–145)." (p. 81, folio not stated); one of the
  three pūjā places at Chaurasi is connected to Guru Nanak (pp. 258, 265, folios not stated); Lakhavat's proof of a
  visit — "He publishes a map from the Atlas Travels of Guru Nanak showing a route allegedly traveled by Nanak on his
  way to Mecca on which he passes by the shrine in Balochistan… he concludes that the footprints found in the valley
  are proof of this visit (Lakhavat 2011, 76)" (p. 257, folio not stated), with the author's distance kept; and the
  head-covering rule at Hinglaj traced to "practices of the Nanak paṃth" (p. 286, folio not stated), together with the
  tikānā, "the temple containing the Guru Granth Sahib, the holy book of Sikhism" (p. 286, folio not stated). **A claim
  of footprints of Guru Nanak inside the Hinglaj valley is a substantial fact and it was parked under "Other".**
- **Guru Gorakhnath → `gorakhnath-goraknath-temple`, `tilla-jogian`** (two rows; principal figures "Guru Gorakhnath" and
  "Guru Gorakhnath (Nath yogis)"). The book's material: the opening legend of Chapter 5 — Hinglaj cooked khic'ṛī (Hin.)
  daily against his arrival and poured the untouched food on one spot; "When Gorakhnath finally arrived at Thatta after
  twelve years, he was very pleased to see all the food that Hinglaj had prepared in anticipation of his arrival, and so
  he changed it all into a pile of limestone. Gorakhnath then declared that from that day forward, anyone who returned
  from Hinglaj wearing a necklace of these limestone pebbles would be freed from the fetters of this world (Lakhavat
  2011, 35)." (p. 206, folio not stated); "Tradition has it that Hinglaj was very fond of the Nath community, especially
  of Guru Gorakhnath. So the Goddess waited day after day in Thatta for Gorakhnath to come to meet her" (p. 205, folio
  not stated); one of the three pūjā places at Chaurasi is connected to "Guru Goraknath" (pp. 258, 265, folios not
  stated); and the branding rite at Koteshwara was performed "especially [by] followers of the Gorakhnath sect" (p. 115,
  folio not stated). **The ṭhum'rā necklace tradition is a Gorakhnath charter myth and it was parked under "Other".**
- **Shiva (Mahadev) → the 8 rows whose `principal_figure` is Shiva**: `amb-temples-amb-sharif`, `bhagnari-mandir`,
  `chandragup-baba-chandragup`, `katas-raj-temples`, `shahwala-teja-singh-mandir`, `shiv-mandir-chiti-ghati`,
  `shree-ratneshwar-mahadev-temple-karachi`, `umarkot-amarkot-shiv-mandir` (the many-row rule of 22 September 2026; the
  count matches the brief's "Shiva 8"). The book's material: the Sati/Daksha dismemberment myth (p. 55, folio not
  stated); Carcikā born from Shiva's sweat during the fight with Andhaka (p. 53, folio not stated); Koṭṭarīśā as the
  associated form of Shiva at Hinglaj (p. 55, folio not stated); the removed liṅga and the `sadā śivo` liṅga on the
  platform (pp. 238, 241, folios not stated); Til Kund as "once the meditation place of Shiva (2001, 340)" (p. 254,
  folio not stated); the cilam smoked at the Hab "to appease the God Shiva (Shastri 1977, 11)" (p. 139, folio not
  stated) and the gāṁjā smoked on the road for the same reason (p. 160, folio not stated); the chaṛī "shaped like
  Shiva's trident, the triśūla" (p. 160, folio not stated) and the branding of "the sign of Shiva and Shakti" at
  Koteshwara (p. 115, folio not stated); Deval "considered to be an incarnation of Shiva" (p. 73, folio not stated);
  "Bhairava [Shiva]" in Rajendra Singh's village and in the Mandapi installation (pp. 174, 176, folios not stated);
  Ansari's claim that "Shiva and the Mother Goddess were already known and honored in the ancient civilization of Sindh
  (Ansari 2001, 111–112)" (p. 93, folio not stated); and Potts's "Nana sometimes 'appears with Oesho (Siva) as her
  consort'" (p. 101, folio not stated).
- **Rama → `ram-mandir-saidpur-ram-kund-mandir`** (principal figure "Rama"). Rama is one of the two largest figures in
  the book after the Goddess herself: "no figure was ever as present in the representations of the traditional
  pilgrimage to Hinglaj as Rama, the seventh incarnation of the God Vishnu" (p. 119, folio not stated); "son of
  Dasharatha, the king of Ayodhya" (p. 119, folio not stated), "an important god-king" (p. 122, folio not stated); the
  whole Rāmāyaṇa-derived charter narrative of the pilgrimage, with Kumbhodar's instruction that Hinglaj is "the only
  place that can purify him" of brahm hatyā (p. 120, folio not stated); Rama's stops on the route, Lalu Jasraj as his
  chaṛīdār (p. 121, folio not stated), "the place where Rama gave his clothing to Lalu Jasraj… in Liari" (p. 78, folio
  not stated), Gorukh Tank "where Rama is said to have retreated after his defeat by the Goddess" (p. 122, folio not
  stated), `rām jharoku` / `rām baiṭhak` inside the valley (p. 257, folio not stated), the Rama pūjā at Khari Nadi
  (pp. 203–204, folios not stated), the roṭ offered "to the God Rama (Hart 1839, 89)" (pp. 212–213, folios not stated),
  siṃdūr placed "on the place where Rama sat down" (p. 78, folio not stated), and one of the three Chaurasi pūjā places
  (pp. 258, 265, folios not stated). Also the televised Rāmāyaṇa of the late 1980s, "refilmed in 2012" (pp. 119, 126,
  folios not stated).
- **Hanuman → `shri-panchmukhi-hanuman-mandir-karachi`** (principal figure "Hanuman (Panchmukhi)"). "Rama's close ally"
  who "goes to Lanka" (p. 119, folio not stated) and who on the Hinglaj route "tries to extract water from the soil by
  stomping his foot violently on the ground, but this produces only a dry riverbed" (p. 121, folio not stated); the HSM
  "erected a statue of Hanuman right next to that of Ashapura" (p. 220, folio not stated) "so that people would know who
  is worshipped there" (p. 220, folio not stated); the disputed paṛāv outside the valley, "At one point they even put a
  small Hanuman statue there!" and "Although there are still people who worship Hanuman there" (pp. 279–280, folios not
  stated); Abro's objection that "the Hanuman statue is out of place in the valley and does not fit into the site's
  cosmology (Abro 1991, 58)" (p. 221, folio not stated); and the author's verdict that the statue is "a paradigmatic
  example for the HSM's cultural hegemony" (p. 221, folio not stated). **A statue programme and a live dispute over a
  deity who heads an archive row, parked under "Other".**
- **Valmiki → `lal-kurti-temple-balmiki-mandir-rawalpindi`, `valmik-mandir-naqi-road`,
  `valmiki-swamiji-mandir-gracy-lines-rawalpindi`** (three rows; principal figure "Valmiki (Bhagwan Valmik)"). The book
  names him only as an author, which the 18 September ruling covers explicitly: "Valmiki's Sanskrit version of the
  Rāmāyaṇa (in many ways the 'canonical' one)" and "the canonized corpus of Valmiki's Rāmāyaṇa" (p. 120, folio not
  stated). **Thin evidence; it still takes the ids under the ruling, with a Doubts line.**
- **Kali → `kalat-kali-temple`, `kali-bari-mandir`, `kalka-cave-temple-asthan-of-kalka-devi`** (three rows; principal
  figures "Kali", "Kali" and "Kalka Devi (Kali)"). Kali runs through the whole book: the Kali Mata shrine inside the
  Hinglaj valley with its cavity, its blood rite, its ban and its own pujārī (pp. 221–223, 284–285, 294–296, 306, 311,
  312, folios not stated); the ruined Kali temple on Astola and the goats sacrificed there (p. 260, folio not stated);
  Kali Kund (p. 255, folio not stated); Abro's equivalence "Nānyā, Anāhītā, Kālī, Durgā [or] Pārbatī" (p. 94, folio not
  stated); "For the Vagris, Kali is the guard (Hin. `caukīdār`) of the Goddess Hinglaj." (p. 311, folio not stated);
  Khodiyar named with Kali as the forms in which the Goddess appears to the Devipujaks (p. 310, folio not stated);
  "Calcutta's Kali temple" (p. 127, folio not stated); and the author's own film "Mother Calling: Kali in Karachi"
  (p. 335, folio not stated). **The Pass 1 worker considered and refused all three rows for the Hinglaj-valley Kali
  shrine, which is correct — that shrine is a separate place and is listed above as a candidate row — but Kali as a
  principal figure named repeatedly is a separate question and falls to the coordinator.**
- **Durga → `churrio-jabal-durga-mata-temple`** (principal figure "Durga (Mata)"). "the demon Mahishasura, a figure of
  the famous Devī Māhātmaya", killed by Durga (pp. 46–47, folio not stated); Abro's equivalence list (p. 94, folio not
  stated); and Potts on "[Nana's] conflation with Durga" and "the lion-riding goddesses of some seventh century A.D.
  silver bowls from Choresmia 'may be related to Nana or Durga'" (Potts 2001, 27) (p. 101, folio not stated).
- **Lava (Luv), son of Rama → `loh-temple-lava-temple`** (principal figure "Lava (Luv)", Lahore Fort). "Thakur writes
  that the general belief among Lohanas is that their community originated from the heirs of Rama's son, called Lava,
  from whom the name Lohana ultimately developed (Thakur 1997, 56). This is also the story I heard most frequently when
  talking to Lohanas in Sindh." (p. 80, folio not stated) **The custodial community of the Hinglaj shrine traces itself
  to the figure of an archive row, and the Pass 1 notes parked it.**
- **Varuna (Varun Dev) → `shri-varun-dev-mandir`** (principal figure "Varuna (Varun Dev)"). "the Lohanas were formerly
  Rathor Rajputs who obtained the help of the God Varuna in the battle against the Raja of Kanauj. According to that
  story, Varuna built the Rathors an iron fort that saved them from the Raja's attack." (p. 80, folio not stated)
- **Ramdev Pir (Ramdevji / Ramapir) → `ramapir-temple-tando-allahyar`** (principal figure "Ramdev Pir (Ramapir)"). "the
  fourteenth-century saint Ramdevji", named in the Introduction's list of the sects the book distinguishes (p. 13,
  folio not stated). **The Narayanpur "Ram Pir temple" (p. 163, folio not stated) is a separate Karachi building and is
  listed above as a candidate; it must not be folded into the Tando Allahyar row. The named saint, however, falls under
  the single-row person rule.**
- **Krishna → `krishna-mandir-kabari-bazar`, `krishna-mandir-ravi-road`** (two rows; principal figure "Krishna"; the
  brief's many-row count gives "Krishna 2"). **Flagged as marginal, not asserted**: the only occurrences in this
  material are "Well, they all have gone Hare Krishna, they are the ISKCON people, they tell us not to kill it."
  (p. 294, folio not stated) and the index headword `Kr̥ṣṇajanmakhaṇḍa, 44` (p. 350, folio not stated). **Both are
  compounds rather than references to the deity, and an index headword is not a statement; the coordinator should
  decide whether this fires at all.**

### 6. Counted

**Three hedges in this book's Pass 1 material were measured as containing the phrase "reversal of (a)", and the
material above resolves considerably more than three parked items.** Counting distinct heads converted out of "Other"
and handed to the archive section here: **1** site wrongly parked as a bare toponym that is itself an archive row
(Chandrakup); **14** place-level toponym attachments under ruling (a); **1** further place carrying a shrine and a road
fact (Lahoot); **2** names left unchosen between two candidate rows each (Noorani, Shah Inayatullah); and **12**
person- or deity-level attachments under the 18 and 22 September rulings (Shah Abdul Latif, Guru Nanak, Guru
Gorakhnath, Shiva, Rama, Hanuman, Valmiki, Kali, Durga, Lava, Varuna, Ramdev Pir), plus **1** flagged as marginal
(Krishna). **Total: 31 heads handed to the coordinator, of which 2 are deliberately left unresolved between candidate
rows and 1 is flagged as possibly not firing at all.**

## Province-level material

*Swept from the raw chunks (`chunk_001`–`chunk_019`) under ruling (c); the Pass-1 notes were not used as a source. This book prints no recoverable folio, so every reference is `(p. N, folio not stated)` with p. = PDF page. Only statements about a province or region in general are kept. Statements that are really about the Hinglaj shrine, the Hinglaj valley, Las Bela, Liari, Karachi, Thatta or another single named site are toponyms: they are left out and listed in the Sweep notes. Register is marked on every bullet: **author's argument** (Schaflechner's own analysis), **ethnographic present** (his fieldwork, 2009–2015, as he dates it, or "at the time of writing"), **quoted source** (the author quoted is named), **endnote**, and **mytho-historic narrative** (caste legends the book reports as legend, not history). The book's own period wording is carried; nothing has been dated from outside the book. Every bullet is province-level: weigh accordingly.*

### province: punjab

- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "Currently", the time of writing; endnote): "the Punjab has 8 reserved minority seats out of 371" (the same note gives Sindh 9 of 168; the note cites the provincial assembly websites) (p. 28, folio not stated, endnote 5 to p. 15).
- (book's word: "Punjab Textbooks Board"; refers to: a class V Urdu textbook dated "Lahore 2002"; author's argument citing Nayyar and Salim 2005): in Pakistani schoolbooks "the Hindu" is not only a danger to national security but "has always been an enemy of Islam" (p. 20, folio not stated). The endnote gives the source as "“Hindū hameśah se islām kā duśman rahā hai,” Urdu class V, Punjab Textbooks Board, Lahore 2002 (see Nayyar and Salim 2005, 20)" (p. 28, folio not stated, endnote 7).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "the eighteenth century" for the Bhatia and Khatri migration, period not stated for Thakur's general remark; quoted source, Thakur 1997, and Cook 2010 as reported): migrants "from Rajputanta[text-layer?] [Rajputana?] and the Punjab" who "professed Lohana occupations have acquired membership of the Lohana caste" (Thakur 1997, 68f.); "The Bhatia and the Khatris, for example, arrived in the eighteenth century from cities in the Punjab—like Multan—and were ultimately absorbed as Lohanas (Cook 2010, 135)" (pp. 80–81, folio not stated).

### province: sindh

- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: "Today", the fieldwork present; author's argument, with NGO figures): Sindh is "home to the majority of Pakistan's Hindus", and "The country's instability, the feudal environment in Sindh ... or perhaps a simple lack of interest" have kept scholars away. Of Pakistan's Hindus ("between 6 and 10 million people—an estimate that varies widely according to the source"), "over 90 percent—belong to the underprivileged Hindu tribes or sub-castes, such as the Meghvar, the Bhil, and the Kohli, who frequently work as manual laborers for the powerful landowning elite in Sindh" (p. 13, folio not stated). The 6-to-10-million estimate comes from "the Human Rights Commission of Pakistan (HRCP) in Sindh" (p. 28, folio not stated, endnote 1). "In Sindh, the Kohli community is mainly a disempowered scheduled caste whose members work as agricultural peasants on the fields owned by Sindh's feudal elite"; Kohlis, Meghvar and Bhil make up most festival visitors, "which also reflects the overall population of Hindus in Sindh, and more than 90 percent originate from these communities" (p. 154, folio not stated). "according to nongovernmental organizations (NGOs) in Sindh, the biggest bulk of Pakistani Hindus, over 90 percent, consist of so-called scheduled caste communities" (p. 276, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Sindh province", "Sindh"; refers to: "Currently" for the seats and "within the past decade" for the restorations, i.e. the fieldwork years; endnote and author's argument): "the Sindh province has 9 minority seats out of 168" (p. 28, folio not stated, endnote 5). Government funding for minority community places "is seen in a wave of restorations of temples across Sindh within the past decade"; "While in Sindh, I visited many temples that were renovated within the past decade, coinciding with changes in the political landscape" (p. 16, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "rural Sindh", "Sindh"; refers to: the fieldwork present, and March 2014 for the rumour; author's argument and reported rumour): "In rural Sindh, in particular, the clan or the barādarī serves as a guarantee of security rather than the nation state" (p. 18, folio not stated). In one of the rumours about the March 2014 Larkana temple attack, recounted by NGO friends in Hyderabad: "According to tradition in Sindh, the culprits took the Quran and asked the Hindu family for remission" (p. 17, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the province of Sindh"; refers to: Partition and "Today in Pakistan"; author's argument): "The partition of the subcontinent brought major changes to the province of Sindh. The departure of the Hindu elite and the influx of Muslim refugees (“Muhajirs”) from India dramatically altered the local demography, and the Sindhi community became increasingly defined by its perception of being ostracized and subjugated by Muhajir power. Today in Pakistan, one commonly hears Sindhi patriots and nationalists lamenting the degenerated state of the province"; in these discourses, "found on both sides of the border", Sindh is "presented as a distinct entity lacking any cultural links to other provinces or the state of Pakistan generally" (p. 87, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: after Partition, from an exile's point of view; quoted source, K. R. Malkani, *The Sindh Story* (1997), "a Sindhi in exile who, as a Hindu, left Hyderabad after Partition and settled in New Delhi"): "As for the state of Pakistan, Sindh rejects it wholly. Sindh has always been there, Pakistan is a passing show. Sindh is a fact, Pakistan is a fiction ... [sic]" (Malkani 1997, 134); "A Sufi Sindh and an Islamic Pakistan cannot coexist ... If Pakistan continues, Sindh will die" (Malkani 1997, 136). The author calls the book full of "rants against Pakistan" (p. 87, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: "the time before the British invasion", as nationalist writers describe it; quoted source, Hussain Soomro 2009, and author's argument): Sindhi nationalist discourse also blames "British rule, which they view as having victimized Sindh"; Soomro: "Sindh was so prosperous that it was commonly described that there is abundance of milk and honey in Sindh ... [sic]" (Soomro 2009, 3). These narratives make Pakistan and the British "the culprits in the case of lost harmony and affluence in Sindh" (pp. 87–88, folio not stated). In the chapter conclusion, Sindhi nationalist discourse links Hinglaj "to a “real” Sindh that prospered before outsiders began suppressing the province" (p. 97, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sindhi nationalism", "Sind"; refers to: "the 1920s and 1930s" and "the 1947 publication", as reported from Verkaaik 2010; quoted source, Oscar Verkaaik): Sindhi nationalist narratives are organized around the "Sindhi language, the poetry of [national poet] Shah Abdul Latif, the Indus, the peasant or hari, and above all, Sufism and mysticism" (Verkaaik 2010, 197), plus the link to Mohenjo-Daro. Verkaaik "describes Sindhi nationalism as a relatively new phenomenon that began with the struggle for independence from the Bombay Presidency in the 1920s and 1930s"; important to its later growth was "the 1947 publication, Save Sind, Save the Continent, by Ibrahmin Joyo[text-layer?] [Ibrahim Joyo?]" (p. 88, folio not stated). (The book's own phrase for this unit is "the Bombay Presidency"; the book adds no boundary history.)
- (book's word: "the province of Sindh"; refers to: G. M. Syed's lifetime "(1904–1995)" and the present ("to this day"); quoted source, Verkaaik 2004 and 2010, on Syed): Syed, a politician "who spent many years in prison for his separatist views on Sindh and Pakistan, directly linked the province of Sindh with a tradition of Sufism"; he finds Sufism "in its purest form in the province of Sindh", proven "by the various shrines of Sufi saints that are, to this day, visited by Hindus and Muslims alike" (pp. 88–89, folio not stated). Verkaaik: "both Sindhi Hindus and Muslims were influenced by mysticism and religious tolerance. This was the Sindhi essence that both Hindus and Muslims shared" (p. 89, folio not stated). According to Verkaaik 2004, it was Syed "who held primary responsibility for generating Bhittai's role as the most important poet of Sindh" (p. 101, folio not stated, endnote 52).
- (book's word: "the history of Sindh"; refers to: "today", as a common notion; author's argument): writers stress "the common notion among Sindhis today that in the history of Sindh, there is no religious tension between the two communities—Muslims and Hindus. This notion of the harmonious coexistence of the religions, however, is merely a nationalist utopian ideal, as Aggarwal (2013) has shown" (pp. 90–91, folio not stated). The chapter plan names the shrine as "a hallmark of Sindh's “golden past”" for "modern-day Sindhi nationalism" (p. 51, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: "five thousand years ago" and "the ancient civilization of Sindh", as the Sindhi writer claims; quoted source, Ishtiaq Ansari 2001, reported critically): Ansari "directly associates the ancient civilization of Mohenjo-Daro with today's Sindhi people"; he claims "the composition of the Veda occurred on the shores of the Indus", that Shiva and the Mother Goddess "were already known and honored in the ancient civilization of Sindh", and that Goddess veneration "was ... already in place in Sindh before the Aryans conquered the area “five thousand years ago”" (Ansari 2001, 154) (p. 93, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "real Sindh" (Sin. aṣal sindh); refers to: an undated ancient past, as Abro claims, and the present; quoted source, Badar Abro 1991): "Sindhis once lived in the valleys between Rohri and east Balochistan, an area he defines as the “real Sindh” ... that was once “prosperous”" (Abro 1991, 22); "Since Sindh remained under the influence of Sufi mysticism ... the area is “fortunately in terms of faith, secular [in English]”" (Abro 1991, 23), so the religious places of the region belong to "the collective heritage of a “real Sindh”" (p. 94, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sindhi", "the Sindhi"; refers to: "this modern age" and Latif's eighteenth-century journey; quoted sources, Abdul Qadir Mangi in Kanasro 2007, Khadim Soomro 2002, Agha 2004): Mangi: "Every Sindhi, of whatever faith he may be, believes in the greatness of Bhitai. From a Kohli Bheel to a Syed, every one considers Bhitai his spiritual guide" (p. 95, folio not stated). Sindhi nationalists see Latif "as the ideal Sindhi—the perfect example for the tolerant and “syncretic” history of Sindh", while "more orthodox writers aim to explain away Latif's journey as “youthful recklessness”" (p. 95, folio not stated). Khadim Soomro: "A Sindhi has a special place in human civilization because he stands for good and liberal attitude and behavior" (p. 96, folio not stated). Latif is dated "(1690–1751)" here (p. 95).
- (book's word: "Sindh", "The south of Pakistan, and especially Sindh"; refers to: the fieldwork present; author's argument): "The south of Pakistan, and especially Sindh, offers a whole range of salvific spaces that are frequently visited by different religious communities and form a network of pilgrimage routes or ritual journeys. Sindhi nationalists often emphasize these characteristics of Sindh as a landscape of “coexistence”" (p. 107, folio not stated). Legends linking Latif and the Goddess "connect Hindu and Muslim sacred spaces in Sindh and show that imagined landscapes do not necessarily have to be separated using clear-cut religious taxonomies" (p. 109, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sindh Hindoos", "Sindh", "Sind Hindus"; refers to: Burton "1851", McMurdo via Mubarak 1985, Thakur 1997, and "the past few centuries" up to today; quoted sources): Burton: "a description of them [the Lohanas], therefore, will be a general portrait of the Sindh Hindoos" (Burton 1851, 314); McMurdo: "The Lowannas or Lohannas compose the great body of Hindus in Sindh" (Mubarak 1985, 55); "Over the past few centuries, the Lohana community absorbed many other groups and clans, now encompassing a significant portion of the upper classes of Pakistani Hindus" (p. 79, folio not stated). Thakur: Lohanas form "the general body of the Sind Hindus" (p. 80, folio not stated). "Due to the heterogeneity of the Lohanas in Sindh, it is not possible to make any universal claim for the whole community" (p. 216, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: "the eighteenth century" (Kalhora rule), "the nineteenth century", and "Around the eighteenth or nineteenth century"; book's historical prose, citing Lachaier 2008, Cook 2010, Lari 1994, and Zahid-Khan quoted in Cook): "in the nineteenth century, the Lohanas were the most important trading community in the areas of Sindh, Kutch, and Saurashtra (Lachaier 2008, 82). One reason for this was the established rule of the Kalhoras in Sindh in the eighteenth century, which brought new business opportunities for merchant professions (Cook 2010, 140)" (p. 80, folio not stated). Zahid-Khan "attributes this merging of different groups to the small number of non-Muslim Sindhis who united in the face of the majority"; "Sindh's location between trading routes connecting Afghanistan, Khorasan, Arabia, Persia, Turkey, and other places made the province appealing to a wide array of merchants from the entire continent"; "Around the eighteenth or nineteenth century, a separation occurred within these merchant groups of Sindh": Lohanas working for local rulers became "Amils", "initially 10 to 15 percent of the Lohanas (Lari 1994, 211)", and those who kept trading were "Bhaiband" (p. 81, folio not stated). (Kutch and Saurashtra are named alongside Sindh; see `not attached`.)
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: the fieldwork present, plus the censuses of "1881" and "1891"; author's argument citing Lari 1994 and Thakur 1997): "Many Lohanas in Sindh follow the teachings of Nanak, somewhat obscuring their religious affiliation for the colonial officers. Hence, in the 1881 census, Lohanas from Shikarpur and Hyderabad were counted as Sikhs, while ten years later, in the 1891 census, they were classified as Hindus (Lari 1994, 211f.)" (p. 80, folio not stated). "many middle- and upper-class Hindus in Sindh are followers of the Nanak paṃth" (p. 257, folio not stated); "Many upper-caste Hindus in Sindh, especially from the Lohana community, refer to Nanak and his teachings (Thakur 1997, 141), and it is common to cover one's head before walking into a tikānā (Sin.), the temple containing the Guru Granth Sahib" (p. 286, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sind", "Sindh", "other provinces of Pakistan"; refers to: Thakur's *Sindhi Culture* "(1951)", cited as Thakur 1997, and the fieldwork conversations; quoted source and ethnographic present): Thakur writes that the "[c]aste system is almost absent in Sind" (Thakur 1997, 25), a perception the author links to "the assimilative role of the Sindhi Lohana jāti" and to "Sindhi nationalistic discourses, which portray Sindh as a place of religious harmony and tolerance". Devoted Sindhi Hindu nationalists told him caste discrimination "exists today only in India or other provinces of Pakistan"; "Many upper-caste Sindhi Hindus often deny or ignore acts of segregation", which "Dalit organizations" fiercely oppose (pp. 277–278, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: the fieldwork present; author's argument and ethnographic present): the HSM's idea of progress and education "stands in sharp contrast to low-caste rituals in Sindh such as the sacrifice of animals or the consumption of alcohol as prasād" (p. 278, folio not stated); "many other Hindu groups in Sindh, and in fact all over Pakistan, sacrifice animals for the fulfillment of certain wishes or during particular rites of passage" (p. 310, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: Burton, "1851", colonial; quoted source, Burton, via endnotes): Burton "confirms the unusual use of the term “Vaishiya” in Sindh, which is mainly applied “in a limited sense to signify operatives and mechanics, opposed to merchants and shopkeepers”" (Burton 1851, 237) (pp. 98–99, folio not stated, endnote 16). Burton says the Saraswat Brahmins, many of whom were thought to be low caste, "were known for their devotion to the Devi who, in most areas of Sindh, was called Hinglaj (Burton 1851, 312)" (p. 100, folio not stated, endnote 28).
- (book's word: "the province of Sindh", "the Sindh", "the people of Sindh"; refers to: mythic time in the Brahmakshatriya story, and for the Charani Avar a birth "in the year 888 CE" and Huns "dated to the eighth century by Samaur", both as the sources state; mytho-historic narrative, as reported by the author): in the Brahmakshatriya story, Parashurama "arrives in the province of Sindh" to kill Kshatriyas, and "The mighty king Ratnasena, the ruler of Sindh" flees (pp. 61–62, folio not stated). In the Charan story of Avar, famine drives her family "to go to Sindh, then ruled by Umar Sumra who had recently converted to Islam and was now known by the name of “Hamir”". The Samma and Bhati Rajput kings, who "had wanted to occupy the Sindh for some time", help Avar kill him and "then divided the Sindh between them and ruled in peace". Another story has Avar kill "fifty-two of these Hun-demons" and so save "the people of Sindh" (Samaur 1999, 509) (p. 72, folio not stated). These are legends, not historical statements.
- (book's word: "Sindh"; refers to: "for many generations" before Partition, and after it; ethnographic present, Vagri oral history): "According to Vagri history, the community migrated back and forth between Sindh and Gujarat for many generations and, like other nomadic groups, were eventually forced to settle on one side of the border after the Partition. Since there is no written account of their movement, the clan's detailed history can only be based on speculation" (p. 308, folio not stated).

### province: balochistan

- (book's word: "Balochistan, a province"; refers to: "since the establishment of Pakistan" for the insurgency, and "At the time of writing" for the visa rules; author's argument citing Axmann 2012, and quoted source, Ratnu 2007): Indian pilgrims "must obtain an additional visa allowing travel to Balochistan, a province that has been in a state of a latent insurgency since the establishment of Pakistan (Axmann 2012)". "Access to Balochistan is further limited by its legal ambiguity since “Non-Objection Certificates” (NOC) must be obtained from the government, but the legal status of an application for such a certificate is unclear"; Ratnu writes that Pakistan "actually does not issue visas for the journey to Balochistan" (Ratnu 2007, 28), and so "the journey has no proper legal status and thus is likely to involve bribery and nepotism" (p. 169, folio not stated). Elsewhere the book calls it "a restricted area of Balochistan" (p. 179, folio not stated): "the status of visas for Balochistan is legally ambiguous" (p. 184, folio not stated), and "there is currently no solid legal base for the journey into Balochistan" (p. 192, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "Balochistan's shrines"; refers to: "November 2016"; author's argument): after the bomb blast at Lahoot la Makan, "a terrible bomb blast in November 2016, killing more than fifty people, has curbed the growing influx of visitors and has created a volatile situation for Balochistan's shrines" (p. 108, folio not stated). (This date falls after the book's 2009–2015 fieldwork.)
- (book's word: "Balochistan"; refers to: period not stated for the rainfall and rivers (Snead 1964 is cited), the travellers' past for the landscape, and the fieldwork present for the heat; book's historical prose and ethnographic present): mud cones last because "due to Balochistan's low rainfall average, [the mud] is not subject to rapid erosion (Snead 1964, 548)" (p. 211, folio not stated); "Unlike many other rivers in Balochistan, the Hingol flows throughout the year" (p. 215, folio not stated); travellers came "through the dry and barren landscape of Balochistan" (p. 251, folio not stated); during "the spring gatherings ... the extreme heat in Balochistan makes fieldwork a grueling task" (pp. 293–294, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Balochistan coast", "Balochistan's coastal area"; refers to: "2001" and "the beginning of the new millennium" onward; author's argument): the Makran Coastal Highway, "originally intended to boost economic development of the Balochistan coast", reshaped the pilgrimage, and "the recent infrastructural development in Balochistan's coastal area" is set against the older hardship (p. 322, folio not stated). "After the first part of the Makran Coastal Highway (N 10) was completed in 2001, the area around the River Hingol became connected to the National Highway 25, a heavily traveled road that links Karachi with Quetta" (p. 83, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "east Balochistan"; refers to: an undated ancient past, as Abro claims; quoted source, Badar Abro 1991): Abro's "real Sindh" covers "the valleys between Rohri and east Balochistan" (Abro 1991, 22). This is a Sindhi nationalist claim about the region, not a statement about Balochistan in its own right (p. 94, folio not stated). The same passage is under `sindh`.

### not attached

- "Khatris of the Punjab and United Provinces": Dirks (2001, 222) quotes Risley on their successful attempt to be classified as Rajputs in the colonial census (the context is "the census of 1901"). This is a colonial-era, all-India caste claim made jointly with the United Provinces, and the book does not place it in any part of present-day Pakistan (p. 60, folio not stated). The "Punjabi Bania" in the 1904 Varma quotation is a caste label, not a region (p. 61).
- "Kutch, and Saurashtra" alongside Sindh in Lachaier's nineteenth-century Lohana trade statement (p. 80): only the Sindh part is carried under `sindh`.
- "Rajasthan": the famine that drives Avar's family to Sindh, and Avar's birth "in a village called Calkun in Barmer district, India" (p. 72). This Indian material stays out of `sindh`; only the Sindh half of the legend is carried.
- "Gujarat": the Vagri back-and-forth "between Sindh and Gujarat" (p. 308); only the Sindh half is carried.
- "Kashmir": the wanderer Shastri, "In 1940, on the way back from Kashmir to central India", meets a Nath in Lahore. Which Kashmir is not stated, and the statement says nothing about the region (p. 138).

*Sweep notes:* Hit counts are matching lines as Grep reports them (case-insensitive) across `chunk_001`–`chunk_019`: "Balochistan|Baluchistan" 111; "Sind\b|Sindh\b" 138; "Punjab" 13 lines (3 inside "Punjabi"; 3 more are Masson's title, a Dawn URL and an index entry); "Kashmir" 1; "Frontier" 2 (the newspaper *Frontier Post*, p. 28, and a bibliography title, p. 331); "Pakhtunkhwa", "NWFP", "Panjab", "suba" 0. The adjective query "Sindhi|Punjabi|Panjabi|Baluchi|Balochi|Kashmiri" returns 261 lines, but 111 of those are the province name itself (the case-insensitive "Balochi"/"Baluchi" matches inside it). Of the rest, roughly 150 lines, almost all are "Sindhi" naming a language, a text, a writer or a person. These were not taken as province statements, except where the sentence is about Sindhis as a regional people (nationalism, caste, Lohanas, all above). No `khyber pakhtunkhwa`, `islamabad capital territory` or `azad kashmir` material was found. The Balochistan hits are overwhelmingly the shrine's own location ("the shrine in Balochistan", "the desert of Balochistan", passim). Excluded as toponyms or single-site statements (ruling a): Thando Allah Yar, "the Sindhi city" (p. 13); Larkana, "a city in the north of Sindh", and the 2014 attacks in Hyderabad and Tharparkar (p. 17); Hinglaj as pīr "Bibi Nani" within "modern-day Sindhi" traditions (p. 24); Shikarpur "(Sindh)" Khatris in the nineteenth century (p. 58); Thatta, "a city in the south of Sindh", and Payaka's "Hala in Sindh" with the Goddess leading the Tumbel Charan "from Sindh to the Makran" (p. 71); the provincial "Balochistan government" funding the Hingol bridge, shelters and roads at the shrine (pp. 84, 215, 226, 245, 323), its political support for the HSM (p. 262), the "Balochistan police" at the valley gate (pp. 218, 284) and the "Balochistan Assembly" stopping the 2008 WAPDA Hingol dam (p. 217); Zikris of Las Bela and the Makran coast (p. 90); the Rama/Kapri sayings that no priest could be found "in Balochistan" (p. 100, n. 28) and that Liari is "in Balochistan" (p. 79); Indian pilgrims' fears of the journey "to Balochistan" (pp. 100–101, n. 42; p. 170); the "dangerous political situation in the area" on the Lahoot–Hinglaj path (p. 147, n. 10); "In Sindh today, “Seth” simply implies an employer or a boss" (p. 147, n. 19, a lexical note); kāpṛī-like ascetics "still found in the interior Sindh today" (p. 101, n. 54); Sehwan Sharif and Lal Shahbaz (p. 107); Lahoot "in the Khuzdar district in Balochistan" (pp. 107–108); Bhit Shah "(Sindh)" and the Thatta cave (pp. 108–109); "imagined landscapes of Sindh and Balochistan particularly in the area between Karachi and the Hinglaj Valley" (p. 119); the yātrā's role "for many Sindhis" before the colonial period (p. 146); pilgrims from near Umerkot, rural Hyderabad and "the interior of Sindh" (pp. 152, 154, 156); the pre-1980s path "through the deserts of Sindh and Balochistan" (p. 186); the "picnic" debate "at other temples in Sindh" (p. 187); Uthal, called "the present capital of the province" in a Khan Baloch 1970 citation (p. 202); HSM posters in "rural Sindh" (p. 204); the Mata Bhawani temple, "generally known in Sindh" (p. 205); Quetta, "the capital of Balochistan" (p. 206); Lohana initiation at the Hingol (p. 216); Jam Bambhaniya "from Sindh" in a fourteenth-century Ashapura legend (p. 264, n. 23); visitors "from Karachi and interior Sindh" (p. 272); "peasants from Sindh" at the festival (p. 290); the "Minority Minster"[text-layer?] [Minority Minister] "of Sindh" donating festival food, a pilgrims' report (p. 291); Meghwar and Kali Mata informants "from the interior Sindh" (pp. 303–304); local communities "living in Sindh and in Balochistan" claiming a link to the shrine (p. 321); the author's call for research on Hindus "particularly in the Sindh province" (p. 326). Also excluded: the 2012 informant's list "the Sindhi, the Mohajir, the Punjabi, the Balochi" (p. 16), which names ethnic groups rather than the provinces; the Sindhi press's "liberal outlook" (p. 28, n. 4); the lack of Hindu texts in "Urdu ..., Punjabi ..., and Sindhi" (p. 270); a "Punjabi pilgrim" (p. 147); and bibliography and index entries (pp. 327–364). Flagged for a human: `Rajputanta`[text-layer?] [Rajputana?] (p. 80) and `Ibrahmin Joyo`[text-layer?] [Ibrahim Joyo?] (p. 88), both carried as printed. Shah Abdul Latif is dated "(1690–1751)" at pp. 90 and 95 but "(1689–1753)" at p. 108; both are printed by the book and neither is preferred. Thakur's *Sindhi Culture* is dated "(1951)" in the text but cited as "Thakur 1997". Uthal as "the present capital of the province" (p. 202) must not be read as a statement about Balochistan's capital. The book names Quetta as that capital at p. 206. The Punjab textbook bullet rests on one endnote citation to a secondary source (Nayyar and Salim 2005). The Lahoot blast bullet (p. 108) is dated November 2016, later than the fieldwork. The two mytho-historic Sindh bullets (pp. 61–62, 72) are legends and must not be used as dated history.

## Cross-cutting material

*This section merges the practices, institutions and economy material and both arguments extracts
across all 19 chunks. Almost all of it is about one archive row,
**shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir**, so the id is named at a bullet head only where the bullet is
specifically and narrowly about the shrine's own fabric, calendar, custodianship or money; elsewhere
the context is the Hinglaj pilgrimage network as a whole. Two other archive rows are touched
incidentally and are flagged where they occur; all such cases are also collected at the end under
`## Misfiled — belongs in the archive section`.*

### The transliteration policy that governs how every name in this book must be read

- **The book states its own orthographic rules, and they determine what can and cannot be matched to
  an archive row** (pp. 9–10, folios not stated). Non-English words "are italicized", with language
  and system in parentheses, "e.g., tīrtha (Hin.), chaśmah (Urd.)". Hindi and Bengali follow "the
  conventional system for modern Indo-Aryan languages in Indic scripts (see Das 1983, 66n2)"; "Other
  languages (Urdu, Sanskrit, Sindhi) follow the guidelines of the Library of Congress (with the
  exception of 'sh' being written as 'ś')"; "All transcripts of interviews in Hindi or Urdu are
  provided with the Hindi transliteration"; "It is possible that the same word may be transliterated
  in two different ways… (e.g., Hin siṃdūr; Sin. sindūr)"; plurals take an English `-s` ("e.g.,
  pīṭhas, tīrthas"); "Termini technici relating to the Hindu faith appear mainly in their Sanskrit
  base form (e.g., liṅga, piṇḍa)"; "Sic erat scriptum ([sic]) is sometimes placed at the end of a
  complete paragraph or quote instead of following each erroneous word"; "Films are quoted in the same
  way as books, and the time code is mentioned (e.g., Schaflechner 2011, 40:40)"; "Narrative accounts
  summarized from texts follow a synoptic present whereas narratives summarized from oral accounts are
  written in the past tense"; "Words that have been subjected to significant changes are additionally
  mentioned in their root form in parentheses, for example, Seeta Koowas (probably from Hin. <sītā
  kuāṁ>)".
- **The rule that matters most for archive matching**: "Names of individuals (historic persons or
  authors), deities, organizations, and places that have found their way into the English language are
  written without diacritics (e.g., Shiva, Karachi, Brahmo Samaj). Names of various groups and
  communities are also written without diacritics. Sometimes this diction follows the group's own way
  of spelling in English." (pp. 9–10, folios not stated) **And the warning that limits every personal
  name in the fieldwork chapters**: "A few names have been changed to protect privacy." (p. 10, folio
  not stated)

### The shrine's institutional history, in the dates the book prints

- **shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir** — **1986: the pilgrimage is institutionalised.** "This
  situation changed rapidly after 1986 when a temple committee, the Hinglāj Śevā Manḍalī (HSM), took
  over the organization of the pilgrimage" (p. 30, folio not stated); "the impact of the pilgrimage's
  institutionalization in 1986" (p. 80, folio not stated); "since 1986 [the posters] have been annually
  distributed in the temples of major Pakistani cities and in rural Sindh" (p. 204, folio not stated —
  **`1986` is cleanly printed**); and, in the Conclusion, "the institutionalization of the pilgrimage
  by the Hinglaj Sheva Mandali (HSM) in 1986" (p. 319, folio not stated).
- **2001: the first stage of the Makran Coastal Highway reaches the Hingol.** "The MCH was built in
  consecutive stages, and the first part, linking the Hingol valley with Karachi, was completed and in
  use by 2001." (p. 85, folio not stated) "After the first part of the Makran Coastal Highway (N 10)
  was completed in 2001, the area around the River Hingol became connected to the National Highway 25,
  a heavily traveled road that links Karachi with Quetta." (p. 83, folio not stated) "after 2001, the
  site quickly developed into a stage for one of the biggest annual Hindu festivals in Pakistan"
  (p. 30, folio not stated).
- **2003: the whole highway.** "The completion of the 650-kilometer MCH from Liari to Gabd at the
  Iranian border in 2003 (Hasan 2009, 161)" (p. 85, folio not stated). **The two road dates are not a
  contradiction — the book distinguishes the first stage (2001) from the full 650 km (2003) — but both
  must be carried, since the shrine's change is attributed to the 2001 stage.**
- **2004, and a second date: the animal-sacrifice ban.** Animal sacrifice inside the valley stopped for
  "organizational difficulties" and was banned officially "as recently as 2004" (p. 47, folio not
  stated). Elsewhere the same ban is dated only as "recently imposed" (p. 27, folio not stated) and
  narrated as the outcome of petitions "in those years" by the Vishnu paṃth, "mostly the Maheshvari
  community and followers of the ISKCON" (p. 296, folio not stated), following a mid-1990s supply
  crisis (p. 295, folio not stated). **The three accounts are kept side by side and the book does not
  reconcile them.**
- **2008 / 2009: the ticketing system begins. The book gives two years and does not reconcile them.**
  "In 2009, the HSM decided to introduce an elaborate ticketing system in place of the former, rather
  loose arrangement of years past." (p. 165, folio not stated); "Inside the Hinglaj Valley, this
  bhag'vā tradition was rearticulated between 2009 and 2013" (p. 209, folio not stated). Against that:
  "in 2008, the Mandali introduced a new ticketing system requiring the purchase of an ochre-colored,
  plastic wristband called the bhag'vā paṭṭī (Hin. ochre cloth/ribbon) in place of the former paper
  tickets." (p. 219, folio not stated) **Both stand; neither is preferred here.**
- **2011: the two signboards.** "In 2011, the organization decided to post signs in Urdu and English
  stating the 'do's' and 'don'ts' inside the gates of the valley's enclosed area" (p. 282, folio not
  stated). Before that, "official rules of conduct were only presented during a few days of festive
  activity, and then only by oral communication" (p. 284, folio not stated).
- **2012: the bridge over the Hingol.** "In 2012, the HSM supervised the construction of a bridge over
  the Hingol River with a total cost of 11 crore Pakistani rupees (about €920,000), funded by the
  Balochistan government." (p. 84, folio not stated) Its ritual consequence is dated in the same breath
  (see the festival section below).
