- **2013: the ticketing system is abolished.** "They eventually quit charging this fee in 2013."
  (p. 83, folio not stated); "in 2013, the HSM decided to abolish the ticketing system" (p. 209, folio
  not stated); "until the ticketing system was aborted in 2013" (p. 166, folio not stated).
- **2015: the author's last visit.** "construction of additional buildings to accommodate the thousands
  of pilgrims was ongoing the last time I visited the shrine in 2015" (p. 221, folio not stated); the
  fieldwork span is "between 2009 and 2015" (pp. 14, 282, folios not stated).
- **The author's own summary of the sequence.** "it is evident that since the middle of the 1980s, one
  finds a network of increasing infrastructure, publications, and ritual journeys that have enabled a
  fast-paced development of the whole Hinglaj phenomenon." (p. 84, folio not stated) He declines to
  name a prime mover: "It is difficult to state what might have been the primary catalyst… However, in
  medias res of all of these events, one finds time and again traces of the HSM's efforts." (p. 84,
  folio not stated)

### The temple committee: the Hinglaj Sheva Mandali (HSM)

- **shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir** — **Custodianship here is a committee, not a lineage, and
  the book says so repeatedly.** The organisation of the pilgrimage passed to the `Hinglāj Śevā
  Manḍalī (HSM)` after 1986 (p. 30, folio not stated); the committee holds "a vast institutional
  network attached to the temple committee—largely composed of Lasi Lohana group members" (p. 27, folio
  not stated); it is "the main organizational body in charge" and "the temple committee" (p. 324, folio
  not stated). **No sajjada nashin, no mahant, no hereditary custodian, no Auqaf, no Evacuee Trust
  body, no endowment and no land holding is named anywhere in this material: all (not stated).** That
  absence is itself a finding for an archive of tomb shrines.
- **The name is printed at least three ways and the book does not harmonise them**: `Hinglāj Śevā
  Manḍalī` with full diacritics in the body (p. 30, folio not stated); `Hinglaj Sheva Mandali` without
  diacritics in an endnote two pages earlier (p. 28, folio not stated); and "Shri Hinglaj Sheva
  Mandali" inside a quoted Hindi source (p. 169, folio not stated). It is also called simply "the
  Mandali", "the temple committee" and "the temple organization" (pp. 209, 215, 219, 220, 244, 245,
  folios not stated), and in one whole chunk the acronym is never expanded at all: **(not stated)**
  there (pp. 227–249, folios not stated).
- **What the HSM actually does, listed by the book.** It has "for many years been overseeing the
  renovation of temples, the building of bridges, and the construction of community places inside the
  valley" (p. 324, folio not stated); it produces "paths, sign plates, statues, temples, wells, compound
  walls, or whole pilgrimage stops" (p. 195, folio not stated); it chooses which halting places to
  advertise (p. 204, folio not stated); it sets the festival date (p. 215, folio not stated); it
  appoints the priest (p. 47, folio not stated); it bans and enforces (pp. 27, 296, 325, folios not
  stated); and it allocates ground at the melā (p. 272, folio not stated).
- **The annual maintenance cycle, as observed.** "Weeks before the big phālgun/caitra melā, the HSM
  cleans the valley, repaints the temples, and does minor renovation work. A few days before the
  beginning of the festival, paths are newly marked, and small arrows are painted on the ground to
  guide people through the site." (p. 271, folio not stated) The HSM "even responds to suggestions and
  requests by previous visitors" (p. 271, folio not stated), and this "refurbishing" itself
  "contributes to new interpretations of existing temples and pilgrimage paths" (p. 271, folio not
  stated). Quotidian labour counts as ritual practice in the author's frame: spatial practices "include
  ritual performances but also quotidian acts, such as the cleaning of the valley and general patterns
  of mobility" (p. 197, folio not stated).
- **The volunteer corps, its caste line and its unacknowledged payment.** "Today there is a sizable
  corps of HSM volunteers (Hin. sevā kar'nevāle)… Basically, anybody can volunteer… However, there is a
  cadre of 'professional' volunteers who have worked with the HSM for many years. In interviews, these
  volunteers always emphasize that they are not employed by the HSM, but during my fieldwork between
  2009 and 2015, I noticed that they regularly received 'money donations' from the organization for
  their work." (p. 282, folio not stated) **The amounts are (not stated).** Their powers: "These
  volunteers have the authority to exclude visitors who misbehave, but normally their job consists
  mainly of pointing out minor transgressions" (p. 282, folio not stated); they are also "the pilgrims'
  primary source of information on sleeping options, water supplies, or food" (p. 282, folio not
  stated). **The caste line inside the corps is stated as a rule without exception**: "While many
  low-caste communities 'volunteer' as masons, plumbers, or guards for the HSM, those volunteers posted
  in front of the Hinglaj temple, advising visitors on how they should perform their offering, are
  always Lohana. Prescriptions for pilgrim behavior inside the Hinglaj temple therefore remain firmly
  under the control of the Lasi Lohana community." (p. 282, folio not stated)
- **Caste composition of the custodial body, stated flatly.** The HSM is "a body consisting mainly of
  (Lasi) Lohana caste members" (p. 277, folio not stated), "whose members consist almost entirely of
  Lohanas from Las Bela" (p. 209, folio not stated), its institutional power "gained in large part
  through its upper-caste members and connection to a family with long-standing ties to the Goddess
  Hinglaj" (p. 277, folio not stated).
- **Escalation of the means of control, in three stages** (pp. 282, 284, folios not stated): personal
  contact between HSM representatives and pilgrims "at the beginning of the institutionalized melā in
  the 1980s"; then loudspeakers "as the number of visitors began to surge"; then the 2011 boards. The
  method under pressure: at the 2012 havan "the crowds started to rush toward the fire and many people
  were pushed to the ground. Some volunteer workers took megaphones and appealed to the sanctity of the
  place and the wrath of the Goddess to calm the mob." (p. 282, folio not stated)
- **The committee's own self-understanding.** "the Mandali perceives itself as a guardian of truth and
  in the role of an educator seeking to rid the Goddess's tradition of antiquated and premodern
  conceptions" (p. 278, folio not stated); Dewani "understands the temple organization as a part of a
  progressive movement that attempts to rid the Hindu community (Urd. barādarī) of its backward ideas
  and practices" (p. 279, folio not stated). And, on the chaṛīdār's office: "The Mandali sees itself as
  the successor of the chaṛīdār, who also received money for his services." (p. 219, folio not stated)
- **Contestation of the committee's authority, with a named community.** "Many communities claim Hinglaj
  as a ritual space in their own tradition, refusing to follow the particular guidelines perpetuated by
  the HSM and the Lasi Lohana community. Smaller groups and individuals, however, lack the Mandali's
  influence and thus the ability to spread their own perception of the histories and practices at the
  shrine." (p. 267, folio not stated) The HSM is also accused of "allegedly shallow knowledge of the
  history and the rituals at Hinglaj and its surroundings, a direct challenge to the organization's
  hegemonic position and religious expertise at the shrine" (p. 292, folio not stated); "others are
  skeptical about this Lohana version of history at the shrine" (p. 292, folio not stated).
- **Friction with the newer welfare organisations.** "With their growing influence among increasing
  numbers of visitors, these organizations also bring new interpretations of tradition and practice that
  are sometimes inconsistent with the understandings propagated by the HSM." (p. 274, folio not stated)
- **Funding politics as a constraint on the custodians.** Cooperation with the Balochistan government
  "creates an element of uncertainty within the conceptual design and limits the agency of the HSM, for
  example, by the difficulty of gathering funds for roads and shelters or a change in political
  patronage after an election" (p. 245, folio not stated). The fuller list of actors at the shrine: "the
  provincial government, the federal government, Hindu welfare organizations, authors, international
  clientele, television documentaries, and many others" (p. 247, folio not stated).

### The Dewani family, the priest, and the founder of the gathering

- **shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir** — **The custodial family, named.** "Hinglaj is the clan
  Goddess (Hin. kul'devī) of the Dewanis, a Sindhi Lohana family from Las Bela that has played an
  important role in organizing the event ever since the beginning of the institutionalized yātrā."
  (p. 274, folio not stated) "Virsimal Dewani is currently the pilgrimage's chief organizer and the
  face of the HSM in the media." (p. 274, folio not stated) "Ever since the inception of the HSM,
  Virsimal Dewani has been in charge of the organization and has thus played a central role in the
  Mandali." (p. 275, folio not stated) The clan is "currently responsible for many decisions made
  within the HSM" (p. 274, folio not stated). **No office title, no term of office, no formal
  appointment and no succession is given: (not stated).** He is elsewhere called only "the main
  spokesman of the HSM" (p. 295, folio not stated) and "one of the main HSM pilgrimage organizers"
  (pp. 208–209, folios not stated).
- **The family's authority and its transnational reach.** "the family's history and long relationship
  with the Goddess adds to the HSM's overall authority and is one reason for its extensive influence"
  (p. 276, folio not stated). Dewani "has ties to Bombay and Dubai where parts of his family reside,
  and he maintains connections with various travel agents in India and internationally who organize
  trips to the Balochistan temple" (p. 276, folio not stated); his "regular correspondence with various
  spiritual experts from India, in particular, raises his prestige in the eyes of many devotees"
  (p. 276, folio not stated). The ritual tie "requires even members living abroad to return to Pakistan
  for certain ceremonies" (p. 275, folio not stated). "the Dewani family's influential position is, to
  a certain extent, also linked to their upper-class (caste) background" (p. 276, folio not stated).
- **A dated change in the HSM's function, credited to him.** Dewani "changed the role of the HSM from a
  mere travel operator in the 1980s to an active designer of the tradition" (p. 275, folio not stated),
  consistent with "the beginning of the institutionalized melā in the 1980s" (p. 282, folio not stated).
- **The founder of the annual gathering, named once and never again.** "Even though the idea for an
  annual gathering at Hinglaj came from a Karachi devotee, Vasrup Chand Tusli, Virsimal Dewani was,
  from the beginning, significantly involved in the melā's establishment." (p. 275, folio not stated)
  **Tusli is given no dates, no caste, no office and no further appearance: (not stated).**
- **The priest, and how he got the job.** Gopal Maharaj is "the only permanent priest (Hin. pujārī) at
  the Hinglaj temple" (p. 46, folio not stated) and "the resident priest at the temple" (p. 48, folio
  not stated); he "gained his position as a pujārī through appointment by the local temple committee
  (the HSM) in 2008" (p. 47, folio not stated); he had served at the Makli temple in Thatta before
  Hinglaj (p. 253, folio not stated). **No lineage, caste, age, origin or predecessor: (not stated).**
- **The Muslim caretaker who preceded the committee, and the trading right his family kept.** "Only the
  descendants of Dost Mohammad, the Muslim caretaker at the shrine before the HSM gained its influence,
  were allowed to sell cigarettes and snacks inside the valley during the time of my fieldwork."
  (p. 219, folio not stated) **His dates, his term and the basis of his office: (not stated). This is
  the book's only statement of a pre-committee custodianship at the shrine and it is a Muslim one.**

### Money: fees, tickets, donations, state subsidy and building work

- **shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir** — **The HSM's fee history, in full, with every figure as
  printed.** "In the second half of the 1980s, the HSM started organizing tours and charged pilgrims 500
  rupees for transportation, boarding, and lodging per trip. Later, from the 1990s forward, the price
  was raised to 1,025 rupees for the whole trip." (p. 82, folio not stated) "However, those who traveled
  in their own vehicles still had to pay 525 rupees to the Mandali for their stay in Hinglaj." (p. 83,
  folio not stated) "This caused the HSM to completely cease their business of arranging domestic
  transportation in 2009 and instead charge 250 rupees per person for entry to the shrine during the
  annual festival in April (Hin. phālgun/caitra melā). They eventually quit charging this fee in 2013."
  (p. 83, folio not stated) A separate chapter note records that during the 1990s "this fee varied from
  year to year" and was "documented on the announcement sheets of that time" (p. 100, folio not
  stated) — **that note does not say which fee or how much: (not stated)**.
- **The fee converted, and its social weight.** "Since its establishment, the HSM has always charged a
  fee for the organization of the pilgrimage (transport, food, water, etc.)." (p. 219, folio not stated)
  "Notwithstanding the fact that 250 rupees (about €2 in 2012) might not seem a large sum, for simple
  peasants, this amount equals a day's wage for work in the fields." (pp. 219–220, folios not stated)
  "Since the wristbands are not transferable to others, families tend to send the male members inside
  with the funds they have available, leaving the female members outside of the pay-only area." (p. 220,
  folio not stated) And who could not pay at all: "many visitors attending the festival are peasants from
  Sindh who come along with their entire family, usually consisting of eight to ten people. For them, it
  was impossible to afford the entrance costs for all of their family members, so many tried to gain
  access without paying the fee." (p. 290, folio not stated)
- **The wristband itself.** "The new system forced visitors wanting to enter the main part of the valley,
  where the Kali and Hinglaj temple are located, to wear a plastic wristband called the bhag'vā paṭṭī
  (Hin. ochre ribbon). According to HSM officials, the main purpose of the nontransferable wristband was
  to prevent several pilgrims from sharing one ticket, a common practice in the past. The plastic
  wristband had to be purchased in the valley for 250 rupees." (p. 165, folio not stated) "the new system
  strictly required the display of the paṭṭī at two separate checkpoints for access to the western part
  of the valley." (p. 219, folio not stated) Many "mocked the way in which the HSM attempted to connect
  the selling of tickets to the traditional practice of the bhag'vā" (p. 209, folio not stated).
- **The revenue claim, from an endnote, and the suspicion it produced.** "One particularly interesting
  incident occurred in 2010 when the organization claimed to have sold forty thousand tickets. These
  sales would have generated revenues totaling 10 million rupees, about 85,000 Euros (2012). Many
  visitors wondered where this money had gone and argued that petrol and water alone could never have
  cost such a huge sum of money." (p. 317, folio not stated, note 15) **The arithmetic of 40,000 × 250
  rupees = 10 million rupees is internally consistent; the book's own euro conversions — €2 per ticket
  (pp. 219–220) against €85,000 for forty thousand tickets (p. 317, note 15) — do not quite agree, and
  both are recorded as printed.**
- **Accounting and its opacity, after abolition.** "Even its decision to put an end to the ticketing
  system could not stop the rumors about the proceeds the HSM had received before 2013; to this day,
  people still wonder aloud how the organization is suddenly able to manage the supposedly vast
  expenses." (p. 292, folio not stated) "When I inquired about how the HSM was able to deal with the
  deficit following the abolition of the ticket system, I received the answer that they now have donors
  supporting them. Nobody was willing to tell me who these donors were and how they are able to organize
  the necessary amount of money." (p. 292, folio not stated) **The donors are (not stated).**
- **Bribery, entered in the author's own voice as an endnote.** "I personally would add that a certain
  amount of money has to be distributed among local agents and the police to ensure a smooth course of
  events during the `melā`. I obviously have no direct statement about this from the HSM, which in
  general keeps quiet about such issues, but I myself witnessed situations in which bribes had to be
  paid to the local police." (p. 317, folio not stated, note 14) A witnessed instance on the route: "On
  one occasion, I witnessed a huge sum of money extracted from one of the tour organizations at a new
  checkpoint that was recently erected along the route… they demanded a comparatively large sum of money
  to let the bus pass." (p. 193, folio not stated, note 17) **No sum and no date: (not stated).** The
  checkpoint is placed "when approaching Chandrakup" (p. 291, folio not stated).
- **Donations, state subsidy and a costed building project.** "Over the years, the HSM has also become
  increasingly responsible for the distribution of large amounts of money received by the temple as
  donations or (until 2013) as fees. A significant amount is also allocated by the provincial
  administration, which subsidizes the construction of pilgrimage shelters and roads in the valley. In
  2012, the HSM supervised the construction of a bridge over the Hingol River with a total cost of 11
  crore Pakistani rupees (about €920,000), funded by the Balochistan government." (p. 84, folio not
  stated) "This capacity for overseeing the allocation of funds also adds to the HSM's local influence in
  Las Bela since they not only create jobs in the area—albeit with the help of the provincial
  government—but also possess significant contacts in the business communities of Las Bela and Karachi."
  (p. 84, folio not stated)
- **Who paid for the buildings — two accounts, side by side.** "many buildings inside the Hinglaj Valley
  were financed by the provincial government of Balochistan" (p. 226, folio not stated), and the Hingol
  bridge was "funded by the Balochistan government" (p. 215, folio not stated); against which the HSM
  says the temple's own reconstruction "was supported mainly by donations from the Hindu community"
  (p. 226, folio not stated). **Both are recorded; the book does not resolve them.**
- **Donor inscriptions exist in the valley but not in the temple.** "while many new pilgrimage shelters
  and water tanks inside the valley bear a visible inscription somewhere mentioning who or which
  organization donated the money for its construction, aside from the ambiguous inscriptions mentioned
  earlier in this chapter, no such signs are found inside the temple" (p. 249, folio not stated).
- **Commerce inside and outside the wall.** "Most shops and tea stalls are not allowed to be set up in
  the valley, and so there is a clear separation between the commercial area and the sacred area… In
  recent years, the commercial area outside the wall has expanded, and the HSM has had to clear out
  additional space for shops and parking lots." (p. 219, folio not stated) Ground at the melā: "many
  Hindu organizations and politicians approach the HSM to reserve a spot in the valley where they can set
  up stalls and stages. Hindu welfare organizations usually build small information stalls, and sometimes
  local politicians even sponsor dance and singing programs to reach out to the population and publicize
  their candidacy." (p. 272, folio not stated) **No fee, lease or rent is mentioned: (not stated).**
- **Liari's economy of the stop.** "Moreover, financial advantages in the form of donations and proceeds
  from the sale of coconuts and ochre-colored cloth caps at the Liari stop certainly also plays a major
  role in the HSM's attempt to keep the paṛāv a significant visiting site." (p. 209, folio not stated)
  "Today during the phālgun/caitra festival, this traditional bhag'vā is also worn in the form of an
  ochre-colored cap, which is sold by locals around the Liari temple." (p. 209, folio not stated)
- **chandragup-baba-chandragup** — **Chandrakup's economy.** "During the phālgun/caitra festival, however,
  locals offer the pilgrims lifts in trucks and off-road vehicle up to the foot of the main volcano to
  make some extra money." (p. 211, folio not stated) "In addition, locals immediately catch the coconuts
  with long poles and pull them out of the mud in order to resell them at the foot of the slope.
  Chandrakup volcano is generally used by many local Balochis to make some money by selling coconuts and
  tea or by begging for donations." (p. 214, folio not stated)
- **State money for minority temples, as a general mechanism the book names.** "The most important effect
  of such political participation has been an increase in government financial support for minority
  communities, for example, for the restoration or construction of churches, temples, and other community
  places. This is seen in a wave of restorations of temples across Sindh within the past decade. Since
  many minority ministers receive funding from the state, the best way to promote their political
  endeavors is to invest in community places such as temples. While in Sindh, I visited many temples that
  were renovated within the past decade, coinciding with changes in the political landscape." (p. 16,
  folio not stated) **No temple restored is named and no sum is given: (not stated).** The counter-report
  is printed in the same paragraph: "conversations with many Pakistani Hindus today, however, reveal a
  harsh criticism of their representatives in the national assembly who, in their opinion, are part of an
  elite group that has little contact with the communities' fundamental problems." (p. 16, folio not
  stated)
- **The historical pilgrimage tax, and the only early visitor estimate in the book.** "A 1907 Baluchistan
  Gazetteer (ed. McConaghey) mentions that at a Karachi outpost (possibly Sonmiani), the pilgrims were
  required to pay a tax of between 1 and 5 rupees, depending on their income and ability. The state
  earned around 600 rupees annually from these taxes, which would imply a rough average of two hundred
  visitors to the shrine yearly via the Sonmiani route." (p. 147, folio not stated) **The inference "two
  hundred visitors" is the author's own, drawn from the revenue figure; the Gazetteer is not quoted as
  giving it.** The same institution appears as living practice in 1940: "the yātrīs register their names
  and pay the pilgrimage tax" at Sonmiani (p. 140, folio not stated); and in 1946, "In Sonmiani, the
  group must report to the police station to pay their taxes for the pilgrimage. The officer in charge,
  however, is not present, and so they have to wait for a few days." (p. 128, folio not stated) **The
  amount, authority and legal basis in the 1946 episode are (not stated).**
- **The tax as a cause of route change, and a lesser pilgrimage to the port.** "Isthtiaq[text-layer?]
  [Ishtiaq] Ansari counts ten stops between Karachi and the port of Sonmiani (Ansari 2001, 29–32), where
  every pilgrim was required to pay a tax." (p. 113, folio not stated) "According to oral narratives,
  this tax at Sonmiani eventually caused many pilgrims to change their route, taking a more difficult path
  over the mountains of Sehwan in order to avoid the port (and the port tax)." (p. 113, folio not stated)
  "Apparently, there used to be a small yātrā only to Sonmiani, which was called choṭī māī (Joshi 2010,
  21)." (p. 113, folio not stated) The author's conclusion: "then, as now, pilgrimage routes to Hinglaj
  were often chosen not only according to particular religious motivations but also to accommodate time
  constraints, financial circumstances, and the physical condition of the pilgrims." (p. 113, folio not
  stated)
- **Payment in kind on the old route, a wage structure stated twice.** "For this service every pilgrim is
  bound to give one roṭī (Hin. bread) a day to the camel drivers. Pilgrims are also obliged to give one
  roṭī to those people who safeguard the drinking water in this barren environment (Shastri 1977, 14).35"
  (p. 139, folio not stated) The norm tested: "the starving guard demands not just one roṭī from the
  pilgrims, according to the tradition, but insists on having two from each of them (Shastri 1977, 26).
  The pilgrims refuse and leave the hungry man behind." (p. 140, folio not stated) A later guard is found
  dead of starvation and "according to tradition, they put some flour on the watchman's dead body and
  leave" (p. 141, folio not stated).
- **Other payments recorded without sums.** The `gurudān` to the ascetic at the Aghor (p. 131, folio not
  stated); the `dān-dakṣiṇā` to the chaṛīdār after the twig rite at the Hingol (p. 216, folio not
  stated); a Jain businessman financing a whole 1946 party and hiring a camel for an elderly woman
  (p. 127, folio not stated); and, in Hart's 1839 account of the rebirthing rite, ornaments surrendered
  so that "even the nose ring was obliged to be ransomed" (p. 250, folio not stated). **All (not
  stated) as to amount.**

### The Makran Coastal Highway, the N 25, and access

- **The road is the book's engine, and the claim is stated three times.** "the current struggle over the
  prevailing, or hegemonic, interpretation of the shrine is directly linked to its significant
  infrastructural changes" (p. 30, folio not stated); the highway of "the beginning of the new
  millennium" made the site "comfortably reachable by car" (p. 30, folio not stated); "the tradition's
  relatively recent dislocation—triggered (among other reasons) by the less arduous access—kindled a
  process in which many actors involved with the shrine started to reshape the various rituals and
  narratives at the site." (p. 29, folio not stated) Also: "With its recent dislocatory events—for
  example, the construction of a road—many powerful actors are currently in the process of producing an
  objective tradition at the site. An increasing institutionalization was conspicuously taking place."
  (p. 49, folio not stated) And: "The already briefly introduced institutional and infrastructural changes
  around the shrine rather caused a variety of sedimented objectivities from different traditions to
  collide." (p. 46, folio not stated)
- **The road's purpose and its politics.** "The project aimed to develop industries along the coastal
  areas and to link Pakistan's commercial metropolis, Karachi, with the ports of Omara, Pasni, and Gwadar
  in the far west of the country. With the help of the Chinese, the Pakistani government, under General
  Musharraf (in office 2001–2008), invested in the deep-water port at Gwadar, intending to develop it into
  a major commercial center at the entrance to the Persian Gulf (Lieven 2011, 343)." (p. 85, folio not
  stated)
- **Travel times before and after, as printed.** "Before the turn of the millennium, the journey from
  Karachi to Gwadar took two to three days and required cross-country vehicles, causing many to prefer the
  longer route via Quetta. After the construction of the road, the Hinglaj Valley, at the mouth of the
  Hingol River, became connected to urban Pakistan, making a trip from Karachi possible in just three and
  a half hours." (p. 85, folio not stated) "Today, buses run on a schedule connecting Karachi and
  Gwadar—and therefore—Karachi and the Hinglaj Valley." (p. 84, folio not stated) "From that time onward,
  various private companies increasingly assumed the transportation to the valley." (p. 83, folio not
  stated)
- **The earlier road, and what it did to Liari.** "In the early 1970s, the RCD Highway N 25 connecting
  Quetta with Karachi was completed. In the 1960s, the road had already been constructed up to the city of
  Bela, several kilometers north of Uthal, the present capital of the province (Khan Baloch 1970, 125)."
  (p. 202, folio not stated) "It was the construction of the N 25 between Karachi and Quetta in the 1960s
  that transformed Liari into an important site, even for pilgrims coming from the south traveling over
  Karachi." (pp. 207–208, folios not stated) "Liari was located in a strategic position, close to the N 25
  and at the beginning of the west-leading desert road, rendering the village an important organizational
  stop. At this juncture the means of transportation changed and scattered groups traveled to Hinglaj on
  camels or in off-road vehicle." (p. 208, folio not stated) Figure caption: "FIGURE 5.1 The journey from
  Liari village after the construction of a road between Quetta and Karachi" (p. 208, folio not stated).
  Then: "In 2001, after the MCH had been constructed up to the Hingol River, Liari lost its functional and
  strategic position as the last major settlement before the desert journey." (p. 209, folio not stated)
- **A roadside economy created by the traffic.** "The traffic on the much-traveled MCH triggered the
  opening of little stalls and petrol pumps that sell water and snacks on the side of the road. Not
  infrequently, cars also stop to offer the walkers drinking water." (p. 150, folio not stated)
- **Shelter-building follows the road.** "Nowadays, there is an increasing number of pilgrimage shelters
  inside the valley's confined area, almost all of which were built after the completion of the Makran
  Coastal Highway in 2001." (p. 220, folio not stated) "By 2009, many more shelters had been built in the
  area around this stop, and construction of additional buildings to accommodate the thousands of pilgrims
  was ongoing the last time I visited the shrine in 2015." (p. 221, folio not stated)
- **The generalisation the author draws.** "simple changes like the construction of train tracks, highways,
  and bus connections can lead to new flows of pilgrims and the construction of new imagined landscapes."
  (p. 110, folio not stated) And, on the ambivalence: "Over the past few years, the highway has been
  responsible for giving many people the chance to experience the shrine. On the one hand, the increasing
  number of visitors draws more attention to the Goddess and the various narratives at Hinglaj; on the
  other hand, such an accumulation of individuals, together with such smooth access to the shrine,
  contradicts themes of danger and exclusiveness in the pilgrimage experience as it is perpetuated in many
  narratives." (p. 190, folio not stated)
- **shah-noorani-shrine-syed-bilawal-shah-noorani** — **the same road argument applied to a second site.**
  Alongside the Hinglaj case the book names "a newly constructed road leading to the shrine" at Lahoot
  (p. 108, folio not stated). **That is the whole of the statement; no date, cost, builder or consequence
  is given: (not stated).** (See `## Misfiled` — Lahoot / Lāhūt Lā Makān is an archive row.)
- **Pre-road logistics, as reported from before Partition.** "Hopeful pilgrims often waited several weeks
  at the akhāṛā until a group of twenty to twenty-five people had assembled and camel drivers hired from
  Las Bela had arrived." (p. 206, folio not stated) "Abadhut also mentions that the Naths started small
  businesses around the akhāṛā since they could not make a living solely from guiding pilgrims to Hinglaj
  (Abadhut 1964, 1)." (p. 206, folio not stated) In the 1970s "the camels still had to be left at Ashapura
  while the pilgrims continued on foot since the remainder of the path was too dangerous for the animals
  (Khatri 1973, 24f.)" (p. 221, folio not stated).
- **The WAPDA dam and the state's retreat, dated.** "In 2008, the Balochistan Assembly abandoned a plan put
  forward by the Water and Power Development Authority of Pakistan (WAPDA) to build a dam in the Hingol
  River close to the MCH (Lakhavat 2011, 147–148). The dam would have flooded the accommodation roads to
  the temple and endangered the locality and its associated festivals. So the WAPDA initially suggested
  relocating three holy places to a higher elevation and guaranteed the construction of a new access
  road.20 This proposition was rejected by the HSM" (p. 217, folio not stated). "In 2008, the lawmakers in
  the Balochistan Assembly reacted to the concerns and protests of the Hindu community and asked the
  federal government to stop the project. In 2009, after a year of suspension, the WAPDA chose to continue
  with the controversial plans but decided to shift the site of the dam a few kilometers north in order to
  protect the temple. This resolution was in keeping with a consensus between the WAPDA, the Balochistan
  Assembly, and the Hindu community." (p. 217, folio not stated) **The three holy places are not named:
  (not stated).** The HSM's line against the dam is quoted: "This is a site of pilgrimage which is place
  specific." (p. 217, folio not stated)
- **Institutional names as the book gives them**: **HSM**, also "the Mandali", "the temple committee",
  "the temple organization" (pp. 209, 215, 219, 220, folios not stated); **MCH**, expanded once as "the
  Makran Coastal Highway" (p. 220, folio not stated); **WAPDA**, expanded as "the Water and Power
  Development Authority of Pakistan" (p. 217, folio not stated); **the Balochistan Assembly** and **the
  Balochistan police** (pp. 217, 218, folios not stated).

### The pilgrimage route and its halting places (paṛāv)

- **Terminology, with its two languages.** "These paṛāv (Hin. halting place, camp, stage of a march) or
  maqām (Urd. dwelling, station, halt) thus do not stand in isolation but rather form a part of a wider
  pilgrimage network that connects mytho-historic narratives with landscape." (p. 202, folio not stated)
  Also "asthāns (Urd.; here: places for pūjā)" (p. 202, folio not stated) and "akhāṛās (Hin. here 'place
  of assembly')" (p. 205, folio not stated).
- **The route's shrinkage, counted.** "Jethamalani writes that thirteen paṛāv and twenty-three places for
  pūjā (Skt. ritual worship) once comprised the traditional pilgrimage from Karachi to Hinglaj
  (Jethamalani 1994, 10)… He adds that today, pilgrims' time constraints have reduced this number of stops
  to just three paṛāv, which contain seven pūjās." (p. 202, folio not stated) The three-paṛāv list as
  printed: 1 Khari Nadi (a. Khari Nadi, b. Chandrakup); 2 Aghor River (a. The River Aghor, b. Ganesh);
  3 Ashapura (a. iśṭādev, b. Kali Mata, c. Hinglaj temple) (pp. 202–203, folios not stated). **The printed
  list carries seven lettered items, matching "seven pūjās".** The author disclaims it in advance: "This
  list is not exhaustive, but at least it provides a sense of the relationship between landscape, route,
  and the individual halting places in the area." (p. 205, folio not stated)
- **The general trend, stated as a finding.** "Even though an array of stops (paṛāv) along the route from
  Karachi to Hinglaj have been documented over the past two hundred years, a conspicuous decline in the
  quantity and complexity of these ritual stops has occurred over the past few decades. Yet over this same
  time period, certain localities have gained more elaborate ritual practices and have become increasingly
  central to the pilgrimage." (p. 260, folio not stated) And: "While some stops have gained increasing
  importance over the years (primarily those closer to the temple), others have lost significance or even
  slipped into complete oblivion." (p. 202, folio not stated)
- **The Khari stop, dropped — a dated worked example, and one of the sharpest institutional facts in the
  book.** "when Jethamalani published his book in 1994, transportation to the shrine was arranged mainly
  by the HSM. It is thus likely that many visitors in the 1990s visited that stop since at that time, the
  Khari paṛāv constituted a decisive part of the ritual journey, visible on the HSM's pilgrimage program.
  In that same year, however, the HSM stopped announcing the Khari stop, and the paṛāv vanished from the
  advertisements. According to one official, this was due to the fact that, by then, pilgrims were already
  traveling in off-road vehicle and no longer needed to bathe in the ocean to feel refreshed." (p. 204,
  folio not stated) The outcome: "Throughout the entire course of my fieldwork at the shrine—including
  hundreds of interviews about the yātrā, the sacred valley, and the shrine—not a single person mentioned
  the Khari stop or the Rama pūjā." (p. 204, folio not stated) **The official is not named: (not stated).**
  **Note the internal tension: Jethamalani's three-paṛāv list, quoted above from the same chapter, still
  heads its first paṛāv "Khari Nadi" (pp. 202–203, folios not stated). Both are recorded.**
- **The HSM as the institution that makes and unmakes stops.** "With the reinstitutionalization of the
  yātrā in the mid-1980s, however, this became the work of the HSM, which ultimately chose to highlight
  certain stops along the way and leave others out. This process is easily traceable through a study of
  the advertisements for the pilgrimage, which are printed by the HSM and describe the pilgrimage route to
  be followed in the year of publication." (p. 204, folio not stated)
- **Why the network was never canonical.** "The diverse descriptions and enumerations of important stops
  found in texts and oral traditions find their roots in the pilgrimage's long-standing lack of
  far-reaching institutionalization." (p. 204, folio not stated) "This association of certain
  motifs—here, the delay in the rising of bubbles and the renunciation of sins—with different places is
  paradigmatic for the pilgrimage network where, due to the lack of institutionalization, such attributes
  were for a long time not fully attached to certain places." (p. 213, folio not stated) And the loss:
  "without canonization, connections of topography and mytho-historic narratives associated with Hinglaj
  have gradually withered" (p. 123, folio not stated), with the qualification "The reasons for this shift
  cannot be fully reconstructed on the basis of the material available to me." (p. 123, folio not stated)
- **The modern route, stop by stop, as walked in 2012** (§4.3.1): Karachi (departure from the outskirts
  that year because of a police operation) → "the village of Hab Chawki" → "Vindar, a small village on the
  way" → "the next paṛāv in Liari" → Chandrakup → "Kanda Devi stop" → "the Hingol River" → the valley
  (pp. 159–160, folios not stated). "Their pilgrimage route does not deviate from the current most popular
  stops, which arose along with the construction of the MCH and together represent the widely accepted
  modern pilgrimage route (see Fig. 4.2)." (p. 157, folio not stated)
- **The 2010 cross-border itinerary, with the author's own film timecodes**: Swaminarayan temple, Karachi
  → Mata Bhawani temple, Thatta, offerings (11:00) → back to Karachi for the night → Liari, bhag'vā
  distributed (17:00) → hut on the Makran Coastal Highway, transfer to off-road vehicle → Chandrakup,
  coconut into the crater (23:00) → the shrine of Hinglaj, pūjā "until late in the night", news teams
  (29:30) → next morning Anil Kund, "the last purification bath" (33:00) → the garbha and circumambulation
  of the mūrti (34:30) → Karachi, "another day that was free for shopping in the nearby markets"
  (pp. 180–181, folios not stated). **The climax is named**: "It was obvious that the circumambulation
  through the tunnel in the mountain was the climax of the group's journey." (p. 181, folio not stated)
- **The old routes from Sonmiani, three of them.** "Wilford writes that from the town of Sonmiani onward,
  pilgrims had three options: one sea route, which was the least frequented; one route that followed along
  the shore, the path chosen by most pilgrims; and one inland route, traversable only with the help of
  camels (Wilford 1851, 243)." (p. 113, folio not stated) Wilford "not only describes the paths
  originating in Karachi but also includes routes from Thatta and even as far away as Bhuj. Between
  Karachi and the port of Sonmiani, the paths were fairly similar." (p. 113, folio not stated) A mixed
  route is also recorded: "some pilgrimage groups walked as far as Sonmiani and then took a boat from
  there to cross the bay (Wilford 1851, 250f.)" (p. 115, folio not stated). Wilford's informants included
  "a devotee from Thatta who must have already visited the site around 1770 (Wilford 1851, 233)" (p. 113,
  folio not stated).
- **The sea route, attested and contested.** "There are not many accounts of the sea route, which
  apparently took pilgrims up to the mouth of the Hingol River where they disembarked and continued on
  foot." (p. 114, folio not stated) "Abro, too, confirms that in the 'former days' (Sin. 'avā'ilī vaqtan
  meṉ') of the yātrā, the easiest way to reach the shrine was via the sea route." (p. 114, folio not
  stated) **Against that**: "most of my informants insisted that the most frequented route for pilgrims
  from Gujarat was the coastal route described above via the Rann of Kutch; this is confirmed in
  Kshatriya's writings (Kshatriya 2005, 75)." (p. 115, folio not stated) **A direct contradiction, kept
  side by side.**
- **The medieval archive, and what it is.** "Only a few records from the akhāṛās (Hin. wrestling ground,
  arena; here: pilgrim shelter) in the city of Thatta are able to shed some light on the practice in
  medieval times. Various Sindhi authors quote these annals as historical evidence of royal visits to the
  temple (Chhabria 2000; Jethamalani 1994; Ansari 2001)." (p. 112, folio not stated) "Ansari adds that in
  addition to these caravans, many pilgrims from Kutch and Jaipur used to visit the shrine (Ansari 2001,
  22)." (p. 113, folio not stated) The claim drawn from it: "an elaborate pilgrimage system to the shrine
  has existed at least since the fourteenth century" (p. 145, folio not stated).
- **The route was dictated by water, and camels navigated it.** "To find the proper route, the pilgrims
  must depend on the intuition of the camels, who play an important role as navigators.34 The camel
  guiders are able to find the way at night according to the position of the stars, but during the day the
  animals' sense of orientation is vital for finding the way and locating sources of necessary drinking
  water (Shastri 1977, 12)." (p. 139, folio not stated) "the westbound path toward Chandrakup proceeds
  mostly according to the location of wells in the area" (p. 140, folio not stated), with note 41 quoting
  "Gantavya vahī hai, jahāṁ kuāṁ mil jāye" (Shastri 1977, 25) and adding "Hart also mentions that the
  route followed the location of the wells (Hart 1839, 81)" (p. 148, folio not stated).
- **New stops generated by urban growth.** National Highway 25 shifted the start of many pilgrimages to
  Liari in Balochistan and caused the first resting places to be bypassed; Karachi's growth erased them
  (p. 121, folio not stated). "This urban growth in Karachi and in villages on the way to the shrine also
  led to the pilgrimage's further elaboration and even to the evolution of new `paṛāv`. Today, many walking
  pilgrims visit temples or Hindu neighborhoods on their way to Hinglaj to receive blessings and food
  donations." (p. 322, folio not stated) "Formerly important pilgrimage stops... have disappeared over the
  years due to the rapid swelling of the city." (p. 322, folio not stated)
- **Fixed dates and one prevailing route, as the mark of institutionalization.** "Also, the festivals are
  institutionalized and fixed to a certain time and date. While traditional paths were marked by
  insecurity, even to the extent that return from the journey was not a guarantee, now there is a certain
  sense of regulated mobility not only at the Hinglaj shrine but also on the journey there. This is visible
  in the solidification of different pilgrimage paths into one prevailing route following a certain set of
  chosen stops on the way." (pp. 84–85, folios not stated) Against the earlier variety: "Many different
  communities documented different kinds of mandatory rituals, routes, and places on the way." (p. 30,
  folio not stated) **No route is traced and no waystation is named in that sentence: (not stated).**
- **The roads shrank the sacred geography.** "While traditional travelogues and accounts of pilgrims
  walking to the shrine generally demonstrate an extended conception of the Goddess's sacred geography,22
  the disappearance of the old pilgrimage paths led to the elimination of many stops en route and
  subsequently also to a change in the scale and perception of Hinglaj's 'imagined landscape' (Eck 1998).
  This development reflects a general shift from the kinesthetic experience of the pilgrimage route to a
  more 'place-centered' or 'sedentary' tradition in which the valley and its localized rituals have become
  increasingly prominent." (p. 217, folio not stated)
- **Liari, being solidified for two reasons at once.** "Liari occupies an important place within the Lasi
  Lohana tradition and is now—at a time when the HSM is seeking to detach the pilgrimage from its mere
  sectarian roots—solidifying as a necessary stop on the pilgrimage." (p. 209, folio not stated) The
  historical judgement: "Judging from these historical accounts, it is evident that Liari was included in
  the southern route only fairly recently. This supposition is also supported by Samaur, who writes that
  pilgrims used to travel the road via Sonmiani, but currently people take the route via Liari (Samaur
  1999, 504f.)." (p. 207, folio not stated) The author explains the older silence as an artefact: "Since
  all of the sources mentioned above traveled to Hinglaj from Karachi, it comes as no surprise that they
  would not have considered the stop." (p. 207, folio not stated) **Note the spelling rule the book sets:
  "Here the village 'Liari' in Balochistan is referred to. The famous neighborhood in Karachi of the same
  name is spelled 'Lyari' to avoid confusion." (p. 192, folio not stated, note 3)**

### The walking practice: abandoned, then revived by the road

- **The rule, as the nineteenth century had it.** Goldsmid's elderly pilgrim "chose to walk because 'the
  journey to the shrine was, according to the rule, to be made on foot' (Goldsmid 1863, 190)" (p. 114,
  folio not stated); the century had "an implied requirement that the path to Hinglaj be taken on foot and
  through the desert" (p. 114, folio not stated).
- **The mechanism: austerity.** "The physical movement through the harsh desert is associated with
  atonement for one's sins and the notion of austerity, gathered in the wider sense in the term tapas or
  tapasyā." (p. 110, folio not stated) "Tapas, in fact, is closely linked to the concepts of heat, pain,
  and purification (Shee 1986, 193)" (p. 110, folio not stated). "the mobile, kinesthetic aspect of the
  journey through the desert serves as a necessary cleansing or testing process before pilgrims can receive
  the darśan from the Goddess (Kshatriya 2005, 65)" (p. 110, folio not stated). "means of transportation
  are often judged according to the degree of tapas/tapasyā or austerity they entail" (p. 117, folio not
  stated).
- **What the road did first.** "The journey's kinesthetic experience, for example, has been significantly
  changed by the construction of the MCH, which shaped a new pilgrimage route and created new rituals and
  patterns of mobility. On the one hand, the practice of walking to the temple has notably diminished
  because of bus and tour operators." (p. 150, folio not stated) "Because of the new road, the inevitably
  demanding and arduous journey by foot, ship, or, more recently by off-road vehicle, suddenly became
  optional for visitors and a matter of choice." (p. 85, folio not stated)
- **And then what it did second — the book's most counter-intuitive finding.** "But, as will be discussed
  in more detail later, the road has not only influenced the number of visitors at the shrine but has also
  triggered a renewed tendency toward the walking tradition. Unburdened by the scarcity of water and the
  lingering possibility of getting lost in the desert, many pilgrims have started to peregrinate along the
  highway itself to reach the shrine of Hinglaj." (p. 85, folio not stated) "The practice of walking to the
  shrine underwent a revival in the past decade as a result of the construction of the MCH." (p. 152, folio
  not stated) "Their path no longer cuts through the arid desert but simply leads along the highway almost
  directly up to the shrine. This route has proven advantageous as it eliminates two of the main problems
  that pilgrims once faced on the traditional route: the possibility of getting lost in the barren desert
  and the scarcity of water sources." (p. 150, folio not stated) "most of the walking pilgrims today began
  their practice of traveling on foot after the completion of the MCH. The infrastructure that developed
  along with the road, the easier access to water sources, and the reduced chance of getting lost on the
  way led to a revitalization of walking patterns." (p. 191, folio not stated)
- **It is a revival, not a survival, and the book is explicit.** "It is evident, however, that most people
  do not follow old family traditions when walking to Hinglaj but instead have only recently decided to go
  on the tour." (p. 152, folio not stated) Motives "frequently involve the fulfillment of wishes, certain
  vows, or, simply, wanderlust" (p. 152, folio not stated).
- **The Kohli walking group, its regime and its politics.** "The group started their daily walk at around
  2:00 a.m. and kept going until noon, when the heat became too difficult for them to bear. On the way,
  they stayed at temples or private houses and also in the courtyards of mosques where people often
  supplied them with food and water." (p. 154, folio not stated) They kept almost none of the traditional
  offices: the group "did not have an aguā or chaṛīdār, nor did they follow the practice of wearing the
  ochre-colored clothes that are donned by many at the Liari temple. There was no obvious ritual hierarchy…
  The group had no stern practice of penance, nor did they follow any particular codes of conduct. They
  also consumed meat, if they got the chance." (p. 156, folio not stated) "the men did not seem to be
  familiar with the concept of the chaṛīdār" (p. 154, folio not stated). **Walking read as caste
  self-assertion**: "See, the Lohanas come in their cars because they are not strong—they are too fat for
  walking! We work in the fields all day so we are strong, we are able to go on this journey, but they are
  too weak!" (p. 156, folio not stated), which the author reads as "a means by which the group defined
  itself and also undermined a more powerful community… the kinesthetic part of the journey represents
  strength and distinguishes the Kohlis from the physically weaker upper-caste Hindus." (p. 156, folio not
  stated) The structural background: "In Sindh, the Kohli community is mainly a disempowered scheduled
  caste whose members work as agricultural peasants on the fields owned by Sindh's feudal elite." (p. 154,
  folio not stated)
- **Walking as sociality, in the pilgrim's own words.** "For Putu, the walking practice is like a satsaṃg
  (Hin. congregation to sing devotional songs; a gathering of those seeking the truth), lifting up the
  hearts of the pilgrims." (p. 155, folio not stated) "The walking is also a place to meet [mi'lne kī jagah
  hai]. People get much more when they walk. This is our satsaṃg… Those who sit in their cars are actually
  trapped in them [gāṛī meṃ baṃd haiṃ]. We all help each other. We have the chance to serve each other
  [sevā kar'nā]." (pp. 155–156, folios not stated)
- **Where the salvific power sits — two readings, side by side in the same section.** "For them, it was not
  necessarily the salvific powers of the walking that were able to rid the pilgrims of their sin. Rather it
  was the power of the Mother's garbha… For Putu, the experiences on the way and the wanderlust that drove
  them were at the root of their walking." (p. 157, folio not stated) Against that, for Giresh "the
  kinesthetic aspect of the yātrā was the carrier for the journey's salvific powers" (p. 153, folio not
  stated). **The book does not choose between them.**
- **The Narayanpur walk, sized and dated.** It "started among the Gujarati community of Narayanpur in 2006
  and has since become an annual feature taking place a few days before the April melā"; the 2012 group was
  "about twenty-five male pilgrims… among them several youngsters between the ages of 12 and 16
  (Schaflechner 2012)" (p. 157, folio not stated). "The journey along the road is about 240 kilometers
  long; the group originally intended to cover the entire distance in six days but ultimately ended up
  taking twice as long. Some pilgrims also stopped walking due to exhaustion and instead rode on one of the
  many buses carrying people to the Hinglaj Valley in the days leading up to the melā." (p. 158, folio not
  stated)
- **The Hajj comparison, and what it is and is not about.** "this is our hajj" / "yah hajj hai hamārā" is
  "by far the most common answer to the question about why people have the incentive to travel… to the
  shrine" (p. 155, folio not stated). "This comparison, however, does not seem to relate to the mandatory
  aspect of the Hajj for devout Muslims but rather to its quality of redemption and cleansing of sins." The
  author grounds its availability in the public sphere: "because the Hajj is one of the most prominent
  forms of pilgrimage in Pakistan's public sphere, often mentioned, for example, in schoolbooks and
  television programs, the frequent comparative references to it are not surprising." (p. 155, folio not
  stated) "the epithet 'Hinglaji,' equivalent to the title 'Hajji,' is bestowed upon people who have
  successfully accomplished the journey" (p. 155, folio not stated).
- **The word used for the journey, and the word not used.** "In Pakistan, the term yātrā is used mostly by
  Hindu pilgrims. The Urdu equivalent, ziyārat, is rarely applied in conjunction with Hinglaj." (p. 146,
  folio not stated) **The book never uses the word ʿurs of anything at Hinglaj: (not stated).**

### The offices of the yātrā: aguā, chaṛīdār and the chaṛī

- **The office, hereditary and rotating, as the 1940 account has it.** "The pilgrim group is then led by the
  aguā (Hin. leader, guide), or the chaṛīdār (Hin. stick bearer), the spiritual head of the journey,
  traditionally belonging to the Nagnath clan (Shastri 1977, 4). Every year, the responsibility for guiding
  the pilgrims through the desert and orchestrating the ritual performances is assumed by a different man
  from one of the Nagnath families (Shastri 1977, 4)." (p. 138, folio not stated) Guides were also recruited
  locally: "Pilgrim groups were usually guided by aguās (Hin. guide; also aguvā) who originated from areas
  close to Hinglaj (Brohi 1998, 30)." (p. 113, folio not stated)
- **The chaṛī, its manufacture and its rule of precedence.** "These aguās also carry the chaṛī, a bar in the
  shape of a triśūla (Skt. trident), taken from the Hinglaj Valley and wrapped in yellow, ochre, and red
  cloth (Shastri 1977, 5). This chaṛī is a symbol for the Goddess and plays a central role during the ritual
  journey.31 So, for example, it is not allowed to travel in front of the one who carries it (chaṛīdār). The
  same is true even during the stops along the way at each paṛāv (Hin. holding place, camping ground), where
  the chaṛīdār sticks it in the earth. This indicates the point beyond which no one is allowed to go in the
  direction of the pilgrimage route. In addition, the chaṛī must be worshipped and honored by all the
  pilgrims." (p. 138, folio not stated) Note 31 quotes "Chaṛī kā bhī viśeṣ mahattva aur svarūp, hotā hai"
  (Shastri 1977, 5) (p. 148, folio not stated). The sticks are "cut from a tree in the Hinglaj Valley and
  had the power to protect the group on their dangerous journey, provided they properly worshipped it on the
  way" (p. 207, folio not stated). In the 1946 account: "Rupalal carries the chaṛī, a stick, which is
  meticulously worshipped and symbolizes the protection of the Goddess." (p. 127, folio not stated) Its
  formula is in note 4, untranslated by the book: "'Is chaṛī ke prasādāt—arthāt is kī avahel'nā nahīṃ kar'ne
  se ham logoṃ kī yātrā nirvighna pūrī hogī' (Abadhut 1964, 6)." (p. 193, folio not stated) **No translation
  of that formula is printed: (not stated).**
- **The office's other duties, then.** The aguā's authority in the desert: "the aguā tells them they must
  reach the next resting spot or else perish in the desert" (p. 129, folio not stated). Journey's end is
  marked materially: "Rupalal digs the chaṛī into the earth to indicate the journey's completion (Abadhut
  1964, 201)" (p. 131, folio not stated). At Chandrakup the augury "was once done by the chaṛīdār and is
  done today either by someone from the HSM or any ritual expert traveling with the group" (p. 212, folio not
  stated); the chaṛīdār also read whether sins were forgiven (Shastri 1977, 41) (p. 213, folio not stated),
  and received the dān-dakṣiṇā at the Hingol (p. 216, folio not stated).
- **The office dissolving, stated as a finding.** "Whereas before the construction of the road, pilgrim
  groups needed a clearly appointed leader with substantial knowledge of the surroundings, today, a
  formalized hierarchy is no longer necessary." (p. 191, folio not stated) "The `aguā`'s formerly critical
  role as a source of life-saving knowledge has however devolved into a peripheral one today, as the road
  leads devotees directly to the shrine without the assistance of a guide." (p. 322, folio not stated) The
  author calls the result a "democratization... which led to new dynamics among walking groups in which each
  member bears more responsibility for the overall welfare of the group" (p. 322, folio not stated). And for
  one group specifically: "Thus, among the Narayanpur pilgrims at least, the role of the aguā has effectively
  dissolved, shifting toward a wider distribution of power among the elders of the group." (p. 162, folio not
  stated)
- **The rules recorded, and then recorded as not kept.** As the Narayanpur group described it beforehand:
  "the pilgrim group is guided by one or two chaṛīdārs who carry with them a chaṛī, a walking stick shaped
  like Shiva's trident, the triśūla." (p. 160, folio not stated) The chaṛīdār "must walk a few hundred meters
  ahead of the group and is responsible for the community's general well-being… Raj Kumar is the pilgrims'
  representative, accepting donations of food and water given to the group along the way." (p. 160, folio not
  stated) In the event: "Raj Kumar did not carry the chaṛī in his hand, nor was he walking in front of the
  others." (p. 160, folio not stated) "Raj Kumar also did not become the chaṛīdār of the group through some
  kind of ritual initiation, but, as he himself said, he became so simply because of the 'order of the
  Mother' (mātā kā hukm)." (p. 161, folio not stated) **The author's method note against his own informants**:
  "It was obvious that the pilgrims had given a very traditional description of their journey to me
  beforehand, which was, however, not sustained along the way." (p. 160, folio not stated) The practice is
  "loosely modeled on codes of conduct adhered to in previous journeys described by authors such as Shastri
  (1977) and Hart (1839)" (p. 160, folio not stated).
- **The modern aguā is a fixer.** "In representations of the traditional pilgrimage introduced above, the
  aguā served as the group's leader who provided important ritual and geographical know-how that guaranteed
  the journey's success. Similarly, the modern aguās are responsible for the well-being of the pilgrims, but
  today they must also provide bureaucratic connections and the political sophistication necessary for
  leading groups over the border." (p. 183, folio not stated)
- **The cart that replaced the stick.** "Modern-day walkers rearticulate common elements associated with the
  yātrā—such as the chaṛī that was once the centerpiece of ritualistic attention—and furnish them with new
  meaning. Instead of designating one person as macebearer—the chaṛīdār—today, for example, the Narayanpur
  pilgrims jointly push a cart that serves as the center of their ritual practice." (p. 191, folio not stated)
  "Two chaṛīs, instead of being carried by the leader as in the traditional accounts, are instead attached to
  the two front corners of the cart." (p. 164, folio not stated) "The primary position of the chaṛī is
  substituted by the central position of the cart." (p. 164, folio not stated)
- **The rath (cart), its contents and its economy.** "The ornamented cart contains a 30-centimeter tall mūrti
  (Hin. idol, statue) of Sheravali Mata (literally 'the Mother with the lion') and a picture of the Goddess,
  the oldest the Narayanpur Gujarati community possesses. Before the framed print, there is a small glass
  cabinet containing a flame from the Santoshi Mata temple in Narayanpur… The rath is painted ochre and bears
  ochre-colored flags on each of its four corners. Ochre is traditionally the color that pilgrims wear for the
  journey and so resembles the practices described in older accounts (Khatri 1973, 19)." (p. 163, folio not
  stated) "Inside the cart, the yātrīs also store their food, which consists mostly of dāl, some vegetables,
  and rice. Many within the multireligious neighborhood of Narayanpur consider donating food for this journey
  to be spiritually beneficial and thus give provisions irrespective of their religious affiliation. The rath
  also contains a small generator that produces light for walking during the night and electricity for a small
  amplifier that plays devotional songs." (p. 164, folio not stated) "The Narayanpur pilgrims insist that the
  long and demanding journey fills the cart with the blessings of the Goddess." (p. 164, folio not stated)
  "This role as an alternative temple cannot be filled by just any kind of religious object; it requires use of
  an entity associated with an exceptional practice, like the pulling of the cart from Karachi." (p. 166, folio
  not stated)
- **The rath used against the ticket — a documented act of resistance.** "One attempt to resist this ticketing
  system came from the Narayanpur yātrīs who positioned the rath on the way to the ticket checkpoint. They
  explained that by putting the cart there, those who were not able to pay to enter the inner part of the
  valley could still receive a genuine darśan from the cart's mūrti." (p. 165, folio not stated) The author's
  reading: "By offering the cart to those who were not able to afford tickets into the melā, the Narayanpur
  yātrīs thereby extended the blessings of the Goddess—gained from their own physical efforts—to other pilgrims
  whose plans to enter the valley had been thwarted by the ticketing system. In this way, the Narayanpur
  pilgrims directly challenged the monopoly of the HSM as the sole access point to the Goddess until the
  ticketing system was aborted in 2013." (p. 166, folio not stated)
- **The departure rite.** "The night before the group leaves Karachi, the chaṛīdār Raj Kumar organizes a
  jāg'raṇ, a religious wake in the local Ram Pir temple… The departure of the cart is initiated with the
  cracking of a coconut and a small ceremony attended by many in the neighborhood. In 2012, the group intended
  to leave after the jāg'raṇ at 4:00 a.m., but because of the neighborhood gunfire and shooting mentioned
  above, the group was forced to postpone their departure. Usually, influential local people are present at
  this occasion to bid the group farewell." (p. 163, folio not stated)

### Oaths, purity rules and codes of conduct on the road

- **The oath at the River Hab, in four clauses (Shastri, 1940).** "the ritual journey begins with an oath by
  each of the pilgrims: Until they return once more to this river following the pilgrimage, they will not
  engage in any sexual activities; they will help each other; they will avoid thoughts of envy, hate, or
  blame; and under no circumstances will they let anyone drink from their own water jar (Hin. surāhī). The
  oath is accompanied by the tying of an ochre-colored piece of cloth around the head of each pilgrim
  (Shastri 1977, 11)." (p. 139, folio not stated)
- **The same oath in three clauses (Abadhut, 1946).** "There the group gives an oath to an old priest (prauṛh
  paṃḍā) who ties an ochre-colored piece of cloth around their heads and makes them swear that they will
  refrain from any violence, that they will help each other, and that under no circumstances will they give
  any other pilgrim water from their own water bottle (surāhī)." (p. 127, folio not stated) **Three clauses
  against four, and the two are kept side by side.** The oath is broken almost at once: Bhairavi "sprinkles
  water on their faces, breaking the oath given just a few days earlier at the river's edge (Abadhut 1964,
  20)" (p. 127, folio not stated).
- **The bhag'vā, and where it is now put on.** Shastri has the journey begin at the Hab River, "where the
  pilgrims say an oath and put on an ochre-colored piece of cloth (Shastri 1977, 11)" (p. 207, folio not
  stated). Today: "pilgrims coming from the south also put on the ochre-colored piece of cloth in Liari, which
  previously was done at the River Hab. This cloth, called the bhag'vā (Hin.; in Urd. bhagvā), was tied around
  the head or worn around the neck (Khatri 1973, 19).7 It assured the yātrīs the Goddess's protection from
  wild animals or other obstacles along the way (Jethamalani 1994, 7)." (p. 208, folio not stated) The
  organiser's gloss: "The bhag'vā means that the pilgrims become Yogis and ascetics at this place [Liari].
  When they wear the bhag'vā, they leave the mundane world behind. It is actually a sign of the faqīrs [ . . .
  ]. Here their pilgrimage starts—this is their first stop [paṛāv]. This means that they now focus completely
  on the pilgrimage and that they are protected from animals like snakes and scorpions [ . . . ]. By wearing
  the bhag'vā they receive a protective covering [kavac lag jātā hai]." (p. 209, folio not stated, note 8)
  "the pilgrims received the bhag'vā there, the saffron-colored piece of cloth that serves as a sign of the
  Goddess's protection on the way" (p. 181, folio not stated).
- **Purity and caste on the route, twice tested in one account.** Jivraj "hits her and yells that she must not
  spoil her dharma (Shastri 1977, 17). Jivraj explains that they are from the Pushtimarg Vaishnava sect, which
  does not allow the consumption of onions." (p. 139, folio not stated) By the next day, "all of the Gujarati
  Vaishnavas start to leave their sectarian constraints aside and ask Shastri for onions, too" (p. 139, folio
  not stated). And at the well: "The pilgrims are afraid of becoming impure by touching the body of a dead
  Muslim. In the end, only Shastri and the two Muslim cameleers stay and bury the corpse in the desert
  (Shastri 1977, 34)." (p. 141, folio not stated)
- **Ritual purity as a live dispute inside a party.** Jayashankar "becomes increasingly upset with the
  pilgrimage because, according to him, the rules of ritual purity are not being followed. He complains, for
  example, that it is not proper to do a darśan at one of the many holy places along the way without first
  taking a bath. Because this is not possible in the desert, he is upset at the loss of some of the potential
  spiritual merit to be gained (Abadhut 1964, 50)." (p. 128, folio not stated)
- **Death on the route and the rites that cannot be done.** "the group is unable to perform any death rituals
  for the Brahmin, so instead, they simply bury him in the hard desert ground (Abadhut 1964, 91)." (p. 129,
  folio not stated)
- **Rules actually observed on the road today.** "The only rule that all of the yātrīs seemed to follow was
  that no one consumed any meat or fish on the journey. However, many smoked a substantial amount of gāṁjā
  (Hin. marijuana), which they considered necessary to appease Shiva. The older men in the community also had
  some whiskey with them, which they drank as the Goddess' prasād (Hin. food offered to an idol)." (p. 160,
  folio not stated) Meat resumes only at the end: "After cooling down the chaṛī in Thatta, the pilgrimage ends,
  and the pilgrims are allowed to eat meat again." (p. 165, folio not stated) Marijuana is reported without
  comment in the older account too: at a local's house Thirumal "even starts to talk and smokes some marijuana
  with the other pilgrims (Abadhut 1964, 124)" (p. 130, folio not stated).
- **The two signboards inside the valley, and what they do.** "Specific guidelines are suggested by two
  signs—one in English and one in Urdu—that lay out the 'do's' and 'don'ts' of the sacred geography." (pp.
  217–218, folios not stated) "These rules reflect some of the traditional behaviors required of pilgrims on
  their way to Hinglaj (Shastri 1977, 11) but in some ways also appear to respond to new challenges that
  evolved out of the temple's increased accessibility." (p. 218, folio not stated) "Although they are only
  suggestive in nature, the boards still indicate the existence of particular 'ideal' behaviors and practices
  that are not required outside the walls of the confined area." (p. 218, folio not stated) "Since the Urdu
  version does not deviate from the English, I only quote the English sign here" (p. 284, folio not stated).
- **shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir** — **The nine posted rules, dated 2011, as the book prints them**
  (p. 284, folio not stated): care for "the sanctity of Mata Mandir"; "When witnissing [sic] Mata take care of
  other pilgrims"; abstain from "creating a nuisance in the premises of Mata Mandir"; "Drinking alcohol is
  prohibited in the premises of `Mata Mandir offenders`[text-layer?] [Mata Mandir. Offenders] shall be handed
  over to the police."; "Eating of any kind of meat and fish are [sic] prohibited"; "Mata Mandir is open only
  for the purpose of paying homage to Mata."; "Don't come here for picnicking as Mata Hinghlaj [sic] Mandir is
  a sacred pilgrimage place"; "Please don't litter"; "Please cover your head when entering Mata Mandir."
  **Both `[sic]` marks are the book's own.**
- **Two unposted rules, and the police as enforcer.** "the HSM also propagates two additional rules not
  mentioned on the board that are enforced by volunteers and the Balochistan police during the gathering days:
  the ban on animal sacrifices at the shrine of Kali Mata… and the official prohibition on Muslims entering the
  valley during the April festival." (p. 284, folio not stated) **No department, order or legal instrument is
  named: (not stated).**
- **The alcohol ban and its date.** "the strict banning of alcohol—a rule that emerged around the beginning of
  the millennium when the crowds started to swell" (p. 281, folio not stated). The rest of the printed
  guidelines "mainly address organizational issues… the parking of cars, the setting up of stalls for
  commercial purposes, and the mandatory display of identification cards during the festival" (p. 281, folio
  not stated).
- **The head-covering rule, traced to a neighbouring tradition with the author's own hedge kept.** "The
  practice of covering one's head before entering a sacred area might be influenced by practices of the Nanak
  paṃth. Many upper-caste Hindus in Sindh, especially from the Lohana community, refer to Nanak and his
  teachings (Thakur 1997, 141)… This rule thus provides one of the most conspicuous marks left by the Lohana
  tradition upon Hinglaj and simultaneously is the one least followed during the festival." (p. 286, folio not
  stated) **"might be influenced" is the author's own hedge and is kept.** The model named is "the tikānā
  (Sin.)… the temple containing the Guru Granth Sahib, the holy book of Sikhism" (p. 286, folio not stated) —
  **a building type, not a particular building: (not stated).**
- **Rule-making as a method, in the author's summary.** "for some years now, the HSM has perpetuated a set of
  rules and regulations during the annual festivals with the goal of fashioning a certain behavior among
  visitors. According to the organization's spokespersons, these regulatory norms serve merely as suggestions
  and are never strictly enforced." (p. 267, folio not stated) "Within this process, certain ritual practices
  and narratives are singled out and either condemned as improper Hindu practices or emphasized as proper
  ones." (p. 267, folio not stated) The practices opposed are named: "low-caste rituals in Sindh such as the
  sacrifice of animals or the consumption of alcohol as prasād (Hin. remnants of food offered to an idol)"
  (p. 278, folio not stated); more broadly "many rituals and performances that are generally linked with the
  'superstitious' practices of Pakistan's lower castes and tribes (Hin. jāti; Urd. qaum)" (p. 277, folio not
  stated).
- **The free community kitchen as an instrument of the food rule.** "With the help of the free community
  kitchen (Urd. bhanḍārā), the HSM exerts direct influence on food consumption and therefore, according to
  widespread Hindu theories on the relationship between diet and the constitution of the human mind/body, on
  the amount of tamas (Hin.) present in pilgrims during the festival." (p. 285, folio not stated) **Who funds
  or runs the bhanḍārā, and what it serves: (not stated).**
- **The survey that undercuts the vegetarian rule.** Note 12: "I handed out questionnaires to one hundred
  people, from which I received eighty-nine back. All of the devotees I talked to claimed that they follow the
  rule of keeping a vegetarian diet in the valley, even though more than two-thirds indicated they consumed
  meat at home." (p. 316, folio not stated, note 12) **The author qualifies his own instrument**: those
  answering in conversation "answered affirmatively to the question of whether they ate meat at home", while
  in the unattended written questionnaires "the respondents always stated they were vegetarians" (p. 316, folio
  not stated, note 12).

### Rites at the shrine: the garbha, the ponds, the havan

- **shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir** — **The rebirthing ritual is the pilgrimage's core, and the book
  says so.** "The primary feature of the journey to Hinglaj and the pilgrimage's sine qua non ritual is the
  crawling through a narrow U-shaped tunnel underneath the image of the Goddess, called the garbha (Hin. womb,
  lap), or sometimes the garbhaguphā (Hin. 'womb-cave'). This circumambulation is said to be a rebirthing of
  the devotee out of the body of the Devi." (p. 247, folio not stated) "Some reports also show that after
  coming out of this tunnel, devotees used to roll on the floor and make sounds like a newborn as part of the
  ritual (cf. Wilford 1851 and Jethamalani 1994)." (p. 247, folio not stated) "In most cases, however, the
  ritual is associated with purification and the repentance of sins." (p. 247, folio not stated) Earlier
  statement of the same rite: pilgrims "crawl through a small tunnel, signifying the womb of the Goddess from
  which they are born again"; entry is conditional on "a rigid pattern of purification and worship" (p. 57,
  folio not stated).
- **Access control at the garbha.** "The doors leading into the tunnel are usually locked, and visitors must
  ask the pujārī or the volunteer workers at the shrine to have them opened. At the time of the major melā,
  the doors are only open for a certain period of time in the middle of the night." (p. 248, folio not stated)
  The tunnel was extended after the late 1980s (p. 251, folio not stated).
- **Hart's 1839 account of the rite, as quoted.** "At midnight all the women and children were directed to
  bathe in the pool … sent two by two, the right hand of one sister being placed on the leg of the other, on
  their hands and knees into the narrow opening in the rock under the platform. On coming out on the opposite
  side they again bathed, and then resumed their clothes." (p. 250, folio not stated, citing Hart 1839, 97)
  And the earliest description of the circumambulation: Hart's raised earthen platform "'between which and the
  rock is a narrow passage forming the segment of a circle' … This is the earliest available description I
  found of the practice of parikramā (Hin. circumambulation) underneath the image and around the earthen
  platform." (pp. 249–250, folios not stated)
- **The purification bath at Sharan Kund, and its lapse.** Three submersions with a chosen `śaraṇ'bhāi/bahan`
  before entering the garbha (p. 251, folio not stated); the bath "is frequently mentioned in literature
  (Samaur 1999, 506; Joshi 2010, 22; Jethamalani 1994, 8)" (p. 252, folio not stated). "The gradual drying up
  of the pond and the subsequent ceasing of the purification practice in the pool first triggered efforts by
  the HSM to prevent its desiccation." (p. 252, folio not stated) By 2011 the site was concreted over (p. 251,
  folio not stated).
- **The sesame rite at Til Kund, with its arithmetic of purity.** "The main ritual practice at the Til Kund
  consists of the washing of til (Hin.), black sesame seeds that pilgrims either bring themselves or receive
  from the pujārī. After the pūjā at Hinglaj and the subsequent spiritual rebirth out of the Goddess's womb,
  the yātrīs travel on to Til Kund." (p. 254, folio not stated) "According to the percentage of seeds that
  have turned white, the pilgrim can determine the extent to which his or her soul has been purified and how
  much still remains stained. So, for example, if a quarter of the seeds stay black, the pilgrim's soul has
  now become 75 percent pure." (p. 254, folio not stated) The rite is repeatable, including a second rebirth:
  "The pilgrims may also return to the Mother's garbha to be reborn yet again." (p. 254, folio not stated)
- **The eye-cure at Kir Kund.** "the devotee seeking healing is supposed to stand beneath the ledge and allow
  the drops to fall directly into his or her eyes" (p. 254, folio not stated). The healing water at Kali Kund:
  "Here the pilgrims are supposed to step into the water and drink from the chaśmah (Urd. spring). This water
  is said to heal all kinds of diseases." (p. 255, folio not stated)
- **The ascent to Anil Kund as required labour — and a direct contradiction between two traditions, kept side
  by side.** "In the local Sindhi (Lohana) tradition, pilgrims must ascend to Anil Kund after they have
  emerged from the Goddess's lap in order to successfully complete their yātrā (Jethamalani 1994, 8)."
