  (pp. 255–256, folios not stated) In the Gujarati tradition the order is reversed: "one must bathe in its
  waters to purify the body before proceeding to enter the Goddess's lap" (p. 256, folio not stated). **The
  book does not reconcile them.** The climb: "only accessible through a long, two-and-a-half-hour ascent by
  foot which, due to the burning heat during daylight hours, is often started before sunrise." (p. 255, folio
  not stated)
- **A substitute rite for those who cannot climb.** Seven circuits of the boulder: "if they were unable to
  reach Chaurasi, then they should instead make seven circuits (Hin. cakkar lagānā) around the stone and would
  thereby gain the same merit as if they had taken a bath there" (p. 259, folio not stated). "Nowadays,
  elderly and handicapped people who are unable to make the arduous climb to Chaurasi use the stone as a proxy
  to acquire a similar benefit as from a bath in the mountain pond." (p. 259, folio not stated)
- **The havan, the melā's climax, and crowd pressure as the driver of building.** "Over the past few decades,
  the havan ceremony has become the climax of the phālgun/caitra melā, with thousands of visitors attending.
  Due to the ceremony's popularity, the inner temple could no longer accommodate the crowds, requiring the
  construction of the havan kuṃḍ outside." (p. 233, folio not stated) **No absolute visitor count and no melā
  date are given in that passage: (not stated).**
- **Rites at the River Hingol.** "Historically, as well as today, pilgrims take their last bath in the Hingol
  before they enter the valley of Hinglaj. Today, depending on the water level, this is often only a symbolic
  gesture in which the face is washed or some water is sprinkled on the forehead." (p. 216, folio not stated)
  "The riverbank is also used by male and female devotees for the pitr̥tarpaṇ (Hin. the ancestor worship)
  (Abadhut 1964, 197), and many come here to shave their heads as a sign of mourning after a family member has
  died (Abro 1991, 58)." (p. 216, folio not stated) The twig rite: "the pilgrims use the nīm or babūl twig
  (Hin. dātun) to clean their teeth after they have bathed in the river (Shastri 1977, 51). After this, they
  give a donation (Hin. dān-dakṣiṇā) to the chaṛīdār… After the donation, the twig is then dug into the earth
  on the other side of the Hingol." (p. 216, folio not stated) Abadhut's variant uses two twigs (Abadhut 1964,
  196) (p. 216, folio not stated); the earlier account has two nīm (Lat. Azadirachta indica) twigs broken at
  the Hingol, "One they use to clean their teeth before crossing the river, and the other must be planted
  there at the shore" (p. 142, folio not stated). Kshatriya's variant makes it an oracle of partnership: "Each
  pilgrim picks a twig and digs it into the earth while saying the name of his or her chosen śaraṇ'bhāī. On the
  way back from the tīrtha, the pilgrims must pass by the twigs again. Only when the twigs have started to
  blossom can the pilgrim be sure that his or her choice of śaraṇ'bhāi has been accepted by the Goddess
  (Kshatriya 2005, 77–78). This concept of the śaraṇ'bhāī is not bound to any specific gender constraints and
  is often taken very seriously by the pilgrims." (p. 216, folio not stated)
- **Lohana life-cycle rites at the river.** "The Hingol is also of special importance for certain parts of the
  Lohana community that perform the ceremonies of the jane'o (Urd.), the bestowing of the sacred thread, and
  the munḍan pāṭh (Urd.), the shaving of the head, at the shore of the river (Jethamalani 1994, 7). Many Sindhi
  Lohana communities wear this string of cloth (Burton 1851, 315), and some perform a ceremony at the River
  Hingol for young males being initiated into the community. The age of those to be initiated is not fixed, and
  many young Lohanas undergo the ceremony just before their wedding." (p. 216, folio not stated) "In general,
  however, most members of the Lasi Lohana community perform their rituals at the River Hingol. Similar to
  other paṛāv, so too at the Hingol it is mainly the ritual practice of the Lasi Lohana community that is
  promoted by the HSM and that increasingly prevails among visitors." (p. 216, folio not stated)
- **chandragup-baba-chandragup** — **The vigil and the confession of sins at the volcano.** "The night before
  ascending Chandrakup, the group stays awake (Hin. jāg'raṇ), fasting and meditating on the sins they will
  confess at the rim of the crater. At the top, the yātrīs first have to introduce themselves with their full
  name and place of origin and then call out their sins in front of the group." (p. 213, folio not stated)
  "Nowadays, there are no such strict regulations at the volcano, and it is impossible to verify the extent to
  which restrictions were actually enforced in the past. Furthermore, during the phālgun/caitra festival,
  pilgrims, eager to continue on their journey, do not have the time to wait for their offerings to sink into
  the mud (even if its consistency would allow it on that day)." (pp. 213–214, folios not stated) The author's
  general verdict on the site: "As with many stops along the pilgrimage route, there exist differing narratives
  concerning the volcano, and a consistent practice or interpretation at Chandrakup is missing." (p. 212, folio
  not stated) Against that, continuity is argued from Hart: "Captain Hart tells of a similar ritual performance
  at the rim of the volcano from the middle of the nineteenth century, which hints at a somewhat coherent
  practice." (p. 212, folio not stated) **Both stand.**
- **The temple's fabric, as the travellers described it before the rebuilding.** Hart describes the image as
  resting on a 20 foot by 12 foot "mud edifice" (p. 250, folio not stated, citing Hart 1839, 95) and the temple
  as "a mud edifice that contained two rooms in which music was played", with "a doorway [that] opened to
  another room where the image of Hinglaj was found" (p. 249, folio not stated, citing Hart 1839, 95).
  Goldsmid: "The shrine was on a kind of raised mud platform, perhaps three feet from the level." (p. 250,
  folio not stated, citing Goldsmid 1863, 208); it lacked "architectural magnificence or beauty" and the wooden
  construction "appeared as something that 'an infant taste for architecture would create out of wooden
  toy-bricks'" (p. 234, folio not stated, citing Goldsmid 1863, 207); "in the middle of the nineteenth century,
  [it] consisted of two rooms" (p. 234, folio not stated). Khan Baloch on the 1961 fabric: he "refers to the
  architecture of the temple and the platform (Sin. chap) inside, which had already been lined with timber by
  the time of his visit. Atop the platform were scattered several instruments used by the pilgrims for pūjā;
  Baloch estimated them to be around half a century old (Khan Baloch 1970, 132)." (p. 240, folio not stated)
  Abro on the pre-renovation liṅgas: "He mentions that on one side, six or seven liṅgas were kept, and that
  another one, about a foot tall, was located at the base of the platform, which is called 'sadā śivo' (Sin.)
  (Abro 1991, 62f.)." (p. 241, folio not stated)
- **The renovation as an act of interpretation, not maintenance — the book's central claim about the fabric.**
  "the manner in which the HSM carried out the renovations significantly changed the way visitors are able to
  approach the image, which has subsequently also influenced ritual practices at the shrine" (p. 229, folio not
  stated); "the changes around the mūrti are paradigmatic for the politics of 'Hinduization' around the
  construction of religious space at Hinglaj (see also Chapter 6)" (p. 236, folio not stated). "the HSM felt
  the need to change the image in a way that would lessen its ambiguity. Today, the temple organization
  interprets the refurbishing work as a way to crystalize the 'actual' practice at the shrine. Due to the
  svayaṃbhū characteristic of the central image, however, work at the sanctum is often downplayed publicly."
  (p. 244, folio not stated) The stated purpose, in the HSM's own account: "the constructional changes made
  around the image have been done with the purpose of making the worship (read: the ritual practices) of the
  Goddess easier." (p. 245, folio not stated) The eyes and noses "according to the organization, are intended
  to help the devotee approach the divinity in a proper way and allow his or her gaze to wander in a certain
  direction" (p. 245, folio not stated).
- **Two conflicting reasons for removing the liṅga, one official and one private.** "The HSM's official
  reasoning for the choice is that the liṅga caused an obstruction for pilgrims to reach the shrine; however,
  one elderly HSM volunteer from Karachi told me that the liṅga had to be removed since it was a very graphic
  image of a penis that had disturbed the visitors" (p. 238, folio not stated). The removed liṅga was thrown
  into Sharan Kund (p. 238, folio not stated).
- **What the rebuilding backgrounded.** "the shape of the rebuilt sanctum suggests that those parts that did
  not benefit the prevailing Hindu interpretation of the image—or the HSM's version of it—were backgrounded,
  while others that are more in concert with this concept were foregrounded. Through this process, the physical
  shape of the oblong stone, described in older accounts and frequently interpreted as the top of a grave, was
  made less prominent." (p. 236, folio not stated) "These descriptions of the sanctum as 'tomb shaped' or as a
  'Muslim grave' do not appear in current accounts of the image, where such alternative interpretations are no
  longer present." (p. 237, folio not stated) **The dating of the image is declared impossible**: "such changes
  have rendered accurate assessments of the figure's age all but impossible" (p. 232, folio not stated); the
  author reads travelogues and "a few old photographs" (p. 232, folio not stated) and concludes the image
  "actually had a different shape just a few decades ago" (pp. 232–233, folios not stated). **And a
  self-correction against his own evidence**: of Stein's 1943 photograph, "No one from the HSM or from the
  local villages could remember having seen the image in such a form during their first visits to the shrine,
  and it was not possible to find anyone today who could recall a śiva liṅga on this spot." (p. 238, folio not
  stated)
- **Physical works by the organisation, listed.** "the temple organization constructed new routes to the shrine
  and altered the topography by filling up or digging new ponds" (p. 261, folio not stated); a ladder to `rām
  jharoku`, since destroyed (p. 257, folio not stated); a new road and a cemented front courtyard in 2012
  (p. 265, folio not stated, note 30); the tunnel extension after the late 1980s (p. 251, folio not stated);
  "projects like constructing pilgrimage shelters and bridges emerge at the nexus of environment and individual
  and organizational efforts that together solidify social order into social space at Hinglaj." (p. 196, folio
  not stated) The conceptual level "is the realm of the engineers and architects of the Balochi government, but
  the various organizations at the shrine (first and foremost the HSM) aiming to make the place more 'visitor
  friendly' also play a role." (p. 198, folio not stated)
- **The statue programme as an instrument of interpretation.** The HSM says the Ashapura statue "was built by
  the HSM itself at the end of the 1990s", replacing "a small stone at that location, smeared with siṃdūr
  (vermilion), which was once worshipped as the Goddess" (p. 220, folio not stated); the Hanuman statue was
  built there "so that people would know who is worshipped there." (p. 220, folio not stated) "I want to add
  that this is not the only example where the HSM erects a statue of a certain deity for the sake of telling
  people 'who is worshipped there.'" (p. 221, folio not stated) The author's verdict: "By putting up the statue,
  the temple organization solidifies its own interpretation, and I have not met any devotee today who claims
  that the Hanuman worship at this place is somehow wrong." (p. 221, folio not stated) "I see this as a
  paradigmatic example for the HSM's cultural hegemony, which for the past decades enforces its own particular
  interpretation at the site." (p. 221, folio not stated)
- **New images brought in by communities.** "certain Hindu communities have brought new statues and related
  myths to the valley" (p. 261, folio not stated). **Neither the communities nor the statues are named there:
  (not stated).**

### Offerings, and animal sacrifice and its ban

- **What is offered, and where.** Black goats and coconuts at Kali Mata; the sacred thread (jane'o) and head
  shaving at the Aghor River; bathing in the ocean and Rama worship at Khari Nadi; yagya, havan and darśan at
  the Hinglaj temple (p. 203, folio not stated). Bhajans are "(Hin. religious songs) in praise of the Goddess"
  (p. 190, folio not stated).
- **chandragup-baba-chandragup** — **Offerings at the volcano, then and now.** "Traditionally, the pilgrims
  bake a type of cake, or sweetbread, called the 'roṭ,' before ascending the slope of Chandrakup (cf. Hart
  1839, 89; Shastri 1977, 43; Abadhut 1964, 157). The roṭ is made out of ingredients contributed by all the
  yātrīs and serves as a shared offering to Chandrakup or, as Hart says in his description, to the God Rama
  (Hart 1839, 89)." (pp. 212–213, folios not stated) "Aside from coconuts, other oblations include gāṁjā (Hin.
  marijuana) and cilams (Hin. pipe to smoke marijuana), which are also thrown into the volcano (Shastri 1977,
  42; Abadhut 1964, 157; Lakhavat 2011, 138).15 Nowadays, the suggested offerings consist of coconuts (Urd.
  nāriyal), betel nut (Urd. supārī), coins (Urd. sikkah), and dal (Urd. dāl) (Jethamalani 1994, 44)." (p. 213,
  folio not stated) "Yet the ritual of throwing coconuts into the crater seems to be the most commonly
  practiced over the years.14" (p. 212, folio not stated) Earlier: the roṭ cake prepared overnight under a fast
  (p. 141, folio not stated); "gāṁjā (Hin. marijuana), coconuts, and cilams" thrown into Chandrakup (p. 141,
  folio not stated).
- **Other offerings on the route.** "a cilam (Hin. clay pipe)" smoked at the River Hab "to appease the God Shiva
  (Shastri 1977, 11)" (p. 139, folio not stated); "dān-dakṣiṇā (Hin. gifted food)" given to Aghori Baba (p. 142,
  folio not stated); a mālā taken from the vedī (p. 142, folio not stated); at Hinglaj the pilgrims "bathe and
  put on fresh clothes to enter the womb of the Mother", and the altar is "decorated with oleander flowers,
  incense, and candles" (p. 131, folio not stated). **No cash offering at the shrine itself is described in the
  1946 account: (not stated).** Hair as a stated motive for a whole pilgrimage: Thaomal's "main reason for
  undertaking the pilgrimage was to have his son's head shaven at Ashapura paṛāv and to offer the hair to the
  Goddess (Hart 1839, 94)" (p. 220, folio not stated).
- **Animal sacrifice at the Kali Mata shrine: its history.** "There are numerous sources indicating that animal
  sacrifice was once a commonly practiced part of the ritual activities inside the valley, but it was restricted
  to the area of this shrine only. Hart (1839) and Goldsmid (1861) write that in the nineteenth century,
  pilgrims would sacrifice goats the day before finally proceeding to the shrine of Hinglaj." (p. 221, folio not
  stated) "The amount of time required for the blood to dry on the warm sandstone was then interpreted as a sign
  of the Goddess's satisfaction or dissatisfaction with the sacrifice (Hart 1839, 95)." (p. 222, folio not
  stated) "The blood of the animals was then spattered inside a particular cavity in the wall symbolizing the
  mouth of the Goddess Kali" (p. 221, folio not stated); figure caption "FIGURE 5.3 Opening into the stone wall
  at the Kali Mata temple, 2012" (p. 222, folio not stated). **Who did the killing, and the contradiction the
  book leaves open**: "In the nineteenth century, the pilgrims' sacrificial goats were slaughtered by local
  Muslims, very likely belonging to the Zikri Muslim community, who, according to Hart, also kept the carcasses
  (Hart 1839, 95). It is not clear how long this tradition continued or how strictly it was practiced, so one can
  only speculate if this was a special arrangement or not." (p. 222, folio not stated)
- **The sacrificial economy before the ban, in the custodian's own account.** "In the initial days of the
  established annual melā in the mid-1980s and early 1990s, many visitors bought goats from the local Muslims
  living in tiny villages around the valley (Dodiya 1989, 52). This led to variability in price and quality of
  the sacrificial animals." (p. 295, folio not stated) "Later, particularly in the mid-1990s, when the number of
  visitors was steadily increasing, the demand for goats expanded while the supply stayed low." (p. 295, folio
  not stated) Goats were then "sold for high prices but—according to the HSM—were actually not suitable for the
  sacrifice", i.e. "either female, too young, or not healthy enough" (p. 295, folio not stated).
- **The custodian takes over the supply, then abandons it.** "the Mandali began taking the supply of sacrificial
  goats into its own hands, transporting suitable animals on trucks from the nearby city of Las Bela. Dewani
  explained that the goats were sold at one rate and on one day only so that the visitors had an incentive to
  carry out the bali on one particular day—the day designated by the HSM for the worship of the Goddess Kali."
  (p. 295, folio not stated) "the Mandali was no longer able to keep up the supply of goats and eventually also
  had to stop the transport from Bela." (p. 295, folio not stated) A throughput ceiling is given: "it was simply
  not possible to sacrifice more than forty goats a day in accordance with the traditions at the shrine."
  (p. 296, folio not stated) The traditional day: visitors "began slaughtering the goats according to their
  availability rather than according to the traditional days and times propagated by the HSM (usually the eighth
  day of the pilgrimage)" (p. 295, folio not stated).
- **The petitions that produced the ban.** "in those years, members of the Vishnu paṃth—in this case mostly the
  Maheshvari community and followers of the ISKCON—had repeatedly requested that the organization stop the
  slaughter of the goats." (p. 296, folio not stated) Because the easier path ran past the Kali Mata temple,
  "many pilgrims had to pass by the bloody rituals to reach Hinglaj's sanctum sanctorum. Disgusted by the act,
  some appealed to the HSM to stop the activity." (p. 296, folio not stated)
- **Enforcement, and continuation after enforcement.** "the HSM did not react only superficially to the demands
  of certain visitors but actually became the most active agency in the ban's enforcement through the
  establishment of a network of volunteers and ritual experts who help the Mandali to implement it." (p. 296,
  folio not stated) "for some time after the ban was enacted, members of the HSM secretly continued sacrificing
  goats in the mountains for the Goddess Kali. This was done in the name of all visitors to prevent misfortune,
  but eventually this slaughter was also brought to an end, I was told." (p. 296, folio not stated) **No date for
  that ending, and the source is hearsay in the author's own marking ("I was told"): (not stated).**
- **A substitute rite adopted by the custodian's own family.** "While the Dewanis previously gave the blood of a
  goat to the Goddess Kali during the young men's upanayana saṃskāra (Skt.) ceremony—an initiation ritual for the
  community's male members in which a thread is given to symbolize maturity—now on such occasions, the family
  simply cuts the animal's ear and sets it free in front of Kali's temple." (p. 296, folio not stated)
- **Witnessed survivals, which the author sets against the ban.** "I witnessed the sacrifice of a rooster during
  one of the festivals, the blood of which was thrown into the cavity on top of the shrine by a group of
  Devipujak-Vagris from Karachi (see Chapter 6)." (p. 222, folio not stated) "The pilgrims who called themselves
  Devipujak-Vagris were from Karachi and told me that they had offered a goat to the Goddess only the night
  before." (p. 294, folio not stated) His conclusion: "the non-sacrifice of animals was apparently not such a
  stable marker for Hindu identity as many visitors I spoke to had initially presented it." (p. 294, folio not
  stated)
- **Today's practice, as the book states it.** "the practice of animal sacrifice, or bali, is ideologically
  charged and is banned during the festivals. Certain members of the HSM have also expressed their wish that the
  tradition be discontinued completely." (pp. 222–223, folios not stated) "In any case, these days, most pilgrims
  opt to offer coconuts at the Kali temple instead of animals." (p. 223, folio not stated)
- **The survey behind the ban's reception, from the author's own fieldwork.** Note 20: "In a survey I conducted
  during the spring `melā` in 2012 with eighty-nine participants, 44 percent of the respondents stated that they
  sacrifice goats on particular occasions, and 56 percent stated that they do not follow this practice at all."
  (p. 317, folio not stated, note 20)
- **`Bali` as obligation rather than exchange, for one community.** "the ritual slaughter within the Vagri
  community is not simply a quid pro quo action used for the fulfillment of a wish, as seen among other Vagri
  communities (Mann 1980); the `bali` is in fact viewed as obligatory for the health and security of the whole
  community." (p. 310, folio not stated) The offering is "male goats and roosters" (p. 310, folio not stated).
- **`Pyālā denā`, a healer's rite, defined by the author.** "'Pyālā denā' literally means 'to give a cup,' a
  practice carried out on particular occasions such as navarātri, when the bhopā embodies the Goddess and consumes
  a cup (Hin. pyālā) of fresh blood from a rooster or a male goat." (p. 304, folio not stated) The pujārī's own
  account: "Every kālī caudas I had to go to the temple and there I had to give the pyālā." (p. 304, folio not
  stated), later replaced with "I started to offer coconuts to Kali Mata" (p. 305, folio not stated).
- **`baqra cīd`[text-layer?] [baqra ʿīd] as a reason to travel to the shrine.** "in response to the question of
  why so many people had gathered in Hinglaj/Nani during `baqra cīd`[text-layer?] [baqra ʿīd], a frequent answer
  was that people wanted to escape the open slaughter of animals in their towns, a custom with which Muslims
  celebrate Abraham's willingness to sacrifice his first-born son." (p. 294, folio not stated) Most visitors then
  "had come from urban areas like Karachi or Hyderabad" (p. 294, folio not stated). **This is the only occasion in
  the book at which the shrine is said to be busy outside the spring melā, and it is dated by a Muslim festival.**

### The festival: the phālgun/caitra melā

- **shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir** — **The festival is in April and is named.** "the annual festival in
  April (Hin. phālgun/caitra melā)" (p. 83, folio not stated); also "the annual phālgun/caitra gathering" (p. 84,
  folio not stated). **No calendar day and no duration is given with that name: (not stated).**
- **How the date was set — by hydrology, not by calendar, and then changed.** "The Hingol's water level is
  partially responsible for the phālgun/caitra festival's date being chosen in the mid-1980s. Initially, when the
  annual festival was established, the HSM needed to select a weekend when the river's low water level could be
  anticipated so that the pilgrims would be able to safely cross. For this reason, the date of the festival was not
  fixed according to a religious calendar but was scheduled in consideration of local weather conditions. With the
  construction of a bridge over the Hingol in 2012—a project that was funded by the Balochistan government—the date
  of the festival no longer depended on the water level of the river and thus was shifted by the HSM to a date
  considered to be 'more accurate.'19 At the time of writing, the HSM had recently decided to organize the yātrā on
  the weekend after the caitra navarāti." (p. 215, folio not stated) **This is the book's fullest statement of the
  festival date and it still gives no day and no duration: (not stated).**
- **The festival's shape, in days.** Officially "begun with the visit to Chandrakup on the first day and ended with
  a collective havan (Hin. ceremony), in front of the Hinglaj temple on the third day" (p. 281, folio not stated) —
  i.e. three days.
- **How it is advertised.** "Several weeks before the April celebration, the HSM publicizes the exact dates and
  details of the festival on bulletins that are posted in Hindu temples in various Pakistani cities." (p. 281, folio
  not stated) The same sheets carry "a certain set of guidelines that (according to them) ought to be followed inside
  the valley" (p. 281, folio not stated). The route posters have been distributed annually since 1986 (p. 204, folio
  not stated).
- **Scale, in the book's own words.** "after 2001, the site quickly developed into a stage for one of the biggest
  annual Hindu festivals in Pakistan" (p. 30, folio not stated), attracting "thousands of people from all castes,
  classes, and regions" (p. 30, folio not stated). "Various developments over the past few decades have turned the
  melā into one of the largest gatherings for Hindu communities in Pakistan—for most of my interviewees, it was even
  considered the biggest. It now annually attracts devotees from all over the country and even from abroad." (p. 83,
  folio not stated) "Mainly through the HSM's work, the annual festival has become well known and many Hindus I met
  all over the country had gone at least once." (p. 83, folio not stated) "The institutionalization of the
  pilgrimage, combined with infrastructural developments and the completion of the MCH, have transformed the
  pilgrimage into one of the largest annual social gatherings of Hindu communities in Pakistan today." (p. 97, folio
  not stated)
- **The visitor numbers the book prints, all of them.** "This culminated in the April 2010 gathering during which,
  according to HSM's ticket sale records, over forty thousand people visited the shrine." (p. 84, folio not stated)
  The same figure restated as a trend: "from merely a few thousand a year to over forty thousand on a single weekend
  in the spring." (p. 261, folio not stated) And the endnote version, as a ticket count: "in 2010 when the
  organization claimed to have sold forty thousand tickets" (p. 317, folio not stated, note 15). **Every one of
  these rests on the committee's own ticket records and the book says so.** The one older figure is the author's
  inference of "a rough average of two hundred visitors to the shrine yearly via the Sonmiani route" around 1907
  (p. 147, folio not stated).
- **The transformation the Conclusion states.** "the Hinglaj `yātrā` evolved from a highly strenuous bodily
  experience and a once-in-a-lifetime undertaking to an annual sedentary festival" (p. 323, folio not stated).
- **A festival date that sits in the endnotes with nothing attached to it.** "Fourteenth day of the `aśvin` month."
  (p. 317, folio not stated, note 26) **The note does not name the festival it dates, and its callout lies outside
  the extract: (not stated).**
- **Festivals in the plural, against the festival in the singular.** "The establishment of annual festivals at
  Hinglaj" (p. 324, folio not stated) stands in the same paragraph as "numerous recent adjustments and changes at the
  temple and its festival" (p. 324, folio not stated). **The book does not reconcile the singular and the plural.**
  The index run is "festivals, 7, 18, 76, 141, 183, 192, 203, 260–264" with a sub-entry "dates of festivals, 264"
  (pp. 345, 343, folio not stated) — **index locators, printed on those PDF pages, not page references to the
  festival itself.**
- **Medical provision, an accident, and an institution founded because of it.** A young pilgrim was "seriously
  injured when a motorbike ran into the pilgrim group on the road" in 2012; the Edhi foundation ambulance stationed
  in the valley was refused him by "the doctor on call, a member of the Lohana community… since the boy's condition
  was not critical" (p. 158, folio not stated). "A private car was found to carry the boy back to the city—but only
  after the intervention of an influential local member of the Hindu Seva Trust… this incident also demonstrated the
  significant lack of medical facilities at the festival and led, in the subsequent weeks, to the establishment of a
  Gujarati community-driven organization in Narayanpur, the Pakistan Hindu Seva Samaj, which aimed to improve medical
  services in the valley." (p. 159, folio not stated) By the autumn "some members of the Narayanpur pilgrim group
  proudly showed me their medical supplies and an emergency car they had organized for the transportation of patients
  (see Chapter 6)." (p. 159, folio not stated) **The injured pilgrim is not named, and where the death is reported at
  p. 273 the year is not repeated: (not stated) there.**
- **Bathing in the ponds, a new and disapproved practice.** "Now they even take baths in the ponds! They don't come
  for the darśan." (p. 187, folio not stated)

### Caste, community and who speaks for the shrine

- **The custodial community and the pilgrim communities are not the same, and the book makes this structural.**
  "This class and caste division is also tangible in the organization of the festival, where a part of the
  upper-class Lohana community, embodied in the HSM, has taken on the responsibility to 'educate' the low caste
  visitors. Such measures, however, often lead to sneers and tension between the castes, who mutually deny each
  other's ability to know the 'real' tradition." (p. 154, folio not stated)
- **The caste framing of the money dispute.** "many Hindus in Pakistan (especially from the scheduled castes) saw the
  imposition of an entry fee at any time of year as typical profit-orientated behavior by the HSM, which consists
  mainly of members of the Lohana caste" (p. 292, folio not stated); "such accusations were usually baldly linked to
  stereotypes of caste identity and behavior" (p. 292, folio not stated). The Lohanas are described in the same
  passage as "traditionally merchants and business people" (p. 292, folio not stated).
- **Enforcement and friction at the checkpoint.** "As long as the HSM was organizing the transportation, the
  necessity of the fee was more explicable to pilgrims." (p. 290, folio not stated) After 2001 self-organised travel
  widened "the communication gap between the HSM and the melā attendees, many of whom would encounter members of the
  Mandali only as they checked for tickets, gave orders, or organized the crowds" (p. 290, folio not stated). "With
  the imposition of the fee, the utilization of this money became less transparent, and many yātrīs became
  increasingly irritated by the expense." (p. 290, folio not stated) "Frequently, it came to little skirmishes
  between the visitors and these volunteers, some of whom ... took their job a bit too seriously." (p. 291, folio not
  stated) "these unpleasant micro contacts were often the only direct interaction that visitors had with the HSM"
  (p. 291, folio not stated).
- **A survey figure on caste discrimination, from the author's own 2012 fieldwork.** Note 8: "In a survey I conducted
  in 2012 among visitors at Hinglaj with the help of my assistant during the `phālgun`/`caitra melā`, 26 percent
  stated that they felt discriminated against or treated badly due to their caste background (Question in Urdu:
  "`zād`[text-layer?] [zāt] `kī vajah se tacṣṣub`[text-layer?] [taʿṣṣub] `yā nafrat kā sāmnā karnā paṛtā?`")."
  (p. 316, folio not stated, note 8)
- **A demographic figure, attributed but unattributable.** "according to nongovernmental organizations (NGOs) in
  Sindh, the biggest bulk of Pakistani Hindus, over 90 percent, consist of so-called scheduled caste communities"
  (p. 276, folio not stated). **No NGO is named and no year is given: (not stated).**
- **The Lohanas as the type of the Sindhi Hindu, via two colonial witnesses, with the author's qualification.**
  Burton — "a description of them [the Lohanas], therefore, will be a general portrait of the Sindh Hindoos" (Burton
  1851, 314) — and McMurdo — "The Lowannas or Lohannas compose the great body of Hindus in Sindh" (Mubarak 1985, 55)
  (p. 79, folio not stated). The author qualifies: "In terms of ritual practices or sectarian interests, however, the
  Lohanas are quite a heterogeneous community, sharing few universal characteristics aside from the name." (p. 79,
  folio not stated)
- **Absorption as the mechanism of Lohana caste formation**, quoted from Thakur: "The case of Lohana caste is very
  peculiar. Unless a person is a member of any of the non-Lohana castes, he must be assumed to be a Lohana, members of
  which caste form the general body of the Sind Hindus . . . [P]eople . . . who migrated from Rajputanta and the
  Punjab and professed Lohana occupations have acquired membership of the Lohana caste. (Thakur 1997, 68f.)" (p. 80,
  folio not stated) "Zahid-Khan attributes this merging of different groups to the small number of non-Muslim Sindhis
  who united in the face of the majority (quoted in Cook 2010, 134)." (p. 81, folio not stated)
- **Census classification of the Lohanas, a dated reversal.** "Hence, in the 1881 census, Lohanas from Shikarpur and
  Hyderabad were counted as Sikhs, while ten years later, in the 1891 census, they were classified as Hindus (Lari
  1994, 211f.)." (p. 80, folio not stated)
- **Caste, priesthood and intermarriage.** "Thakur writes that the Lohanas share many attributes with the Arora, the
  Khatri, and the Bhanushali communities, who all recruit the Saraswat Brahmins as priests (Thakur 1997, 61)." (p. 81,
  folio not stated) "Lari supports this finding, writing that the Bhatias, the Khatris, and the Lohanas were very
  close communities and even intermarried (Lari 1994, 211)." (p. 81, folio not stated) A commensality rule with a
  mythological warrant: Kshatriya women hiding from Parashurama "had to prepare food for the Brahmin men, who since
  that time accept food prepared by Kshatriaya (Khatri) women (Cook 2010, 144)" (p. 81, folio not stated).
- **The merchant economy behind all of this.** "Lachaier claims that in the nineteenth century, the Lohanas were the
  most important trading community in the areas of Sindh, Kutch, and Saurashtra (Lachaier 2008, 82). One reason for
  this was the established rule of the Kalhoras in Sindh in the eighteenth century, which brought new business
  opportunities for merchant professions (Cook 2010, 140)." (p. 80, folio not stated) "Sindh's location between
  trading routes connecting Afghanistan, Khorasan, Arabia, Persia, Turkey, and other places made the province
  appealing to a wide array of merchants from the entire continent." (p. 81, folio not stated) The Khatris were
  "traditionally associated with certain types of trade and moneylending (Singh 1998, 1721)" (p. 58, folio not
  stated); "the Khatris—together with the Lohanas and the Bhatias—were the main merchants in the city and maintained
  business contacts from the Volga River to Calcutta (Markovits 2000, 60)" (p. 58, folio not stated); "Markovits
  writes that the Khatris made up only 5 percent of the merchant population of Shikarpur, with the Lohanas comprising
  the largest part (Markovits 2000, 63)." (p. 98, folio not stated)
- **The Amil/Bhaiband split, with a proportion.** "Around the eighteenth or nineteenth century, a separation occurred
  within these merchant groups of Sindh. A subset of Lohanas began working for local rulers and soon become known as
  'Amils,' while those who retained their merchant profession were called 'Bhaiband' (Lachaier 2008, 83). The Amils,
  initially 10 to 15 percent of the Lohanas (Lari 1994, 211), soon acquired a higher status than the general Lohana
  and Khatri communities and thus became an independent group." (p. 81, folio not stated)
- **Colonial classification as an institution with material stakes.** Colonial law courts "decided questions of family
  law on the basis of Brahmin interpretations of these Sanskrit sources"; "the (urban) British courts often sought to
  determine a litigant's varṇa before they could rule on certain questions (Carroll 1978, 237–238)" (p. 59, folio not
  stated). "By determining questions of law and possible job prospects, a community's classification was far from a
  superficial category, instead exerting a very tangible impact on the group's social and financial status (Carroll
  1978, 233)." (p. 59, folio not stated) Risley "studied the caste taxonomies of British India by asking colonial
  officers to submit forms classifying the various communities according to their ability to enter temples or
  according to their relationship to Brahmins. His aim to restructure the census of 1901 on the basis of this method
  led to a wave of petitions" (p. 60, folio not stated); the petitions "included genealogies and 'learned statements'
  by Brahmins whose status helped guarantee the origin or bloodline of certain communities" (p. 60, folio not stated).
- **Caste mobility through the colonial census, with the shrine inside it.** "the Khatris, among many other merchant
  communities, sought to improve their varṇa status by writing petitions to H. H. Risley, the commissioner in charge
  of the 1901 census" (p. 67, folio not stated); "the colonial commissioners ultimately recognized the Khatris as
  belonging to the Kshatriya varṇa", and Jareca's book "reprints one of H. H. Risley's letters in its entirety"
  (p. 68, folio not stated). The Khatris were "originally described as members of the Vaishya varṇa (Baines 1893,
  201)" (p. 67, folio not stated). **Brahmins for hire**: during upward mobility "many—often 'low-ranking'—Brahmins
  had a symbiotic relationship with the castes seeking to alter their status, especially the Khatri community. Such
  Brahmin groups supported claims of higher lineage in the form of 'learned opinions' (Rowe 2007, 203)." (p. 67,
  folio not stated) "K. C. S. Varma called Brahmins who were in the service of such lower classes 'hired
  mercenaries' (quoted in Rowe 2007, 203)." (p. 100, folio not stated)
- **The community book as an object with a funding model.** Brahmakṣatriyotpatti Evaṃ Māṁ Hiṃgulāj "was written and
  published by the author Bhamvarlal Jareca, who was supported by the donations of the Khatri community in
  Rajasthan"; it contains "devotional songs (Hin. bhajan) and ritual manuals for the worship of the Goddess (Jareca
  n.d., 116–137)" and reports on the "'brahmakṣatriya samāj,' an organization aiming to preserve the traditions of
  the Khatri community (Jareca n.d., 155)" (p. 67, folio not stated).
- **Who counts as Khatri, and the book's own ruling.** "in Khatri's text, the description 'Khatri' is mainly
  equivalent to Brahmakshatriya (Khatri 1973), the recent census distinguishes between the two and defines the latter
  as a division of the former (Mohidden 2003; Singh 1998)." (p. 98, folio not stated) "In my own experience, however,
  today it is not possible to simply equate these two names. While most Brahmakshatriyas I met identified with the
  category Khatri, the reverse is not always true. Thus, I think it is best to follow Singh (1996) here and understand
  the Brahmakshatriyas as a particular branch of the numerous Khatri communities." (p. 98, folio not stated) Jareca
  disagreed in person: "During our talk, Jareca did not distinguish between the Brahmakshatriyas and the Khatris; for
  him, the communities are one and the same." (p. 99, folio not stated) **A named disagreement between the author and
  his principal informant, kept side by side.** Burton on Sindh usage: "Vaishiya" is applied "in a limited sense to
  signify operatives and mechanics, opposed to merchants and shopkeepers" (Burton 1851, 237) (pp. 98–99, folios not
  stated).
- **The Kapri community, defined and sub-divided.** "The spelling 'Kapri' (kap'ṛī) was provided by members of the
  community, so I will mainly use this transliteration." (p. 100, folio not stated) "Today, the community separates
  itself into four branches: the Ramsnehi, the Ashapuri, the Shravani, and the Makadbanth Kapri." (p. 100, folio not
  stated) "The Kapris (also Kapadi) were introduced above. It is likely that they are a part of the Kanphata community
  as there are repeated references to the Kapris' slit ears in the section Sur Rāmkalī chapter 3, 4–8. (Agha 2004,
  446). In Sindhi, the word 'kāpṛī' simply means a 'caste of faqirs.'… likely refers to a community of ascetics,
  without any particular religious affiliation, like those still found in the interior Sindh today." (p. 101, folio
  not stated) **A gap in the literature stated outright**: "aside from some short descriptions in a few publications,
  not much has been written specifically about the small Kapri community of Gujarat, and, thus, this chapter is mainly
  informed by oral histories gathered in India and Pakistan." (p. 75, folio not stated)
- **Pilgrimage as initiation into a caste, and the limits of that initiation.** "anyone who emerges from Hinglaj's cave
  in Balochistan is automatically initiated into the group and can assume the title 'Kapri'" (p. 75, folio not
  stated), and so "anybody professing Hindu religion can become a Kapadi" (Mohidden 2003, 603) (p. 75, folio not
  stated). But: "people who come back from Balochistan only become 'half Kapris.' They are, for example, not allowed
  to marry into the community. The pilgrimage, however, does bring a certain amount of prestige and even certain
  lasting changes for the yātrīs. According to this tradition, even as 'half Kapris,' they are not burned on the pyre
  after death as they normally would be, but instead are buried in a samādhi (Hin. tomb)." (p. 79, folio not stated)
  **A change in funerary practice is the concrete, stated consequence of the pilgrimage.**
- **The funerary consequence, stated three times and limited once.** "Hinglajis in Kutch are not—as usual—burned on
  the pyre, but rather are buried in small shrines called samādhis, where they are remembered and `worshipped by their
  ancestors`[text-layer?]" (p. 178, folio not stated). "This practice originates from the notion that every visitor to
  Hinglaj is reborn from the womb of the Goddess and thus gains the ātmā of a newborn child. They also may assume the
  name Kapri, as was mentioned in Chapter 2." (p. 178, folio not stated) The worship at those shrines: "On festival
  days particularly, but also during the year, these locations are visited by family members and friends who worship
  the deceased with incense and siṃdūr (Schaflechner 2011, 40:40)." (p. 179, folio not stated) **No festival is named:
  (not stated).** "While Kapris can become dev'ātmās through the journey to Hinglaj, all dev'ātmās automatically
  become (half) Kapris after their successful pilgrimage." (p. 178, folio not stated) **The author limits his own
  evidence**: "Dayaram asserted that this practice is found all over India, but so far, I have only encountered it in
  Kutch, where many little shrines are found as evidence" (p. 178, folio not stated). The set formula he heard
  repeated verbatim in two countries: "In Hinglaj all kinds of sins are destroyed, and there is a rule that whoever
  goes there is called Hinglaji. After pilgrims have lived a full life of 100 years, they are not burnt on the pyre,
  but instead worshipped in a shrine [samādhi] as dev'ātmās [divine souls]. As in temples, other people will do pūjā
  for them. And whoever comes back from Hinglaj becomes a part of the Kapri sect. Like saints they are not simply
  burnt, but everybody gets a shrine, and everybody will be worshipped. On festival days people will come, and put
  siṃdūr [vermilion] on their samādhis. That is the rule!" (p. 182, folio not stated) A research note attached:
  "in Gujarat … among certain communities, it is a custom to entomb pilgrims in small samādhis after their death
  instead of burning them on the pyre (see Chapter 4). A study of these samādhis could shed some light on this
  question." (p. 147, folio not stated)
- **The Hinglaji title.** "Today, the epithet 'Hinglaji' is still used in the community for people who went on the
  yātrā." (p. 172, folio not stated) In Kutch too, "returnees from Hinglaj are also treated with awe and respect and
  are frequently given the epithet 'Hinglaji'" (p. 178, folio not stated).
- **The Charan obligation, with a deadline and a sanction.** "The journey to the shrine in Balochistan ought to be made
  by every Charan once in their lifetime and is thus the sine qua non ritual for many. For the Charans, the pilgrimage
  is also a commemorative ceremony, remembering and renewing a bond they have shared with the Goddess for ages."
  (p. 172, folio not stated) Every Charan male must go "before his 60th birthday; otherwise a rock will tumble down
  into the sacred valley, eventually burying the shrine" (p. 70, folio not stated).
- **The Charans as an institution in their own right.** "Many Charan men were employed as bards and poets, serving
  kings and aristocrats as genealogists and, even more significantly, assembling and composing the histories of their
  masters' dynasties… Their poetry connected the rulers of their day to those of mythological times, placing current
  administrations inside a timeless dharma and thereby legitimizing the rulers' claims to power." (p. 69, folio not
  stated) "While the Charans legitimized Rajput authority by facilitating a connection to an eternal order, the Rajput
  were instrumental in the Charans' upward social mobility." (p. 70, folio not stated) **Traga as a legal and credit
  instrument**: "Charans were also warrantors for contracts, guaranteeing adherence to certain agreements between two
  parties with their lives. This was possible by threatening to commit traga (self-immolation)… The threat of traga
  served as a guarantee for everyday agreements like loans or the settlement of disputes, but also for politically
  sensitive issues such as treaties made between two kings (Tambs-Lyche 2004)." (p. 69, folio not stated) "The ghosts
  of the deceased were feared, especially those of females, because they embodied the horrific aspect of the Goddess."
  (p. 69, folio not stated) **Apotheosis as an institution**: the Charani Sagati (Kamphorst 2008), "a tradition of
  apotheosis, that is, the deification of Charan women, which continues today" (p. 69, folio not stated); "many Charan
  women who were exceptionally honored during their lifetime would eventually become important local Goddesses in
  areas where Charans lived (Westphal and Westphal-Hellbusch 1976, 167)", and Enthoven, cited there: "[s]everal of the
  most popular goddesses of North Gujarat are the spirits of Charan woman who sacrificed themselves to guard the
  privileges of their caste" (p. 70, folio not stated). "Sharma mentions such incarnations also taking place in the
  Rajput and Bhati communities but simultaneously acknowledges the importance of the Charan communities for the Devi
  worship and the great number of avatārīs (Skt.) present in this jāti (Sharma 2011, 15)." (p. 100, folio not stated)
  "This certainly is an exception among the Charani Sagati, who usually live in celibacy (Basu 2004, 191)." (p. 100,
  folio not stated) "The strong heterogeneity of the Charan jāti has led to an assumption that the only link between
  the pastoral communities, merchants, and bards of the caste is the shared name (Westphal and Westphal-Hellbusch 1976,
  93)." (p. 100, folio not stated)
- **The Devipujak-Vagris of Bhimpura, Karachi — the book's one close community study.** "The community consists of
  about fifty to sixty households, belonging mainly to the families of Bhao and Bhachu, the elders of the Ghorava and
  Oughriya clan respectively." (p. 306, folio not stated, callout 27) "The current `bhopā` of the Ghorava family is
  Lali Bhua, the son of Bhao, who serves as the primary medium through which the Goddess addresses the different Vagri
  families." (p. 307, folio not stated) Lali Bhua "also performs their necessary family rituals" for the Oughriyas,
  who "call upon different ancestors and descend from a different genealogy" (p. 307, folio not stated). **No dates
  for Bhao, Bhachu or Lali Bhua: (not stated).** Weekly `ḥāẓrī` (Urd. presence, appearance) sessions are held every
  Thursday, at which "the various goddesses and ancestors possess the `bhopā`—the community's spiritual leader and
  genealogical expert" and the two elders' names are invoked ("children of Bhao!" "family of Bhachu!") (pp. 306–307,
  folio not stated). "The `bhopā`'s wild dance to the sound of the `ḍam'rū` (Hin. a small drum with a playing surface
  on both sides) is called 'shaking' (Hin. `hāl mār'nā`), a state of trance during which he repeatedly addresses the
  community in short, excited sentences." (p. 309, folio not stated) Their `maṭh` in Bhimpura is "the place where the
  sacrifices are performed. There, two separate fireplaces (Hin. `dhūnī`) are kept for the members of Ghorava and the
  Oughriya families, while a third one is designated for use by all other families in the compound. During larger
  celebrations, like the `navarātri`, all the `dhūnī`s are treated equally" (p. 307, folio not stated). Their
  occupations: "Most of the older members of the Devipujak community sell dried fruits by trade; quite a few sell
  their wares in Karachi's Empress Market or at the far end of the city, at the Sunday Bazaar in the posh Defence
  Housing Authority area, where they sell nuts and snacks. But some, especially those of the younger generation,
  today engage in other occupations and support the community with their salaries." (p. 307, folio not stated) Their
  migration claim carries its own limit: "According to Vagri history, the community migrated back and forth between
  Sindh and Gujarat for many generations and, like other nomadic groups, were eventually forced to settle on one side
  of the border after the Partition. Since there is no written account of their movement, the clan's detailed history
  can only be based on speculation. The Ghorava family, however, estimates that their forefathers first arrived in
  Karachi about two hundred years ago." (pp. 307–308, folio not stated) Their own caste rules, and the author's
  counter-observations: "their elevated caste status forbids them to accept water or food from any other low-caste
  Hindu communities, like the Meghwar or the Hindu Kathiyavari" (p. 308, folio not stated); he "witnessed various
  examples where this discriminatory requirement was not strictly implemented" (p. 308, folio not stated), and gives a
  counter-instance at the Jagnath `akhārā` where the Devipujaks "were slightly annoyed with the uninvited guests but
  acted very hospitably, sharing food and water with them" (p. 309, folio not stated). **A change of community name,
  undated**: "According to Bhao Tulsi, this harassment was one of the main reasons the community decided to change its
  name from Vagri to Devipujak." (p. 309, folio not stated) **The year is (not stated).**
- **Partition demography in Sindh, as the background to all of it.** "The partition of the subcontinent brought major
  changes to the province of Sindh. The departure of the Hindu elite and the influx of Muslim refugees ('Muhajirs')
  from India dramatically altered the local demography, and the Sindhi community became increasingly defined by its
  perception of being ostracized and subjugated by Muhajir power." (p. 87, folio not stated)
- **The Sindhi-nationalist denial of caste, and the Dalit counter-position — a conflict the author declines to
  settle.** Thakur, "a historian of Sindh", in Sindhi Culture (1951): "[c]aste system is almost absent in Sind"
  (Thakur 1997, 25) (p. 277, folio not stated). "To devoted Sindhi Hindu nationalists, acts of caste discrimination
  are anathema, and in many of my conversations with them, such individuals told me that it exists today only in India
  or other provinces of Pakistan." (p. 278, folio not stated) Against this: "This behavior is fiercely opposed by
  Dalit organizations that strongly criticize the upper castes, especially the Hindu landowning class, and their
  involvement in the oppression of the lower jātis. Some people even went so far as to blame the difficulties for
  Hindu communities in Pakistan solely on the upper-caste Hindus" (p. 278, folio not stated). **He explicitly declines
  to settle it**: "A separate study is needed to give this important and difficult topic its due." (p. 278, folio not
  stated) He adds that "many efforts by upper-caste Hindus to 'educate' or to bring 'development' to the lower castes
  are mocked as hypocrisy by Dalit organizations" (p. 278, folio not stated).
- **A comparative frame, cited not asserted.** "Fierce debates about the relative positions of Dalit sub-castes is a
  widespread phenomenon, as shown by Moffatt (1979) in his study of several such communities in Tamil Nadu." (p. 309,
  folio not stated) The author then differentiates the Karachi case: "such differentiation seemed more important on an
  imaginary or ideological level... In their daily contact with other Hindu communities, such segregation was not very
  obvious." (p. 309, folio not stated)

### The Zikri Muslims, Bibi Nani, and Muslim claims on the site

- **shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir** — **The shrine carries a Muslim identification that the book takes
  seriously and never resolves.** "the veneration of Hinglaj as the pīr 'Bibi Nani' within certain Muslim traditions
  allows her to exert considerable power within modern-day Sindhi nationalist discourses" (p. 24, folio not stated).
  "Zikris today see Hinglaj as a Balochi 'pīr' (Urd. saint) who is a part of their distinct culture" (p. 90, folio not
  stated). And, in the Conclusion, a third and different Muslim reading: "Because the Zikris consider Hinglaj to be the
  grave of one of Prophet Mohammad's family members" (p. 325, folio not stated); the chapter speaks of "Zikri Muslim
  religious imaginaries at the shrine" and "Zikri Muslim religious markers" (p. 325, folio not stated). **No Zikri
  shrine, leader or settlement is named anywhere: (not stated).**
- **Two further Muslim identifications, stated as informants' claims and not adjudicated.** An informant of Aurel
  Stein "claimed that Hinglaj, or Bibi Nani, is considered to be the wife of Ali (Stein 1943, 202)" (p. 90, folio not
  stated), and the book also reports the version in which she is "the grandmother … of Ali" (p. 90, folio not stated).
  **Wife and grandmother stand side by side and the book chooses neither.** A separate legend places Hazrat Ali at the
  valley: "Hazrat Ali, the son-in-law of the Prophet Mohammad", who "split a rock in two with his sword as he was on
  his way into the valley" (Hart 1839, 94) (pp. 89–90, folio not stated). A Zikri tradition names the place as "where
  Eve fell from paradise and later became known as Hinglaj (Abro 1991, 66)" (p. 255, folio not stated).
- **Who the Zikris are, as the book describes them.** "The Zikri community is a small sect within Islam based upon the
  teachings of Mohammad Jaunpuri from the fifteenth century." (p. 90, folio not stated) **Jaunpuri is given no tomb, no
  place and no dates: (not stated).** "Zikris mainly subscribe to the tenets of Sunni Islam, but practice the ẕikr
  (Urd.), the recitation of the name of Allah instead of the common prayer. Not many details on the community have been
  documented, and the most extensive research to date can be found in Baloch (2010)." (p. 90, folio not stated) "In my
  experience, Zikris in Las Bela and Makran take a different approach to their religious practice than Zikris in
  Karachi, who adhere to more common interpretations of Islam." (p. 90, folio not stated) The works-cited entry is
  "Baloch, Inayatullah. 2010. 'Islam, the State, and Identity: The Zikris of Balochistan.'" (p. 327, folio not stated).
- **The Zikris as the nineteenth-century sacrificers at the site.** "In the nineteenth century, the pilgrims'
  sacrificial goats were slaughtered by local Muslims, very likely belonging to the Zikri Muslim community, who,
  according to Hart, also kept the carcasses (Hart 1839, 95)." (p. 222, folio not stated) **The author marks this as a
  probability, not a fact: "very likely".**
- **The colonial statement of shared worship that the whole Sindhi-nationalist reading rests on.** "Here Hindoo and
  Muhammadan pilgrims alike resort, each claiming the divine protection of the presiding goddess or of the departed
  saint" (Holdich 1904, 45) (p. 92, folio not stated). **The author's own source criticism of that lineage of
  statements is under "Syncretic Sindhi nationalism" below, and it must travel with the quotation.**
- **The Bibi Nani/Nana identification, and the author's refusal to rule on it.** "I am not in the position to judge the
  connection of the Goddess Hinglaj with other ancient goddesses of the Middle East, but I would like to mention the
  work of D. T. Potts (2001)" (p. 101, folio not stated); Potts quoted: "The possibilities for [Nana's] conflation with
  Durga are particularly tempting given the fact that, on Kushan coinage, Nana sometimes 'appears with Oesho (Siva) as
  her consort,' and the lion-riding goddesses of some seventh century A.D. silver bowls from Choresmia 'may be related
  to Nana or Durga'" (Potts 2001, 27) (p. 101, folio not stated); and "[h]er worship in Bactria . . . bespeaks a long
  period of profound influence which has lasted until the modern day, as the shrines in Afghanistan to 'Bibi Nanni'
  surely attest" (Potts 2001, 31) (p. 102, folio not stated). **A plagiarism finding against the standard source for
  the identification**: "The following paragraph corresponds almost verbatim with Crooke's article: 'The title of Bibi
  Nani may be identified with Nanea, the mother-goddess worshipped in Syria, Persia and Armenia, and other parts of
  Asia under the names Anaiti, Anaea, Aneitis or Tanais, the primeval Babylonian Goddess Nana, the lady of the temple
  E-anna of her city Uruk.' … Consequently, I think it highly likely that this part was taken directly from the ERE
  (compare ERE 1913, 715 with Billimoria 1944, 93)." (p. 101, folio not stated)
- **The Muslim-exclusion rule at the festival, and how it actually works.** "this 'threshold' of the (Hindu) sacred
  space, as one enters from the nonsacred space, is also marked by the presence of the Balochistan police standing
  guard at the valley's gate. The police are under an official order to allow only Hindus to enter the festival. Due to
  the huge crowds, however, the mandate represents an impossible task; there is no way to determine the visitors'
  religious affiliations from afar. Yet the police still show their presence through selective searches at the entrance
  as well as uniformed and ununiformed patrolling inside the valley." (p. 218, folio not stated) The author's
  counter-instance: "I myself spent some time with a Muslim traveler from Karachi at Hinglaj who had no problems
  entering the valley whatsoever during the festival." (p. 218, folio not stated) His reading: "This theoretical
  exclusion of Muslims during the festival is a complex issue that is not directly equivalent to their practical
  exclusion. There certainly is no strict enforcement of this segregation" (p. 218, folio not stated). "Even though
  this transformation is not gained through consistent exclusion of Muslims on the practical level, the concept of
  segregation itself influences the religious imaginary. Hinglaj emerges as an imaginary of space, or representational
  space, for Pakistani Hindus, which creates a feeling of unity, coherence, and belonging. In the same moment that the
  police imposes the conceptual (and imaginary) exclusion of Muslims at the valley's gate, major differences in caste,
  language, and ethnicity among the Hindus entering the valley dissolve." (pp. 218–219, folios not stated)
- **A tīrtha need not be sectarian — the book's own warrant for reading a Hindu tīrtha and a Muslim dargāh in one
  frame.** "Tīrthas are often found at or near certain natural features like springs, caves, or volcanoes, but can also
  be at the graves of saints and their disciples. Hence, the term tīrtha is not bound to any one type of site, but
  rather suggests a place that promises some kind of benefit to the visitor." (p. 106, folio not stated) "Hence,
  salvific places do not necessarily have to be bound to religious affiliation and can comprise different communities
  or different faiths." (p. 107, folio not stated) "Such stories connect Hindu and Muslim sacred spaces in Sindh and
  show that imagined landscapes do not necessarily have to be separated using clear-cut religious taxonomies." (p. 109,
  folio not stated) "The south of Pakistan, and especially Sindh, offers a whole range of salvific spaces that are
  frequently visited by different religious communities and form a network of pilgrimage routes or ritual journeys.
  Sindhi nationalists often emphasize these characteristics of Sindh as a landscape of 'coexistence' (see Chapter 2)."
  (p. 107, folio not stated)
- **A conversion legend the author explicitly refuses to underwrite.** "the story of converting pilgrims into Muslims
  may simply be another in the huge corpus of unverifiable legends surrounding the aloof shrine of the Goddess."
  (p. 115, folio not stated)
- **Branding as a certificate of pilgrimage, and its two explanations.** "At one time, pilgrims in Koteshwara,
  especially followers of the Gorakhnath sect, would have the sign of Shiva and Shakti burned onto their forearms. This
  marking bore witness to their successful journey to Hinglaj." (p. 115, folio not stated) Briggs' reading: "the
  branding actually represented the reinstating of the Hindu pilgrims back to their original Hindu faith following the
  pilgrimage (Briggs 1938, 109)." (p. 115, folio not stated) The priests' reading: "They equate this practice to the
  rituals that are performed in modern times after the pilgrimage, which are necessary to purify the homecoming
  pilgrims after their journey to Pakistan." (p. 116, folio not stated) "Because the area in which Hinglaj is located
  is full of 'meat eaters' and the pilgrims on their way necessarily have to stay at the homes of Muslims, Kohlis, or
  other communities considered 'impure' (Hin. bhraṣṭ), purification rituals are necessary." (p. 116, folio not stated)
  **Two explanations of one rite, kept side by side.** The object survives: "the branding iron with which the sign of
  Shiva and Shakti was burned into the forearm of the pilgrims still sits at Koteshwara" (p. 115, folio not stated),
  photographed in 2011 (p. 116, folio not stated). The rite has softened: burning on the forearm has been replaced by
  "the mark of a cold stamp" for hygiene reasons, and "only two or three people came to the temple in the past few
  years to receive the mark" (p. 117, folio not stated). **That is the only visitor figure in that chunk, and it counts
  recipients of a mark, not pilgrims.**

### State, police and communal politics around the shrine

- **The state is present at the shrine as police and as money, and as nothing else.** The Balochistan police guard the
  gate under an order to admit only Hindus (pp. 218, 284, folios not stated); the Balochistan government funds the
  bridge and buildings (pp. 84, 215, 226, folios not stated); the Balochistan Assembly stops and then re-shapes the
  WAPDA dam (p. 217, folio not stated). **No Auqaf department, no antiquities authority, no shrine-management statute
  and no federal religious-affairs body is named anywhere in connection with Hinglaj: (not stated).**
- **Security framing, and the counter-finding.** A Jinnah Institute official branded Hinglaj a "high risk for
  catastrophe" in 2012, yet "it is one of the few places in Pakistan that offers a relatively secure public space for a
  massive Hindu festival to occur" (p. 50, folio not stated). The Conclusion generalises it: "The shrine's growing
  popularity, therefore, stems not only from its ancient history and references in some Sanskrit texts but also from
  the state of insecurity for Hindus as citizens of the Pakistani nation state." (p. 319, folio not stated) "Hinglaj's
  success as a site secluded from urban disturbances also indicates to some degree the current precarious
  circumstances of Hindus living in the Islamic Republic." (p. 319, folio not stated)
- **Violence against temples, as the immediate background.** "In March 2014, an attack on a Hindu temple in Larkana, a
  city in the north of Sindh, paralyzed the entire Hindu community of Pakistan. Rumors had spread that a Hindu man had
  torn or burned a few pages of the Quran. On the same night, March 15, an angry mob attacked the temple and set it on
  fire." (p. 17, folio not stated) "The rumor of the desecration of the Quran also caused rampage in other cities where
  Hindu shops were burned down and looted." (p. 17, folio not stated) "Only a few days later, on March 28, three men
  stormed a temple in Hyderabad and set it on fire, likely also related to this case." (p. 17, folio not stated)
  "Another incident followed in Tharparkar two days later." (p. 17, folio not stated) **None of the temples is named:
  (not stated).** The author "reached Hyderabad two days after the incident" and collected three competing rumours
  from "Friends at a local nongovernmental organization (NGO)" — an abandoned Muslim house cleaned out, a robbery and a
  refused remission, and a debt to be cancelled "by getting rid of the Hindu creditor" (p. 17, folio not stated) — and
  concludes: "Typically, the actual events are not discovered." (p. 17, folio not stated)
- **1992, and the memory of it at Karachi's Hinglaj temples.** "the memory of the situation in 1992, when in
  retaliation for the destruction of the Babri Mosque by Hindu extremists, hundreds of temples were demolished by angry
  mobs, and Hindu property was looted all over Pakistan" (p. 319, folio not stated). **No temple is named and no
  location is given for those demolitions: (not stated).** Two specific consequences are recorded in Karachi: "During
  the 1992 riots in the country, the Vagri community took shelter in the Hinglaj temple of the Jagnath `akhāṛā`"
  (p. 307, folio not stated), and "During the 1992 riots... the Nagnath temple was largely destroyed in the violence."
  (p. 307, folio not stated) The press correlate: a "steep increase around the year 1992 when Hindu zealots demolished
  the Babri Mosque in Ayodhya, causing violent outbursts against Hindus in Pakistan" (p. 15, folio not stated).
- **How festivals are held elsewhere in the country, by contrast.** "Loud celebrations are avoided in many places, for
  example, and critical talks concerning the religious parties or a negligent government occur only off the record.
  Religious festivals are held in private, often under police protection. After the spring festival Holi, when Hindus
  smear each other playfully with colorful powder, many clean themselves of the colors before they step back into the
  streets so as not to attract antagonism and violence." (p. 19, folio not stated) **The only festival named in that
  passage is Holi.**
- **Kidnapping, ransom and forced conversion.** "In rural areas, for example, wealthy Hindu families are frequently
  targets of kidnapping and ransom seeking. Hindu girls and women are often abducted, harassed, and forcibly converted
  to Islam (Schaflechner 2016b, 2017)." (p. 17, folio not stated)
- **Patronage and clan as the real security system.** "Kinship relations (within families, villages, or barādarīs
  [Urd. clan, group]) play an important role in such alliances and are often crucial for acquiring jobs, borrowing
  money for social events (funerals, marriages, etc.), or gaining protection (Chaudhary 2008). Non-Muslim communities
  frequently lack influential kinship ties that could protect them from assaults." (p. 18, folio not stated) "In rural
  Sindh, in particular, the clan or the barādarī serves as a guarantee of security rather than the nation state."
  (p. 18, folio not stated) An informant: "there are two kinds of governments in this country. One is the visible, for
  example the Zardari or Sharif government. But then there is the other, the invisible government, which is controlled
  by landlords or extremists, who are very powerful here." (p. 18, folio not stated)
- **The author refuses a minorities-only framing of insecurity.** "At times, it appears as if everybody in Pakistan
  belongs to some sort of minority" (p. 16, folio not stated); a Hindu lawyer, quoted from 2012, lists "Kidnapping,
  looting, robbery, killing, stealing, life threats, a lack of security… Everyone in Pakistan is insecure—the Sunni,
  the Shia, the Sindhi, the Mohajir, the Punjabi, the Balochi, the Hindu, the Christian, the rich, the poor—every
  Pakistani faces these problems. We Hindus are simply no exception." (p. 16, folio not stated) The author then
  qualifies: "While I generally agree with this assessment, there are, however, characteristic predicaments that many
  non-Muslims share, even if they do not appear as hard and fast rules but rather in the form of recurring patterns."
  (pp. 16–17, folios not stated)
- **Independence Day as a loyalty performance, and the shrine's networks put to it.** "National celebration days like
  Independence Day on August 14 are frequently used by Hindu organizations to show the community's loyalty to the
  state. This includes, for example, highly publicized marches in Karachi to the mausoleum of Mohammad Ali Jinnah, the
  founder of Pakistan, or staged political protests." (p. 23, folio not stated) Speeches "begin and end with 'Long
  live Pakistan' (Urd. pākistān zindābād)" (p. 23, folio not stated). The shrine's own welfare organisation runs it:
  the PHS "mobilize[s] Hindus from all over the country to march to Mohammad Ali Jinnah's mazār (Urd. tomb, mausoleum)
  on August 14, Pakistan's independence day" (p. 273, folio not stated); "These marches, which usually receive a small
  mention in the media, are staged annually to show the Hindu community's allegiance to the state of Pakistan."
  (p. 273, folio not stated)

### The Hindu minority of Pakistan: numbers, electorates, schooling, press

- **The population figure, with the book's own hedge attached.** "Today, Pakistan's Hindu community comprises between
  6 and 10 million people—an estimate that varies widely according to the source.1" (p. 13, folio not stated) "the vast
  majority—over 90 percent—belong to the underprivileged Hindu tribes or sub-castes, such as the Meghvar, the Bhil, and
  the Kohli, who frequently work as manual laborers for the powerful landowning elite in Sindh." (p. 13, folio not
  stated) Pakistan hosts "the world's fifth largest Hindu population" (p. 13, folio not stated). **Note marker `1` is a
  call only.** The endnote it calls: "The National Database and Registration Authority (NADRA) states that 1.4 million
  Computerized National Identity Cards (CNIC) were issued to Hindus in 2012. However, since only a total of 93 million
  Pakistanis currently possess a CNIC (out of an entire population of over 180 million, according to estimates by the
  World Bank), this is an incomplete estimate. Furthermore, most members of the Hindu community are landless peasants
  who often do not possess official documents. The source of the above estimate (6 to 10 million) is the Human Rights
  Commission of Pakistan (HRCP) in Sindh, which has been operating very closely with the Hindu community for many
  decades." (p. 28, folio not stated) **Four numerals — 1.4 million, 93 million, over 180 million, 6 to 10 million —
  all cleanly printed, and the author himself calls the first incomplete.**
- **A rival set of figures, quoted and not endorsed.** "Pakistani Hindus (with 3 million in 1998 and now around 5.5
  million) are the biggest religious minority of Pakistan. 92 percent of them are so-called lower caste Hindus
  (officially declared as Scheduled Castes by Pakistan Government, which means untouchables)... The so-called upper
  caste Hindus are only 8 percent of the total Hindu population of Pakistan." (p. 316, folio not stated, note 9) **The
  book prints this as a quotation from a Facebook page of the Hindu Sabha, "the Dalit organization", and marks it
  "[sic]"; it is that organisation's claim, not the author's count. It stands beside the 6–10 million estimate and the
  book does not reconcile them.**
- **Landlessness as the community's economic position.** "most members of the Hindu community are landless peasants who
  often do not possess official documents" (p. 28, folio not stated).
- **Who speaks for the community, and the economy behind it.** "Only a small minority have made a comfortable living in
  Pakistan for themselves through trade and other professions. This upper-class Hindu elite significantly influences
  the rest of the community as it provides authors, politicians (such as members of the Pakistan National Assembly),
  or, in the case of the temple of Hinglaj in Balochistan, temple committee members who claim the right to speak to
  and for the rest of the community." (p. 13, folio not stated) Against that: "Recent decades, nevertheless, have
  witnessed the increasing emancipation of the unprivileged sub-castes, who have begun to voice their own interests
  nationally and internationally." (p. 13, folio not stated)
- **The sects the book distinguishes**: local Sufi pīrs; the fourteenth-century saint Ramdevji; "the westernized and
  proselytizing ISKCON society" among "urban Hindu youths"; communities who worship "manifold Mother Goddesses as their
  clan or family patrons, who must be regularly appeased with blood sacrifices"; and "A different branch follows the
  teachings of the Guru Granth Sahib, better known as the holy book of the Sikhs." (pp. 13–14, folios not stated)
  **Blood sacrifice is stated there as a practice of clan-Goddess worship generally, not of Hinglaj; that passage
  attaches it to no named site.**
- **Electorates, a dated sequence.** "The abolition of separate electorates in 2002"; "The British Raj originally
  introduced separate electorates in 1909 to ensure Muslims greater representation in majority Hindu areas of British
  India. Governments in Pakistan maintained separate electorates in the years 1956 and 1973, and Zia-ul-Haq
  rejuvenated the system in 1985." (p. 15, folio not stated) "The separate electorates, together with the
  implementation of the blasphemy laws, created an environment in Pakistan in which political representation became
  directly linked to religious affiliation (Rais 2007)." (p. 15, folio not stated) "Under this system, Hindus were only
  able to vote for a Hindu candidate, which broadly marginalized them in their political activities." (p. 15, folio not
  stated) The verdict on the reform: "the joint system did not practically increase their political representation."
  (p. 15, folio not stated)
- **Reserved seats, counted.** "The 1973 constitution provided the first legal basis for minority political
  representation in Pakistan: under its terms, 10 seats out of 342 are reserved for non-Muslims in the national
  assembly, and a variable ratio is retained in the provincial assembly.5" (p. 15, folio not stated) "The Eighteenth
  Amendment, adopted in 2008, improved non-Muslim representation by adding four more seats for minorities in the
  Senate." (pp. 15–16, folios not stated) **The book dates the Eighteenth Amendment to 2008; that is what it prints and
  it is recorded as printed.** "Currently, the Pakistan Muslim League occupies six of the minority seats, while the
  Pakistan People's Party, the Pakistan Tehreek-e-Insaf, the Muttahida Qaumi Movement, and the Jamiat Ulema-e-Islam (F)
  occupy one seat each." (p. 16, folio not stated) The provincial counts, from endnote 5: "Currently, the Sindh
  province has 9 minority seats out of 168 … while the Punjab has 8 reserved minority seats out of 371", each with a
  provincial-assembly FAQ URL (p. 28, folio not stated). **The numerals are clean; the line is badly spaced.**
- **The school curriculum as an instrument, quoted by the author from secondary sources** (endnotes 6–8, p. 28, folio
  not stated): a class II Urdu textbook — "Our country is Pakistan. We live in our country. Pakistan is an Islamic
  country. Here Muslims live. Muslims believe in the unity of Allah. They do good deeds" (cited in Nayyar and Salim
  2005, 11); the national English curriculum of 2002 for classes XI and XII — "To educate and train the future
  generations of Pakistan as a true practicing Muslim" (Nayyar and Salim 2005, 11); and, in transliterated Urdu,
  "Hindū hameśah se islām kā duśman rahā hai," Urdu class V, Punjab Textbooks Board, Lahore 2002 (see Nayyar and Salim
  2005, 20); plus "an English textbook for class III (cited in Aziz 1993, 166f.)". **The Urdu sentence is printed in
  roman transliteration with full diacritics and no Arabic-script original is printed on that page; no English gloss is
  supplied by the book and none is supplied here.** In the body: "'The Hindu' therefore is not only a danger to national
  security but 'has always been an enemy of Islam.'7"; "references to India such as 'Bharat is the country of
  non-Muslims'8 intentionally omit the millions of Muslims living in India." (p. 20, folio not stated) "Such omissions
  enable children to draw a seemingly clear line of identity between 'us' and 'them' consistent with the two-nation
  theory (Saigol 2005, 1010)." (p. 20, folio not stated) His recommendation: "one of these changes would certainly be
  revising Pakistan's schoolbooks." (p. 20, folio not stated)
- **Press and broadcast liberalisation, with a counted series.** Censorship "was first lifted in 1988"; in 2002 the
  television landscape "was further liberalized, and many of the newly established channels started reporting on
  minority issues" (pp. 14–15, folios not stated). "English newspapers such as the News (established in 1991) and the
  Express Tribune (established in 2010) regularly cover Hindu festivals and cultural activities." (p. 15, folio not
  stated) **The count**: "while reporting on Hindu activity in Pakistan averaged twenty-eight articles per year between
  1987 and 2002 (with a steep increase around the year 1992…), the years 2003 to 2011 saw an average of a hundred
  articles annually about issues relating to Hindu communities. The year 2010 witnessed an especially large increase in
  reporting on minorities following the establishment of the Express Tribune.3" (p. 15, folio not stated) **Both
  figures are printed as words, not numerals — "twenty-eight" and "a hundred".** The catch: "Liberal news coverage often
  only appears in English and so frequently preaches to an already sympathetic English-speaking upper-middle class.4"
  (p. 15, folio not stated) The survey behind the figures is the author's own: "These figures are from my own study of
