  minority-issue reporting in English newspapers. The survey encompasses the years 1987–2011"; "Coverage in Sindhi
  newspapers, however, also often tends to have a liberal outlook and a supportive stance on Hindu issues." (p. 28,
  folio not stated) The papers surveyed are listed as "the Dawn, the News, the Daily Post, the Daily Times, the Nation,
  Business Recorder, the Pakistan Times, the Frontier Post, the Express Tribune, the Friday Times, and the Start
  Pakistan" (p. 28, folio not stated).
- **The pulp-fiction trade, with a circulation figure.** "The monthly horror magazine Dar Digest (Urd. Ḍar Ḍā'ijasṭ)
  serves as a clear case in point in this regard. Dar Digest is based in Karachi and has a circulation of around 10,000
  to 11,000 copies per month." (p. 21, folio not stated) The story analysed is "Duśmanī (Hostility; Shaikh 2014) from
  May 2014" (p. 21, folio not stated); its internal page citations run "(p. 131)", "(p. 133)", "(p. 134f.)",
  "(p. 137)", "(p. 138)", "(p. 139)", "(p. 140)" (pp. 21–22, folios not stated). **Those are the magazine's own pages;
  they are neither this book's pages nor PDF pages and must never be cited as such.**
- **Mass media as a participant at the shrine, not an observer.** The 2010 Gujarat party was organised in Pakistan by
  the HSM with a named organiser accompanying; "Local and national news picked up the story of the Indian pilgrims, and
  so two television teams came to the temple" (p. 48, folio not stated).

### New pan-Hindu institutions, and the welfare organisations at the shrine

- **The new organisations, with founding dates.** "Organizations like the Pakistan Hindu Council (established in 2006)
  or the Pakistan Hindu Seva Trust (established in 2010) appeal to the community as a whole." (p. 14, folio not stated)
  They "share an explicit pan-Hindu orientation and thus, differ from the customary paṃcāyat (Hin. council) systems,
  which mainly deal with issues relating to their own village, caste, or sect." (p. 14, folio not stated) They
  "distribute information, organize cultural and religious events, and stage protests concerning human rights issues";
  they "not only help to represent and describe Hindu life to a larger Pakistani public but also prescribe a certain
  Hindu identity to their own community.2" (p. 14, folio not stated) "most organizations adopting it have been
  established within the past decade" (p. 14, folio not stated).
- **The chronology in the author's own voice.** "most of the Hindu welfare organizations now active in Pakistan have
  been established in the past few years... Certainly, there were always local Hindu `paṃcāyat`s (Hin.), temple
  committees (like the HSM) and particular caste bodies representing the demands of their respective communities.
  However, the organizations explicitly working for the promotion of a general (national) Pakistani-Hindu community are
  a recent phenomenon." (p. 316, folio not stated, note 5) The same note gives "the oldest being the Pakistan Hindu
  Council, founded in 2006" (p. 316, folio not stated, note 5).
- **Two organisations active at the melā, with founding years — and a date that contradicts the Introduction.** "the
  Pakistan Hindu Seva Samaj (PHSS) and the Pakistan Hindu Seva Trust (PHS). While the PHSS was established quite
  recently, in 2012, the PHS began its work in 2010." (p. 272, folio not stated) Against that: "Officially, however,
  the PHS was only registered in 2011." (p. 316, folio not stated, note 5) **Both stand; the book does not reconcile
  "began its work in 2010" with "only registered in 2011", nor with the Introduction's "established in 2010" (p. 14,
  folio not stated).** The book also prints the PHSS's name two ways, "Pakistani Hindu Seva Samaj" and "Pakistan Hindu
  Seva Samaj" (pp. 157, 159, folios not stated).
- **What the PHS does at and around the shrine.** It "organizes a wide range of initiatives and uses the various melās
  at Hinglaj to make strategic contacts among Hindus in Karachi, Sindh, and, in fact, the whole country" (p. 272, folio
  not stated). "Almost every year, the PHS collects donations to fund the travel expenses of Hindu widows who wish to
  go to Hinglaj. In 2012, the organization printed leaflets with prayers in Sindhi and Urdu 'to help people' (logoṃ kī
  madad kar'ne ke lie) recite the correct prayers at the shrine." (p. 272, folio not stated) Volunteers distributing
  the flyers "simultaneously exchanged phone numbers and Facebook contacts with other devotees" (pp. 272–273, folio not
  stated), and the organisation "collects these contacts to mobilize people throughout the year" (p. 273, folio not
  stated). It "raises awareness of the problem of discrimination and caste stratification" (p. 317, folio not stated,
  note 28). It also cleaned "the Hindu graveyard in Purana Golimar, Karachi" in 2012, in an action "announced via the
  Internet and text messages" (p. 273, folio not stated) — with a burial note recorded as a correction: "This is indeed
  a graveyard where Hindus are buried, and not a `smaśān` (Hin. cremation ground)." (p. 315, folio not stated, note 7)
  **The graveyard's location is in the note's referring passage, outside that chunk: (not stated) there.** Its
  president "once told me that the avoidance of the slaughter in the cities was one of the main incentives for Hindus to
  come to the shrine at this time of the year." (p. 294, folio not stated) **The president is not named: (not
  stated).**
- **The PHSS, founded out of a fatal accident at the shrine.** "The PHSS is a comparatively younger organization,
  established in response to the tragic death of a walking pilgrim who was hit by a motorbike (see Chapter 4). The
  incident raised questions about the adequacy of security and medical facilities inside the valley and ultimately led
  to the founding of the PHSS." (p. 273, folio not stated) **The pilgrim is not named and the year is not given there:
  (not stated).** Initially it collected donations "in order to set up a medical camp at Hinglaj" (p. 273, folio not
  stated); "Many members also help the HSM volunteers clean up the valley after the festivals" (p. 273, folio not
  stated). "In 2013, just one year after its establishment, the PHSS had already begun organizing religious functions
  in the Narayanpur neighborhood and had started collecting money for the construction of an ashram in Karachi's
  Defence Colony." (p. 273, folio not stated) It uses "identification cards and yellow T-shirts sporting the
  organization's logo" (p. 273, folio not stated).
- **The PHSS's seven stated aims, from Urdu flyers distributed at the Hinglaj melā in 2012** (p. 273, folio not
  stated): "To help poor families and widows with their monthly expenses"; "To assist impoverished youth to arrange
  their marriage"; "To provide free medical treatment"; "To establish medical camps at pilgrimages"; "To supply poor
  children with education and scholarships"; "To establish a society for the donation of blood"; "To establish camps
  for flood-affected people".
- **Who the two organisations reach.** "Both organizations—the PHS and the PHSS—appeal mainly to members of the Dalit
  communities; although the PHS is patronized by high-caste philanthropists, for the most part, its core message of
  Hindu unity and its community-based social work attracts young, low-class urban Hindu men who are accessible through
  social media." (pp. 273–274, folio not stated) "However, sometimes young Christians and even some Muslim men join the
  initiatives by these organizations… Muslim support is in particular often highlighted by the Hindu organizations in
  the media coverage of their events." (p. 274, folio not stated)
- **A request the committee refused.** The HSM "once turned down the request of a local scholar who had approached the
  organization for financial support to publish his work about the Goddess" (p. 317, folio not stated, note 17).
  **The scholar is not named: (not stated).**
- **ISKCON as a distribution machine.** Its "publications are disproportionately present at festivals and in temples
  where religious literature is sold." (p. 305, folio not stated) It "has enjoyed particular success among the Dalit
  (untouchable, from Hin. meaning 'broken') communities in urban areas" (p. 305, folio not stated), and its influence
  in Narayanpur has risen "with its rejection of caste and open proselytization" (p. 157, folio not stated). "The only
  Bhagavad Gītā translation into Urdu I have encountered so far is that of Shri Prabhupada, who has a particular
  interest in the principle of ahiṃsā." (p. 305, folio not stated) **No dates and no place for Prabhupada: (not
  stated).**

### Cross-border pilgrimage: visas, the market, and the border as a filter

- **Border bureaucracy as the Indian pilgrim's institution.** "one of the biggest hurdles for Indians who want to do the
  yātrā today is getting a visa for Pakistan. Further, they must obtain an additional visa allowing travel to
  Balochistan, a province that has been in a state of a latent insurgency since the establishment of Pakistan (Axmann
  2012). At the time of writing, to obtain such a visa—aside from the general formalities like a valid passport, visa
  fee, and so forth—an Indian applicant was also required to present evidence of a sponsor in Pakistan to be
  responsible for their safety. Further, at the time of writing, once in Pakistan, an Indian visa holder must register
  at a police station within twenty-four hours of arrival, after any change of place, and again twenty-four hours
  before their departure.5" (p. 169, folio not stated) "Access to Balochistan is further limited by its legal ambiguity
  since 'Non-Objection Certificates' (NOC) must be obtained from the government, but the legal status of an application
  for such a certificate is unclear." (p. 169, folio not stated) Note 5 cites "http://www.dgip.gov.pk/Files/VisaforIndian.aspx"
  (p. 193, folio not stated); **note 6 cites "Times of India, March 20, 2007 (Chatterjeel 2007" `[text-layer?]`
  [Chatterjee?] with a URL whose slug reads "2007-03-10" (p. 193, folio not stated) — the note's date and its own URL
  disagree and neither is preferred here.**
- **A stalled bilateral process.** "Discussions began in 2007 regarding the inclusion of Hinglaj in a list of religious
  places that can be mutually visited by citizens of each country, but so far, those discussions have produced no
  successful outcome.6 At the time of writing, both parties were in the process of amending the visa formalities for
  group traveling, but relations between the two countries remain volatile." (p. 169, folio not stated)
- **The pilgrimage as a market for intermediaries, priced in three figures.** "a market dedicated to the organization of
  such cross-border journeys has developed" and "members of such communities are willing to pay huge amounts of money to
  complete it" (p. 183, folio not stated). Aguā 1 "charges around 60,000 Indian rupees (at that time approximately €700)
  for the organization of the journey, including the cost of the flight from Bombay to Karachi" (p. 183, folio not
  stated). "the major tour organizers, who usually charge 40,000 to 60,000 Indian rupees, depending on the mode of
  transportation. A journey with the help of aguā 2 costs between 30,000 and 45,000 Indian rupees." (p. 185, folio not
  stated) **"at that time" is not converted to a year: (not stated).** "This opportunity to change one's position within
  society and earn respect through the pilgrimage has also produced an industry on both sides of the border that
  organizes trips for substantial sums of money." (p. 192, folio not stated) "Such logistical challenges may be
  problematic for pilgrims to Hinglaj, but, on the other hand, they also present an opportunity for many travel agents
  with good connections to embassies and consulates to make some money from devotees who are keen on traveling to the
  shrine." (p. 169, folio not stated)
- **What pilgrims sell to pay.** "Some of the members of his pilgrimage group had been saving up money for years or had
  even sold land or cattle to be able to afford the journey." (p. 183, folio not stated) A prospective Ahir pilgrim's
  family "was apparently well off and had offered to pay for the trip with money earned through renting a tractor to
  other farmers" (p. 182, folio not stated).
- **Bribery as an established cost.** "Usually the Pakistani tour organizers were able to ensure a smooth process due to
  their even distribution of money among clerks and the police staff." (p. 184, folio not stated) "This logic of bribery
  and networking has become an important part of the pilgrimage in the era of modern-day nation states and is a
  lucrative business for Indian and Pakistani tour organizers." (p. 185, folio not stated) Rajendra Singh "was also very
  critical of certain wealthy Charan businessmen who, in his opinion, simply bribed some embassy clerks in order to
  obtain their visas" (p. 177, folio not stated). Also: "Since such orders are apparently not strictly enforced, the
  journey has no proper legal status and thus is likely to involve bribery and nepotism." (p. 169, folio not stated)
- **Aguā 2's counter-business, and what it cannot do.** He "lives in a major Pakistani city", is "in contact with Indian
  devotees mainly from Gujarat", "organizes tours for up to seven people", houses pilgrims "in his private house", rents
  "a small bus" for the Hinglaj trip, and helps "with the police registration and the dealings with the army on the
  road" through connections said to follow from a business "important for the military" (p. 185, folio not stated). But
  "since this guide operates only from Pakistan, he has limited connections in India and thus cannot be of much help in
  the visa application process" (p. 185, folio not stated). **His city is deliberately unnamed: (not stated).**
- **The tour operators' dependence on bad relations.** "the political volatility between the countries is a
  double-edged sword; the livelihood of such tour operators depends on the political atmosphere between the two
  countries remaining tense as well as upon the appointing of certain known individuals into influential offices"
  (p. 186, folio not stated). And, theoretically: "the rigid control of movement at and around the geopolitical border
  can actually generate new business opportunities and prompt changes in power distribution" (p. 183, folio not stated).
- **Visa waiting times as pilgrims report them.** "There were five of us who wanted to go—three from Gandhidham
  [Gujarat] and one from Calcutta. The guy from Calcutta had tried for over ten years to get the visa, the Gujaratis had
  even tried for fourteen years! We all went to Delhi and with the grace of the Goddess we got the visa right away."
  (p. 175, folio not stated) Generally: "Many would-be pilgrims try to obtain visas for years, receiving repeated
  rejections." (p. 184, folio not stated) Access for the Charans specifically: "Today, only rarely can Charans obtain
  visas for the journey to Pakistan; however, when they do, they must do so with the help of agents, in many cases as a
  part of tours organized by Pakistani tour operators." (p. 172, folio not stated)
- **How the shrine's contact details travelled, and the invitation letter.** "About four months later, an acquaintance
  from Jaipur who had gone to Hinglaj in 1991 gave me the phone number of the Hinglaj Sheva Mandali in Karachi and I
  contacted them. About seven or eight months after that the invitation letter came." (p. 175, folio not stated) **An
  invitation letter is therefore part of the visa apparatus.**
- **The foreign-pilgrim business the HSM kept.** "To this day, in spite of having ceased to organize transportation from
  within Pakistan, the HSM continues to coordinate transportation for visitors coming from abroad. The Mandali caters in
  particular to devotees from India who often wish to book a fully organized trip due to visa and security concerns."
  (p. 83, folio not stated) "The ease of journeys organized with the help of the Mandali is frequently described in
  newer Hindi travelogues, helping to increase the HSM's profile and contributing to the influx of visitors (Barhath
  2006; Ratnu 2007). The role as a link between the Pakistani-Hindu world and a more international clientele adds to
  the Mandali's aura and its authority among Hindus in Pakistan." (p. 83, folio not stated) Also: "each year, several
  groups from India manage to obtain visas and travel to Pakistan to visit the shrine" (p. 69, folio not stated).
- **The shielded tour.** "The group was completely shielded from the outside world, and contact with (non-Hindu)
  Pakistanis was extremely limited." (p. 183, folio not stated) "Such kinds of `tunnel-`/`pilgrimages`[text-layer?]
  [tunnel-pilgrimages] are made possible through close collaboration between the Indian aguā and the local Pakistani
  tour organizers." (pp. 183–184, folio not stated)
- **Travel danger as a deterrent, and as rumour the author debunks.** "During my research in India, I encountered many
  elaborate rumors of Indian pilgrims either being killed or kidnapped on their way to Hinglaj. All of these stories
  turned out to be false. It is because of such rumors that many pilgrims are reluctant to go on the pilgrimage to
  Balochistan on their own." (pp. 100–101, folio not stated)
- **Video as advertising.** "The video he took of the trip was widely circulated among the devotees of Hinglaj,
  encouraging others to apply for visas. This also served as advertisement for the work of the HSM, who benefitted from
  the circulation as well." (p. 176, folio not stated)
- **Taking stones home as temple-founding matter — and the rule contested inside the community.** "one of the most
  important parts of the journey was that he had the chance to take several stones (Hin. piṃḍ, Skt. piṇḍa) from the
  River Aghor with him" (p. 173, folio not stated). The reported rule: "you can't establish a Hinglaj temple without
  having gone on the pilgrimage to the original [mūl] Hinglaj in Pakistan. You have to go there and you have to take
  something such as a triśūl or a piṃḍ with you." (p. 175, folio not stated) Distribution of the five stones: "Two of
  them—in his reckoning, images of Bhairava and Hinglaj—were installed in the newly constructed temple in his hometown
  in Mandapi. Another one he placed in his family shrine in Jaipur, and two he presented to his family." (p. 176, folio
  not stated) **Contested**: "Other Charans were puzzled by his interpretation and did not support the idea that it is
  absolutely necessary to have been at Hinglaj to establish one of her temples." (p. 176, folio not stated) "Tokens from
  Balochistan are held in high esteem, and some of them are still kept in temples to this day." (p. 172, folio not
  stated)

### Print, film and the devotional publishing economy

- **A devotional print economy in four vernaculars.** Ritual manuals, mytho-historical narratives, travelogues, video
  documentations and institutions are produced by Brahmakshatriya, Charan, Kapri and Lohana devotees (p. 58, folio not
  stated), with three specific books dated 1905 (Gujarati), 1973 (Sindhi) and c. 2006 (Hindi) (p. 61, folio not stated),
  and a 1934 information-gathering date behind the Sindhi one (p. 61, folio not stated).
- **Publication as infrastructure, tied to the road.** "Together with the HSM's publicity work in relation to the annual
  phālgun/caitra gathering, various authors writing in Hindi, Urdu, and Sindhi also began promoting the shrine in
  Balochistan and often refer to the HSM in their publications." (p. 84, folio not stated) "This growth is also evident
  in the lengthening list of publications about the temple, which correspond to the completion of the first part of the
  Makran Coastal Highway (MCH) up to the River Hingol." (p. 84, folio not stated) "The organization certainly has done
  most of the work in propagating the image of the place as an important tīrtha yātrā for all Hindus." (p. 84, folio not
  stated)
- **Film and translation as distribution channels.** The Bengali novel of 1957, the Hindi translation of 1964, the 1959
  film, and the Rāmāyaṇa's "very successful television series in the late 1980s, which was refilmed in 2012" (pp. 119,
  126, folios not stated). "Both of these works contributed hugely to the dissemination of the pilgrimage narrative among
  the wider public." (p. 126, folio not stated) "the motion picture was also already widely known outside the
  Bengali-speaking area" (p. 145, folio not stated). The asymmetry: "Today, Abadhut's story is almost forgotten in
  Pakistan, and only a few people have heard of the film. In India, however, the account is well known not only among
  Hinglaj devotees." (p. 135, folio not stated)
- **The book trade around the shrine, observed in the field — and the author's own intervention in it.** The author
  "gave Shastri's book to one of my most valuable informants in India"; "Rajendra's ears pricked up in particular when I
  mentioned Devadatta Shastri's book, and he made me promise to give him the book as soon as possible"; and afterwards
  Rajendra "offered large parts of Shastri's description to make his point clear. Other questions I asked were also
  often answered with details and events from the book I had given him just a few hours ago." (pp. 136–137, folios not
  stated) The author frames it as demonstration, not anecdote: "An example from my fieldwork demonstrates this."
  (p. 136, folio not stated)
- **How far the texts actually reach, with the author's own caveat.** "It is difficult to measure the influence of such
  texts and ritual manuals on the shrine and its practices... while I doubt that many visitors at the shrine of Hinglaj
  read these texts and manuals themselves, religious authorities often seem to gain their respectability through the
  study of such texts." (p. 315, folio not stated, note 1) And: "for those living in Pakistan, the shrine is in general
  'experienced' more than it is read about in texts" (p. 146, folio not stated). But also: "devotees often treat texts on
  Hinglaj as guidebooks rather than as literary prose" (p. 125, folio not stated).
- **The author's own films, cited with timecodes in the body and without them in the Works Cited.** "Schaflechner,
  Jürgen. 2011. On Becoming Gods. Film", "2012. Walking Pilgrims. Film", "2013. Mother Calling: Kali in Karachi. Film",
  "2014. There They Call Us Hindus. Here We Are Pakistanis. Film", "2016b. Thrust Into Heaven. Film", all
  "www.juergen-schaflechner.com", plus 2015a, 2015b, 2016a and 2017 in print (p. 335, folio not stated). **The timecodes
  appear in the body chapters, not in the Works Cited.** Note 13 describes the filmed trip: "I accompanied the group on
  almost all of their stops during this five-day trip and also visited some of them a year later in Gujarat. This trip
  and their lives twelve months later back in Gujarat are documented in the film 'On Becoming Gods' (Schaflechner
  2011)." (p. 193, folio not stated) Note 16 adds: "One variation of this speech is found in Schaflechner (2011, 7:00)."
  (p. 193, folio not stated)
- **The camera as an actor in the field.** "The video camera in particular affected the group dynamic"; "Many pilgrims
  apparently overestimated my influence on the media landscape and were of the opinion that they would appear on
  television in Europe or America"; "The interviews, therefore, had a double audience: an imagined 'Western' public as
  well as the rest of the pilgrimage group" (p. 180, folio not stated). Pilgrims used it to "amend stereotypes about the
  country in front of an implicit audience" (p. 180, folio not stated).
- **Nineteenth-century merchant patronage, as the economic history behind the vegetarian turn.** "wealthy merchants took
  on the patronage of temples—mainly in the Vaishnava tradition—or cow shelters (Hin. gauśālā)" (p. 301, folio not
  stated); "a certain moral code of dharma had at that time become increasingly important among traders for the sake of
  their reputation and 'credit-worthiness' (Bayly 1999, 217)" (p. 301, folio not stated).
- **One date the back matter itself states, and it is about the book, not a shrine.** "All of the links listed here were
  last accessed on June 1, 2017 unless otherwise specified." (p. 337, folio not stated)

### The author's argument (1): "solidification of tradition"

*Merged from both arguments extracts, deduplicated. The concept is the book's own coinage and it is the thing a shrine
entry citing this book is actually borrowing.*

- **The concept, defined at its first appearance.** "During the course of this process, certain practices at Hinglaj
  became labeled as the 'real,' 'true,' or 'proper' ways to engage with the Goddess, whereas others were termed as
  'fake' or 'notional.' In this book, I call these intricate dynamics of inclusion and exclusion 'a solidification of
  tradition,' and one of my major goals is to utilize this concept for my case study." (p. 29, folio not stated) And in
  full: "It is this attempt by many actors to produce norms and a fairly uncontested history, which I suggest to call a
  solidification of tradition. A solidifying tradition, thus, has not (yet) convinced most of its critics, its
  contingent foundations are still very much visible, while a sedimented tradition has already succeeded in creating a
  reasonably undisputed terrain." (p. 47, folio not stated)
- **The distinction from sedimentation is temporal, and he declines to harden it.** "I suggest to understand
  solidification as acts that follow recent moments of dislocation, while sedimented social practices pertain to already
  fairly naturalized phenomena whose last dislocating moment has long since passed." (p. 47, folio not stated) "Though
  I am not proposing to establish a hard and fast border" (p. 47, folio not stated). Restated in the Conclusion:
  "unlike an already sedimented tradition, solidification describes a phenomenon that has not yet had the time to
  coagulate into a (fairly) uncontested terrain. To succeed, such processes require time and facilities for repeated
  propagation." (p. 324, folio not stated) And, plainly: "what I have witnessed during my research and what is still
  obvious today is the tradition's dynamics of becoming solid" (p. 324, folio not stated); "The HSM's and other
  individual actors' efforts to present certain practices and narrative elements as intrinsic, solid, or natural parts
  of the Goddess's tradition represent tangible forms of what is here termed solidification." (p. 324, folio not stated)
- **Why he built a new term rather than using Laclau's or Butler's.** "None of these metaphors, however, is well suited
  to help describe the events documented at Hinglaj." (p. 46, folio not stated) The charge: Laclau and Butler "avoid
  introducing the operational means of sedimentation"; "A way to measure the concatenation of repeated articulations,
  necessary to speak of sedimented structures, is not supplied." (p. 46, folio not stated) "Laclau fails to clarify what
  he means by a tendency to objectivize, and at no point is a detailed description of the dimensions and texture of
  sedimentation given." (pp. 44–45, folios not stated) "All of these attempts show that the Laclauian theory of
  sedimentation lacks in explanatory force and, hence, needs to be adapted to each individual case study." (p. 45,
  folio not stated) "Similar to Laclau, Butler, too, omits the question of when exactly we are able to speak of
  sedimented structures" (p. 45, folio not stated). **The borrowed terms he is measuring himself against**: Husserl's
  sedimentation via Laclau, routinisation in which "the original intuition which gave rise to them is completely
  forgotten" (Laclau 1990, 34) (p. 43, folio not stated); Laclau's version as "a process in which the `“forgetting of
  the origins’`[text-layer?] ["forgetting of the origins"] tends to occur" (Laclau 1990, 34) (p. 46, folio not stated);
  Butler, who "utilizes sedimentation for criticizing the naturalization of power relations through sex" (p. 46, folio
  not stated) and for whom sedimentation "describes the production of foundations, which themselves are the outcome of
  prior sedimentations… Every sedimentation is, therefore, always already a second sedimentation (Marchart 2013b,
  192)—which makes its origin a moot point." (p. 45, folio not stated); and Torfing's definition quoted in full as "an
  institutional ensemble of rules, norms, values and regularities, which we take for granted in our everyday life.
  [Sedimented structures are] not subjected to the ongoing practices of constitution and subversion. (Torfing 2003,
  70)" (p. 46, folio not stated). Note 9 of chapter 1 grounds the coinage in dictionaries: "Both entries are taken from
  Merriam-Webster http://www.merriam-webster.com." (p. 50, folio not stated)
- **The mechanism he names for it.** "a continuous interpellation of certain characteristics as the tradition's natural
  elements" (p. 324, folio not stated); a rhetoric that "follows a certain regulative norm that refrains from crudely
  direct inclusion or exclusion of certain elements" yet "manages… to establish a certain normative ideal of the
  Hinglaj tradition by linking itself to the notions of enlightenment and progress" (p. 324, folio not stated).
- **It is not a top-down process — argued explicitly, twice.** "the solidification of the Hinglaj tradition is not a
  top-down process but a form of struggle emerging from the clashes between different truth-claims." (p. 293, folio not
  stated) "The case study shows how the solidification of tradition is not a top-to-bottom endeavor but rather the
  outcome of a negotiation process between crowd, landscape, and institution." (p. 325, folio not stated) "This example
  shows that the solidification of the Hinglaj tradition is not (only) the work of omnipotent actors and institutions
  but rather the outcome of a set of regulating norms, which are products of, but also producing, the engagement of
  various communities interacting with each other at the shrine." (p. 325, folio not stated) "solidification is not
  necessarily a one-way street" (p. 268, folio not stated).
- **Retroactive meaning — the move that makes the concept usable on other shrines.** "An examination of the history of
  the ban will show how contingent events were retroactively assigned with meaning, which ultimately led to the
  representation of animal sacrifice as 'improper' Hindu behavior." (p. 293, folio not stated) "contingent—possible but
  not necessary—events led to the formation of a prohibition which then was retroactively interpreted by visitors and
  the organization as an 'actual' element of the tradition." (p. 296, folio not stated) "once the temple committee
  adopted and enforced the prohibition, this debarment was retroactively established as a typical (read: natural)
  characteristic of worship at the shrine" (p. 325, folio not stated). "The case study of the Hinglaj shrine shows how
  meaning is retroactively assigned to contingent circumstances, which then transform into significant truth-claims
  inside the valley." (p. 314, folio not stated) "while the prohibition may have begun as a result of unintentional
  influences, the HSM utilizes these incidents and solidifies them (through annual repetition) into the 'true' Hindu
  practice at the shrine." (p. 314, folio not stated)
- **Two paradigm cases the author himself nominates.** The tiling of the temple: "This is a paradigmatic example of
  attempts that aim to solidify a certain truth-claim as universal through the systematic exclusion of its contesting
  elements." (p. 325, folio not stated) And the Hanuman statue: "I see this as a paradigmatic example for the HSM's
  cultural hegemony" (p. 221, folio not stated).
- **The scope condition — which other shrines the framework is claimed to travel to.** "this methodology is particularly
  useful in cases where traditions have recently undergone significant changes and are now in the process of
  re-evaluating their central concepts." (p. 38, folio not stated) "This analysis is particularly useful after a
  tradition's dislocation or, in other words, when a group is in upheaval. Then, the contingent foundations of
  truth-claims are still visible, and their exclusionary logic is conspicuous." (p. 270, folio not stated)
- **And the declaration that the discourse is not closed.** "the banning of `bali` does not represent a stable marker
  for 'Hinduness' as some Pakistani Hindus, and the HSM, would wish it to be. In other words, the sliding of meaning
  has not been suspended—the discourse is not closed, and the tradition at the shrine not (yet) 'sedimented' (Butler
  2011; Laclau 1990)." (p. 315, folio not stated)

### The author's argument (2): truth-claims, nodal points, hegemony

- **The governing question, asked twice.** "How is it possible to organize a field of voices in which everyone lays
  claim to speak 'the truth' about the Goddess Hinglaj?" (p. 29, folio not stated); "How is it possible to organize a
  plurality of overlapping—but also opposing—truth-claims visible in myriad interviews, texts, or videos?" (p. 31,
  folio not stated) The problem behind it: "Doing fieldwork at the site together with engaging with a variety of texts
  and other media confronted me with the question of how to organize all of these voices that uniformly claimed to
  speak the truth about the shrine and its annexed practices." (p. 24, folio not stated)
- **"Truth-claims" defined.** "In the following, I will call such references, which define what Hinglaj is through
  exclusion, that is, through what Hinglaj is not—'truth-claims.' Such truth-claims are an important characteristic of
  many (learned and/or devoted) opinions on Hinglaj." (pp. 30–31, folios not stated) The evidence base for the term:
  "In many of the sources I analyzed, whether written or oral, claims about Hinglaj were often introduced with words
  such as the 'real,' the 'actual,' or the 'proper' meaning of the tradition. Many authors and interviewees sought to
  point out some 'true' practice at the shrine or an 'actual' story behind certain events." (p. 30, folio not stated)
- **Normative vocabulary as politics.** "The events at Hinglaj constantly display normative categories, for example,
  adjectives like the 'actual,' the 'real,' and the 'proper,' which already pose a political decision. The repeated
  exclusion happening through such truth-claims, ultimately, sediments social practices into naturalized objectivities."
  (p. 49, folio not stated) And their performativity: "These truth-claims, however, are constative statements that not
  only hold true or not but also reveal their performative character. Truth-claims make things happen and thus bear an
  influence on people and their practices." (p. 323, folio not stated)
- **The two-step theoretical move, announced.** "First, I will engage with the so-called ontological turn, which
  promises a novel approach to a multitude of truth—or ontological—claims. In a second step, I will engage with the
  discipline of 'post-foundationalism' to emphasize how such claims are produced through hegemony and political
  struggle." (p. 31, folio not stated)
- **The ontological turn, adopted in part.** Its appeal is to "take others and their real difference seriously" (Candea
  in Carrithers et al. 2010, 175) and to "describe the ontologies of non-Western peoples without recourse (or with as
  little recourse as possible) to our own ontological assumptions" (Course 2010, 247) (p. 32, folio not stated).
  Applied: "The ontological turn, thus, shows how a variety of practices produce the shrine and its tradition instead
  of merely providing us with different opinions on an object 'out there.' Instead of only one Hinglaj that is
  discovered by the anthropologist through assembling and evaluating various truth-claims, we now need to speak of many
  Hinglajs and many ritual journeys produced out of a multitude of truth-claims." (pp. 33–34, folios not stated) —
  sharpened with a bracketed substitution into Law and Lien's sentence: "different and multiple [Hinglajs] subsist in
  different and multiple worlds" (Law and Lien 2013, 4) (p. 34, folio not stated).
- **The ontological turn, then refused.** "If there are ontologies in plural, are we not creating an ontology of plural
  ontologies—a meta-ontology? Furthermore, how can these ontologies be differentiated if there is no meta-ontology
  providing their differentiating principle? In other words, why are ontologies multiple yet not arbitrary?" (p. 34,
  folio not stated) "the discipline fails to further engage with the notion of multiple ontologies, a step that, even
  though it aims to break with foundational claims, produces yet another one—this time the claim that ontologies, in
  fact, are multiple." (p. 34, folio not stated) **He keeps its performativity and drops its flatness**: its "emphasis
  on practice and performativity is helpful for engaging with such truth-claims at eye-level… it does not explain how
  they engage with each other" (p. 49, folio not stated).
- **Post-foundationalism, adopted as the method.** It "puts forward a theory of hegemony and political struggle
  explaining the production and interaction of multiple truth-claims by rendering them as 'nodal points'" and "avoids
  the highly charged term 'ontology' and its recent inflationary use for the sake of the concept 'foundational'"
  (p. 35, folio not stated). Its paradox: "the absence of an uncontested foundation makes repeated attempts to
  establish such a singular and unified foundation necessary" (p. 35, folio not stated); "the absence of an ultimate
  foundation leads to its necessary production (Marchart 2010, 63–64)" (p. 36, folio not stated); it "assumes that last
  foundations are constantly attempted but never completely attained." (p. 49, folio not stated)
- **Derrida applied directly to the Hinglaj archive — the single most transferable passage in the book for anyone
  building a shrine archive.** "Derrida's first argument relates to the impossibility of any subject position to master
  a field's empirical richness. Applied to this case, no research, no institution, and no individual would ever be able
  to overlook the totality of information that has ever been produced on the Goddess Hinglaj and her tradition."
  (p. 36, folio not stated) "even if we were able to cover the totality of voices engaged with the shrine at Hinglaj
  and its practices, no situation would be imaginable in which they all would agree on one central element as its
  undisputable axis mundi." (p. 36, folio not stated)
- **The methodological payoff, stated plainly.** "Translating this into a methodology for this work shifts my attention
  to the history of truth-claims and their contingent foundations. Because the establishment of undisputed foundations
  is impossible, however, dynamics at the shrine can only be identified as processes of solidifying such foundations.
  Due to the relatively recent dislocation of the tradition, the contingency within many of these truth-claims is
  conspicuous and so is the attempt to objectivize or solidify them." (p. 37, folio not stated)
- **Laclau and Mouffe's apparatus as the book uses it.** Articulation, discourse, moments and elements (the block
  quotation from Laclau and Mouffe 2001, 105, pp. 38–39, folios not stated); nodal points as Lacanian "point de
  capiton", "Often translated as a 'quilting point'… the fixation button in upholstery, which shapes and grips the
  fabric underneath" (p. 40, folio not stated); and the bridge to the case: "Similar to how nodal points structure
  their discursive environment, truth-claims, too, aim to organize the field of possibilities around them by excluding
  ambivalence and polysemy." (p. 41, folio not stated) Restated in Chapter 6: "Nodal points unite a group of people and
  supply it with coherency and meaning. The 'equivalential' (Laclau and Mouffe 2001) relationship emerging between
  these people stems from a common opposition to an outside, which is antagonized by all members. In other words, group
  unity will be defined through its constitutive outside." (p. 269, folio not stated) "A nodal point represents, often
  temporarily, a group of heterogeneous members through their common denial of something 'fake.' The same is true for
  truth-claims about Hinglaj, as they supply a group with coherence and closure through a commonly shared denial."
  (pp. 269–270, folio not stated) **And the limit he sets on his own device**: "it is not possible to assume only one
  single nodal point within one particular discourse (Laclau and Mouffe 2001, 139). There is a multitude of nodal
  points and truth-claims that make up Hinglaj." (p. 306, folio not stated)
- **Logic of equivalence, and why groups need enemies.** "The representation of any group (and groups are also a
  particular organization of symbols and representations) requires the establishment of at least one 'anti-group' or
  'enemy' so that its key concepts may be organized… the identity of the chain of equivalence is not provided by some
  common denominator that is found in its equivalent elements but rather through its border, that is, through a common
  negation of some excluded other." (p. 41, folio not stated) **He applies this to caste and to religious community
  directly**: "This rejection of any given foundation within a society can also be applied to supposedly 'fixed'
  identities such as castes or religious communities." (p. 38, folio not stated)
- **The worked example is not Hindu at all: the 1953 anti-Ahmedi commission.** "When the Pakistani government appointed
  a commission in 1953 to investigate the reasons behind the anti-Ahmedi riots, a group of Muslim legal scholars was
  formed to define once and for all who should be considered a true Muslim. After days of debate, their lack of
  consensus caused them to declare: …" followed by the block quotation "(Cited in Shaikh 2009, 61; italics, brackets,
  and sic erat scriptum in the original)" (p. 269, folio not stated). The lesson drawn: "it is always possible to add
  one more meaning to a universal term… Only through the negative definition—through the definition of what the group
  is not—the chain of characteristics is closed" (p. 269, folio not stated); with Farzana Shaikh's conclusion "that the
  anti-Ahmedi sentiment in Pakistan was also a means to bring a feeling of unity to the various Muslim schools in the
  formative period of the Islamic Republic (Shaikh 2009)" (p. 269, folio not stated).
- **Hegemony as the substitute for the absent foundation.** From Gramsci's power over "subordinate classes" "through
  combining coercion and persuasion (Simon 1991, 24)" (p. 41, folio not stated), freed by Laclau and Mouffe of "class
  consciousness and the singularity of hegemonic centers" (p. 41, folio not stated), to "hegemony needs to take its
  place as a de facto objectivity" (p. 42, folio not stated). In Chapter 6, hegemony is "a relation, not of domination
  by means of force, but of consent by means of political and ideological leadership" (Simon 1991, 24) (p. 289, folio
  not stated); with Griggs and Howarth, "the production of 'common sense' in a particular site or sphere of the social"
  and "the winning of 'intellectual and moral leadership' in society, and not just the achievement of political power"
  (Griggs and Howarth, 2008, 130) (p. 289, folio not stated). **Applied to the custodians directly**: "It is precisely
  this production of 'common sense' through 'moral leadership' and a combination of coercion and persuasion that
  characterizes the HSM's modus operandi at the shrine of Hinglaj." (p. 289, folio not stated)
- **The HSM universalises a caste tradition — the sharpest single sentence in the book about custodianship.** "The
  tradition of the HSM—that is, of the Lasi Lohana—seeks to elevate itself to the level of the universal tradition
  rather than simply being one of many. It eliminates other claims to truth by labeling them 'less useful,' 'fake,' or
  'outdated.'" (p. 289, folio not stated) "'Hindu unity'—a concept implying the idea that there exists an 'actual,'
  'eternal,' or 'real' way of practicing at the shrine—is the central myth at Hinglaj." (p. 289, folio not stated)
  Consensus there is "a 'temporary result of a provisional hegemony' (Mouffe 2000, 104) brought about by the HSM"
  (p. 289, folio not stated), and that power "is not a widely sedimented autonomous agency but rather in constant
  dialogue with the landscape and the crowds at the shrine" (p. 289, folio not stated).
- **Governmentality, and why the custodians do not simply forbid.** The author uses Foucault's governmentality "as an
  approach to power that analyzes the structures, practices, and institutions designed to influence people's behaviors"
  (p. 287, folio not stated), distinguishing the term's two senses after Füller and Marquardt 2009 (p. 287, folio not
  stated), quoting Foucault 1982, 789 ("an action upon an action") and Foucault 1982, 790 ("[p]ower is exercised only
  over free subjects") (pp. 287–288, folio not stated), and Howarth 2010, 321 (p. 287, folio not stated). Applied: "The
  HSM does not directly impose its notions of 'proper' behavior onto visitors since doing so would only trigger (more)
  resistance by many. Instead, the organization connects its understanding of the shrine to wider notions of progress
  and education" (p. 288, folio not stated). "Their demands, spread by institutional means or inscriptional devices,
  were formulated in order to change the behavior of people visiting the shrine." (p. 288, folio not stated) The
  organisation's members "can also rely on a strong ideological backing from the Pakistani-Hindu intelligentsia and
  from several Hindu welfare organizations that validate these restrictions" (p. 288, folio not stated). Fairclough is
  used for the same point: power as "controlling and constraining the contributions of non-powerful participants"
  (Fairclough 2001b, 38f.) (p. 261, folio not stated).
- **The education/progress discourse, and the contingency claim under it.** "the HSM—a body consisting mainly of (Lasi)
  Lohana caste members—propagates its own interpretation of the Hinglaj tradition and simultaneously denies those of
  many others. In this process, they create a hierarchical discourse of 'educated,' and thus 'proper,' behavior (that
  which comfortably coincides with the Lasi Lohana tradition) versus 'uneducated,' and thus 'improper,' behavior inside
  the valley." (p. 277, folio not stated) "the selection of practices that are ultimately denied or supported by the
  temple organization is essentially contingent" (p. 277, folio not stated). He places the HSM "in accordance with
  reformist Hindu movements and their historical rejection of folk traditions (Fuller 1992, 99–103)" (p. 277, folio not
  stated). **And he is careful that this is not the committee's invention**: "This 'modern/educated/proper' and
  'primitive/uneducated/improper' divide is not only found at the shrine of Hinglaj but is also present in Urdu
  publications about Hindu communities in Pakistan more generally (cf. Jethamalani 1994; Jajot 2011; Rathi 2010)."
  (p. 278, folio not stated) "Clearly, the HSM is not the sole driver of this brand of progress and change… Within the
  past decade, especially, the community has found a new confidence and voice as a minority in Pakistan." (pp. 278–279,
  folio not stated)
- **The custodian's own formulation of the method, and the author's reading of it.** Dewani (2012 interview):
  "Educated people have the responsibility to pass on knowledge, but not by prohibiting anything!… The goal is the same
  whether you sit in an A/C coach, a car, or `a off-road vehicle`[text-layer?] [an off-road vehicle]. Also in a train
  there are three different classes; the only difference is the fee. But the train arrives." (p. 279, folio not stated)
  The author's gloss: "he does not, therefore, simply oppose certain practices performed at Hinglaj by marking them
  'wrong;' instead, he organizes them according to their supposed efficacy… Dewani's words do not baldly exclude other
  practices; rather, they deny their efficacy." (p. 279, folio not stated)
- **Signage as the decisive step from oral to written norm.** "the step from oral circulation to a written declaration
  should not be underestimated. While orally transported content can be changed easily or might simply be ignored, the
  bold, black-on-yellow letters at the entrance (in two different languages) give an impression of steadiness,
  stability, and normativity." (p. 284, folio not stated) "Just as books stabilize history by limiting the range of
  ways in which its signifiers may be interpreted, these boards stabilize practice, limiting the possible range of
  action by visitors." (p. 284, folio not stated) The signs also "visibly delineate the area as religious space rather
  than merely scenic" (p. 283, folio not stated) and extend the rules across the whole year rather than the festival
  days (pp. 283–284, folio not stated). Latour supplies the vocabulary: "'easily readable' (Latour 1983, 161)" and
  "'inscription devices' (Latour 1983, 161)" (p. 286, folio not stated), with Latour 2005, 30–34 on groups as traces
  (p. 284, folio not stated) and the epigraph "We cannot distinguish between those moments when we have might and those
  when we are right." (LATOUR 1993, 183) (p. 277, folio not stated).
- **The vegetarianism rule read as the production of a body.** "the Mandali not only directs how people should act
  inside Hinglaj but also actually creates an ideal of a literal body that consumes according to the organization's
  understanding of 'proper' practice" (p. 285, folio not stated); the author's name for the object is "an 'ideal Hindu
  body.'" (p. 268, folio not stated) The rule's external legitimation: Dewani "had traveled repeatedly to India and
  visited major religious sites there, so the idea of imposing vegetarianism on a large scale at Hinglaj was legitimized
  by what he had seen at other sacred Hindu places" (p. 285, folio not stated), and ahiṃsā as a long-standing
  pilgrimage practice "is cited by many sources… although this is difficult to prove (cf. Abadhut 1964, Shastri 1977,
  Lakhavat 2011)" (p. 285, folio not stated).
- **The Ganesha installation read as a technique of closure.** "With this blunt installation of its own interpretation
  of the paṛāv—corresponding naturally to its primary membership's own tradition—the HSM denied other voices the power
  to advance their own truth-claims of this place… This example makes clear how the solidification of one particular
  tradition always entails a form of exclusion, but one that is represented by the HSM as a necessary correction of an
  'uninformed' interpretation." (p. 280, folio not stated)
- **Bali as a nodal point — the book's closing analytic move on the ban.** "In their affirmation or rejection, both
  parties introduce the same element, bali, as a primary symbol of what it means to be a 'true' Hindu." (p. 293, folio
  not stated) "Animal sacrifice, thus, is a clear case in point for how alleged natural practices at the shrine reveal
  dynamics of exclusion and contingent foundations at deeper scrutiny." (p. 293, folio not stated) "the rejection of
  bloody sacrifices conjures up a sense of 'Hindu-unity' built on a perceived opposition to the Muslim sacrificial
  practice" (p. 297, folio not stated); "This effort to produce unity through not only the establishment but also the
  exclusion of certain truth-claims is a clear case in point for what I term a solidification of the Hinglaj
  tradition." (p. 297, folio not stated) "bali had shifted from a mere moment within a set of various practices into a
  nodal point (or truth-claim), with the ability to separate 'good' from 'bad' Hindus" (p. 305, folio not stated); "As
  a nodal point, animal sacrifice creates an equivalential chain of attributes, for example, uneducated, low caste, and
  even Muslim." (p. 305, folio not stated) "A 'we' becomes constructed as a community binds together through its common
  denial of one particular practice as foreign to an actual tradition (cf. how Khatri [1973, 10] called the practice a
  foreign element)." (p. 306, folio not stated) **And the charge of amnesia**: "Many visitors at Hinglaj portrayed
  animal sacrifice as an Islamic practice and thus in contradiction to their own Hindu identity, oblivious to the long
  tradition of `bali` among many Hindu communities in the area, particularly at the Kali shrine in the same valley."
  (p. 306, folio not stated)
- **What makes an exclusion stick.** "To be effective, any such exclusion requires a network of tools and institutional
  means endowing it with the power to successfully repeat the rejected practice. At this point, the agency of authors,
  reform movements, and temple committees becomes important. In the case of Hinglaj, the HSM possesses the primary
  ability to create an atmosphere of acceptance or denial of certain practices." (p. 306, folio not stated)
- **Plurality without democratisation — the thesis of §6.13.** "The increasingly plural religious landscape at
  Hinglaj—seen in the vast crowds it attracts from a wide range of castes, languages, ethnicities, and
  nationalities—has not led to the democratization of authority over the interpretation of the shrine's traditions."
  (p. 313, folio not stated)
- **What the shrine condenses.** "Competing views found at Hinglaj relate to discourses among Hindu communities all
  over Pakistan (and in fact all over the world) and serve as a localized condensation of national and international
  discussions of what it actually means to be a (Pakistani) Hindu." (p. 267, folio not stated) Hinglaj "has
  increasingly become a unifying socioreligious public space for myriad diverse Hindu communities" (p. 266, folio not
  stated). And the author's own strongest formulation: "Hinglaj is not simply a particular, unique locality anymore; it
  stands in a synecdochical relationship to Hinduism as such." (p. 287, folio not stated)
- **Materiality defended against the charge that discourse theory is only about words** — the Laclau and Mouffe block
  quotation on the earthquake and the falling brick, whose specificity "as 'natural phenomena' or 'expressions of the
  wrath of God,' depends upon the structuring of a discursive field" (Laclau and Mouffe 2001, 108) (p. 42, folio not
  stated). **A theological example chosen deliberately, and the hinge to the book's later treatment of landslides and
  renovations at the shrine (p. 27, folio not stated).**

### The author's argument (3): space, svayaṃbhū, and the production of Hinglaj

- **Chapter 5's programme, stated in advance.** "The following, therefore, will ask how discourses around Hinglaj and
  the pilgrimage stops take place… For an apt approach to this important nexus between space and culture, I will
  critically engage with theories broadly associated with the 'spatial-turn.' This aims at achieving two goals: first,
  to analyze how Hinglaj is rendered as a meaningful religious space and, second, to emphasize the material aspect of
  the solidification theory through concepts of spatialization." (p. 195, folio not stated)
- **The spatial turn, summarised and sourced.** "the developments surrounding the study of space and place since the
  1980s—the so-called spatial turn—can be summed up as the rejection of any stable Euclidean entity and its
  replacement with the notion of space as continually being produced (Cresswell 2004; Keith and Pile 1993; Urry 1995).
  Space is now perceived as a 'product of interrelations,' 'coexisting heterogeneity,' and 'always under construction'
  (Massey 2005, 9)." (p. 195, folio not stated) "For Massey, such considerations first and foremost mean that the
  'spatial is political' (Massey 2005, 9)." (p. 196, folio not stated)
- **Lefebvre, and the author's frank rewriting of him.** "For Lefebvre, 'space' (l'espace) is not merely a
  three-dimensional area in which life happens but rather is something more complex, produced out of representations
  and discourses about space." (p. 196, folio not stated) Via Shields: "a 'spatialisation of social order' and a way in
  which 'abstract structures such as "culture" become concrete practices and arrangements in space' (Shields 1998,
  155)." (p. 196, folio not stated) The triad "was not fully conceptualized by Lefebvre himself and therefore is widely
  discussed by scholars", with Merrifield quoted: "Unfortunately—or perhaps fortunately—[Lefebvre] sketches this
  [spatial triad] out only in preliminary fashion, leaving us to add our own flesh, our own content, to rewrite it as
  part of our own chapter or research agenda. (Merrifield 2006, 109)" (pp. 196–197, folios not stated) The author
  concedes: "This will naturally result in my own interpretation and thus rearticulation of Lefebvre's concepts."
  (p. 197, folio not stated)
- **The renaming that governs the rest of the book — a reader must have this to follow anything after p. 198.** "For
  the sake of clarity and to avoid confusion between 'spatial practice,' 'representations of space,' and 'spaces of
  representation,' in the following I render these three formants as practice, conceptualization, and imaginary of
  space, respectively." (p. 198, folio not stated) Restated at the end of the chapter: "Lefebvre stresses in his work
  the idea of a mutual dependency of practices, conceptualization, and imaginary of space that together produce
  meaningful sites. I have suggested understanding this interdependent trinity as (ritual) practices, conceptual space,
  and religious imaginary" (p. 244, folio not stated), with the three numbered moments set out at pp. 245–246 (folio
  not stated). **All later uses of "practice / conceptualization / imaginary of space" in this book are Lefebvre's
  terms renamed.**
- **The central claim of Chapter 5.** "inspired by the work of Lefebvre, I have suggested approaching Hinglaj not
  merely as a religious space but also as a space of religious spatialization, where practices, concepts, and
  imaginaries of space constantly work together. These three modes are mutually interdependent and influence each other
  via, in the words of Shields, a 'three-way-dialectic' (Shields 1998, 120)." (p. 261, folio not stated)
- **Why the imaginary dominates at religious sites.** "religious sites, in particular, tend to prioritize the moment of
  religious imaginary, where images of continuity and stability are considered as defining spatial practices or the
  representations of space." (p. 261, folio not stated) Bremer is quoted for the air of permanency: "places regarded as
  sacred often have an air of permanency that transcends the contingencies of human mortality; the sacred places of a
  religious tradition will survive long after individual religious adherents have passed away. In fact, sacred sites
  serve as an enduring symbol of, and orientation for, the continuance of the religious community itself. (Bremer 2006,
  30)" (p. 199, folio not stated)
- **shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir** — **svayaṃbhū, the book's key religious concept, and the claim that denies
  the whole analysis.** "this 'continuance' is represented through the concept of svayaṃbhū (Hin.), of being
  'self-existent' or having 'self-generated.' The perpetuation of this notion requires providing the place with an 'air
  of permanency,' which, at the same time, also calls for opposing a place's dynamic becoming." (p. 199, folio not
  stated) "Svayaṃbhū is an important feature of many tīrthas (Urban 2011)" (p. 199, folio not stated). "With the help
  of the aforementioned triad, the notion of svayaṃbhū can clearly be located within Lefebvre's format of imaginary of
  space (Lefebvre 2012)." (p. 201, folio not stated) Its practical effect: "it makes sense that, for example,
  construction work at the shrine is backgrounded or that the promise of engaging with a 'real' or 'self-generated'
  tīrtha sthān is causing people to go on the long journey to the Balochistan shrine." (p. 201, folio not stated) "At
  Hinglaj, for example, this process is seen in the notion of svayaṃbhū, which is also largely responsible for the
  site's cultural capital and which gains validity through the obfuscation of the site's history and course of
  development. The svayaṃbhū character of the religious imaginary distinguishes Hinglaj from other religious places in
  the Pakistani-Hindu landscape and ignores the involvement of human actors in this production of space." (p. 261,
  folio not stated) And the methodological consequence: "At times, this notion is in conflict with the two other
  modes—especially the conceptual space—since it denies human agency at the shrine. To what extent the image has been
  altered over the past two hundred years, therefore, was often a moot point when talking to devotees and organizations
  about Hinglaj." (p. 246, folio not stated) The alleged physical basis of the claim: "It is this alleged connection of
  the oblong stone with the cave wall that has endowed the image with the characteristic of svayaṃbhū." (p. 227, folio
  not stated)
- **Why coherent spatialization is hard to oppose.** "The more coherent the interaction between these three modes, the
  more solidified and unopposed the produced spatiality will eventually be." (p. 261, folio not stated) "If the
  practice, conceptualization, and imaginary all articulate one coherent narrative, then opposition against such modes
  of spatialization is difficult." (p. 263, folio not stated)
- **Spaces of representation as the emotional register, and as a site of violence.** They are "the location of social
  'fantasy' (Glynos and Howarth 2007), 'myth' (Laclau 1990), or 'mythohistorical knowledge' (Babb 2004) and establish
  coherency between place and group"; "It is in this realm that pilgrimage sites acquire the potential to become the
  axis mundi of a people—a process, as we have seen before, that builds on the rejection of other possible options.
  Such spaces of representation, therefore, might also compete with each other, at times even resulting in violent
  struggles over their interpretation." (p. 198, folio not stated) Merrifield again: "'[spaces of representation]
  don't involve too much head: they're felt more than thought' (Merrifield 2006, 110)." (p. 198, folio not stated)
- **The wall and the signs both produce and are produced by sacred space**, argued through Knott: "[r]itual practice
  itself is interesting when seen from the perspective of spatial practice as it is none other than spatial practice
  transformed by religious meaning, and often—though not always—performed in the context of a space set apart as sacred
  and by an appropriate ritual practitioner. (Knott 2005, 43)" (p. 218, folio not stated). The author's own
  formulation: "In this way, the wall, together with these signs posted in the valley, do both: they contribute to
  producing the valley's space as religious space by regulating its activities, but they are simultaneously also a
  product of the spatialization of Hinglaj." (p. 218, folio not stated) And of the signs: "They are connected to an
  ideal imaginary of the tīrtha sthān and at the same time conceptualize religious space through practices that
  separate the inside of the valley from the routine of quotidian life outside of it." (p. 218, folio not stated) Knott
  again: "Sacred space is not the stimulus for ritual; ritual, as sacred-making behaviour, brings about 'sacred' space.
  Ritual takes place, and makes place in this sense. (Knott 2005, 43)" (p. 283, folio not stated), and again on the
  body (Knott 2005, 60f.) (p. 285, folio not stated).
- **The ticket subdivides the sacred space by class and then by gender.** "the bhag'vā paṭṭī system introduced yet
  another level of religious space inside the valley. While through the construction of the confining walls, the temple
  organization produced a space meant only for the Hindu community, the system of payment further subdivided that
  space, creating an area only for the affluent Hindu community." (p. 219, folio not stated) "In this way, the HSM's
  introduction of the bhag'vā paṭṭī inside the valley led to yet another spatial separation—one that is not only
  economic but clearly gendered as well. The production of this economically advantaged and predominantly male area
  represents the spatialization of social structure and hierarchy within parts of the Pakistani-Hindu society."
  (p. 220, folio not stated)
- **The dam episode as a collision of two conceptions of space.** "This example shows how conceptions of space contrast
  in different discourses. For the WAPDA, the temple was merely an obstacle standing in the way of its plans to advance
  economic development in the area. The department's suggestion to shift the holy sites demonstrates its
  misunderstanding of the Hindu community's conception of space." (p. 217, folio not stated)
- **"Hinduization", dated loosely.** "This development goes hand in hand with a general 'Hinduization' of the shrine
  that began just a few decades ago and has gradually claimed the valley and the shrine as Hindu religious space. Since
  Hindu organizations—foremost the HSM—firmly control the representations of space in the area, their interpretations
  of what is seen and should be seen around the image currently prevail." (p. 262, folio not stated) And in the
  Conclusion, paired with a second term: "not only parts in a process of 'Hinduization' but also represent steps in a
  process of (Lasi) 'Lohanaization'" (p. 325, folio not stated).
- **The method used on the image is Critical Discourse Analysis, named and sourced.** "I wish to borrow from Critical
  Discourse Analysis (CDA), mainly associated with the work of Norman Fairclough (2001b) and Ruth Wodak (2009). Studies
  in CDA concern themselves with how information is conferred by the absence of details, implicit statements, and
  propositions (Riggins 1997, 10–11)." (p. 236, folio not stated) The operative move is Fairclough's
  foregrounding/backgrounding (Fairclough 2001a) (pp. 229, 236, folio not stated), and the author converts Riggins's
  question about texts into one about images (p. 236, folio not stated): "Which group is likely to benefit from the
  visual changes expressed in this image?" (p. 236, folio not stated)
- **A careful negative, which must travel with any use of the image material.** "the absence of a particular
  interpretation of the image in the given sources does not necessarily mean that such an interpretation was not
  assumed by some devotees who came to the shrine; however, it does denote that before certain circumstances led to the
  shrine becoming a central stage for Pakistan's Hindu communities, there was no obvious and self-explanatory central
  image." (p. 244, folio not stated)
- **Hegemony over the conceptual space, flagged forward.** "the aspect of hegemony is important since not all groups
  exercise equal control over the conceptual space of the image—this will become particularly important in the next
  chapter" (p. 245, folio not stated).
- **The limit the author sets on his own spatial analysis.** "Because there is no end to this production of space but
  rather only stages of contestation and decontestation between the three modes, I think it is more accurate to speak
  of the shrine's (and the annexed route's) spatiality rather than only its religious space. Due to the tradition's
  fairly recent dislocation, this work is limited to providing a snapshot of current developments around Hinglaj"
  (p. 201, folio not stated); "the current perception of the religious spaces inside the Hinglaj Valley can only
  provide a snapshot of such continuous processes of spatialization. There is, however, also a state of affairs that
  increasingly tends toward an equilibrium in the trialectic" (p. 246, folio not stated), with "decontestation"
  (Freeden 2006) as the term for what institutions now achieve (p. 246, folio not stated). And a disclaimer on
  taxonomy: "But it is not my aim to present a clear-cut taxonomy, explaining the function of religious sites. I want
  to show the intrinsic dynamics involved in the production of Hinglaj as a religious site." (p. 263, folio not stated,
  note 1)
- **Tropes migrate around the valley — an explicitly first-person argument with a worked example.** "In my opinion, this
  shows how certain tropes migrate around the valley of Hinglaj over time, becoming associated with different locations
  during different periods." (p. 256, folio not stated) The evidence is the eye-cure, which Abadhut placed at `ākāś
  gaṃgā` / Chaurasi (Abadhut 1964) but which today attaches to Kir Kund: "I have not heard any particular mention
  recently of the restorative properties of Anil Kund's waters with respect to such diseases." (p. 256, folio not
  stated)
- **Narrative repair when a ritual fails — the Sharan Kund case.** "The events surrounding Sharan Kund show the close
  connection between practice, conception, and the imaginary of a religious space." (p. 252, folio not stated) "an
  organizational explanation incorporated the event into the official corpus of mytho-historic narratives inside the
  valley" (p. 252, folio not stated). "Gopal Maharaj rearticulates contesting elements into a seamless, logical
  explanation... The mytho-historical explanation by one of the main religious authorities at the shrine helped to once
  more create a harmonious narrative in which the ceasing of the purification practice is the work of the Goddess and
  hence part of the divine will." (p. 253, folio not stated)
- **"Duplication" or "multiplication" of sacred landscape, after Eck.** "Such associations of localities with supralocal
  mytho-historic narratives are quite common and create a kind of 'duplication' or 'multiplication' within the sacred
  landscapes of South Asia (Eck 1998)." (p. 256, folio not stated) "In general, these three locations in the northern
  mountain range and their focus on Gorakhnath, Guru Nanak, and Rama connect the sacred geography of Hinglaj to the
  broader religious traditions of South Asia." (p. 258, folio not stated) Chapter 1's note 8 gives the same idea a
  name: "Such associations are frequent in South Asia's religious landscapes. Eck calls these 'duplication' or
  'multiplication' processes the 'geographical Sanskritisation' (Eck 1998, 166–167)." (p. 50, folio not stated)
- **Networks, imagined landscapes and the śākta pīṭha.** "tīrthas do not exist in isolation but rather form a network of
  many different sacred sites all over the subcontinent" (p. 106, folio not stated); "One could say, for example, that
  the fifty-one (or one-hundred eight) places of the Goddess (Skt. śākta pīṭha) mentioned previously belong to such a
  category of connected tīrthas" (p. 107, folio not stated). **The two counts, "fifty-one (or one-hundred eight)", are
  given exactly as printed and neither is preferred.** Eck's "duplication and multiplication" of sites (Eck 1998,
  166–168) and mythology that "take[s] place" in a network (Eck 1998, 169–170) (p. 107, folio not stated); Feldhaus's
  regions, made of sites "people bring together with their bodies . . . with their minds and imaginations" (Feldhaus
  2003, 100) (p. 107, folio not stated).
- **Two identifications the author offers and then withholds.** Of Ctesias: "The most intriguing aspect of these symbols
  is the mention of such a place by Ctesias in his work Indica... Charles King judges these mountains to be fifteen
  days' walking distance from the desert between Cutch and Multan (1865, 297). This certainly could be a reference to
  the valley of Hinglaj/Nani. To conclusively establish this connection, however, an archeological survey of the signs
  and their age would be necessary." (p. 258, folio not stated) Of Philostratus, refused on evidentiary grounds:
  "Although Philostratus situates this well somewhere in the Indus region, I believe it far-fetched to associate his
  description with Chandrakup due to the relatively short life span of such mud formations. Yet, because the whole area
  is famous for its geothermal features, it is likely that volcanoes of significant size also existed in the third
  century." (p. 212, folio not stated) He also rejects White's alchemical reading: "I have not encountered any traces
  of his alchemical hypotheses at Chandrakup or the Hinglaj shrine today" (p. 212, folio not stated).
- **A negative finding about the Naths, offered as evidence.** "It is yet another sign of the breaking of the connection
  between the Nath saṃpradāy and Hinglaj after Partition that no one at the shrine mentioned to me a link between
  Chaurasi and the eighty-four Siddhas who are so significant within the Nath tradition." (p. 265, folio not stated,
  note 35)
- **Scepticism about a Nanak proof, stated by attribution rather than by refutation.** "It is again Lakhavat who tries
  to provide evidence for the guru's journey to Hinglaj. He publishes a map from the Atlas Travels of Guru Nanak
  showing a route allegedly traveled by Nanak on his way to Mecca on which he passes by the shrine in Balochistan. For
  Lakhavat, this provides sufficient proof of Nanak's presence in Hinglaj, and he concludes that the footprints found
  in the valley are proof of this visit (Lakhavat 2011, 76)." (p. 257, folio not stated) **The words "tries to
  provide", "allegedly" and "For Lakhavat" carry the author's distance; he states no verdict of his own: (not
  stated).**
- **A translation policy stated for one word only.** Note 12: "Ātman (Skt.) or ātmā (Hin.) is a sophisticated concept
  within Hindu cosmology and cannot be discussed here at length. However, due to my engagement with this particular
  tradition in Gujarat, in my opinion, translating it here as 'soul' is fairly accurate." (p. 193, folio not stated)

### The author's argument (4): mobility against locality, and what a pilgrimage is

- **The chapter thesis.** "I argue that mobility and locality are best understood as two mutually influential, albeit
  often imbalanced, aspects of pilgrimage sites in general and of the Hinglaj temple in particular." (p. 104, folio not
  stated) "The case of the ritual journey to Hinglaj clearly shows that both aspects—mobility and locality—significantly
  affect the creation of mytho-historical narratives and ritual practices at the shrine." (p. 103, folio not stated)
  The literature it corrects: Ingold and Vergunst "criticize that ethnographical accounts of religious localities often
  still describe places and destinations and less frequently include the means by which people reached them (Ingold and
  Vergunst 2008, 3)" (p. 103, folio not stated).
- **"Pilgrimage" defined by family resemblance, not by feature.** The term "cannot be easily defined by specific
  physical or ritualistic features" (p. 105, folio not stated); Bharati has emphasized that the tīrtha and tīrthayātrā
  "may also be expressed metaphorically (Bharati 1970, 85)" (p. 105, folio not stated); Wilke's Lalita Tripurasundari
  has her place "not a physical location but rather within the śrīcakra, a mental representation of the Goddess's
  dwelling… sacred landscapes are not missing; they have merely shifted from outer to inner spaces, thus becoming
  allegories (Wilke 2010, 82)" (p. 105, folio not stated). Hence Wittgenstein's "family resemblances" (Ger.
  "Familienähnlichkeiten") (Wittgenstein 2003, §67; Staten 1986, 82–83) (p. 105, folio not stated), and the author's own
  usage: "The term yātrā, which I will translate as 'pilgrimage' or 'ritual journey,' shares a family resemblance with
  many practices, but there is no definite element permeating all expressions of the concept." (p. 105, folio not
  stated)
- **Tīrtha, defined and un-bounded.** "The Sanskrit term tīrtha—literally meaning 'passage,' 'ford,' or 'bathing place'"
  (p. 106, folio not stated); "The earliest extensive noting of tīrthasthāns is found in the Tīrthayātrāpārvaṇa of the
  Mahābhārata" (p. 106, folio not stated). Eck: travel to the tīrthas is "one of the oldest and still one of the most
  prominent features of Indian religious life" (Eck 1981, 323) (p. 106, folio not stated); Jacobsen "goes so far as to
  say that Hindu religiosity is often more centered around sacred space than around sacred text (Jacobsen 2013, 7)"
  (p. 106, folio not stated). But Eck also "argues that the word tīrtha, as it is found in the older classical
  texts—like the Vedic Saṃhitās, the Brāhmaṇas, and the early Upaniṣads—does not mean a place of pilgrimage but was
  rather associated with purity and ritual practice (Eck 1981, 326)" (p. 146, folio not stated); "it seems that every
  stop along the way to the temple can potentially become a tīrtha. Jethamalani demonstrates this very wide
  understanding of the term tīrtha in his book Hinglāj Tīrth. For him, all stops on the way to Hinglaj can be called
  tīrthas (Jethamalani 1994)." (p. 146, folio not stated)
- **Salvific benefit as a category, with a comparison to sacrifice.** Jacobsen's "salvific" "includes desired goals that
  might pertain to worldly wealth and health in life but also to rebirth and salvation"; "many sites promise visitors
  benefits similar to those that could be gained from more elaborate ritual practices such as the aśvamedha (Skt.) or
  the agnihotra (Skt.) (Eck 1981, 338)" (p. 106, folio not stated). Clientele varies: "some might be visited mainly by
  particular jātis (Hin. caste, community), others by women or by low-caste communities (Gold 1990, 5). Yet it is rare
  to find a fully consistent clientele in one or another of these places of worship." (p. 106, folio not stated)
- **Difficulty as the source of value, and the Kedarnath analogy.** "A prominent example of this is the Kedarnath temple
  in the north of the subcontinent, which (until the construction of a road) required an extremely difficult journey to
  reach but offered a proportionally greater benefit to those devotees who complete the pilgrimage (Whitmore 2010)."
  (p. 103, folio not stated)
- **Mobility as the medium of memory.** Lorimer and Lund, "[S]torying with events can render landscape memorable" (1908
  [Lorimer and Lund 2008], 192) (p. 109, folio not stated); Ingold, "cognition should not be set off from locomotion,
  along the lines of a division between head and heels, since walking is itself a form of circumambulatory knowing"
  (Ingold 2004, 331) (p. 109, folio not stated); Olwing[text-layer?] [Olwig], landscape takes form through the
  "repetition of passages walked" (Olwing 2008, 87) (p. 109, folio not stated); Shields, "Over time, spatial practices,
  the habitual routines of 'place ballets' are concretised in the built environment and sedimented in the landscape"
  (Shields 1991, 53) (p. 109, folio not stated). **The reverse process is named too**: "A process in the opposite
  direction—the ceasing of habitual routes—disassociates inhabitants from their (imagined) geographies." (p. 110, folio
  not stated)
- **Cresswell's three-part mobility, restated for ritual journeys.** "(a) that ritual journeys always imply some kind of
  movement; (b) that this movement is located in discourse as well as in space and that it can be represented,
  interpreted, or valued in various ways; and (c) that the journey is experienced through the body, which makes bodily
