  practices significant (Cresswell 2010, 160)." (p. 110, folio not stated) The modernity claim: "one sign of the advent
  of high modernity is the increasing organization of mobility through 'timetables and mechanizations' (Cresswell 2006,
  10)" (p. 84, folio not stated).
- **"Kinesthetic" chosen over Turner's "kinetic".** "Edith Turner called pilgrimage a 'kinetic ritual' (Turner and
  Turner 1978, xiii). The adjective 'kinetic' actually means the motion of material bodies. In this work, however, I use
  the term 'kinesthetic' to emphasize not only physical motion—often a part of pilgrimages—but also the meaning
  associated with that physical motion." (p. 146, folio not stated) And tapas defended in note 12: "The primary meaning
  of the noun tapas, from the root 'tap,' is 'warming up' or 'heating up.' … Because it lacks a moral or ethical
  implication, tapas cannot be used synonymously with the Western idea of asceticism. However, due to a lack of any
  equivalent term, I use tapas in the wider sense of 'asceticism' or 'austerity' (Shee 1986, 187)." (p. 147, folio not
  stated)
- **The gap the book is really after.** "A gap between representation and the actual bodily experience of reaching
  Hinglaj has had an especially significant influence on the dynamics at the shrine in recent years. It is here that
  the imaginaries of the pilgrimage, sedimented in many texts and perpetuated in oral histories, collide with current
  practices relating to the travel." (p. 110, folio not stated) "the recent developments around Hinglaj have exposed a
  gap between the traditional discursive representations of the pilgrimage (Chapter 3) and the current practices at the
  shrine (Chapter 4)." (p. 26, folio not stated) The closing questions: "How do Hindus at the shrine cope with the gap
  between traditional representations of the pilgrimage and current practices at Hinglaj? What kinds of new narratives
  are created to explain the change in ways of getting there?" (p. 151, folio not stated)
- **Accessibility destabilised meaning, not just numbers.** "This new accessibility dramatically changed the
  representations and practices at the shrine, and the newfound ease with which Hinglaj was quickly reachable stirred up
  discussions of identity and religious merit at the place." (p. 85, folio not stated) The sudden connection of the site
  "currently occupies the time and energy of many who aim to coherently incorporate this into the religious imaginary
  and the rituals at the shrine." (p. 86, folio not stated)
- **The Hinglaj case is recent and therefore unfinished**, against Jacobsen's Indian railway comparison: "As the means
  of transportation has improved, starting with the development of the Indian railway, pilgrimage has become easy and
  this has influenced the expectation of the rewards of pilgrimage. In many pilgrimages, but still with a number of
  exceptions, there is no longer any similarity between the wandering of ascetics and the journey of pilgrims.
  (Jacobsen 2013, 21)" (p. 86, folio not stated). "However, while the means of travel at these other pilgrimage
  locations have steadily evolved over many decades, events around Hinglaj are fairly recent, and the incorporation of
  the new practices of mobility at the shrine have not yet become completely final." (p. 86, folio not stated)
- **A shift in what representations of the shrine are about, dated.** "In the centuries prior to the pilgrimage's
  institutionalization in the mid-1980s, the journey was characterized by the strenuous path through the deserts of
  Sindh and Balochistan, the stops en route, and the kinesthetic experience. Later representations, especially after
  2001, started to show more interest in Hinglaj as a location and not as a pilgrimage." (p. 186, folio not stated)
- **"Traditional" elements rearticulated, the chapter's stated finding.** "Building on my interaction with pilgrims in
  Pakistan and India, I will show how certain 'traditional' elements have been rearticulated to fit the novel
  circumstances." (p. 150, folio not stated) The Narayanpur case study "shows how 'traditional' pilgrimage practices are
  reinterpreted—or even retrospectively invented—to adapt to modern circumstances." (p. 26, folio not stated)

### The author's argument (5): the "tourist", the picnicker, and nostalgia

- **A loss of austerity read as a loss of quality.** "Many visitors emphasize the physical endeavor and hardship in such
  allegedly 'traditional' journeys and often connect the current absence of tapasyā (Skt. also tapas), austerity, or
  simply the kinesthetic experience, with a decrease in spiritual quality at Hinglaj." (p. 104, folio not stated)
  "Visitors at the shrine often brought up this discord, emphasizing that the site is decaying as a result of people not
  adhering to the traditional practices; this will be the topic of a later chapter." (p. 110, folio not stated)
- **The "picnicker" as a category of visitor.** "These strategies are largely represented in the spreading notion of the
  'picnic manānevālā' (Hin/Urd. the 'picnicker'), a derogatory designation applied by devotees to those shrine visitors
  they see merely as 'tourists,' unlike the pilgrims of widespread oral traditions and publications who risked their
  lives crossing a harsh desert to reach the Goddess's dwelling place." (p. 26, folio not stated) "visitors to the
  shrine often discredit the allegedly shallow motivations of others by saying that they came only for a picnic ('bas,
  picnic manāne āte haiṃ')" (p. 187, folio not stated); the split between "the 'picnic manānevāle'—a term I understand
  as equivalent to 'tourists'—and the 'devotees'" (p. 187, folio not stated). "Certainly, no one ever perceived himself
  or herself or their group as 'tourists'" (p. 187, folio not stated).
- **The author says the category is empty.** "the label of the picnic manānevalā `[text-layer?]` [manānevālā — the
  macron appears on every other occurrence in the chunk] or the 'tourist' is used to capture a certain discontent with
  the current pilgrimage experience. It represents the projection of a deficit of some sort, creating the defining
  quality of an other at the shrine—a quality that is found only in fellow visitors and never in oneself." (p. 189,
  folio not stated) "certain visitors use these two descriptions in a manner that is void of any positive content but
  use them instead as a vehicle for expressing their resentment over the all-too-obvious gap between representations of
  Hinglaj and the actual practices at the shrine." (p. 190, folio not stated) "the tourist is always some other that
  cannot be described by any positive set of characteristics" (p. 192, folio not stated).
- **The line is not drawn by orthopraxy.** "This distinction, however, is not drawn along gritty lines of orthopraxy.
  The performance of certain rituals is not sufficient to serve as a marker of 'seriousness,' a quality that is denied
  the tourist. Devoted pilgrims might follow all the necessary rituals and bhajans … and still be seen by others as
  'picnic manānevāle.'" (p. 190, folio not stated)
- **The chapter-4 thesis in one sentence.** "This gap must be filled with shape and meaning, a fantasy structure that has
  laid all the guilt upon the figure of the 'tourist,' thereby accounting for the lack of spiritual merit" (p. 190, folio
  not stated); "the tourist serves as a principle that helps to organize the tradition's recent dislocation. It stands as
  an obstacle between devotees at the shrine and the promised spiritual union they hope to achieve there." (p. 192, folio
  not stated)
- **Why kali yuga is refused as the explanation.** "such notions of deterioration are rarely interpreted through the kali
  yuga (Skt.), the cosmic age of decay, given by many Hindus as the reason for spiritual degeneration all over the globe.
  I assume that due to the very recent changes at Hinglaj, kali yuga does not serve as an apt explanation for the gap
  between representation and current practice of the yātrā." (p. 188, folio not stated) Restated with a date: "As some
  visitors claim to remember a better time, kali yuga—which allegedly started in the fourth millennium BC—is not an apt
  concept to explain Hinglaj's spiritual decline." (p. 189, folio not stated) **"the fourth millennium BC" is given as an
  allegation, not as the author's own dating, and must not be lifted as a date.**
- **The implicit theory the complaints rest on.** The complaints are "consistent with an implicit theory of pilgrimages
  according to which tapasyā and renunciation generate merit, purify the soul, and result in a deeper and more abiding
  spiritual experience" (p. 189, folio not stated); "These accounts attribute the pilgrimage's merit to the long and
  arduous path and deem physical demand and tapasyā critical components of the experience." (p. 189, folio not stated)
  The underwriting text is note 18's Abadhut passage: "The secret is indeed that the difficulties during the pilgrimage
  are also tapas, austerity. From austerity, one can get a glimpse of the Brahma. Therefore, it is said that austerity is
  Brahma [tapohī brahma]." (p. 194, folio not stated) And: "If you mount a train for a pilgrimage and you come back
  without any physical toils, then you might see the pilgrimage place [tīrtha], but the austerity is left out." (p. 194,
  folio not stated) Schaflechner's reading of Abadhut: "Abadhut obviously wishes to make a strong case for the necessary
  physical effort of tapasyā on a pilgrimage. For him, it clearly matters which way a pilgrim chooses to reach the
  location." (pp. 117–118, folios not stated) "Especially the metaphor of burning the senses, implied in the adjective
  dagdh, speaks of the connection of tapasyā and the blazing desert heat (cf. Shee 1986, 187). Thus, he also
  distinguishes between the salvific powers of the shrine and those of the journey itself." (p. 118, folio not stated)
- **The contribution claimed against the pilgrim/tourist literature.** Citing Stausberg 2010 on the "fairly blurry
  distinction between pilgrimages and tourist activities" and Humphrey and Laidlaw 1994 on people imposing "various
  meaningful narratives onto ritualistic behavior on their own" (p. 189, folio not stated): "Interesting in the Hinglaj
  case is that the line is drawn by local actors, such as pujārīs, organizers, and the visitors themselves in order to
  distinguish the serious from the non-serious—that is, the pilgrims from the tourists. In this way, the pilgrimage site
  at Hinglaj adds a new dimension to current discussions of what counts as 'tourist' or 'pilgrim' activity and
  reintroduces it with the distinction of the 'picnic manānevālā' and the 'yātrī.'" (p. 190, folio not stated) The
  literature is held at arm's length: "Notwithstanding the fact that Hinglaj is a particular case study, the debate bears
  similar characteristics to those of current discussions about the differences between tourism and pilgrimage (cf.
  Timothy and Olsen 2006; Urry 1995; Cohen 1988)." (p. 187, folio not stated)
- **Nostalgia as an organising principle, not merely a mood — the key analytic move of §4.5.** "Similar to the notion of
  solidification, I also understand this permanent reference to some lost unity as an organization principle with the
  ultimate goal to incorporate the tradition's recent dislocation. Here, too, proper and improper practices are marked
  and separated." (p. 187, folio not stated)
- **The border has replaced the desert as the filter on pilgrims — the chapter's central claim about India.** "While in
  the time before the construction of the Makran Coastal Highway the long and grueling journey to the shrine served as
  this filtering process, today, the established border has a similar role in keeping the journey to the Goddess 'elite.'
  The demanding and potentially lethal trek through the desert once kept access to the shrine restricted; now, this
  restriction remains in place for Indian pilgrims as a result of the fierce visa policies paired with the fear of the
  unknown in neighboring Pakistan." (p. 171, folio not stated) Restated for Kutch: "As in times past when the difficult
  desert route restricted movement to the shrine, now rigid visa regulations keep the number of the Hinglajis and
  dev'ātmās low and thus the practice alive." (p. 179, folio not stated)
- **Scarcity as the condition of a ritual status.** "It is due only to the contingencies surrounding the selection of
  pilgrims—particularly those resulting from opaque visa regulations and the legal ambiguity of the journey to a
  restricted area of Balochistan—that notions of divine selection can be maintained." (p. 179, folio not stated) "from
  the perspective of power, the prestige connected with the journey from India to the remote temple in Balochistan may be
  seen as the exclusion of many for the sake of a few. These exceptional few gain a certain symbolic capital and rise in
  the regard of their communities." (pp. 171–172, folio not stated)
- **The border as a producer of symbolic capital — the theoretical core.** "borders may also play a role in strengthening
  the 'symbolic capital' of a particular place, that is, the value it bestows upon those who cross a border to reach it
  (Bourdieu 1998, 47–52)." (p. 166, folio not stated) "While traditionally it was the fierce journey through the desert
  that bestowed huge symbolic capital onto the pilgrims, today, this restriction of Hinglaj is rearticulated for Indian
  devotees through the fierce India-Pakistan border." (p. 166, folio not stated) "Narratives of longing and separation
  can further validate the spiritual merit of the journey due to the heightened emotional sacrifice connected to the task
  and hence elevate a place's symbolic capital." (p. 168, folio not stated) "I will show that the journey's salvific
  potential for Indian devotees is best understood from the perspectives of power, 'symbolic capital' (Bourdieu 1998),
  and restricted mobility." (p. 151, folio not stated) "Symbolic capital converted into a funerary practice" is the point
  of the Kapri case study: "there is another practice in this area that translates the symbolic capital earned on the
  journey into tangible practices" (p. 178, folio not stated); "the journey to Hinglaj has the power to change devotees'
  ritual status, not only transforming them into a Hinglaji but also changing them into dev'ātmās (Hin. divine soul)
  after their death" (p. 178, folio not stated).
- **Borderlands after Purewal, extended by the author.** "Purewal writes that the hinterlands of the geopolitical
  border—the 'borderlands'—not only extend for several kilometers into each country but also permeate opportunities for
  interaction between Indians and Pakistanis inside the country", quoting "borderlands are constituted not merely at
  geopolitical international borders, but also in day-to-day interactions and exchanges" (Purewal 2003, 542) (p. 184,
  folio not stated). Schaflechner's extension: "the borderlands consist of checkpoints, administration offices, and
  police stations" (p. 184, folio not stated); "the border is not only positioned at the margins of two nation states but
  also seems localized in multiple trials along the way, each of which has the potential to end their journey" (p. 184,
  folio not stated); and it "begins with the visa application process", so "the Pakistani borderlands start for Hinglaj
  pilgrims before they ever leave India" (p. 184, folio not stated). The field he positions himself in: "This analysis of
  geopolitical borders and their impact on particular communities at and in contact with them forms an independent field
  of study that aims to understand identity formation within modern nation states (cf. Hastings and Wilson 1999; van
  Schendel 2005; Gupta and Ferguson 1992)." (p. 167, folio not stated), also Althusser 1971 on "interpellation", Purewal
  2003, 546, Flynn 1997, Hastings and Haller 2000, and "borders can also reshape the routes of ritual journeys and affect
  practices at pilgrimage sites (Harder 2013, 151)" (p. 167, folio not stated).
- **Stereotype as a producer of aura.** "In general, such stereotypes about Pakistan add to the dangerous aura and the
  mystification of the shrine, which, in some ways, newly articulates discourses of danger and difficulty found in older
  representations of the journey." (p. 170, folio not stated) "In India, discourses on the Hinglaj pilgrimage draw power
  from the image of Pakistan as a savage country, further mystifying the journey and raising its symbolic capital."
  (p. 192, folio not stated) "stereotypes linked to Pakistan play a significant role in the 'mystification' of the
  pilgrimage across the border, in India, and… such visions of the Islamic Republic serve to bolster the shrine's symbolic
  capital." (p. 26, folio not stated) **"Symbolic capital" is the author's borrowing from Bourdieu, used at p. 26 without
  attribution and attributed at pp. 151, 166.**
- **Exclusivism survives on the Indian side.** "the kind of exclusivism surrounding the pilgrimage to Hinglaj—only known
  in Pakistan before the construction of the MCH—is still very much alive in India"; "those who come back from Pakistan
  stand out as exceptional and are treated differently in their social environment." (p. 191, folio not stated) "Many of
  the oral histories presented above can also be read from the perspective of restricted mobility and power." (p. 191,
  folio not stated)
- **A decline narrative running the other way across the border.** The Thatta priest's contrast is read by the author as
  "the debasement and decline within the Pakistani-Hindu community compared to the 'real' traditions coming from India"
  (p. 171, folio not stated), a second axis on top of the "'real' and 'proper' practice" distinctions the book has
  already analysed (p. 171, folio not stated).
- **The Indian pilgrim's difficulty is displaced, not removed — Depavat quoted at length.** "The problems for which the
  pilgrimage to Hinglaj is considered difficult are no longer present today. But it is necessary to make it clear that to
  this day this pilgrimage is still difficult to accomplish. Earlier there were certain kinds of difficulties and now
  there are other kinds. Today there are the formalities of the passport and the visa, the problems of Islamic countries
  like Pakistan, there is some doubt in guaranteeing the security of the Indian travelers, and [also] due to local terror
  attacks the pilgrimage cannot be considered easy . . . . Without the help of the Shri Hinglaj Sheva Mandali this
  pilgrimage is impossible. (Depavat 2010, 153)" (pp. 168–169, folios not stated) The chapter title of the Hindi source is
  "'The Hinglaj Pilgrimage Is Not Easy' (Depavat 2010)… a chapter in the Hindi book Śrī Kar'ṇījī Durlabh Darśan" (p. 168,
  folio not stated). The pilgrim's own theory of value: "Whatever you want to get in this world, you have to work for it.
  The bigger the thing is that you wish for, the more you need to work for it. But this is not only hard work; it also
  means you need devotion. The old pilgrimage was difficult, the pilgrimage now is difficult, and it seems that in the
  future, too, it is going to be difficult." (p. 177, folio not stated) And: "If the border were not there, then people
  would not value the pilgrimage so much" (p. 176, folio not stated).
- **An internal contradiction the author flags in one of his sources.** Singh Ratnu "emphasizes in his travelogue how
  dangerous it is to travel to Balochistan with a visa that is valid only for Karachi. However, in the very next
  paragraph, he writes that the government of Pakistan actually does not issue visas for the journey to Balochistan and
  that in fact the High Commission has explicitly forbidden the handling of such applications (Ratnu 2007, 28)." (p. 169,
  folio not stated)
- **Proxy shrines do not replace the original.** "Even though such 'proxy shrines' are widely distributed all over South
  Asia, they rarely live up to the fame of the sites they represent." (p. 168, folio not stated)
- **Different classes of pilgrim read the same trip differently.** "While some of the better-educated members of the group
  interpreted the trip in philosophical or political terms, it seemed that the aspects of purification, remission, and
  ridding oneself of sins were the main incentives for the members of the Ahir community." (p. 182, folio not stated) One
  returnee's political conclusion is reported: "that the trip had showed him that people in Pakistan were not so different
  from people in India and that terrorists are only a small part of the Pakistani population" (p. 183, folio not stated).
- **Continuity with older legend, not a break from it.** "his own account is also a tale of difficulty, faith, and
  devotion. In this way, his description resembles Charan mythologies and general representations of the yātrā dealing with
  tapasyā and divine intervention, and so connects current events to the vast body of Charan legends surrounding the
  shrine." (p. 177, folio not stated)
- **Pilgrimage as a route to social mobility, flagged forward twice.** "This interesting possibility of altering one's
  community background through the pilgrimage to Hinglaj—often used to gain prestige and upward social mobility in today's
  Gujarat—will be discussed in detail in Chapter 4." (p. 76, folio not stated), and again at p. 79 (folio not stated).

### The author's argument (6): "syncretic Sindhi nationalism" and the antiquity claim

- **The coined term, and its working definition.** "Within this discourse, Hinglaj emerges in a completely different
  role—as the hallmark of a phenomenon I will term 'syncretic Sindhi nationalism.' Such texts portray Hinglaj as an
  intrinsically Sindhi religious place, appropriating and rearticulating it as an instrument of Sindhi nationalism that
  aspires to transgress religious boundaries." (p. 86, folio not stated) His working definition of nationalism: "a kind
  of group formation in which the so-called unity of a large and heterogeneous (or even 'imagined') group is evoked
  through the articulation of certain commonly shared nodal points, gaining their influential role in opposition to some
  excluded other." (p. 86, folio not stated) "As will be demonstrated, nodal points of Sindhi nationalism emphasize
  religious harmony and thus subsume the difference between Hindus and Muslims under the superior signifier of ethnic
  identity." (pp. 86–87, folio not stated)
- **He defends the word "syncretism" while conceding it is discredited.** "I am conscious of the fact that the term
  'syncretism' does not hold to current theories of culture and has been widely criticized due to its implicit
  hierarchization of traditions (Ringgren 1969). I nonetheless deem its conceptual logic precise in the context of
  Sindhi nationalism." (p. 89, folio not stated) "While syncretism has become obsolete due to its inherent assumption of
  a clear-cut border between religions traditions, Sindhi nationalism, on the contrary, needs such hard and fast
  separations. It must emphasize the basic differences between Islam and Hinduism in order to show its outstanding
  ability to unite them. Within this discourse, the shrine of Hinglaj emerges as a materialized form of Sindhi religious
  unity." (p. 89, folio not stated)
- **The three-part structure of the nationalist claim on Hinglaj, given twice.** First at p. 90 (folio not stated): the
  shrine is "a part of the Mohenjo-Daro civilization and thus a five-thousand-year-old—but intrinsically Sindhi—cultural
  heritage site"; and "even though Hinglaj is older than both these religions, Muslims and Hindus have both been
  worshipping at the shrine for many centuries", proved by Latif's journey. Then in summary at p. 96 (folio not stated):
  "First, the equation of Hinglaj with Persian and Chaldean deities—mainly through the writing of colonial
  authors—furnishes the shrine with an ancient history. Second, the association of the place with Mohenjo-Daro
  historically binds it to a culture that is pre-Aryan (or pre-Hindu) and pre-Islam, but intrinsically Sindhi. And
  third, the poetry of Shah Abdul Latif presents Hinglaj as an important religious site for both Islam and Hinduism".
  **The third leg of that claim rests on a figure who is an archive row's principal figure; see `## Misfiled`.**
- **A source-critical demolition: the "ancient Goddess" claim is a citation loop.** Of the Las Bela Gazetteer's account:
  "Scrutinizing the quote within Ansari's argument, one finds that the Gazetteer's account does not build upon new,
  independent research in this area but is, in fact, also directly derived from Thomas Holdich's book India (1904)."
  (p. 93, folio not stated) Of Billimoria: "Although Billimoria's contribution seems, at first, to build upon new
  research, he also quotes Holdich (1910) when illustrating the Goddess's history… Billimoria also refers extensively
  (often without citation of direct quotations) to Crooke's 1913 article in the ERE… Crooke quotes Masson's argument to
  provide support for his own thesis, thus creating a direct connection between Abro's synopsis and Masson's writing
  from 1844. Billimoria also cites more than five pages directly from Captain Hart's 1839 travelogue. His contribution
  to the study of the Goddess Hinglaj is therefore, in fact, an amalgamation of older sources rather than a new approach
  to the question of Hinglaj's origin." (p. 94, folio not stated) And of the root of it all: "Masson apparently was the
  first who hypothesized a connection to an ancient Near Eastern Goddess, an idea that from that time onward became
  integral to the description of Hinglaj." (p. 92, folio not stated) "However, it is primarily the writings of Masson
  and Holdich that exert significant influence on contemporary literature concerning the shrine in Balochistan and that
  also have been ultimately integrated into contemporary Sindhi publications about the Goddess Hinglaj." (p. 92, folio
  not stated) Masson himself "provides no further evidence for this belief" (p. 91, folio not stated). **Any use of the
  "ancient Goddess" claim in a shrine entry must carry this finding with it.**
- **What the antiquity claim buys.** "With this statement, Holdich proclaims the shrine's antiquity to surpass both
  Islam and Hinduism, placing the Goddess in an independent position, untouched by any present religious current. The
  work of many later writers bolstered this claim." (p. 92, folio not stated) Of Ansari: "Thus, in his estimation, the
  Goddess Hinglaj predates Hindu and Muslim invasions into Sindh and thus renders her immune to religious and sectarian
  skirmishes." (p. 93, folio not stated) Of Abro: he "places the Goddess in a distant sphere where she is 'related to
  the human past' (Sin. insān jo māẓī)—that is, detached from any specific religion—and hence removed from any kind of
  communal conflict (Abro 1991, 42)." (p. 94, folio not stated) "Since, Hinglaj is thereby associated with almost every
  Goddess west and east of Balochistan; she is all-embracing, transcending, and surpassing communal differences."
  (p. 94, folio not stated)
- **Ansari's pre-Aryan Mother Goddess, reported not endorsed.** "To broaden his argument of Sindh's 'golden past,'
  Ansari claims that the composition of the Veda occurred on the shores of the Indus." (p. 93, folio not stated) "The
  veneration of the Goddess was, he emphasizes, already in place in Sindh before the Aryans conquered the area 'five
  thousand years ago' (Ansari 2001, 154). Hence, the cult of the Mother Goddess predates the coming of the Aryans
  (Ansari 2001, 161)." (p. 93, folio not stated) "In this view of history, Hinglaj was worshipped in Sindh before the
  coming of the Aryans and was only later adopted by the (Aryan) Kshatriyas and hence by Hinduism (Ansari 2001, 322)."
  (p. 93, folio not stated) Mother worship is "connected to other Goddess veneration in, for example, ancient Egypt,
  Greece, and Austria (Ansari 2001, 144–162)", and "besides Egypt, Greece, and Europe also in places like Brazil and
  Turkey", indicative to Ansari of an "evolution of human consciousness" whose roots are found in "matriarchy" (Ansari
  2001, 15) (p. 93, folio not stated). "Hinglaj falls into the same time period as the ancient Mohenjo-Daro or might be
  'even older than that' (Ansari 2001, 335)" (p. 93, folio not stated); "Abro also connects Hinglaj to the Mother
  Goddess from Mohenjo-Daro" (p. 94, folio not stated). **All of these are ascribed positions; none is the author's
  own.**
- **Abro's "real Sindh".** "At one point, he writes that Sindhis once lived in the valleys between Rohri and east
  Balochistan, an area he defines as the 'real Sindh' (Sin. aṣal sindh) that was once 'prosperous' (Abro 1991, 22; Sin.
  sukhī satābī). According to Abro, the whole Sindhi civilization originated in these mountains." (p. 94, folio not
  stated) "Since Sindh remained under the influence of Sufi mysticism (Sin. taṣavvuf), the area is 'fortunately in terms
  of faith, secular [in English]' (Abro 1991, 23; italics added). This means that religious places in the region are not
  bound to particular communities or religious affiliations, but are a part of the collective heritage of a 'real
  Sindh.'" (p. 94, folio not stated) His equivalences: the Goddess is not distinct from "Nānyā, Anāhītā, Kālī, Durgā [or]
  Pārbatī" (Abro 1991, 51) (p. 94, folio not stated).
- **The harmony claim is called a utopia.** "These writers emphasize the common notion among Sindhis today that in the
  history of Sindh, there is no religious tension between the two communities—Muslims and Hindus. This notion of the
  harmonious coexistence of the religions, however, is merely a nationalist utopian ideal, as Aggarwal (2013) has
  shown." (pp. 90–91, folio not stated)
- **Sindhi nationalism dated and sourced.** "Verkaaik describes Sindhi nationalism as a relatively new phenomenon that
  began with the struggle for independence from the Bombay Presidency in the 1920s and 1930s (Verkaaik 2010, 197).
  Important for the further development of this discourse was, according to Verkaaik, the 1947 publication, Save Sind,
  Save the Continent, by Ibrahmin Joyo." (p. 88, folio not stated) Joyo's distinction: communalism is "a 'ridiculous idea
  with absurd consequences,'" while true nationalism "'is not based on religion, but history, culture, and geography'"
  (Verkaaik 2010, 202) (p. 88, folio not stated).
- **G. M. Syed's evolutionary scheme and its paradox.** "He asserts that starting from primitive Hinduism and Buddhism,
  mankind developed toward Christianity and Islam and, in his view, eventually gained a scientific approach to
  existence, which ultimately excels in Sufism (Verkaaik 2004, 33). Syed then finds Sufism, as the universal goal of the
  human race, in its purest form in the province of Sindh. For him, this is proven by the various shrines of Sufi saints
  that are, to this day, visited by Hindus and Muslims alike." (p. 89, folio not stated) "Syed's approach, however, is
  paradoxical in its basic assumption: on the one hand, it is universal to all, transcending petty religious
  boundaries… but on the other hand, it is geographically deeply rooted in Sindh and its inhabitants." (p. 89, folio not
  stated) Verkaaik, quoted approvingly: "In short, both Sindhi Hindus and Muslims were influenced by mysticism and
  religious tolerance. This was the Sindhi essence that both Hindus and Muslims shared" (Verkaaik 2010, 210) (p. 89,
  folio not stated).
- **Decline narratives with two culprits.** Against Pakistan: "Malkani's book teems with such rants against Pakistan and
  everything broadly associated with it… Here, Sindh is described as solid, ancient, and refined, while Pakistan and its
  'Urdu-wallas' are portrayed as temporary, shallow, and uncultured." (p. 87, folio not stated) Against the British:
  "They also aim at British rule, which they view as having victimized Sindh. Here, another kind of intruder, the British
  colonizer and not the Muhajir, holds responsibility for the slow destruction of Sindh's glorious past." (p. 87, folio
  not stated) "Soomro also renders the history of Sindh as a story of decline in which the province lost some 'golden
  past,' which it had enjoyed before the invasion of outsiders." (p. 88, folio not stated)
- **The methodological refusal that closes Chapter 2 — and it is the sentence an archive entry should quote when citing
  any antiquity claim from this book.** "Although I do not doubt the ancient history of the Hinglaj shrine in
  Balochistan, I want to emphasize that at this point, I am more interested in the current usage of such topoi of origin
  (or the 'chimera of origin' as Foucault says) and how they shape Pakistan's and Sindh's recent religious and
  nationalist landscape." (p. 102, folio not stated)
- **Two Sindhi sentences printed without any English gloss.** "Sindh xūśqismatī'a sān jī'un tahcqīdan[text-layer?]
  [taḥqīqan] jī mcāmulī[text-layer?] [maʿāmulī] men sekyūlar rahe āhe." (p. 101, folio not stated) And: "Asān ke dhartī
  te insān jo d̤āke ba d̤āke śacūrjaṇ[text-layer?] [śaʿūrjaṇ] jo tasalsal ai rābt̤o naz̤ar ache tho. Ītrī qadr jo hinan
  jo pīro khaṇje tho tah sabhnī jūn pāṛūn pātāl tā'īn vañi hik jag̈h te milan thyūn, yacnī[text-layer?] [yaʿnī] sabhnī
  jo b̤ij hik mādar parastī āhe" (Sin.) (p. 101, folio not stated). **No translation is printed for either: (not
  stated). No translation has been supplied here.**

### The author's argument (7): mytho-history, caste and the colonial census

- **Mytho-history as the chapter's frame.** "a concept which emphasizes how memories of a mythological past become
  significant moments of present day politics"; "What makes a narrative mythical is 'not its truth or falsity, but the
  fact that it [is] concerned with order in a fundamental, cosmological sense' (Malkki 1995, 55)"; the term is "inspired
  by the work of Lawrence Babb ('mythohistorical knowledge,' Babb 2004, 24) and Liisa Malkki ('mythico-history,' Malkki
  1995)." (p. 51, folio not stated)
- **Narrative as the carrier of orthopraxy.** "I understand narratives in their role as bringing together a particular
  set of elements into a meaningful sequential order... they address a certain audience that has a relationship with or
  simply an interest in their content (Hinchman and Hinchman 1997, xv). A community's narratives convey what must be
  done and how it should be done but also—and perhaps most important—what should not be done. They can be case studies
  or examples simplifying abstract concepts of social behavior (Berrenberg 2007, 30)." (p. 118, folio not stated) "In the
  case of many ritual journeys, mytho-historic narratives connect landscapes with bodily memories. They recall the
  hardships of former travelers as well as their successes and faults during their travels. Such narratives give ritual
  journeys meaning and locate them within the wider religious discourse of a community." (p. 118, folio not stated)
- **Foucault's genealogy as the method.** The author applies "Foucault's reading of Nietzsche" from "Nietzsche,
  Genealogy, History" (1977): "Instead of beginning at some distant point in the past and trying to follow its traces
  into the present, Foucault suggests analyzing how supposed past events are rearticulated to fit into a present
  framework", against "a search for unknown 'chimeras of origin' (Foucault 1977, 144)" (p. 66, folio not stated), with
  the block quotation at Foucault 1977, 146 (pp. 66–67, folios not stated). Consequence: "the work containing the text
  about the Brahma Khatris' origin becomes central to the narrative's analysis" (p. 67, folio not stated).
- **Caste categories as political instruments.** "It is important to note that categories of identity always embody a
  certain kind of reduction. Such caste names—in the form of jāti or varṇa—are best understood as political instruments
  (as will be demonstrated) and thus malleable." (p. 58, folio not stated) The People of India series is called "a
  version of the census introduced by the British and unfortunately reproducing the worst aspects of colonial
  ethnography" (pp. 58–59, folio not stated).
- **The colonial-census argument, with its documents.** Dirks is quoted at length: "Risley noted several major
  struggles, including an attempt by Khatris of the Punjab and United Provinces to be classified as Rajputs [Kshatriyas]
  that was ultimately successful, asserting that these efforts vindicated his belief that 'the sole test of social
  precedence . . . was native public opinion.' (Dirks 2001, 222)" (p. 60, folio not stated). Risley's premise: he "saw
  India as a perfect environment for his studies since, in his thinking, the 'races' on the subcontinent had not mixed
  to the same extent as they had in Europe (Dirks 2001, 214)" and "believed strict marriage codes in the subcontinent
  had helped to preserve the physical characteristics of the different races" (p. 60, folio not stated). The Kayastha
  parallel: the case "Raj Kumar Lal v. Biseshwar Dayal, heard at the Calcutta High Court in 1884", in which "a Kayastha
  man could not legally adopt his sister's son due to the declaration of the Kayasthas as Shudras in the 1891 census
  (Carroll 1978, 239)", led to "the production of pamphlets, genealogies, and publications with which the community
  attempted to prove its Kshatriya descent (Rowe 2007, 202)" (p. 60, folio not stated). Varma's hostile verdict is
  quoted: "The Khatri who would fain rank himself with the Sesodias of Meywar, with the Jadus of Karauli, or with the
  Bhatis of Jaisalmere, still remains a Punjabi Bania; and no species of argument will ever transform the quill-driving
  caste of Kayasthas into a Rajput clan." (cited in Rowe 2007, 204) (pp. 60–61, folio not stated) The author's
  conclusion: "These and similar discussions were common in the early twentieth century, and they triggered waves of
  self-reflection and identity-searching in many communities. It is with this background that I will analyze the
  mytho-historic corpus consolidating the genealogies of the Khatri, Brahmakshatriya, and associated communities through
  their relationship with the Goddess Hinglaj." (p. 61, folio not stated)
- **The narrative's social work, stated plainly.** "the story of the five princes explains the rupture between the
  glorious past and the present state of the (Brahma) Khatri community… their fall from the ruling class to the status of
  (mere) traders is not the community's fault but rather the will of the gods… The story assures that the Brahma Khatris
  are both mighty kings by blood and prosperous merchants by the will of the gods." (p. 66, folio not stated) And: "it is
  here where the divine and the mundane meet and where the humans can exercise some agency over their fate" (p. 66,
  folio not stated). Occupational instruction is written into the myth: Vishvakarman "instructs them in producing red
  color, printing cloth, and selling goods, with the exception of ghee, honey, and oil. He also tells them that although
  they can no longer use weapons themselves, they should now utilize their Kshatriya knowledge to sell them to others."
  (p. 65, folio not stated); and in a second version, Vishvakarman "advises the princes to sell liquids (except oil), to
  sell grains, and to produce weapons (see chapter 89). The book Hinglāj Jī Yātrā does not mention any particular
  occupational advice (pp. 88–89)." (p. 99, folio not stated)
- **The text as a "learned opinion", offered as a hypothesis and marked as such.** "it is possible that the aforementioned
  text centering around the Goddess Hinglaj was produced for this exact purpose—as a 'learned opinion' in this time of
  upward social mobility. Taken as a given, this would also explain the repeated demand found in the texts—uttered by
  different deities such as Hinglaj or Vishvakarman—to always respect the Saraswat Brahmins." (p. 68, folio not stated)
  **The author states the limit in the same breath**: "A definite answer as to the presented mytho-historic text's exact
  date of production… cannot be offered here" (p. 68, folio not stated). "the ongoing discussion about the 'highly
  speculative' Khatri/Brahmakshatria identity (Singh 1998, 1721) is connected to two powerful and authoritative entities:
  Hinglaj, as divine assurance for the community's aristocratic bloodline (Foucault's metaphysical ground of history par
  excellence) and Risley, as the census commissioner for the British Raj." (p. 68, folio not stated)
- **Shared genealogies asserted by the shrine literature.** "The Hiṃgulā Purāṇ, for example, also asserts that the Bhats
  and the Bhanushali share the same genealogical roots. Chapter 86 is titled 'The Origin of the Brahmakhatri, the
  Pushkarana, and the Bhati,' which associates these three communities and provides them with the same genealogy. For
  some reason, Jareca's publication omits this heading, most likely because it directly addresses the Brahmakhatri
  community (Jareca n.d.). Hinglāj Jī Yātrā also associates the Ahir community with the Khatris (Khatri 1973)." (p. 99,
  folio not stated)
- **Local adaptation of a pan-Indian theme**, with Gail quoted: "The content of the Parashurama story is a clear case in
  point of how an old and formerly local story remains alive by moving through India being adapted to various local
  traditions" (Gail 1977, 206) (p. 65, folio not stated).
- **Devi traditions as a technology of dynastic legitimation.** "The mytho-historic narratives of the bards and the
  Rajput communities reveal a strong connection between the establishment of ruling dynasties in the area and the
  apotheosis of many Charan women… The deification of the Charani was often related to their social and material status
  and was closely linked to their relationships with local rulers." (p. 74, folio not stated) And on Avar: "The
  mytho-historic corpus around Avar Mata is a telling example for the general relationship between the local Charan
  communities and the former (Muslim) king… Friction, suppression, and eventual release are motifs found in many such
  stories revolving around west Indian Goddesses." (p. 73, folio not stated)
- **Caste history read as political instrument, the chapter's conclusion.** "The Brahmakshatriya community, for example,
  employed so-called learned opinions by the Saraswat Brahmins, including mytho-historic genealogies, to advance their
  upward political and social mobility in the early twentieth century. In Sindhi nationalist discourse, on the other
  hand, the shrine of Hinglaj is linked to a 'real' Sindh that prospered before outsiders began suppressing the province.
  Within such nationalistic notions of a 'golden past,' the shrine is appropriated to fit modern concepts of an ideal
  Sindhi nation." (p. 97, folio not stated)
- **Dating the shrine's own scriptures, sceptically.** "Thus, according to my research, the Hiṃgulā Purāṇ did not appear
  in writing before the twentieth century. Other texts presenting the same story are even more recent." (p. 99, folio not
  stated) "The mere fact that Jareca prints a Sanskrit text he proclaims as ancient does not necessarily prove that the
  text is not a Sanskrit translation of an older Gujarati version. This assertion stems from the fact that I have not been
  able to find the Hiṃgulā Khaṇḍa anywhere, nor have I ever met anyone who has laid hands on this version of the Skanda
  Purāṇa." (p. 99, folio not stated) An expert opinion is entered: "Professor Isaacson from the University of Hamburg
  estimated that the text was written in the past two or three centuries (from a personal email communication January 2,
  2009)." (p. 100, folio not stated) A source claim refused: "The association is bolstered through the claim that this
  narrative is part of the eighteen Purāṇas, namely the Skanda Purāṇa; I have not been able to verify this claim."
  (p. 65, folio not stated) And the translation base declared: "Since the Hiṃgulā Purāṇ is available to me in a
  translation from Gujarati only, I chose to base the story mainly on my own translation from Hindi/Sanskrit as it is
  found in the work of Jareca. This is supported with interpolations from the Sindhi work not mentioned in Jareca's
  text." (p. 99, folio not stated)
- **Sanskrit attestation is symbolic capital, not evidence of antiquity.** "The Tantras and Purāṇas are notoriously
  difficult to date; there is no universally accepted canon, and many important texts have been lost." (p. 52, folio not
  stated) Rocher is quoted: "On numerous occasions scholars have taken notice of the existence of manuscripts the
  contents of which did not correspond to those of any of the published versions" (Rocher 1986, 61) (p. 52, folio not
  stated). "This mentioning in the Sanskrit corpus serves as 'symbolic capital' for the temple in Balochistan" (p. 52,
  folio not stated). And on the living side: "Discussions about the earliest occurrence of the name 'Hinglaj' in Sanskrit
  sources have, arguably, a limited influence on the perception of the Goddess among visitors at the shrine today.
  Sanskrit certainly has a strong symbolic value for Pakistani (and Indian) devotees, but the actual content of many of
  the aforementioned texts is often secondary." (p. 57, folio not stated)
- **A published error corrected.** Mohidden's "Hingadajki", placed by that author "somewhere in today's Saudi Arabia":
  "It is very likely that this is simply a misreading of 'Hinglaj.'" (p. 75, folio not stated)
- **Colonial ethnography as a co-production, acknowledged and set aside.** "There are many works that hint at the
  entanglement of British and local informants in the production of what is generally understood today as 'Hinduism.'
  Since this is not the topic of this study, I will simply refer here to a few influential works (Sen 1993; Kopf 1969;
  Dirks 2001; King 1999)." (p. 98, folio not stated)

### The author's argument (8): autobiography, the witnessing-I, and the pilgrimage's textual corpus

- **Why the old accounts matter as sources.** "Because many devotees in Pakistan and India consider these sources as some
  of the few authentic documentations about the pilgrimage's former reality, these texts form a significant part of
  Hinglaj's sedimented history. Particularly, more recent sources create forms of authenticity upon the truthfulness of
  the autobiographical accounts." (p. 104, folio not stated) "Āgneya Tīrtha Hiṅglāj, therefore, not only is a product of
  but also produces discourses on the desert Goddess." (p. 136, folio not stated) "Both novels, Marutīrtha Hiṃlāj and
  Āgneya Tīrtha Hiṅglāj, exert strong influence on public perception of the ritual journey and form, undoubtedly a
  significant part of the Hinglaj discourse." (p. 144, folio not stated)
- **A methodological limit stated twice.** "Due to the scarcity of sources, it cannot simply be assumed that the
  pilgrimage described in them was the form generally followed in those days" (p. 122, folio not stated); and on Hart's
  `Hiṃgulā Purāṇ` claim, "a fact that I cannot confirm, at least not with the version available to me" (p. 121, folio not
  stated).
- **The narrating-I and the experiencing-I.** "This split within the I-narrator can be described as having two layers:
  the 'narrating-I' and the 'experiencing-I' (Vogt 1990, 71).24" (p. 123, folio not stated) The narrating-I "is able to
  weave contingent events together into a coherent story in which every detail fits into a flow of events, ultimately
  climaxing in the temporal location of the narrating-I. The experiencing 'I,' by contrast, acts and reacts to the events
  within the story's timeline without the benefit of hindsight." (p. 123, folio not stated)
- **Autobiography as a literary regime, after Renza.** Autobiography necessarily "borrows [ways of writing] from the
  methodological procedures of the imaginative fiction" (Renza 1977, 2), which "places autobiographical accounts written
  from the perspective of an I-narrator somewhere between literary 'design' and historical 'truth'" (p. 124, folio not
  stated). "discussions about historical accuracy in autobiographical accounts are structurally limited as such accounts
  follow a certain literary regime." (p. 124, folio not stated) Memory is dated from its moment of retrospection: "The
  temporal moment of this retrospection is crucial to the composition of memory because a coherent life narrative helps
  to construct the 'durable character of an individual' (Ricoeur 1991, 77)"; the point of recall may "'subordinate' the
  history to such an extent that the narrative represents 'the writer's present self-identity' (Renza 1977, 3).25"
  (p. 124, folio not stated) McLean, Pasupathi, and Pals describe autobiographical narratives as "situated stories",
  personal memories "created within a specific situation, by particular individuals, for particular audiences, and to
  fulfill particular goals" (McLean, Pasupathi, and Pals 2007); so "autobiographies are not stable presentations of a
  certain part of life but... change and grow alongside the author's own development" (p. 124, folio not stated). "Here,
  questions such as publication date, publishing house, and even the language in which the story is composed must be
  considered." (p. 125, folio not stated) A caution about memoirs: "Such is often the case in the memoirs of public or
  politically active people, where certain kinds of actions are retrospectively justified (cf. Musharraf 2006)."
  (p. 148, folio not stated)
- **The move the author makes with all of it — away from factuality.** "it may be more appropriate to turn one's analysis
  away from notions of factuality in favor of questions about why and for whom such authenticity might be important."
  (p. 125, folio not stated)
- **The `witnessing-I`, the chapter's own coinage for this corpus.** "the authorial name (the association of author,
  narrating-I, and experiencing-I) confirms the truth of the witnessed events, which has crucial implications for this
  particular corpus of texts" (p. 125, folio not stated). Two ways of establishing it, after Lejeune: the obvious, "when
  the name of the author is the same name mentioned in the following text" (p. 125, folio not stated) — exemplified by
  Jasol, "'One of them asked: "Where can I find Narasimha Jasol among you?" I was startled. Who could be this man asking
  for me in Pakistan?' (Jasol 2008, 92)" (p. 126, folio not stated) — and the implicit "autobiographical pact" (Lejeune
  1989), which requires that "'vital statistics' (date of birth, ethnicity, language) should be identical between the
  narrator and the author (Lejeune 1989, 11)" and that "the story should relate to a world that can be linked to the
  vital statistics of the author" (p. 126, folio not stated). **Why it matters at this shrine specifically**: "For many
  devotees and authors I have met, this witnessing aspect of the autobiographical pact was more important than the
  texts' literary quality. Texts are often only judged according to this witnessing aspect." (p. 126, folio not stated)
- **The author's own working rule for the re-narrations, which governs how every summary in §3.6 should be read.**
  "Accepting obvious and implicit forms of the autobiographical pact, in the following re-narrations I will, for the sake
  of clarity, associate the name of the author with the narrator and the protagonist of the story." (p. 126, folio not
  stated)
- **Authenticity as the criterion devotees actually apply, and the two books judged by it.** "For many devotees in India
  and Pakistan, the narrative presents an accurate account of the 'authentic' or 'traditional' yātrā (mainly due to its
  descriptive style), and thus people frequently refer to the text." (p. 136, folio not stated) Against Abadhut: "the
  work is often judged according to a certain standard of accuracy due to which the strong narrativization of the account
  appears obstructive and seen as a sign of 'mere storytelling'" (pp. 135–136, folios not stated); "for many, Marutīrtha
  Hiṃlāj represents a work of fiction rather than a reliable account of the pilgrimage" (p. 136, folio not stated).
  Abadhut's romantic plot causes "the 'witnessing-I' to lose his credibility" (p. 133, folio not stated); the tunnel
  scene "constitutes a significant break from the 'autobiographical pact'" because the narrator publishes what the ritual
  authority forbade him to speak of, so "the witnessing-I either does not respect the rules set out by the Aghori Baba
  or, more significantly, does not fear the Goddess's potential wrath and hence is not a good devotee. This contradiction
  undermines the notion of the witnessing-I." (p. 134, folio not stated) In Shastri, by contrast, "there is no distinct
  line between the author and the protagonist/narrating-I" and the line "is kept as malleable as possible throughout,
  since the identification of the narrating-I with the author stands for the uniqueness and authenticity of the story"
  (pp. 142–143, folios not stated). The pact's mechanics in Abadhut: "The narrative implies a link between the author and
  the narrator, even though the 'I' in the text does not explicitly relate to the name on the book's cover. In the
  preface to the Hindi translation, however, the translator, Nihararanjan, refers to a personal meeting with Abadhut,
  which provides additional support for the implicit link between author and narrator." (p. 132, folio not stated)
- **Secrecy as symbolic capital**, via Harder on the Maijbhandari: the paradox is one where "a certain code of secret
  knowledge [here, the image of the Goddess] is exposing itself to the non-initiated public [here, the reader], while
  insisting all the while on its marked property as a secret code [here, Aghori Baba's advice]" (Harder 2011, 279)
  (p. 135, folio not stated). "This language of secrecy carries a certain 'symbolic capital' (Bourdieu 1998) gained
  through the indication of a potential additional element without any further elaboration (Urban 1998). The author
  validates the implied content precisely by referring to its secrecy." (p. 135, folio not stated) The inverted reading
  is offered too: "the reading of the text would assume the same status as actually undertaking the journey, and so the
  initiated reader gains some share in the Goddess's darśan." (p. 135, folio not stated)
- **Shastri's protagonist as rationalist-devotee, and why that matters for the shrine's reach.** "On the one side, he
  paints a picture of himself as a spiritual seeker deeply rooted in the teachings of the Bhagavad Gītā, a character who
  believes in the agency of volcanoes and does not question the presence of spirits. On the other side, though, he is a
  fierce agitator against blind faith, orthodoxy, and traditional interpretations of the classical Hindu scriptures
  (Shastri 1977, 20)." (p. 143, folio not stated) What distinguishes him is "not his devotion to the Goddess" but "his
  practice of ātmaviśvās (Hin.), his self-confidence" (p. 143, folio not stated). "While all the others are passive
  sinner-subjects of the volcano's agency, the protagonist is active, even receiving the volcano's approval for his
  words." (p. 143, folio not stated) **The transferable argument**: "The protagonist's endless doubts about the useless
  rituals and cruel oaths lead to a unio mystica which, in my opinion, appears even more 'genuine' because it happens to
  a skeptic, an event that even the rationally inclined can believe in. Thus, the yātrā emerges as a mystical experience
  for all; it has meaning not only for those stuck in blind faith—that is, people who believe in purity and rituals—but
  also for those who follow humanist principles." (p. 144, folio not stated) "This mystical aspect finds its climax in
  the final darśan at the shrine, an event far from any known worldly experience and thus indescribable." (p. 144, folio
  not stated) The communalism critique in that book is attributed to Shastri, not to the author: "Shastri argues that the
  reality of the dharma can't be bound by the limitations of communalism.37 The saṃpradāy (Hin. established doctrine)
  for him is merely a system of useless laws and rites, whereas only the Gītā offers solutions for the real predicaments
  of human beings and thus provides an explanation of dharma (Shastri 1977, 20)." (p. 140, folio not stated) Note 37
  gives "Dharmatattva sampradāyoṃ kī sīmā se baṁdhā nahīṃ hotā hai" (Shastri 1977, 20) (p. 148, folio not stated).
- **The novel's three motifs, as Schaflechner numbers them.** "(a) renouncement and austerity are portrayed as an
  important part of the ritual journey (Abadhut 1964, 154); (b) the narrator is critical of the pilgrimage and its rigid
  rituals (Abadhut 1964, 49, 91); and (c) the ritual journey is described as a metaphor for life in which all humans are
  pilgrims on an unknown path (Abadhut 1964, 132)." (p. 132, folio not stated)
- **Exoticization named as such.** "an exoticization of the noble Balochi cameleers is found in both accounts (cf.
  Shastri [1977, 10] and Abadhut [1964, 4])" (p. 143, folio not stated).
- **The influence question, deliberately left open.** "This coincidence of very closely corresponding themes … might
  suggest that Shastri copied parts from Abadhut's much earlier work. However, there is no definite proof of such
  borrowing." (p. 145, folio not stated) The author's preferred explanation is ritual sedimentation: "certain themes were
  commonly present on the pilgrimage and expressed in different fashions by different writers" (p. 145, folio not
  stated); "the assumption that Abadhut's writing influenced Shastri's work to some degree is likely, but the idea that
  Shastri copied his description directly from Abadhut's is much less so" (p. 145, folio not stated). And he declares the
  question immaterial: "Notwithstanding the fact that the mutual influence is an intriguing question, it ultimately does
  not have an important impact on this study. I am interested in the various ways the yātrā was represented and how
  certain common themes arose that eventually sedimented into an allegedly 'true' pilgrimage—or, in other words, into one
  of the tradition's most conspicuous truth-claims." (p. 145, folio not stated) Which themes carried over: "Themes such as
  the carrying of onions, the idea that the camels are the actual guides along the way, or the breaking of the oath given
  at the River Hab reappear in Shastri's work." (pp. 144–145, folios not stated) "The closest similarities in the two
  stories are found in the description of the oath at the River Hab and the appearance of the Aghori Baba at the shrine
  of Hinglaj.45" (p. 145, folio not stated), note 45 giving the pairs: "Compare here Shastri (1977, 11) with Abadhut
  (1964, 6), and Shastri (1977, 54) with Abadhut (1964, 211)." (p. 149, folio not stated)
- **Austerity as the through-line of the whole corpus.** "Representations of the pilgrimage, such as the travelogue by
  Abadhut, the account given by Shastri, and the mytho-historic narrative of Rama, emphasize the necessity of austerity
  and kinesthetic practice, captured by the concept of tapas or tapasyā, along the way." (p. 146, folio not stated)

### The author's argument (9): the ahiṃsā debate, reform movements, and the economics of vegetarianism

- **The Indological debate, summarised by the author and not adjudicated.** Alsdorf (2010), whose "seminal piece on the
  practice of non-violence and vegetarianism in 1962" (p. 298, folio not stated) is cited, "assumes that Vedic traditions
  did not know the concept of ahiṃsā and that the link between adhering to a certain diet and salvation was established
  only later in the Manusmr̥ti (200 BCE–200 CE)" (p. 298, folio not stated), and reads Manusmr̥ti chapter 5 (at 5, 48–49
  against 5, 56) as uniting "three different positions ... an initial stage where the eating of animals is allowed, a
  second stage where it is forbidden outside a ritual framework, and a third stage, where vegetarianism is propagated and
  animal sacrifice is completely outlawed (Alsdorf 2010)" (p. 299, folio not stated). **The author's caution**: "Whether
  these stages appeared in subsequent historical order and eventually evolved into ahiṃsā cannot, however, be
  definitively established." (p. 299, folio not stated)
- **Schmidt against Alsdorf.** Schmidt "disagrees with Alsdorf's thesis that there was no concept of ahiṃsā in the Vedic
  times", understands ahiṃsā as an oath "not expected of the common man" (Schmidt 2010, 103) binding only on "the Vedic
  brahmacārin", and argues "that Vedic sacrifice developed into symbolic, or interiorized, sacrifice and eventually to
  renunciation (Schmidt 2010, 124)" (p. 299, folio not stated).
- **Heestermann.** Violence "became replaced by some 'system of ritualism that minimized' brute force (Heestermann 1984,
  122)", visible in the śrauta rituals where "the (animal) victim was no longer beheaded but instead was led away from the
  sacrificial stake to a different location where it was then suffocated" (p. 299, folio not stated); and he claims "the
  connection between `ahṃisā`[text-layer?] [ahiṃsā] and vegetarianism was not at all natural in the beginning and emerged
  only out of a Brahmanical ritual", via the dīkṣita's meat abstention (pp. 299–300, folio not stated). "Buddhists and
  Jains, both strong advocates of non-violence, had—according to Heestermann—no particular connection to vegetarian
  philosophy (Heestermann 1984, 126)." (p. 300, folio not stated)
- **Doniger.** She "posits that a vigorous debate on the morality of animal slaughter was already present at the time of
  the Brāhmaṇas (800–600 BCE)" and that criticism became open "in later works such as the Mahābhārata (Doniger 2011,
  150–151)" (p. 300, folio not stated).
- **The author's own dissent from Urban.** Urban holds that bali survived only through "the existence of śākta and
  tantric traditions (Urban 2011, 52)"; the author replies: "I believe this position is too general since not all
  traditions in which blood sacrifice plays a role can be subsumed under the concept of tantra. The case study of the
  Devipujak-Vagris—introduced below—is just one such exception." (p. 300, folio not stated)
- **The author's critique of an Indological generalisation.** Of Lodrick's sentence (Lodrick 1981, 55) he writes that it
  and similar statements "exclude many Indian communities who practice bali or at least do not adhere to vegetarianism"
  and imply "that Islam is excluded from the possibility of 'expressing Indian conceptions'"; "the virtues defined here
  can be seen as the views of a particular elite" (pp. 297–298, folio not stated).
- **Bayly's economic explanation, endorsed.** "a history of non-violence or vegetarian politics must take into account
  economic developments of the nineteenth century" (p. 301, folio not stated); "The rejection of meat and alcohol was
  already a trend among commercial communities starting from the late eighteenth century (Bayly 1999, 217)"; "By the
  early twentieth century, different clusters of such trading communities had formed a distinct 'bania-merchant culture'
  (Bayly 1999, 217)" (p. 301, folio not stated). The author's summation: "it was as a result of these economic and social
  advantages that ahiṃsā as a way of life began to flourish among the trading middle class." (p. 301, folio not stated)
- **Reform movements and colonial reason.** Brahmo Samaj and Arya Samaj "found common ground in their strong opposition to
  elements of 'folk religion' widespread within Hindu practice. Blood sacrifice was 'a clear case in point' (Fuller 1992,
  101)—a conspicuous remnant of an alleged superstitious past that, in the reformers' view, must be overcome by reason."
  (p. 302, folio not stated)
- **Bali being written out of the tradition by Hindu authors, and the author naming the move.** "Recent texts describe
  this practice, and a few (Hindu) authors attempt to distance themselves from it (Motyani 2011, 283). This all serves to
  support the claim that bali is not original to Hinduism and has never been a part of the 'original' Hinglaj tradition
  (Khatri 1973, 10)." (p. 223, folio not stated) He defers the analysis: "This issue of animal sacrifice as a nodal point
  in the distinguishing of 'proper' from 'improper' Hindu behavior inside the valley will be dealt with separately in
  Chapter 6." (p. 223, folio not stated) And he concedes the long-running character of the wider argument: "Undoubtedly, a
  long controversy exists regarding the affirmation or rejection of blood rituals." (p. 325, folio not stated)
- **The author's reading of "awareness" and "education" in his informants' mouths.** Of the Meghwar interviewee: "I
  interpret this statement as suggesting that sacrificial rituals are a part of the community's superstitious past, which
  is to be overcome gradually through progress." (p. 303, folio not stated) Of the pujārī: "the denial of animal sacrifice
  is connected to the study of religious texts, in particular the Bhagavad Gītā" (p. 305, folio not stated), an
  interpretation that "may have its roots in the presence of religious texts sponsored and distributed in Pakistan by the
  ISKCON society" (p. 305, folio not stated).

### The author's argument (10): the Pakistani-Hindu predicament, reflexivity, and the research agenda

- **The coherence problem, stated and then refused.** "one may wonder about the coherence of a group that ultimately seems
  connected merely by a common designation as 'Hindu.' The longer one's gaze lingers on this diversity, the more any
  general statement about Hinduism in Pakistan appears to be sheer presumption. In short, Hinduism as an identity marker
  seems an elusive term that has no beginning and no end, made up only of thriving and ongoing discussions about what it
  means to be a Hindu in Pakistan." (p. 14, folio not stated) His resolution: "the difficulty of producing general
  descriptions about the Pakistani-Hindu community as a whole, I argue, must be juxtaposed with certain unifying
  particularities of Hindu (and non-Muslim) life in Pakistan." (p. 19, folio not stated) Why so little has been written:
  "The country's instability, the feudal environment in Sindh—home to the majority of Pakistan's Hindus—or perhaps a
  simple lack of interest have all effectively kept scholars from inquiring" (p. 13, folio not stated).
- **Two predicaments, historical and everyday.** The historical: "The complex link between Pakistani citizenship and Hindu
  identity has its roots in the two-nation theory (Urd. do qaumī naz̤ariyah), later also accentuated in the Pakistan
  ideology (Urd. naz̤ariyah-i pākistān). This founding myth, central to the Islamic Republic, claims that Muslims and
  Hindus are two distinct nations that can only thrive through their separation, and ultimately led to the partition of
  the subcontinent in 1947." (p. 19, folio not stated) **He then disowns the narrative**: "Today, however, we know that
  the movement for Pakistan (Urd. taḥrīk-i pākistān) was not a straightforward development or the manifestation of a clear
  demand for a separate Muslim homeland (cf. Shaikh 2009; Jalal 1995; Talbot 2012)." (p. 19, folio not stated) And, citing
  Farzana Shaikh: "the very existence of minorities inside the country constantly contests the two-nation theory and the
  understanding of who is a 'real' Pakistani (Shaikh 2009, 69)." (p. 19, folio not stated) The section's epigraph is
  Herzfeld: "Nationstate ideologies tend to divide the world into Manichaean pairs and to coerce or seduce their citizens
  into adopting the same rhetoric for the moral organization of their own everyday social relations. (Herzfeld 2004, 15)"
  (p. 19, folio not stated).
- **Althusser applied, and explicitly amended.** "Louis Althusser, a French Marxist, termed institutions that help to
  disseminate such ideologies 'ideological state apparatuses' (ISA) (Althusser 1971)… Here, the educational ISA do not
  primarily reproduce relations of production as it is found in Althusser, but rather aim to sediment differences between
  Muslims and Hindus, which serves as a legitimization of the very existence of the Islamic Republic of Pakistan."
  (p. 20, folio not stated) **The amendment is the author's own and should be cited as such.**
- **Synecdoche as the mechanism of prejudice, and pars pro toto as the mechanism of violence.** "schoolbooks suggest,
  through synecdoche, that the habits of some Hindus are the habits of all Hindus." (p. 20, folio not stated) "alleged
  desecration conducted by non-Muslim communities often leads to pars pro toto actions in which religious identity
  temporarily becomes a sufficient reason for mob violence to occur." (p. 17, folio not stated) **He immediately broadens
  it**: "Such pars pro toto actions are triggered not only by religious identities, as the manifold and complex cases in
  the city of Karachi exemplify. Similar incidents are also connected to multiple other identity signifiers including
  ethnicity, language, class, and so forth." (p. 18, folio not stated)
- **The Urdu horror genre as a second archive of demonisation.** "Duśmanī is a characteristic example for how Hindus
  feature in such and similar Urdu horror tales in Pakistan (Schaflechner 2016a). The story uses a wide set of
  genre-typical tropes including power-hungry Hindu villains, black magic, and pious Muslim saviors." (p. 23, folio not
  stated) "In Duśmanī, this also extends to India as a nation as it is the silent supporter of Dr. Shankar's insidious
  plan… India, thus, emerges as a cunning nation, which is conniving with an evil scientist and a tantric to gain world
  domination." (pp. 22–23, folios not stated) "These structures abound within the genre, and—with few exceptions—can be
  seen as the main characteristic of stories that feature Hindu characters (Schaflechner 2016a)." (p. 23, folio not
  stated)
- **The summary verdict, and the mirror finding on the Indian side.** "Such examples show that being a Hindu in Pakistan
  today means to be split between one's religious affiliation and one's loyalty to the nation state." (p. 23, folio not
  stated) "Due to a host of factors, thus, including the absence of patron-client relationships, the structural exclusion
  of Hindus within the governmental education system, their demonization in parts of popular culture, and the very
  foundation of the two-nation theory itself, the Pakistani-Hindu identity exists in a kind of purgatory, caught betwixt
  and between." (p. 23, folio not stated) "Hindu migrants to India also face extreme difficulties after their migration.
  This time it is not their religious identity, but their Pakistani heritage that causes them to be eyed with suspicion by
  their environment (Schaflechner 2014). This marginalizes many Pakistani Hindus on both sides of the border." (p. 23,
  folio not stated)
- **Why a single remote site can carry the whole argument.** "notwithstanding that this study is centered on the rituals
  and narratives of one particular site located somewhere in the far-off desert of Balochistan, its recent developments
  reveal much about the Hindu communities of Pakistan." (p. 24, folio not stated) And what the site supplies: "how Hindu
  cultural practices flourish in Pakistan today and also how they need a certain environment—such as Hinglaj—to thrive"
  (p. 23, folio not stated).
- **Reflexivity: the book counts itself as an actor in the process it describes.** "As the production of written or
  recorded material is often a step within the solidification of tradition, due to its production of central nodal
  points, naturally, the research for this work also left its traces on the object under investigation." (p. 47, folio
  not stated) "any kind of social study should not be blind toward its effect on the studied object" (p. 48, folio not
  stated), citing Latour: "[A]ny study of any group by any social scientist is part and parcel of what makes the group
  exist, last, decay, or disappear." (Latour 2005, 33f.) (p. 48, folio not stated) And: "the present study plays its own
  role in the solidification of the Hinglaj tradition and thus also produces—intentionally and unintentionally—another
  truth-claim." (pp. 48–49, folio not stated) The custodian put the demand on him directly: "You come from a university,
  you will find out what the image really stands for!" (p. 48, folio not stated) — and at another point the Goddess was
  invoked to stop him: "the Goddess had come to him in a dream and instructed him to tell me not to conduct any further
  inquiries" (p. 201, folio not stated).
- **The author's own position inside the field, treated as data.** The Charan community's help was itself read as the
  Goddess's will: "I received much help, and I was not permitted to give anything in return… 'I did not do anything,
  whatever you get is all the Goddess's mercy. She herself wants that your work should be successful.'" (p. 173, folio not
  stated) Rajendra turned the research question back on him: asked what had changed after his pilgrimage, he "simply
  repeated the question back to me, asking me how I felt as someone who had been to Hinglaj and was now staying in a
  Charan community" (p. 176, folio not stated). And: "The surge of interest that I have already encountered during my
  research at Hinglaj" (p. 326, folio not stated).
- **The book's stated scope, and what it declines to do.** "This work portrays Hindu thought and practice in Pakistan
  through the lens of one particular religious site. It offers just a brief glimpse into the Pakistani-Hindu community's
  current situation and therefore, needs to leave broader questions unanswered." (p. 325, folio not stated)
- **The research agenda the author sets for others, in four moves.** "Future research needs to build upon these
  foundations by investigating the impact of recent political changes for Hindus in the Islamic Republic." (p. 325, folio
  not stated); "I hope that this work triggers additional research that addresses the widely unstudied field of Hindu
  rituals and religious imaginaries in Pakistan." (pp. 325–326, folio not stated); "Finally, future scholarship should
  examine the topic of Hindu political and economic engagement, particularly in the Sindh province." (p. 326, folio not
  stated); and "I, furthermore, wish that other such studies into the rich cultural landscape of Pakistan will give the
  Islamic Republic a place within South Asian studies beyond the dominant narratives of a failed state, ethnic violence,
  and terror." (p. 326, folio not stated)
- **What the Works Cited shows about the evidence base** — a structural observation, not a claim about any shrine. The
  list combines (i) European critical theory in quantity — Althusser, Bourdieu, Butler, Derrida, Foucault, Gramsci (via
  Simon), Laclau and Mouffe, Lefebvre, Marchart, Rancière, Saussure, Wittgenstein, Žižek (pp. 327–337, folio not stated);
  (ii) colonial-era travel, survey and gazetteer literature — Burton 1851 and 1877, Goldsmid 1863, Hart 1839 and 1840,
  Holdich 1904 and 1910, Hughes 1877, Masson (1844) 1977, McConaghey et al. "Las Bela" 1907, Pottinger 1816, Stein 1943,
  Wilford 1851, Baines 1893 (pp. 327–336, folio not stated); (iii) a large body of Hindi, Gujarati, Sindhi and Urdu
  devotional and pilgrimage literature about Hinglaj; and (iv) the author's own films and articles (p. 335, folio not
  stated).

### Legends and miracles not attached to an archive row

*Compact, one line each, page referenced. Every legend that belongs to a row — the Sati
dismemberment, the Hingol foundation story, Rama's pilgrimage and its water miracles, the milk
miracle of Mekan Dada, the Charan bell and the falling rock, Eve at Hinglaj, Hazrat Ali's split
rock, the wife of `cAli`[text-layer?] [ʿAli], the Muslim woman priest, the stone ship at
Goorab-i-Sung, the dream commands, the volcano's confession augury, the eighty-four and eighteen
Koops, the Chandrakup possession and exorcism, Shah Abdul Latif's milk and his flight to Thatta,
Ali's camel in the Lahoot cave, and the Lal Shahbaz henna legend — has been folded into that row's
**Legends & Miracles** run above and is not repeated here. Attribution follows the book's own
exactly.*

- **The supernatural of the Urdu pulp story `Duśmanī`, attributed by the author to the story and not
  believed**: a tantric's "mantra that will supply the superstitious doctor with the power to control
  other peoples' minds" (p. 21, folio not stated); eleven hearts "cut out of unnamed male victims"
  (p. 21, folio not stated); men transformed "into man-machines with the ability to
  breath[text-layer?] [breathe] outside of Earth's atmosphere" (p. 22, folio not stated); "a gang of
  ghosts… all from pious descent" who kill the villains (p. 22, folio not stated). The author's gloss:
  the story turns on "Black magic, gained from the brutal murder of the innocent and executed by the
  Hindu doctor, versus an army of pious spirits under the command of a saintly figure." (p. 23, folio
  not stated)
- **Jinns, named only in the acknowledgments** — thanks "to Hasan Ali Khan for introducing me to his
  world of jinns" (p. 7, folio not stated). **Nothing further is said**: **(not stated)**.
- **Clan Goddesses appeased with blood, attributed generally and to no named site**: some Hindu
  communities in Pakistan "worship manifold Mother Goddesses as their clan or family patrons, who must
  be regularly appeased with blood sacrifices" (p. 14, folio not stated).
- **Indra, disguised as a bumblebee, stings Jayasena's thigh to expose him as a Kshatriya**, and
  Jayasena "stabilizes his mind and ignores the pain" rather than wake Parashurama (p. 63, folio not
  stated) — told as the narrative of Jareca's book.
- **Parashurama's curse on the five princes**: "You will forget all that I have taught you, and from
  now on, you and your ancestors will be known to the world as Brahmakshatriyas!" (p. 64, folio not
  stated).
- **The curse strikes generations later in battle** — "their arrows begin turning into grasslike
  sticks" (p. 65, folio not stated).
- **The Brahmakshatriya pre-history**, paraphrased by the author from three devotional books (pp.
  61–62, folio not stated): Parashurama's vow to kill all Kshatriyas; Ratnasena's flight with his five
  pregnant wives; Dadhichi's ashram where "due to his spiritual power, even the wild animals live
  there in peace"; the protective forest in which "they would be safe from Parashurama's arrows"; the
  boar hunt that draws the king outside it and his death; the wives' self-immolation; Dadhichi's lie
  that the princes "were the sons of Brahmins"; the sacred threads and the Veda recitation.
- **The Karna and Parashurama parallel**, offered by the author as a structural comparison and not as
  a Hinglaj legend: Karna "disguises himself as a member of the sage Bhrgu's family"; an insect bites
  his thigh while Parashurama sleeps in his lap; Parashurama "curses Karna, foretelling that he will
  forget the powerful knowledge of the brahmā weapon in the moment when he will need it the most
  (compare chapters 2 and 3 of the Śānti Parva)." (p. 99, folio not stated)
- **The Khatri escape legend** — "the Kshatriya kings had to adopt the pseudonym 'Khatri' to escape
  the wrath of Parashurama (Mohidden 2003, 670)" (p. 58, folio not stated) — and its rival, "that the
