  Kshatriya rulers began referring to themselves as Brahmakshatrias in the twelfth century to avoid
  the conversion to Islam by Muslim invaders (Mohidden 2003, 670)" (p. 59, folio not stated).
- **Kshatriya women saved by Brahmin households**, attributed to Khatri mythology via Cook (p. 81,
  folio not stated).
- **Avar Mata reveals herself as the Goddess**, kills "Bakha, the buffalo demon", and the taste of its
  blood "sent the Goddess flying into a frenzy" (p. 72, folio not stated); she kills fifty-two
  Hun-demons (p. 72, folio not stated).
- **Karni Mata "miraculously drew the bows of the army of Bikaner, allowing them to defeat their
  enemies from a safe distance (Kamphorst 2008, 248)"** (p. 73, folio not stated).
- **Mekan Dada's buried pilgrim clothes**, laid down in a former life as Lakshmana at "the village
  Kombhri, in Nakatrana", and rediscovered when his parents dig where he forbade (pp. 76–77, folios
  not stated) — "paraphrases from interviews conducted with members of the Kapri community in 2010 and
  in 2011" (p. 76, folio not stated).
- **Sita's ordeal by fire and her disappearance**, from the Rāmāyaṇa summary: "Sita responds to this
  insult by burning herself on the pyre but when the fire does not touch her, her purity and divine
  nature are proven." (p. 120, folio not stated); "the ground opens, and the princess disappears."
  (p. 120, folio not stated)
- **Parvati kills a demon with a trident near Thatta and the defiled khic'ṛī turns to limestone**,
  attributed to Briggs: "Shiva and Parvati were on their way to Hinglaj, and they stopped for a rest
  in the area of Thatta… a demon approached Parvati and tried to attack her, but Parvati killed the
  demon with a trident… he told Parvati to throw away the khic'ṛī. Thus, the grains of the food turned
  into the limestone found at Thatta today (Briggs 1938, 104–105)." (p. 206, folio not stated)
- **The Nereid of Astola**, attributed to Arrianus (AD 96–180) via Eggermont: "Once a Nereid lived on
  Astola, who tricked sailors into coming to the island and on their arrival turned them into fish.
  The sun got irritated with the nymph and so told her to leave... which also freed the sailors and
  they all turned back into humans, thus creating the people later called the fish-eaters (Eggermont
  1975, 81–82)." (p. 259, folio not stated)
- **Trance utterances recorded at the Devipujak Thursday sessions in Karachi**: "I will kill those who
  cast a spell on you!" and "Ramesh, you only called on me one time, but I will answer you twenty-one
  times!" (p. 310, folio not stated). "These Thursday meetings also commemorate particular incidents
  in the community's past, as when the Goddess saved the Ghorava's ancestors from misfortune, or how
  she once abandoned one of the family's `bhopā`s." (p. 310, folio not stated) **Neither incident is
  narrated or dated**: **(not stated)**.
- **Protection by scriptural study** — "you have your energy, and if you study the śāstras [Skt.
  religious texts], then the spirits won't catch you" (p. 304, folio not stated).
- **Divination by grains, twice refused and once granted**, in Rajendra's account of founding a Karni
  Mata temple in his Rajasthani village: he "performed the `iki`[text-layer?]" at the Bhairava temple
  and "did not get the permission from Bhairava to build the temple" (p. 175, folio not stated); after
  the buffalo-herder's message, "I took some of the grains again to ask Bhairava for his permission
  and I immediately got it!" (p. 175, folio not stated). **`iki` is not glossed in the running text**:
  **(not stated)**.
- **The buffalo-herder who delivered the message**, in Rajendra's words: "In my village, there is a
  person who frequently comes to the fields close to the temple to give water to the buffalos. But on
  this day, he left the animals alone. To this day, when you talk to him, he does not know what
  happened. I had not told anyone about my plans yet, but he came straight up to me and said:
  'Brother, you can't establish a Karni Mata temple in your village! We already have one!'" (p. 175,
  folio not stated)
- **Stones and springs as the gods' natural forms**, attributed to Sircar via Erndl and to Dyczkowski:
  "Pools and water springs were associated with the reproductive organs of the Devi, and hill chains
  were imagined as her breasts (cited in Erndl 1993, 35)." (p. 200, folio not stated); "The
  presentation of Shiva or the Devi in the form of stones is very common all over the subcontinent
  (Dyczkowski 2009, 247)." (p. 193, folio not stated, note 9)
- **Index lines that point to legend material and are evidence of that and nothing more**: "miracles,
  163" (p. 353, folio not stated), the whole of the book's `miracles` entry; "divine intervention,
  125, 163" (p. 344, folio not stated); "healing, 240–242, 289" and "eye disease, healing, 240–242"
  (pp. 346, 344, folios not stated); "caves, disappearance into, 234n30" (p. 342, folio not stated);
  "footprints, 243" (p. 345, folio not stated); "ghosts, 19, 62, 133, 289" (p. 345, folio not stated);
  "foundation tales, 66" (p. 345, folio not stated); "possession by spirits, 133" and "spirits, 19,
  133, 289" (pp. 357, 361, folios not stated); "visions, 122, 125" (p. 363, folio not stated);
  "mystical experience, 135–136" (p. 353, folio not stated); "traga ritual, 65", "self-immolation,
  62", "self-sacrifice, 62, 124", "suicide, 121–122, 124" (pp. 362, 359, 361, folios not stated);
  "will of the Goddess, 160", "service to Goddess, 140", "Well of Truth, 199", "`Macar's bone`
  [text-layer?] [reading uncertain], 190", "Treta Yuga, 67, 68", "kali yuga, 175, 176" (pp. 364, 359,
  363, 352, 362, 350, folios not stated); "Mahishasura, 35" and "Raktabija, 295" (pp. 352, 357, folios
  not stated); "Karna myth, 57n24" and "mytho-historical narratives, 40, 67, 91, 92, 96–97, 109, 184,
  238, 242, 295" (pp. 350, 353, folios not stated). **No index locator has been converted into a
  `(p. N)`.**

## Figures and sites not in the archive

*Every name below was checked against `shrine_index.tsv` (169 rows) before being listed here. Names that in fact
answer to an archive row — including deities and persons who are a row's principal figure but appear in Schaflechner
only in passing or as authors, and bare toponyms that carry archive rows under ruling (a) — have been moved out of this
section and are collected at the end under `## Misfiled — belongs in the archive section` for the coordinator. One
entry per distinct figure or place, merged across all 19 chunks. **Strong candidates for new archive rows are in
bold.***

### A. Sites in Pakistan with a named shrine, temple or cult — candidates for new rows

- **Mata Bhawani / Bhavani temple, Makli, near Thatta, Sindh** — the single strongest candidate in this book. "This
  Devi temple is located in Makli, near the Makli hill sites, close to the city of Thatta." (p. 205, folio not stated)
  Its naming is contested: "Historically, it is linked to names such as Ashapura (Briggs 1938), Hinglaj (Lakhavat
  2011), or Bhavani Devi (found at inscriptions in the temple), depending on the religious affiliation of the community
  asked (Boivin 2007, 35)." (p. 205, folio not stated) **Four origin claims stand unreconciled on one page**: "An
  inscription on one of the southeastern walls claims that the temple (or parts of it) were constructed in 1872";
  "Ansari writes that a group of Yogis placed an idol of the Goddess at this place around 1300 CE (Ansari 2001, 21)";
  Thatta "once had two akhāṛās… a smaller one for common pilgrims and a large one for the aristocracy on their way to
  Hinglaj. Apparently, lost inscriptions from these akhāṛās commemorated the journeys of several kings and aristocrats
  over several centuries (Ansari 2001, 22)"; "People at the temple today claim that it was constructed by members of
  the Patel community from Gujarat." (p. 205, folio not stated) **No king or aristocrat is named: (not stated).** Its
  standing and calendar: "it enjoys the status of one of the most significant places of worship east of Karachi for
  Hinglaj pilgrims. The temple is home to an annual festival in October but also sees many visitors subsequent to the
  main festivals at Hinglaj." (p. 205, folio not stated) The prescribed sequence: "It is said that pilgrims should go to
  this temple before they set off for Hinglaj but also visit it once more after their return (Ansari 2001, 22)."
  (p. 205, folio not stated) Its craft: "The Mata Bhawani temple is also famous for a particular necklace made there
  from limestone and called ṭhum'rā." (p. 205, folio not stated) It is also the terminus of the Narayanpur walk: "the
  end of the ritual journey is reached when the chaṛī attached to the rath is taken to the Mata Bhavani temple in
  Thatta, where it is 'cooled down' in the temple's pond" (p. 165, folio not stated); the 2010 Gujarat group "did their
  offerings at the Mata Bhawani temple in Thatta (Schaflechner 2011, 11:00)" (p. 181, folio not stated); and its priest
  is a named source, unnamed personally, interviewed in November 2010 (p. 170, folio not stated). Gopal Maharaj served
  at "the Makli temple in Thatta" before Hinglaj (p. 253, folio not stated). Also: "there is, for example, a cave
  immediately behind the Mata Bhawani temple in Thatta that is rumored to be the place where Latif miraculously
  reappeared after he was forced to flee from Hinglaj" (p. 108, folio not stated). Index runs: `Mata Bhawani temple, 99,
  152, 158, 168–169, 191–193` (p. 352, folio not stated) and `Makli, 191–193` (p. 352, folio not stated). **Warning:
  there is no Thatta row and no Makli row in `shrine_index.tsv`. Do NOT fold this into
  `shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir` merely because it is a Hinglaj-cult satellite, and do NOT fold it into
  `jagannath-temple-sialkot`. Note also that the book spells it `Bhawani` in its own heading and `Bhavani` from the
  temple's inscriptions, both on p. 205; and that the index (chunk 019) does not place it at Thatta at all, so the index
  cannot be used to confirm the location.**
- **Jagnath akhāṛā and its Hinglaj Mata temple, Bhimpura, Karachi** — a living Karachi satellite of the Hinglaj cult,
  and the second strongest candidate. "Nearby on an old confined compound lies the Jagnath akhāṛā, which, according to
  local residents, was once a part of the Nagnath akhāṛā. It still shelters an active Hinglaj Mata temple where the
  chaṛīs (Hin. stick) for the pilgrimage are kept in a corner closet." (p. 206, folio not stated) "The Hinglaj effigy
  inside is a rough image, made of stone and without any elaborate ornamentation. It shows the Goddess in her
  eight-armed form. On the inner temple wall is an image of a snake, a representation of one of the Goddess's many
  forms." (p. 207, folio not stated) "it was in the Jagnath akhāṛā that the aguā, the leader of the pilgrimage group,
  accepted the chaṛī before the group set off" (p. 207, folio not stated). "The local priest at the Jagnath akhāṛā
  remembers that in his father's time, many pilgrims would come to this temple and begin their journey by taking one of
  the chaṛīs brought from the valley of Hinglaj." (p. 207, folio not stated) Still in use: "the pilgrims from
  Narayanpur mentioned in Chapter 4—still treats the Jagnath temple as the journey's main starting point." (p. 207,
  folio not stated) It "houses one of the oldest active Hinglaj temples in the city" (p. 307, folio not stated); it
  sheltered the Vagri community during the 1992 riots (p. 307, folio not stated); the bhopā's weekly `ḥāẓrī` is held
  there (pp. 307, 309, folio not stated); and it "was once a main gathering spot for Hinglaj pilgrims" until "just a
  few decades ago" (p. 159, folio not stated). **The local priest is not named, and no founding date, custodian or
  address beyond Bhimpura is given: (not stated).** Index: `Jagnath akhāṛā, 147, 292–294` (p. 349, folio not stated).
  **Warning: this is in Karachi. Do NOT fold it into `jagannath-temple-sialkot` — the Pass 1 worker considered and
  refused that match, and the name resemblance is the only thing linking them. Under ruling (a) its toponym is Karachi;
  see `## Misfiled`.**
- **Nagnath akhāṛā, Karachi** — the institution that ran the pilgrimage before Partition, now closed. "The Nagnath
  akhāṛā (Hin. a courtyard or an assembly place for a paṃcāyat, musicians, or sādhus) played an important role for the
  yātrā until the partition of the subcontinent and the subsequent relocation of many Hindu families from the city of
  Karachi to India (Zamindar 2008; Hasan 2009)." (p. 206, folio not stated) "In the days before the separation, this
  area belonged to the Nath saṃpradāy (Hin. a religious sect) and served as the main gathering place for pilgrims
  coming from all over British India intending to peregrinate to Hinglaj." (p. 206, folio not stated) "Originally built
  for the sādhus of the Nath community, the place turned into a refuge for beggars and the homeless in the 1940s
  (Abadhut 1964, 1)." (p. 206, folio not stated) "Nowadays, the Nagnath akhāṛā in Karachi is closed and, it is not
  possible to enter the main building anymore." (p. 206, folio not stated) "Some years ago, the area around the Nagnath
  `akhāṛā`, which once consisted of many acres of open land, was turned into a commercial space with stores and
  workshops. During the 1992 riots... the Nagnath temple was largely destroyed in the violence. Today, visitors are not
  allowed inside the site, and the temple is no longer frequented by devotees." (p. 307, folio not stated) It is
  "the main gathering place for the pilgrimage to Hinglaj" in the 1946 account (p. 127, folio not stated) and the
  Aghori Baba's former residence (pp. 131, 142, folio not stated). **No founder, no date of building, no custodian, no
  acreage, no date for the commercial conversion and no owner: (not stated).** Index: `Nagnath akhāṛā, 117, 129, 133,
  292` (p. 354, folio not stated). **Warning: two Nath archive rows were considered and refused by the Pass 1 worker
  (`gorakhnath-goraknath-temple`, Peshawar, and `tilla-jogian`, Salt Range); neither is in Karachi and neither should
  absorb this. Its toponym is Karachi; see `## Misfiled`.**
- **Liari village and its Goddess temple, Las Bela District, Balochistan** — the pilgrimage's modern first paṛāv, with a
  temple, an economy and a datable rise. "The small village of Liari is situated in the Las Bela district, west of the
  N 25 RCD Highway connecting Karachi to Quetta, the capital of Balochistan. The village is home to only a few hundred
  people but for several reasons has become the pilgrimage's main starting point over the past fifty years (Jethamalani
  1994, 7)." (p. 207, folio not stated) It is a recent addition: "In older travelogues, Liari and its Goddess temple do
  not play any role, and neither Hart nor Abadhut mention it in their accounts. Shastri also does not mention the
  place… Nor does Wilford allude to the village or its temple, in spite of describing three different routes to Hinglaj
  (Wilford 1851)." (p. 207, folio not stated) "According to local oral histories, however, Liari served as the first
  paṛāv for many Lohanas from the city of Bela who approached Hinglaj from the north." (p. 207, folio not stated) "The
  construction of the N 25 changed the sequence of stops and also helped a small Hindu temple in Liari village to gain
  more importance as the new starting point for the yātrā." (p. 202, folio not stated) Also: "the next paṛāv in Liari
  where the group stayed at the local Devi temple" (pp. 159–160, folios not stated); ochre clothes "are donned by many
  at the Liari temple" (p. 156, folio not stated); the bhag'vā and the ochre cap are put on and sold there (p. 209,
  folio not stated); "the bus took them straight to the village Liari where, according to Dayaram, the fireplace of
  Lalu Jasraj, the aguā of Ramacandra's party and the first Kapri, is found" (p. 181, folio not stated). **The Goddess
  of the Liari temple is not named and the temple is not otherwise described: (not stated).** Index: two separate
  headwords, `Liari, 71, 78, 112, 148–149, 168, 189, 192–196, 195f` and `Liari temple, 144` (p. 351, folio not stated).
  **Warning: no Liari row exists. Do NOT fold it into `shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir`, and note the book's own
  spelling rule — "Here the village 'Liari' in Balochistan is referred to. The famous neighborhood in Karachi of the
  same name is spelled 'Lyari' to avoid confusion." (p. 192, folio not stated, note 3) — so it must not be conflated
  with Karachi's Lyari either.**
- **The Kali Mata shrine inside the Hinglaj Valley** — a distinct shrine within the valley, the focus of the whole
  animal-sacrifice history, and arguably a row in its own right. "One of the most contentious and intriguing areas in
  the valley today is certainly the shrine of Kali Mata, which is also associated with the practice of animal sacrifice
  (Hin. bali/balidān)." (p. 221, folio not stated) Its position: "the Kali shrine located just some meters before the
  temple of Hinglaj (Goldsmid 1863, 207)" (p. 285, folio not stated); "the Kali shrine in the same valley" (p. 306,
  folio not stated). Its fabric: "The blood of the animals was then spattered inside a particular cavity in the wall
  symbolizing the mouth of the Goddess Kali" (p. 221, folio not stated); "FIGURE 5.3 Opening into the stone wall at the
  Kali Mata temple, 2012" (p. 222, folio not stated). It has "a long tradition of `bali`" (p. 306, folio not stated) and
  its own priest, "the Kali priest who stopped sacrificing goats due to his study of the scriptures" / "the Kali
  `pujārī`" (p. 312, folio not stated) — **not named, and the date of that change is (not stated)**. For the Vagris:
  "For the Vagris, Kali is the guard (Hin. `caukīdār`) of the Goddess Hinglaj." (p. 311, folio not stated) **Warning:
  three Kali rows exist in the archive — `kalat-kali-temple` (Kalat, Balochistan), `kali-bari-mandir` (Peshawar) and
  `kalka-cave-temple-asthan-of-kalka-devi` (Aror, Sukkur) — and the Pass 1 worker considered and refused all three. Do
  NOT fold this shrine into any of them, and do not silently absorb it into
  `shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir` either: the book treats it as a separate shrine with separate rules and a
  separate priest.**
- **The Ashapura temple inside the Hinglaj Valley** — "The first stop inside the confined boundary walls is the
  Ashapura temple." (p. 220, folio not stated) The name is glossed: "the paṛāv of Ashapura ('the one who fulfills
  wishes')" (p. 220, folio not stated). "This paṛāv was traditionally where the pilgrimage groups rested and is
  considered the last stop of the yātrā (Goldsmid 1863, 207; Jethamalani 1994, 7)." (p. 220, folio not stated) Its
  decline: "The plentitude of pilgrimage shelters available in the valley has caused the Ashapura stop to lose its
  central position as the main resting stop before, and in between, the necessary rituals, and its role has been
  degraded to that of merely another temple inside the valley." (p. 221, folio not stated) Its statue was "built by the
  HSM itself at the end of the 1990s", replacing "a small stone at that location, smeared with siṃdūr (vermilion), which
  was once worshipped as the Goddess" (p. 220, folio not stated). Index: `Ashapura, 55–56, 67, 70, 176, 189–191,
  208–209` and `Ashapuri, 67n36` (p. 340, folio not stated). **Warning, and this is the named hazard of this run: the
  string "Asha Pura" appears inside `shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir`'s own `location_short` ("Hingol Balochistan
  National Park, Road، Asha Pura, Pakistan"). That is a place-name in the archive row's address. It must NOT be treated
  as a match to the Goddess Ashapura, to the Ashapura temple inside the valley, to the Kutch Ashapura temple, or to the
  Ashapuri branch of the Kapris. Do not merge any of them.**
- **Astola Island and its ruined Kali temple, Arabian Sea, off the Makran coast** — "the westernmost stop mentioned in
  reports describing the traditional pilgrimage. Today, however, Astola no longer plays a significant role within the
  pilgrimage network. The island is located in the Arabian Sea, between the two ports of Ormara in the east and Pasni in
  the west. It is located about 40 kilometers away from the Makran shore." (p. 259, folio not stated) Hart: an
  "uninhabited rock, upwards of a mile in circumference, on which neither water nor wood is to be found" (Hart 1839,
  105) (p. 260, folio not stated). "Today there are only a few ruins of a Kali temple on the island." (p. 260, folio not
  stated) "According to his informants, the place is dedicated to the Goddess Kali, and goats are sacrificed at a
  particular spot on the island." (p. 260, folio not stated) Also "it is not the custom to stay at the island and that
  no pilgrim dares to remain there at night (Hart 1839, 105)" (p. 260, folio not stated). Access: "To reach the place,
  the yātrīs take boats from nearby ports, or even set out from Karachi." (p. 260, folio not stated) "Today, because of
  the military presence in the Arabian Sea, pilgrims rarely visit Astola. Some members of the HSM tried to organize a
  trip to the island with the help of the military in 2012 but did not succeed, and I do not know if such a tour has
  been successfully organized since then." (p. 260, folio not stated) **The recorded names for it**: `Astola`,
  `"Satadip"` by Hindus, `"Habtala"` by the local Balochis (Hughes 1877, 154), `"Ashtola"` and `"Haftola"` in Goldsmid
  (Goldsmid 1863, 203), `"Asthala," "Carnine," and "Nosala"` in Greek sources (Eggermont 1975, 81), and `"Sata Deveep"`
  in Hart (p. 259, folio not stated). Index: `Astola Island, 245–246` (p. 348, folio not stated). **Warning: no Astola
  row exists, and this is a candidate for one. Do NOT fold it into any of the three Kali rows.**
- **The Ram Pir temple, Narayanpur Colony, Karachi** — the site of the Narayanpur group's departure jāg'raṇ (p. 163,
  folio not stated). **Warning: the Pass 1 worker considered and refused `ramapir-temple-tando-allahyar`; that row is at
  Tando Allahyar and this temple is in Karachi. Do NOT merge them.** Note separately that Ramdevji, the principal figure
  of that Tando Allahyar row, is named in the book at p. 13 — see `## Misfiled`.
- **The Santoshi Mata temple, Narayanpur Colony, Karachi** — source of "a flame from the Santoshi Mata temple in
  Narayanpur, which symbolizes the Goddess and stands for her protection along the way" (p. 163, folio not stated).
  Index: `Santoshi Mata temple, 151` (p. 360, folio not stated). No archive row.
- **Melari (Meladi) Mata temple, inside the Hinglaj Valley** — a Karachi Gujarati foundation in the valley: a booth by
  an unstated year, then by 2013 "a small concrete temple" with "a statue of the Goddess about one and a half meters
  tall" (p. 271, folio not stated). Index: `Melari Mata, 254–255` (p. 353, folio not stated). **A new structure built
  inside the valley by an outside community — a real candidate, and evidence that the valley acquires shrines.**
- **Momai Mata shrine, on the way to Chaurasi, Hinglaj Valley** — a vision "around the beginning of the millennium" on
  the way to Chaurasi, and a wooden edifice built there afterwards (p. 271, folio not stated). "Momai Mata is worshipped
  by a few Gujarati Hindu communities in Karachi." (p. 316, folio not stated, note 4) Index: `Momai Mata, 254–255`
  (p. 353, folio not stated).
- **The Devipujak-Vagri `maṭh`, Bhimpura, Karachi** — "the place where the sacrifices are performed. There, two separate
  fireplaces (Hin. `dhūnī`) are kept for the members of Ghorava and the Oughriya families, while a third one is
  designated for use by all other families in the compound." (p. 307, folio not stated) **A functioning community
  sacrificial site in Karachi, with no archive row.**
- **The Hindu graveyard, Purana Golimar, Karachi** — cleaned by the PHS in 2012 (p. 273, folio not stated), with the
  book's own correction: "This is indeed a graveyard where Hindus are buried, and not a `smaśān` (Hin. cremation
  ground)." (p. 315, folio not stated, note 7) Index: `Purana Golimar, 256` (p. 357, folio not stated). No archive row.
- **Mausoleum of Mohammad Ali Jinnah, Karachi** — the destination of annual Independence Day marches by Hindu
  organisations, named twice as "the mausoleum of Mohammad Ali Jinnah, the founder of Pakistan" (p. 23, folio not
  stated) and "Mohammad Ali Jinnah's mazār (Urd. tomb, mausoleum)" (p. 273, folio not stated). Index: `Jinnah, Mohammad
  Ali, 12, 256` (p. 349, folio not stated). **No Jinnah row exists in `shrine_index.tsv`; this is a named tomb with a
  recurring annual observance and is a candidate. Do NOT fold it into any Karachi shrine row.**
- **An unnamed Karachi temple** where the Sanjesh / Kali pujārī conversation took place (p. 304, folio not stated) —
  **the temple is not named: (not stated)**, so it cannot become a row.
- **An unnamed paṛāv just outside the Hinglaj valley wall**, "located only at a short distance from the entrance"
  (p. 279, folio not stated) — the site of the Hanuman/Ganesha dispute, now carrying an HSM-built Ganesha statue and a
  temple over it (p. 280, folio not stated). **It is not named beyond "a paṛāv": (not stated).**

### B. Named stations inside or immediately around the Hinglaj Valley

*These are features of the valley and the route rather than separate institutions; they are listed for completeness and
are most likely sub-features of `shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir` rather than candidate rows. The coordinator
should decide; **do not create rows for them without deciding that question first.***

- **Sharan Kund** (Hin. refuge-pond) — a pond "which, before it dried up, was directly in front of the Hinglaj temple"
  (p. 238, folio not stated); site of the three-submersion purification with a chosen `śaraṇ'bhāi/bahan` (p. 251, folio
  not stated); the removed liṅga was thrown into it (p. 238, folio not stated); concreted over by 2011 (p. 251, folio
  not stated).
- **Til Kund** (in Sindhi also `Taru Kund`) — "Upon entering the valley farther past the Hinglaj temple at a short
  distance to the west, one finds a pond called Til Kund (in Sindhi also Taru Kund)." (p. 253, folio not stated) "Til
  Kund and other nearby ponds in the northwest of the valley are not, however, mentioned in any older textual sources
  that I have found so far." (p. 253, folio not stated) "Between 2009 and 2015, Til Kund was always full of fresh water
  and partly covered with reeds. The bottom of the pond is not visible from its banks" (pp. 253–254, folio not stated).
  "Ansari writes that this pond was once the meditation place of Shiva (2001, 340), which, as people at the shrine say
  today, purified its water." (p. 254, folio not stated)
- **Brahma Kund** — "said to have been named after Brahma, who had sat there in `deep mediation`[text-layer?] [deep
  meditation] for thousands of years" (p. 255, folio not stated). Index: `Brahma Kund, 240` (p. 341, folio not stated).
- **Kir Kund** — "actually only the small, fresh reserve filled by a drizzle of water from a ledge on the western side
  of the valley. These drops are considered to have the power of healing all kinds of eye disease" (p. 254, folio not
  stated).
- **Kali Kund** — "a small pool just above Brahma Kund, which feeds into it from above." (p. 255, folio not stated)
- **Anil Kund** (also `Aṇīl Kuṃḍ`, `Alīl Kuṃḍ`, and `Chaurasi`) — "one of the more important ponds within the sacred
  geography of `Hinglaj.32`[text-layer?] [Hinglaj, with superscript callout 32]" (p. 255, folio not stated); "The fairly
  deep, clear water upwelling is situated on the top of a mountain and is only accessible through a long,
  two-and-a-half-hour ascent by foot" (p. 255, folio not stated); "Chaurasi, a two-and-a-half-hour ascent into the
  mountains, to a freshwater pond called Anil Kund where she had taken refuge. The area of Chaurasi is only accessible
  by way of a small walking path, and no paved road leads up to the place." (p. 188, folio not stated) **The naming is
  itself disputed and the book does not settle it**: "Some accounts state that the whole area is called Chaurasi,
  including Anil Kund, and the three pūjā places within are connected to Guru Goraknath, Guru Nanak, and Rama. Others
  say that Chaurasi is only the name of the pond on top of the hill chain." (p. 265, folio not stated, note 32) Index:
  `Anil Kund, 70, 167–168, 175, 201, 240–245` and `Chaurasi, 175, 201, 240n32, 241, 241n33` (pp. 339, 342, folio not
  stated).
- **`rām jharoku` / `rām baiṭhak`** — the third station on the northern mountain, "which literally means 'Rama's window'
  or 'veranda' but which in the local mythology refers to something like a reception hall. In Hindi/Urdu, the site is
  also called rām baiṭhak (Hin.), Rama's seat." (p. 257, folio not stated) "The place is visible when looking up from
  the Hinglaj shrine at the bottom of the valley and is often mentioned in the literature (cf. Abadhut 1964, 212; Hart
  1839, 80; Ansari 2001, 338)." (p. 257, folio not stated) A ladder built to it has since been destroyed (p. 257, folio
  not stated).
- **`Chaurasi ka Cakka`** — "In front of the Hinglaj temple there is a huge boulder named Chaurasi ka Cakka (Hin.
  caurāsī kā cakkā 'the Chaurasi stone' or alternatively caurāsī kā cakkar 'the Chaurasi tour')." (pp. 258–259, folio
  not stated) Index: `Chaurasi ka Cakka, 244–245` (p. 342, folio not stated).
- **Akash Ganga (`ākāś gaṃgā`)** — "another pilgrimage stop where they are to pluck a twig from a certain kind of tree"
  (p. 132, folio not stated). Index: `Akash Ganga, 122` (p. 339, folio not stated).
- **The River Hingol / River Aghor** — "The River Hingol, also called River Aghor,18 is the last major stop before the
  valley of Hinglaj. Unlike many other rivers in Balochistan, the Hingol flows throughout the year, even though it
  carries very little water during drier months." (p. 215, folio not stated) A bridge was built over it "in 2012"
  (p. 215, folio not stated). **A contradiction to carry**: the body text equates the two names, but the index prints
  them as two headwords with a reciprocal cross-reference that does not say they are the same watercourse — "Hingol
  River, 55, 64, 75–78, `104–195`[text-layer?] [reading not supplied], 133, 176, 203–205, 248. See also Aghor River"
  (p. 348, folio not stated) and "Aghor River, 121–122, 161–163, 189. See also Hingol River" (p. 339, folio not stated).
  Meanwhile Jethamalani's three-paṛāv list treats "The River Aghor" as its own asthān (pp. 202–203, folios not stated),
  and pilgrims take stones "from the River Aghor" (p. 173, folio not stated). **Both readings stand.**
- **Hingol National Park** — index headword only, "Hingol National Park, 140, 279–280" (p. 348, folio not stated); the
  author met a walking pilgrim in April 2012 "only a few kilometers away from the entry to the Hingol National Park"
  (p. 152, folio not stated). **This toponym does carry archive rows; see `## Misfiled`.**
- **Kanda Devi station** — "Kanda Devi station (Hin. paṛāv), a few kilometers after Chandrakup, is unique to the group's
  route. I have never been to this paṛāv myself. However, I was told that the community worships the Mother there in the
  form of a tree and traditionally it is at this paṛāv that the pilgrims choose their śaraṇ bhāī/bahan" (p. 159, folio
  not stated). **The author states he has never been there and marks the tree worship as hearsay; carry both marks.**
- **The River Hab / Hab River, and Hab Chawki** — the boundary at which the oath is taken and the pilgrimage begins
  (pp. 127, 139, folios not stated); "the other side of the Hab River" is the transition into the Goddess's area
  (p. 122, folio not stated); "the village of Hab Chawki" is a modern stop (p. 159, folio not stated). Index: `Hab
  Chawki, 111, 147` and `Hab River, 112, 130, 136, 190, 194` (p. 346, folio not stated).
- **Sonmiani (`śon'beṇī` / `śoṇ'veṇī`)** — "a coastal town in Las Bela district" and the historical tax post (pp. 127,
  140, folios not stated); the group waits there "two days and two nights" (p. 128, folio not stated); the site of the
  lesser `choṭī māī` yātrā (p. 113, folio not stated). Index: `Sonmiani, 103–105, 111–112, 118–119, 131, 190` (p. 360,
  folio not stated). **No archive row.**
- **Vindar, Bela, Uthal, Ormara, Pasni, Gwadar, Gabd, the Pab Mountains, the Mor hills, the Bahur River, the Lotan
  River, Cape Monz, the Makran shore** — route geography with no shrine attached (pp. 85, 112, 121–122, 159, 202, 259,
  folios not stated); index runs at pp. 339–363, folio not stated. **No archive rows.**

### C. Lost, unidentified or vanished stations on the old route

- **Khari Nadi / the River Khari** — the first paṛāv of Jethamalani's list, "where people take a bath in the ocean and
  worship Rama" (p. 203, folio not stated); dropped from the HSM's advertisements in 1994 and named by no one in the
  author's fieldwork (p. 204, folio not stated). Index: `Khari Nadi, 189–191` and `Khari River, 191` (p. 350, folio not
  stated).
- **The Guru-Shishya paṛāv** — a stop "located before Liari" which "almost completely disappeared from the pilgrimage
  network" after the N 25 was built (p. 202, folio not stated). Wilford's inland route "passes by a memorial for a
  deceased 'Jogi, or Dervish' and his disciple (Wilford 1851, 243), and a similar memorial is described by each of the
  two authors in their writing"; "This description of the Guru-Shishya paṛāv is almost completely forgotten today."
  (p. 203, folio not stated) **Neither jogi nor disciple is named: (not stated).** Index: `Guru-Shishya paṛāv, 190`
  (p. 346, folio not stated). **A named grave-memorial of a jogi and a disciple, now lost — worth a line in any future
  survey, but it cannot become a row without a name or a location.**
- **"Gopi Chund Raja"** — "a temple called 'Gopi Chund Raja'" near the Seeta Koowas (Hart 1839, 83) (p. 122, folio not
  stated). The author "could not identify any of these landmarks today" (p. 122, folio not stated). A note links the
  name to the Naths: "Also 'Gopichand Raja' is a figure associated with the Nath saṃpradāy. This is but another link in
  the strong association between the Goddess and the Nath sect." (p. 147, folio not stated)
- **"Thonga Bheero"** — "the site of a ruined temple [Hart 1839, 81]" (p. 122, folio not stated), "no longer commonly
  known".
- **"Imlee" and "Gorukh Tank"** — lost stations; Gorukh Tank is "where Rama is said to have retreated after his defeat
  by the Goddess" (p. 122, folio not stated). Index: `Imlee, 112`, `Gorukh Tank, 112` (pp. 348, 346, folio not stated).
- **"Seeta Koowas"** — five wells made by Sita's palm, named for her, the book's own root-form gloss being "(probably
  from Hin. <sītā kuāṁ>)" (pp. 121–122, 9, folios not stated).
- **Ram Bagh / Aram Bagh, Saddar town, Karachi** — "the place where Rama and Sita rested", "commonly associated with
  Aram Bagh" today (p. 122, folio not stated); Rama's first stop and the point he is ordered back to (pp. 120–121,
  folios not stated). **A toponym with a mytho-historic association but no shrine, tomb, ʿurs or custodian stated: not
  a candidate row on this evidence. Its city is Karachi; see `## Misfiled`.**
- **"Macar's bone"** — from Wilford: "(probably from Sin. machu or Hin. macch 'fish'), believed by pilgrims to be a
  whale's bone dug into the earth by some holy man to stop the surf from reaching beyond this point (Wilford 1851,
  252)" (p. 203, folio not stated). **The holy man is not named: (not stated).**
- **"Camala Devi"** — an area "which pilgrims cross quickly, in just a few hours, since there they are not allowed to
  spit, blow their nose, or throw anything else on the ground. According to the belief of devotees, the Goddess would
  punish any pilgrim who dared to do so with leprosy (Wilford 1851, 257)" (pp. 203–204, folios not stated). Index:
  `Camala Devi, 190` (p. 342, folio not stated).

### D. Sites outside Pakistan (India, and beyond) — outside the archive's scope, listed so no one re-derives them

- **Karni Mata temple, Deshnok, Rajasthan** — the post-Partition substitute for Hinglaj for many Charans. "the heirs to
  Bikaner's throne remained loyal to the Goddess, and over time, they elaborately adorned her temple in Deshnok"
  (p. 73, folio not stated); "Today, the Karni temple in Deshnok has become an important gathering spot for many Charan
  branches. Her devotees often mention that after the 1947 Partition and the subsequent problems in reaching Hinglaj,
  Karni has become the most important site for the community." (p. 74, folio not stated); "Another similar account
  claims that Karni Mata in Deshnok would now have the same significance as Hinglaj and that devotees should frequent
  her place instead." (p. 167, folio not stated)
- **The shrine of Temra Rai, Rajasthan (near Jaisalmer)** — the other named proxy: "A Charan devotee by the name of
  Anoop Dan claimed some decades ago that he had a dream in which the Goddess appeared to him saying that since her
  shrine in Balochistan was no longer reachable, the site of Temra Rai should be visited by Charans in its place."
  (p. 167, folio not stated) "The annual 42-kilometer-long circumambulation of the temple is especially an attraction
  for tens of thousands of people." (p. 74, folio not stated) **That is the only large attendance figure in the book
  that is not for Hinglaj, and it is for an Indian site.**
- **Koteshwara temple, Kori creek, Kutch, Gujarat** — "the temple of Koteshwara, on the coast of Gujarat" from which a
  ship reportedly left daily for the Hingol River (p. 114, folio not stated); "Abadhut, a pilgrim from Bengal, describes
  Koteshwara as the necessary end point of the pilgrimage, without which the whole yātrā would be invalidated (Abadhut
  1964, 222)" (p. 115, folio not stated); "the pilgrimage's last stop" (p. 132, folio not stated); the branding iron
  still sits there (p. 115, folio not stated); a priest there reports a fourteenth-century record and "the tomb of a
  saint close to Koteshwara on which the date of his journey was marked" (p. 147, folio not stated).
- **Mekan Dada's samādhi, Dhrang, Kutch** — "Mekan Dada's samādhi (Hin. state of enlightenment; but also 'tomb of a
  saint') today is found in the village of Dhrang, also in Kutch, where he is buried together with his donkey and his
  dog." (p. 75, folio not stated)
- **Samādhis of Hinglaj returnees near Bhuj, Kutch** — "Figure 4.5… Samādhis of returnees from Hinglaj near Bhuj,
  Gujarat, 2011" (p. 179, folio not stated).
- **Dayaram Kapri's ashram near Bhuj, Kutch** (p. 182, folio not stated) — **the ashram is not named: (not stated).**
- **The Hinglaj Mata temple at Mandapi, near Jaipur** — founded by Rajendra Singh with stones brought from Pakistan
  (pp. 173–174, folios not stated); Figure 4.4's caption: "Rajendra Singh in front of a Hinglaj mūrti with the
  installed piṃḍ in Mandapi village" (p. 174, folio not stated).
- **A "fairly new Hinglaj temple" in Delhi** — its head interviewed in October 2011 (p. 170, folio not stated).
  **Neither the temple nor the guru is named: (not stated).**
- **A new Hinglaj temple announced at Nakatrana, Gujarat** — "In Gujarat, one devotee called in a press conference in
  2011 to introduce a new Hinglaj temple in Nakatrana." (p. 167, folio not stated)
- **The Bhairava temple and the Karni Mata temple in Rajendra Singh's village, Rajasthan** — "There is a Bhairava
  [Shiva] temple in my village. It was only on account of his grace that an ordinary man like me was able to go to
  Hinglaj" (p. 174, folio not stated); the aborted plan to "turn this place into a Karni Mata temple to solve the
  problem" of a land fight, and an existing Karni Mata temple "a little further away" visited "on Holi or Diwali"
  (pp. 174–175, folio not stated).
- **The temple in Thatta held by Charan and Udasin saṃnyāsīs to be "as important as the main shrine in Balochistan"**
  (p. 71, folio not stated) — in Pakistan, not India, and probably the Mata Bhawani temple above, but the book does not
  say so; recorded separately for that reason.
- **The Devipujak-Vagris' ancestral `maṭh` at Palvodar / Palvadar, Bhavnagar district, Gujarat** — the Ghorava family's
  place of origin and "the community's main `maṭh`, the slaughtering place for animals" (p. 307, folio not stated); the
  Vagris "are obliged to perform all their rites of passage at their ancestors' shrine in the village of Palvadar in
  Gujarat" (p. 311, folio not stated). **The book spells the village two ways within four pages, `Palvodar` and
  `Palvadar`; both are recorded.**
- **Kedarnath temple, Jwalamukhi temple (Himachal Pradesh), Kamakhya, Kashi/Benaras and Vishvanath, Haridwar, Badrinath,
  Vrindavan, Calcutta's Kali temple, Mother Annapurna** — Indian sites named comparatively or inside quoted passages
  (pp. 103, 107, 117, 127, 193–194, 199, 211–212, 285, folios not stated). **None is in the archive's scope.**
- **Babri Mosque, Ayodhya** — named twice as the trigger of 1992 (pp. 15, 198, 319, folios not stated); "The destruction
  of the Babri mosque in Ayodhya (Uttar Pradesh, India) by nationalist Hindu zealots in 1992 is but one example of
  clashing imaginaries of religious space that triggered communal violence." (p. 198, folio not stated) Outside
  Pakistan.
- **New York City / Manhattan, and Norway** — used only as theoretical examples: the Twin Towers as element and moment
  (p. 39, folio not stated); "an anonymized firm in Norway" as Law and Lien's salmon fieldwork site (p. 33, folio not
  stated). **Not religious sites in this usage.**

### E. Living and recent persons at or around the shrine (none is an archive row's principal figure)

- **Virsimal Dewani** — the pilgrimage's chief organiser and the face of the HSM in the media; see the Cross-cutting
  section above for his full dossier (pp. 48, 208–209, 227, 274–279, 295–296, 316–317, folios not stated). Index:
  `Dewani, Virsimal, 37, 195, 214, 258f, 262` and `Dewani family, 258–260` (pp. 343–344, folio not stated). **No dates,
  no office title beyond "chief organizer" and "main spokesman": (not stated).**
- **Gopal Maharaj** — the resident pujārī at Hinglaj, appointed by the HSM in 2008, previously at the Makli temple in
  Thatta (pp. 46, 47, 48, 225, 253, folios not stated). **No lineage, caste, age, origin or predecessor: (not
  stated).**
- **Dost Mohammad** — "the Muslim caretaker at the shrine before the HSM gained its influence" (p. 219, folio not
  stated); his descendants alone were allowed to trade inside the valley during the fieldwork (p. 219, folio not
  stated); named as the head of the caretaker family (p. 115, folio not stated). **No dates and no term: (not
  stated).**
- **Vasrup Chand Tusli** — "a Karachi devotee" from whom "the idea for an annual gathering at Hinglaj came" (p. 275,
  folio not stated). **No dates, caste, office or further appearance: (not stated).**
- **Lali Bhua** — "The current `bhopā` of the Ghorava family… the son of Bhao" (p. 307, folio not stated). Index: `Bhua,
  Lali, 292, 295–296` (p. 341, folio not stated). **Bhao, Bhachu, Bhao Tulsi, Tulsi and Haza Dada** — the Devipujak-Vagri
  elders and ancestors invoked in the weekly `ḥāẓrī` (pp. 306–309, folios not stated); index `Tulsi, Bhao, 294–296`
  (p. 362, folio not stated). **No dates for any of them: (not stated).**
- **Dayaram Kapri** — "the head of a Kapri ashram in Kutch and one organizer of cross-border pilgrimages to Hinglaj"
  (p. 79, folio not stated); "a modern-day aguā who organized the trip and also served the pilgrims as a ritual expert.
  Dayaram hails from the Kapri community… and he himself went to Hinglaj for the first time in 2004. From then onward,
  he continued going regularly and also began taking larger groups, mainly from Gujarat." (p. 177, folio not stated) At
  each paṛāv he "recited Gujarati verses out of what he claimed to be the Hiṃgulā Purāṇ" (p. 180, folio not stated);
  he headed the 2010 Gujarat party "who also served as the spiritual guide" and was interviewed on television (p. 48,
  folio not stated). He "was not willing to show me his written notes" (p. 193, folio not stated).
- **Rajendra Singh** — "one member of the Maru Charan community… a government employee"; he "went to Hinglaj in 2004
  with a group of five people under the guidance of the Hinglaj Sheva Mandali" and "lives with his family on the
  outskirts of Jaipur" (p. 173, folio not stated); met "On my last visit to Rajasthan in fall 2011" (p. 136, folio not
  stated). Index: `Singh, Rajendra, 127, 160, 162f` (p. 360, folio not stated). **No dates of birth: (not stated).**
- **Raj Kumar** — "the most senior member of the walking community and also the man who initiated the annual pilgrimage
  tradition in the group", "the aguā or xalīfah (Urd. caliph; here: leader) of the group since 2006" (p. 160, folio not
  stated); the Narayanpur chaṛīdār (pp. 163, 191, folios not stated). Index: `Kumar, Raj, 147–151, 178–179` (p. 350,
  folio not stated).
- **Putu** — "a man of around age 50 who had assembled the group from members of his and other nearby villages. He came
  to Hinglaj for the first time in 1997 and was proud to tell me that his father had visited the shrine in 1992."
  (p. 154, folio not stated) **Shridevi** — "a middle-aged woman who traveled with a group of fifteen to twenty people
  from a village near Umerkot in Sindh" (p. 152, folio not stated), met in April 2012, walking with "her mother who was
  in her mid-60s", the group "walking for over three weeks" (p. 152, folio not stated); index `Shridevi, 140–141, 141f`
  (p. 360, folio not stated). **Giresh** — "another pilgrim from the same party… in his early 30s" (p. 153, folio not
  stated).
- **Anoop Dan** — "A Charan devotee" whose dream founded the Temra Rai substitution (p. 167, folio not stated); index
  `Dan, Anoop, 155` (p. 343, folio not stated). **Kandan Barhath** — "a pilgrim who accompanied former Indian minister
  Jaswant Singh's party and lamented the shallowness of the journey resulting from its political leanings (Barhath
  2006)" (p. 171, folio not stated). **Jaswant Singh** — "former Indian minister", no portfolio and no dates: **(not
  stated)** (p. 171, folio not stated). **Colonel Akhtar** — interviewed in Karachi in 2011, "who supervised the
  construction of the highway in the beginning of the millennium" (p. 101, folio not stated). **Ramesh Kumar Vankwani**
  — president of the Pakistan Hindu Council and "currently a member of the national assembly under the Muslim League
  government" (p. 14, folio not stated). **Sanjesh** — "a Karachi Hindu" in the recorded conversation with an unnamed
  Kali Mata pujārī (p. 304, folio not stated). **Jagdish Rathi** — "an independent writer and author of many books on
  Goddess cults and Hindu traditions in Pakistan" (p. 82, folio not stated).

### F. Mythological, legendary and hagiographical figures (deities that answer to archive rows are in `## Misfiled`)

- **Hingol, the demon** — "a local tyrant killed by the Devi in mythological times", "a part of the local tradition",
  later displaced in the priest's telling by **Mahishasura**, "the demon Mahishasura, a figure of the famous Devī
  Māhātmaya" (pp. 46–47, folio not stated). **Note for the coordinator: this is a mythological demon, not the Hingol
  place-name; do not conflate them.**
- **Sati, Daksha, Shiva and Vishnu** — the dismemberment myth as narrated at p. 55 (folio not stated). **Shiva and
  Vishnu answer to archive rows; see `## Misfiled`.**
- **Koṭṭarī, Koṭṭavī, Koṭṭarīśā and Bhīmalocana** — the Pīṭhanirṇaya's names for the Goddess resident at Hinglaj and for
  the associated form of Shiva (p. 55, folio not stated). **Carcikā** — "a girl named Carcikā is born out of Shiva's
  sweat" during Shiva's "fights against the demon Andhaka" in the Vāmana Purāṇa (p. 53, folio not stated).
- **Parashurama** — "the sixth avatar of Vishnu", who "swore to rid the earth of all Kshatriyas" (p. 58, folio not
  stated) and "wandered the earth and fought against unrighteous kings and warriors" (p. 65, folio not stated); the
  antagonist of the Brahmakshatriya origin story (pp. 61–62, 66, 81, folios not stated).
- **Ratnasena** — "The mighty king Ratnasena, the ruler of Sindh", who flees into the jungle with his five pregnant
  wives and is killed by one of Parashurama's arrows while hunting a pig (pp. 61–62, folio not stated). **A figure of a
  paraphrased myth, not a historical ruler the book vouches for.**
- **Dadhichi** — "the sage Dadhichi, a Brahmin of the Saraswat lineage", whose ashram shelters Ratnasena and later the
  orphaned princes, who "lies to Parashurama" and gives each prince "a yajñopavīta [Skt. sacred thread worn by
  Brahmins]" (p. 62, folio not stated); his "lineage… sprang from the Sarasvati River" (p. 64, folio not stated).
- **The five princes — Jayasena, Binduman, Vishala, Chandrashala, and Bharata** (p. 62, folio not stated); Jayasena is
  cursed to be known with his ancestors "as Brahmakshatriyas" (p. 64, folio not stated) and is stung by **Indra**, "the
  king of all gods", disguised as a bumblebee (p. 63, folio not stated). **Viduratha and Shrutasena**, whose "arrows
  begin turning into grasslike sticks" (p. 65, folio not stated). **Vishvakarman**, "the architect of the universe"
  (p. 65, folio not stated). **The Goddess Sarasvati**, who sends 1,296 Brahmins (p. 76, folio not stated).
- **The Goddess Ashapura** — worshipped at "Narayanpur" on the Hinglaj route (p. 64, folio not stated); Viduratha and
  Shrutasena "worship the Goddess Ashapura for twelve years until she finally appears" (p. 65, folio not stated); the
  Kapris "are followers of the Goddess Ashapura" (p. 75, folio not stated); Mekan "went directly to the temple of
  Ashapura and asked the pujārī there to give him his blessing" before setting out (p. 77, folio not stated). **No
  location is given for that temple: (not stated).** An endnote adds a Kutch foundation legend: "it was the Goddess
  Hinglaj who helped Jam Bambhaniya from Sindh to victory in Ghumil (Gujarat) in the fourteenth century, a place where
  his father Unnar had been defeated years before. Hinglaj advised Bambhaniya to build a temple for her in Kutch since
  she had fulfilled the wish of Bambhanaiya's father, Unnar. Hence, Bambhaniya built a temple for the Goddess in her
  form as Ashapura (Joshi 2010, 8)." (p. 264, folio not stated, note 23) **The Kutch temple is not named or located
  beyond "in Kutch": (not stated). Note the two spellings of the same man in one sentence, `Bambhaniya` and
  `Bambhanaiya`.** Index: `Bambhaniya, Jam, 208n23` (p. 340, folio not stated).
- **Avar Mata (Āvaṛ Mātā)** — "worshipped as a full incarnation of Hinglaj Mata in many parts of Rajasthan and Gujarat
  (Samaur 1999, 508)"; "born in the year 888 CE in a village called Calkun in Barmer district, India"; of "the Mahdu
  Charan community (Lakhavat 2011, 35)"; father "Mamad"; six sisters (p. 72, folio not stated). Her story runs against
  "Umar Sumra who had recently converted to Islam and was now known by the name of 'Hamir'"; she kills "Bakha, the
  buffalo demon", begins "a traga ritual", allies with "the Samma and Bhati Rajput kings" and kills Hamir (p. 72, folio
  not stated). Separately she "killed fifty-two of these Hun-demons ('hūṃ rākṣas') and so saved the people of Sindh
  (Samaur 1999, 509)" (pp. 72–73, folios not stated). Index: `Avar Mata, 64–65`, `Mamad, 64, 65`, `Hamir, 64–65`
  (pp. 340, 352, 346, folio not stated).
- **Karni Mata (Karṇī Mātā)** — "a partial incarnation of the ādiśakti", who "married and even had children with Deval,
  who is considered to be an incarnation of Shiva", "a disciple of Avar", who "lived at the end of the fifteenth
  century" (p. 73, folio not stated). Patron of Rao Shekha, of Ridmal (whose son Jodha "later conquered Jodhpur") and of
  "Bika Rathore, Jodha's younger son", for whom she "provided him with five hundred oxen for his conquest and at a later
  date miraculously drew the bows of the army of Bikaner" (p. 73, folio not stated).
- **Mekan Dada (1666–1729)** — "one of the community's last reformers, considered by my interviewees to be an
  incarnation of Lakshmana, Rama's younger brother"; "Mekan himself went to Hinglaj around the end of the seventeenth
  century"; he "spent the remainder of his life wandering the Rann of Kutch accompanied only by a donkey (named Laliyo)
  and a dog (named Motiyo)"; "famous for preaching against untouchability and promoting social equality" (p. 75, folio
  not stated). Born "into a Bhati Rajput family", father "Hardhol" (p. 77, folio not stated); called "Dādā Marvān" by
  the Charan author Depavat (p. 75, folio not stated); "a widely acknowledged Kapri reformer from the seventeenth and
  eighteenth centuries" (p. 78, folio not stated). Index: `Mekan Dada, 67n36, 68–71` (p. 352, folio not stated). **His
  samādhi is at Dhrang, Kutch — India, so no row.**
- **Lalu Jasraj Kapri** — "Rama's aguā (Hin. guide) who helped the king of Ayodhya and his entourage on their way
  through the desert (Joshi 2010, 21)" (p. 74, folio not stated); "the founder of the Kapris" (p. 78, folio not stated);
  "the pious Lalu Jasraj, a hermit who resides nearby at some hot springs", called by Rama "to be their chaṛīdār [Hin.
  stick bearer, leader] for the yātrā" (p. 121, folio not stated); "the chaṛīdār (Hin. stickbearer) of Rama and his
  entourage", associated with Liari (Hart 1839, 80) (p. 209, folio not stated). "Today, the place where Rama gave his
  clothing to Lalu Jasraj is said to be in Liari in Balochistan. This story, however, is mainly told by the Kapri
  community, and I have only spoken with a few other people in Sindh who actually knew of this account." (p. 78, folio
  not stated) **What is shown at Liari is his fireplace, not a grave: "the fireplace of Lalu Jasraj… is found" (p. 181,
  folio not stated). No dates and no tomb: (not stated).** Index: `Jasraj, Lalu, 67, 71, 168, 196`, `Kapri, Lalu
  Jasraj, 69`, `Lalu Jasraj, 111` (pp. 349–351, folio not stated) — **three headwords for one man.**
- **The Aghori Baba, also called Nani's Pir** — the ritual authority at the River Aghor in both travelogues. "The
  ascetic originally lived at the Nagnath akhāṛā in Karachi, the place from which the group started their journey, but
  he had eventually decided to leave everything behind to serve the Goddess at the shores of the Aghor River." (p. 131,
  folio not stated) He takes the `gurudān`, overrules the aguā's scruple, imposes silence about the tunnel and shows the
  sun and moon carvings (pp. 131–132, folios not stated). In Shastri: "an ascetic called Aghori Baba who had been the
  head of the Nagnath akhāṛā in Karachi many years before (Shastri 1977, 51). As a young man, he had accompanied a group
  of pilgrims to Hinglaj, but when they prepared for their return journey, he had decided to stay in the valley to serve
  the Goddess." (p. 142, folio not stated) His line to the narrator: "tū beṭā kal kā baccā hai"—"you're a greenhorn"
  (p. 134, folio not stated), with the fuller Hindi in note 26, "Avaśya sakeṃge. Sīdhe sab ko māṁ ke sthān par le jā. Tū
  beṭā kal kā baccā hai, tū vidhān-qānūn kyā sam'jhegā?" (Abadhut 1964, 204) (p. 148, folio not stated), and his
  warning, "sāvadhān beṭā—kabhī māṁ kā yah ādeś mat bhūlo" (Abadhut 1964, 210) (p. 134, folio not stated). **No personal
  name, no dates, no samādhi or tomb of his own: (not stated). He is named by an epithet only, and "Nani's Pir" attaches
  him to the Bibi Nani identification.** Index: `Aghori Baba 121–122`[text-layer?] [`Aghori Baba, 121–122`]`,
  125–126, 133–134, 136, 161, 203` (p. 339, folio not stated).
- **Charani Ai Shri Sonal Mataji** — "the most recent well-known incarnation the community speaks of", "a social
  reformer who died in 1975. She supported the education of young women in her community, preached against alcohol
  abuse, and spread ideas that were inspired by the politics of Gandhi (Basu 2004, 199–207)." (pp. 70–71, folios not
  stated) **Shayam Mata**[text-layer?] [Shyam Mata?] — "One of the bigger incarnations is Shayam Mata, whose followers I
  met in Barmer in 2011." (p. 100, folio not stated) **No shrine, dates or lineage for either: (not stated).**
  **Khodiyar** — named with Kali as a form in which the Goddess mostly appears to the Devipujaks (p. 310, folio not
  stated). **Melari**, "the dirty Goddess", referred to Pocock (1973, 62–64) (p. 315, folio not stated, note 3).
  **Sheravali Mata** — "literally 'the Mother with the lion'", the mūrti inside the Narayanpur rath (p. 163, folio not
  stated). **Bibi Nani / Nana / Nanea / Anaiti / Anaea / Aneitis / Tanais** — the Near Eastern identifications of
  Hinglaj (pp. 24, 90, 101–102, folios not stated); index `Bibi Nani, 13, 83–86`, `Nani, 222`, `Nana, 84, 90n55`, `Near
  Eastern Goddesses, 85–86` (pp. 341, 346, 354, folio not stated). **Ganesha** — the Lasi Lohana claim for the paṛāv
  outside the valley, now carrying an HSM statue and temple (pp. 279–280, folios not stated); **no archive row has
  Ganesha as its principal figure.** **Bhairava** — "There is a Bhairava [Shiva] temple in my village" (p. 174, folio
  not stated) and one of the two stones installed at Mandapi (p. 176, folio not stated). **Eve** — "the place where Eve
  fell from paradise and later became known as Hinglaj (Abro 1991, 66)" (p. 255, folio not stated). **Hazrat Ali** —
  "the son-in-law of the Prophet Mohammad" who "split a rock in two with his sword as he was on his way into the
  valley" (Hart 1839, 94) (pp. 89–90, folio not stated); **he is not the principal figure of any archive row — Data
  Darbar's is Hazrat Ali ibn Usman al-Hujwiri, a different person — so no id.** **Mohammad Jaunpuri** — the Zikris'
  fifteenth-century founder (p. 90, folio not stated), index `Jaunpuri, Mohammad, 83` (p. 349, folio not stated); **no
  tomb, no place, no dates: (not stated).** **Murśid Mulaṇ Śāh (1883–1962)** — named only inside a works-cited title
  (p. 328, folio not stated); **no archive row.**
- **Rāmāyaṇa cast as the book summarises it** — Dasharatha, Kaikeyi (who "demands that the king first fulfill two
  promises he had made to her in the battlefield"), Bharata, Janaka, Sugriva ("the king of the monkeys"), and Ravana,
  "the demon Ravana", who kidnaps Sita and keeps her "captive... on the island of Lanka" (p. 119, folio not stated) and
  whose killing makes Rama guilty of "brahm hatyā" (Urd. slaying of a Brahmin) (p. 120, folio not stated); Rama is
  exiled "for fourteen years" (pp. 119–120, folios not stated). **Sita** — "the adopted daughter of King Janaka", found
  "in a furrow as a child (in Skt. 'sītā' means 'furrow')" (p. 119, folio not stated); on the Hinglaj route she "places
  her palm down on the soil and thereby makes five wells appear" (p. 121, folio not stated). **Lakshmana** — at the
  Ganges festival he is ordered "to see to everybody's well-being and to make sure that no one leaves the festival
  without having eaten" (p. 120, folio not stated); on the route he "shoots an arrow into the mountain range but only
  succeeds in detaching one of the hills" (p. 121, folio not stated). **Kumbhodar / Kumbodhar** — "one sage who goes by
  the name of Kumbhodar and who refuses to eat the prepared food"; he tells Rama "he is not willing to eat from the
  hands of a Brahmicide (i.e., one who has murdered a Brahmin)" and advises "a pilgrimage to Hinglaj Mata, the only
  place that can purify him" (p. 120, folio not stated). **Two spellings on one page; both recorded.** **Rama, Hanuman
  and Valmiki answer to archive rows; see `## Misfiled`.**
- **Characters in the two travelogues, treated by the book as literature, not as persons** — **Kunti and Thirumal**,
  whose "unfortunate love story" frames Abadhut's book and who "each succumb to their desperate situation, eventually
  finding separate tragic deaths in the desert" (p. 133, folio not stated); Thirumal is "a simple musician and day
  laborer" who "escapes into madness" and at Chandrakup "jumps into the volcano in an act of self-sacrifice" (p. 133,
  folio not stated). **Bhairavi**, "an elderly Bengali woman... who cannot walk by herself" and later "the rich Bengali
  woman", who breaks the oath (pp. 127–128, 134, folios not stated). **Gulmohammad and his son, Dilmohammad**, "two
  Balochi camel drivers" (p. 127, folio not stated). **Rupalal and Sukhalal**, the `aguās`, "two young boys around the
  ages of twelve and eighteen" (p. 127, folio not stated). **Jayashankar**, a Brahmin "a staunch believer in purity",
  and **Maniram**, one of his students (pp. 128–129, folios not stated). **Popatlal** (pp. 130, 134, folios not
  stated). **Jayaben and Jivraj**, "a Gujarati couple … who are undertaking the pilgrimage in hopes of having an
  offspring with the blessing of Hinglaj"; "The Gujarati woman is the only female pilgrim in the party (Shastri 1977,
  9)" (p. 138, folio not stated). **A devotee from Barmer** whose offering is refused until he confesses (p. 141, folio
  not stated). **An unnamed Gosain** who "was so devastated by his own sins that he leapt into the crater (Hart 1839,
  90)" and took "four attempts to finally sink into the volcano" (p. 133, folio not stated). **An unnamed Nepali
  pilgrim** said to have "committed suicide by jumping into the mud volcano (Ansari 2001, 296)" (p. 133, folio not
  stated). **An unnamed Jain businessman**, an unnamed **old priest (prauṛh paṃḍā)**, an unnamed **well-keeper** and an
  unnamed **local** who houses the group (pp. 127–130, folios not stated).
- **Named pilgrims and patrons of the akhāṛā records and the legends** — Raja Bij Singh, Raja Todarmal, Raja Bharimal,
  Raja Madhu Singh, Raja Jagat Singh, Hamir Gohil (Gehlote), Seth Hotchand, the Shet Kowaljee (pp. 112–114, folio not
  stated); **Thaomal**, "the rich Sindhi merchant who initiated the pilgrimage in which Hart took part" (p. 220, folio
  not stated); **Haji Abdula Nabi**, "the traveler" quoted by Briggs (p. 213, folio not stated). Index: `Bharimal, Raja,
  102`, `Brahmakhatri, Raja Todarmal, 102`, `Gohil, Hamir, 103`, `Nabi, Haji Abdula, 201` (pp. 341, 346, 353, folio not
  stated). **A terminological note the book enters on one of the titles**: "Shet (Shett) is likely the title of a
  businessman from the West Coast of India. The title, however, could also be a wrong spelling of the 'Seth,' which
  refers to a merchant cast in north India. In Sindh today, 'Seth' simply implies an employer or a boss." (p. 147, folio
  not stated) **`merchant cast` is printed thus.**
- **Fictional figures from the Urdu pulp story `Duśmanī`, expressly not historical** — Dr. Shankar, "the megalomaniac,
  atheist supervillain" and "quintessential image of the untrustworthy and simply evil Hindu"; "a tantric, Ram Lal Das"
  (given as "Ram Das'" once); "Nirmala, his 'lady assistant'"; a helper "Mohan"; and "Baba Abdulla, a pious Muslim man"
  with "a gang of ghosts with him—all from pious descent" (pp. 21–22, folios not stated). **Characters in a May 2014
  magazine story, not saints.**

### G. Authors, travellers and scholars the book uses as sources (none is an archive row's principal figure)

- **Colonial-era observers of the shrine and the route.** **Captain Hart** — "probably the earliest European visitor to
  the shrine who left an extensive account" (p. 233, folio not stated); he travelled "with a large group of Hindu
  devotees and a chaṛīdār, the pilgrimage leader who instructed the pilgrims extensively along the way" (p. 233, folio
  not stated), on a "twenty-one-day journey" (p. 233, folio not stated), cited as Hart 1839 and 1840. **His first name,
  rank beyond "Captain" and the year of the journey itself are (not stated).** **Sir Frederic John Goldsmid** —
  "Another British traveler who personally visited Hinglaj in 1861 … whose travels along the Makran coast are well
  documented in his article from 1863" (p. 234, folio not stated), accompanied by a Sindhi "Moonshee" (from Sin.
  "munśī" a scribe, a language teacher) (p. 234, folio not stated) — **the Moonshee is not named: (not stated)**.
  **Colonel Wilford** — "another British military officer, who had probably worked with pilgrims traveling to Hinglaj as
  early as 1811" (p. 234, folio not stated), who "studied the pilgrimage route to Hinglaj around the year 1810" (p. 113,
  folio not stated); "His account of the valley is fairly fragmentary, leading one to assume that the colonel himself
  never visited Hinglaj." (p. 234, folio not stated) **Two dates for the same work, 1810 and 1811, are printed and
  neither is preferred.** **Marc Aurel Stein** — "the Hungarian-British archeologist mentioned above", who visited in
  1943 and "provides the oldest photograph of the image available to me" (p. 234, folio not stated). **Charles Masson
  (1800–1853)** — "whose actual name was James Lewis", who "worked as a soldier for the East India Company" (p. 91,
  folio not stated). **Thomas Holdich (1843–1929)** — "an English geographer who worked for the Survey of India and
  traveled widely in the border areas of the Empire" (p. 92, folio not stated). **Henry Pottinger** — "worked as a spy
  for the British Crown" (p. 113, folio not stated). **Richard Burton**, **William Crooke** (whose ERE article "Hinglāj"
  is dated 1913, p. 101, folio not stated), **Alexander Cunningham**, **H. H. Risley** — "the commissioner in charge of
  the 1901 census", "a staunch believer in anthropometrics" (p. 60, folio not stated), **K. C. S. Varma** — author of
  the 1904 *Kshatriyas and Would-be Kshatriyas* (p. 60, folio not stated), **M. N. Billimoria**, **McConaghey et al.**
  (the 1907 "Las Bela" Baluchistan Gazetteer). Index runs at pp. 351–363, folio not stated.
- **The vernacular authors of the Hinglaj corpus.** **Abadhut = Dulalchandra Mukhopadhyay (1910–1978)**, author of the
  Bengali `Marutīrtha Hiṃlāj`, who "claim[s] to have gone to Hinglaj … in 1946" (p. 145, folio not stated) — **no first
  name is given in the body chunks: (not stated)**; the Hindi translator **Nihararanjan Chaudhari**; the actor **Uttam
  Kumar (1926–1980)** of the 1959 film (pp. 132, 137, 144–145, 475–480 range, folios not stated). **Devadatta Shastri**
  — author of `Āgneya Tīrtha Hiṅglāj` (The Fiery Pilgrimage to Hinglaj), "published in 1977" (p. 136, folio not stated),
  who "claims to have gone on the pilgrimage himself in 1940 (Shastri 1977, 6)" (p. 137, folio not stated); **no dates
  of birth or death, no place of origin: (not stated)**. **Ashok Jethamalani** — the 1994 author of the route
  description, given his forename once (p. 204, folio not stated), who "describes the route from the perspective of a
  local (Karachi) Khatri devotee of Hinglaj" (p. 203, folio not stated). **Bhamvarlal Jareca** — interviewed "From a
  personal conversation in March 2008" (p. 99, folio not stated); his work is "largely informing this chapter" (p. 98,
  folio not stated). **Omkar Lakhavat** — author of `Hiṃg'lāj Śaktipīṭh` (Lakhavat 2011, 23), "the only person I met who
  claimed to have a copy of this version of the Tantracūḍāmaṇi" (p. 98, folio not stated). **Dr. Nabi Bux Khan Baloch**
  — "undertook the journey to Hinglaj in 1961. His account appears in the Sindhi journal Mehrān, which was published in
