## Citable passages

*Fifteen, chosen from several hundred candidates for direct use in a shrine entry: each is under 25 words, each is page
referenced in this book's only possible form, and none is drawn from an index or an endnote locator. Two are damaged
and are given in their printed form with the `[text-layer?]` flag kept on the word and the reading in brackets after
it. The book is written in English; the Hindi, Urdu, Sindhi and Gujarati it quotes appears in roman transliteration
only, so there is no original script to reproduce for any of these.*

- "For centuries, the shrine of Hinglaj was famous for being extremely difficult to reach." (p. 29, folio not stated) —
  the one-line statement of the site's pre-2001 condition.
- "This situation changed rapidly after 1986 when a temple committee, the Hinglāj Śevā Manḍalī (HSM), took over the
  organization of the pilgrimage" (p. 30, folio not stated) — the founding sentence for the shrine's custodianship, and
  the only place the committee's name is printed with full diacritics.
- "after 2001, the site quickly developed into a stage for one of the biggest annual Hindu festivals in Pakistan"
  (p. 30, folio not stated) — the road's effect in one clause.
- "from merely a few thousand a year to over forty thousand on a single weekend in the spring." (p. 261, folio not
  stated) — the book's compact attendance figure; the forty-thousand count rests on the committee's own ticket records
  (p. 84, folio not stated).
- "Hinglaj is the oldest and thus the greatest of all śākta pīṭhas (Skt. seats of power) in the world" (p. 51, folio not
  stated) — a devotional truth-claim the author reports, not a finding he endorses.
- "it is one of the few places in Pakistan that offers a relatively secure public space for a massive Hindu festival to
  occur" (p. 50, folio not stated) — the book's explanation of the shrine's growth as a minority-security effect.
- "Here Hindoo and Muhammadan pilgrims alike resort, each claiming the divine protection of the presiding goddess or of
  the departed saint" (Holdich 1904, 45) (p. 92, folio not stated) — the colonial sentence on which the shared-worship
  claim rests; quote it only with the author's finding that this literature is a citation loop (pp. 92–94, folios not
  stated).
- "Zikris today see Hinglaj as a Balochi 'pīr' (Urd. saint) who is a part of their distinct culture" (p. 90, folio not
  stated) — the living Muslim claim on the site.
- "Because the Zikris consider Hinglaj to be the grave of one of Prophet Mohammad's family members" (p. 325, folio not
  stated) — the same claim in a different and irreconcilable form, from the Conclusion.
- "an oblong stone within a railed space, in size and shape like a small Mahomedan tombstone" (p. 233, folio not stated)
  — Hart 1839, 96, on the central image; the description of the sanctum that the rebuilding backgrounded.
- "if you look at the roof of this place, you will realize that it has naturally generated. No one constructed it."
  (p. 200, folio not stated) — Gopal Maharaj to *Dawn*; the svayaṃbhū claim in the priest's own words.
- "In former times the womb [of the Goddess] was into the mountain, now it has become fortified with stone" (p. 249,
  folio not stated) — Jajot 2011, 56, with callout 28; the tunnel before and after the works.
- "the epithet 'Hinglaji,' equivalent to the title 'Hajji,' is bestowed upon people who have successfully accomplished
  the journey" (p. 155, folio not stated) — the status the pilgrimage confers, and the book's own Hajj analogy.
- "Drinking alcohol is prohibited in the premises of `Mata Mandir offenders`[text-layer?] [Mata Mandir. Offenders]
  shall be handed over to the police." (p. 284, folio not stated) — the fourth of the nine rules posted in English and
  Urdu inside the valley in 2011; **the damage is a lost full stop that runs two sentences together, and the printed
  form is kept.**
- "in response to the question of why so many people had gathered in Hinglaj/Nani during `baqra cīd`[text-layer?]
  [baqra ʿīd]" (p. 294, folio not stated) — the only statement in the book that the shrine is busy outside the spring
  melā, and it is dated by a Muslim festival; **`cīd` for `ʿīd` is this book's dominant damage class, the ayn rendered
  as a Latin `c`.**

## Doubts and review points

### 1. No folio is recoverable for this book, anywhere — the decisive measured fact

**This book prints NO recoverable folio.** Measured across all nineteen chunks of the Pass 1 notes:
**0 folio numbers and 3,073 occurrences of the string "folio not stated".** Every page reference in
this document is therefore `(p. N, folio not stated)`, and every page number is a **PDF page index**,
never a printed page number. **No folio has been invented and none has been reconstructed.** If a
later worker or a coverage audit expects a printed-folio-to-PDF offset for this book, there is none
to supply: **(not stated)**. The four chunks that touch the index (pp. 339–364) state the position
plainly — "The index locators are printed page numbers, not PDF page indices, and this chunk gives no
offset: (not stated)" (chunk 019) — so **the one place in the book where printed page numbers do
appear is the index, and those numbers are locators inside somebody else's numbering, not folios of
the pages they are printed on.** The calibration document's `PDF − 21` rule is local to Kugle and has
not been used here.

### 2. The archive row now has an id, and the `(no id — …)` head is obsolete

- **The row `Shaktipeeth Shri Hinglaj Mata Mandir` has the id `shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir`,
  confirmed in `shrine_index.tsv` and filled on 21 September 2026 — one day after these notes were
  written.** All nineteen of this book's Pass 1 archive bullets for the shrine were headed
  `**(no id — Shaktipeeth Shri Hinglaj Mata Mandir)**` and every chunk's Doubts section repeated
  that the material was "unmapped pending Rauf's ruling". **That is now resolved. All nineteen
  bullets' material has been carried into the `### shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir` subsection
  above, and the `(no id — …)` form does not survive into this document.** Nothing else in the Pass 1
  notes changes as a result: no fact was withheld because of the missing id, only the mapping.
- **That row's `category` cell is empty in the archive and has been deliberately left alone**
  (CLAUDE.md RULE 2). The book would supply "Hindu Temple" on its Library of Congress heading
  ("Hinglaj Devi (Temple : Hinglaj, Pakistan)", p. 4, folio not stated) and calls it a Hindu temple
  throughout, **but the archive's category vocabulary is the archive's and no value has been written
  into that cell here.** A human should fill it.
- **The same row's `principal_figure`, `figure_died` and `silsila` cells are also empty.** The book
  supplies a candidate figure — "the Goddess Hinglaj" / "Hinglaj Devi" / "Hinglaj Mata" (pp. 29–30,
  64, 68, 70, folios not stated) — and supplies no death year and no silsila, which is correct for a
  Hindu temple. **Whether "Hinglaj Devi" should be written into `principal_figure` is a human
  decision and is not made here.** Compare the archive's treatment of the neighbouring
  `chandragup-baba-chandragup`, whose figure cell reads "Shiva (associated)", i.e. the archive has
  already faced this question once in Hingol National Park.
- **That row's `location_short` contains an Arabic comma (U+060C):** "Hingol Balochistan National
  Park, Road، Asha Pura, Pakistan". **It has been left exactly as the file spells it and was not
  normalised.** Anything that string-matches on an ASCII comma will mis-split it. The book corroborates
  only the park: "the Hingol National Park, in which the shrine is located" (p. 295, folio not
  stated); **it nowhere names a road and nowhere names "Asha Pura"**: **(not stated)**.
- **"Asha Pura" in that address must not be merged with the book's Goddess Ashapura.** The book uses
  Ashapura in at least five distinct senses, none of them the archive's address string: the Goddess
  worshipped at Narayanpur on the route (p. 64, folio not stated); the Goddess of the Kapris
  (p. 75, folio not stated); "the temple of Ashapura" Mekan visits before departure, whose location
  the book never gives (p. 77, folio not stated); the third paṛāv of the Jethamalani route and a
  water source in the Hinglaj valley (pp. 188, 203, 220, folios not stated); and one of three
  historical names for the Mata Bhawani temple at Makli near Thatta, "Ashapura (Briggs 1938)"
  (p. 205, folio not stated). Note 23 adds a sixth: the Goddess Hinglaj advising Jam Bambhaniya to
  build her a temple in Kutch "in her form as Ashapura (Joshi 2010, 8)", citing Joshi's *Jay Āśāpurā*
  (p. 264, folio not stated). **A human should check the archive row's address before any Ashapura
  material is attached to it; the coincidence of spelling is not treated as an identification here.**

### 3. Damage: the measured inventory, and the ayn-as-`c` class

**Measured across this book's nineteen chunks: 241 `[text-layer?]` flags, 19 `[OCR?]`, 0
`[illegible]`.** The route produces a text layer in generally good condition — **no digit anywhere in
the book is reported corrupt, and no proper name is destroyed** — and the heavy Indic and Perso-Arabic
transliteration diacritics survive almost everywhere (`śākta pīṭha`, `svayaṃbhū`, `brahmarandhra`,
`Tantracūḍāmaṇi`, `Kr̥ṣṇajanmakhaṇḍa`, `Hiṅgulāyāṃ`, `dr̥ṣṭvā`, `piṇḍī`, `kul'devī`, `chaṛīdār`,
`bhanḍārā`, `muḥarram`, `ẕikr`, `naz̤ariyah`, `xālis`, `mātā xud bali letī` and hundreds more are
clean). **Every inline flag in this document sits on the word it was attached to in Pass 1; nothing
has been silently repaired.**

**(1) The dominant class: the superscript ʿayn rendered as a Latin lowercase `c`.** The class is
**systematic but NOT total-and-mechanical** — a real Latin `c` exists throughout the book's
transliterations (`cīz`, `caṛhānā`, `caukīdār`, `caitra`, `paṃcāyat`, `cilam`, `choṭī māī`,
`Caṇḍīmaṅgala`, `Cāraṇ-Baṃdhī`, `gaur'viyā cāraṇ`, `caṛh'te`, `cāh'tī`, `cal'nā`, `Pāṁc`, `socā`,
`macch` are all genuine) — so **standing convention (b) applies in full: the inline flag stays on
every affected token AND the class is inventoried once, here.** The occurrences, by page:
`curs`[text-layer?] [ʿurs] (pp. 108, 160); `dacvat`[text-layer?] [daʿvat] (p. 100);
`mcāmulī`[text-layer?] [maʿāmulī], `tahcqīdan`[text-layer?] [taḥqīqan or taḥqīdan],
`yacnī`[text-layer?] [yaʿnī], `śacūrjaṇ`[text-layer?] [śaʿūrjaṇ] (p. 101); `cīd`[text-layer?] [ʿīd]
(pp. 159, 253, 291, 295); `cAli`[text-layer?] [ʿAli] (p. 235); `nastaclīq`[text-layer?] [nastaʿlīq]
(p. 270); `baqra cīd`[text-layer?] [baqra ʿīd] (pp. 272, 293, 294, 303, 306, 314, 317);
`cāśūrah`[text-layer?] [ʿāśūrah] (pp. 272, 340); `cīds`[text-layer?] [ʿīds] (p. 277); the heading
"6.9.1 Baqracīd at Hinglaj" (p. 293); `cīd ul fit̤r`[text-layer?] [ʿīd ul fiṭr] (p. 317, note 22);
`tacṣṣub`[text-layer?] [taʿṣṣub] (p. 316, note 8); `Abdul Acla Maududi`[text-layer?] [Abul Aʿlā
Maududi] (pp. 20, 352); and, uncertain, `Macar's bone`[text-layer?] (p. 352) — **no reading is
supplied for the last, because the headword's referent is nowhere stated.** **Two independent proofs
of the reading exist in the index's own alphabetisation:** `cāśūrah` is filed between "Astola Island"
and "aśvamedha", i.e. under A, which only works if the initial is ʿayn; and `baqra cīd` is filed
between "Bania community" and "barādarī" (p. 340, folio not stated). **`Maududi, Abdul Acla` is filed
under M-a-u, which fixes `Acla` as `Aʿlā`** (p. 352, folio not stated). **The one thing a human must
still settle: if the plain `c` is in fact the author's own transliteration convention for ʿayn rather
than an extraction fault, then these are not damage at all and every flag in the class should come
off.** Pass 1 read them as extraction faults because the surrounding non-ʿayn transliterations on the
same pages are undamaged; **one printed page settles it for the whole book.** Note also that the
class is **unevenly distributed** — it is absent from pp. 117–132, pp. 170–187, pp. 188–205 and
pp. 206–226, all of which are Sanskritic and Indic in vocabulary, and dense in pp. 269–305 and in the
index, which are full of Arabic-derived festival names. **A negative finding in one chunk does not
extend to the book**; chunk 010 reported the class absent and chunks 013, 014, 015, 016, 017, 018 and
019 each reversed that for their own range.
**A word-division fault sits on top of the ayn loss:** the festival is set solid as `Baqracīd` in the
heading at p. 293 and as two words `baqra cīd` in the body, **so string matching on this term will
fail in both directions.**

**(2) Dropped, doubled or transposed letters in words that carry facts — each flagged inline above,
none mended.** `Hingaj Sheva Mandai (HSM)`[text-layer?] for Hinglaj Sheva Mandali (p. 197) — **the
only place in that chunk where the organisation's name is expanded, so a human must check the printed

**(3) End-of-line hyphenation carried into the text as a real hyphen plus a line break — present in
almost every chunk, and the class that will corrupt a quotation if it is not mended.** Measured
instances include `actor-`/`networks`, `eye-`/`level`, `day-`/`to-day`, `post-`/`foundationalism`,
`anti-`/`foundationalism`, `truth-`/`claims`, `free-`/`floating`, `minority-`/`issue` (pp. 28–39);
`sixteenth-`/`century` and `quill-`/`driving` (pp. 57, 60–61); `mytho-`/`historical`,
`Westphal-`/`Hellbusch`, `re-`/`emerge`, `single-family` (pp. 100–108); `cross-`/`border`,
`tunnel-`/`pilgrimages`, `X-`/`ray` (pp. 175–184); `three-`/`dimensional`, `non-`/`religious`,
`self-`/`generated` (pp. 196–200); `"Chandra-`/`Kups."` inside a quotation (p. 214) — **there the
hyphen is printed and the line break is not**; `mytho-`/`historic`, `clear-`/`cut`,
`northern-`/`most` (pp. 256–263); `anti-`/`Ahmedi`, `caste-`/`transgressing`, `low-`/`caste`,
`non-`/`vegetarian` (pp. 269–285); `non-`/`violence` (p. 301). **Any word carried into an entry must
be rejoined or separated as sense requires.**

**(4) Superscript endnote callouts rendered as plain digits glued to the preceding word or
punctuation — systematic throughout and, in one place, dangerous.** Examples: `both authors.20`,
`"Gopi Chund Raja"21`, `the God Rama.22`, `any details.23` (pp. 121–122); `(Khatri 1973, 19).7`,
`hai].8`, `(Hart 1839, 80),9`, `(see Fig. 5.2).10`, **`residue11`**, `(Snead 1964, 548).12`,
`dormant,13`, `years.14`, `138).15`, `followed.16`, `Rama.17`, `Aghor,18`, `"more accurate."19`,
`road.20`, `specific."21`, `geography,22`, `Gujarat.23` (pp. 208–220); `Hinglaj.32`,
`(Jethamalani 1994, 9).33`, `blessing.34`, `(Briggs 1938).35`, `found.36`, `Hinglaj,"37`,
`island.38`, `broader reach.1`, `(italics added).2` (pp. 255–268); and the sixteen glued calls at
pp. 64–79. **`residue11` (p. 211) is the dangerous one: it sits three words before "a current height
of almost 150 meters", and a reader who takes the `11` for part of a measurement will produce a false
figure.** Every trailing digit in these strings is a note number, **not part of the sentence and
never a page or folio number**, and must be stripped before quoting.

**(5) Small capitals rendered as full capitals at chapter and section openings, throughout the
book.** `THERE IS A multitude` (p. 7), `WORDS FROM LANGUAGES` (p. 9), `WHAT IS PAKISTANI Hinduism?`
(p. 13), `HINGLAJ DEVI IN Balochistan` (p. 29), `PLACES FOR PUBLIC       religious practice are
scarce` (p. 319), and the figure labels `FIGURE 0.1` / `FIGURE 0.2` (pp. 11–12), all folios not
stated. **This is a design rendering, not corruption, but it means printed case is unreliable at
every opener in this book**, and the extra internal whitespace at p. 319 must be collapsed if that
line is quoted. **Do not quote `HINGLAJ DEVI IN Balochistan` as evidence of the printed
capitalisation.**

**(6) Layout artefacts with no effect on wording, recorded because they break mechanical parsing.**
Block-quotation indentation is lost at page breaks throughout (pp. 22, 39, 61, 67, 70–77, 117, 119,
171, 175–176, 215, 230–231, 246, 253, 280, 295); the section heading `1.3
Post-Foundationalism—From Identity to Identification` stands at the foot of p. 34 with its text on
p. 35, and `6.5 Education, Progress, and Denial` sits at the foot of p. 276 with its epigraph on
p. 277, **so a section's opening page is ambiguous in two places**; justified-line spacing collapses
into runs of spaces in the endnotes (p. 28); the section heading `2.3.2 Sons of the Devi: The Charan
Community` is printed **twice**, at the foot of p. 68's run and again at the head of p. 69, almost
certainly an extraction artefact or a catchword; a song line is duplicated across the p. 151/152
column break, and the whole two-column Devanagari-and-gloss layout of pp. 151–152 was interleaved by
the extractor, **so the song's true line order must be read off the printed page**; figure captions
run into the body text with no separator (Figs. 5.1–5.6), and **p. 224 carries nothing but the
captions to Figures 5.4 and 5.5**; Fig. 5.8's caption is split across pp. 231–232 and its date,
"early 1980", is **singular and has been left exactly as printed, not expanded to "the early
1980s"**; index sub-entry lists are split across pages (`Goddesses:` pp. 345–346, `Hinglaj:`
pp. 347–348) and the repeat-author em-dash entry "———. 1840." at the head of p. 331 has its author
name only on p. 330. **Every multi-page quotation and entry used above was reassembled by hand.**

**(7) Conventions that are NOT damage and must never be stripped.** The apostrophe inside
transliterated words marks a suppressed inherent vowel and is the book's own: `kul'devī`, `khic'ṛī`,
`ṭhum'rā`, `bhag'vā`, `dev'ātmās`, `śaraṇ'bhāī`, `jāg'raṇ`, `ār'tī`, `dar'bār`, `Kar'ṇījī`,
`Hiṃg'lāj`, `sam'jhegā`, `jit'nā`, `ut'nā`, `ap'nī`, `mār'te`, `an'paṛh`, `bhūt pā'lnā`. The `x` in
`xālis`, `xūn caṛhānā`, `xalīfah` and `mātā xud bali letī` is the book's transliteration of xe, **not
a substitution artefact**, and the index proves it by filing `xālis sthān, 41` under X between W and
Y (p. 364, folio not stated). The book's own `[sic]` and `(sic)` markers are the author's and must
not be stripped: "witnissing [sic]", "are [sic]", "Mata Hinghlaj [sic]" twice on the signboard
(p. 284, folio not stated), "the ulema (sic)" twice in the 1953 quotation (p. 269, folio not stated,
where the book states explicitly "italics, brackets, and sic erat scriptum in the original"), "'After
the abduction of Seeta by Rawan,' [sic]" in the Hart quotation (p. 214, folio not stated),
"Without, however, pressing, [sic] this point" (p. 68, folio not stated), "Sindhi has 52 letters,
Urdu has only 26. [sic]" (p. 87, folio not stated), "no desire of conquering other people. [sic]"
(p. 88, folio not stated), and `billionair` and "It better if they can rename it" inside note 9
(p. 316, folio not stated). The book's transliteration note warns that `[sic]` is used sparingly and
"sometimes placed at the end of a complete paragraph or quote instead of following each erroneous
word" (p. 9, folio not stated), **so an uncorrected error inside one of its summaries is exactly what
the book's own policy predicts and may not be extraction damage at all.** Angle brackets around a
headword — `<yajña>`, `<caurāsī>`, `<sītā kuāṁ>`, `<joār>` — are probably the extraction's rendering
of a differently-styled headword and **must be stripped before the phrase enters an entry**, but a
human should confirm they are not the book's own convention. Two anusvāra glyphs are in use — `ṁ` in
`gāṁjā` against `ṃ` in `saṃpradāy`, `caṃdrakūp`, `siṃdūr`, `Hiṃgulā` — and **may be the book's own
distinction**; four nasal transliterations appear across one chunk (`ṃ`, `ṁ`, `ṉ`, bare `n`), which
is probably the book reproducing several source conventions. **A single Hinglaj spelling must not be
imposed on quoted material.**

**(8) The `[OCR?]` and `[illegible]` counts.** **19 `[OCR?]` and 0 `[illegible]` are recorded for
this book.** No `[illegible]` flag exists anywhere in it, which is why no `[illegible: N lines]`
appears in this document. **A human should check whether the 19 `[OCR?]` occurrences are flags on
words or occurrences of the string inside the chunks' own negative statements**, since several chunks
declare the class absent in their range; the Pass 1 extracts carry no `[OCR?]`-flagged token into the
archive, legends or doubts material this part consolidates, so none has been carried here.

### 4. The hedge conversion: what was converted, and what was not

**Thirty ruling-(a) and many-row-person hedges have been converted into real mappings above and the
hedge language has been deleted.** They were phrased in the Pass 1 notes as "flagged reversibly as
convention (a) requires", "if the toponym ruling reverses, the row it would take is …", "a reversal
of convention (a) would still leave this ambiguous", "under the interim convention no id is assigned
on a bare deity name", "escalated to Rauf by this run", and "parked under Other". **The Pass 1
workers named the rows in every case; this document applies rulings that have since been made and
decides nothing new.**

- **Eighteen place-level conversions**, under ruling (a) of 21 September 2026: Karachi (11 rows),
  Lahore (35), Peshawar (10), Multan (8), Islamabad (4), Sukkur (5), Rohri (2), Shikarpur (3),
  Tharparkar (3), Umerkot (2), Quetta (2), Hyderabad (1), Larkana (1), Hala (1), Sehwan Sharif (2),
  Lahoot/Khuzdar (1), Las Bela and Hingol National Park (2), and Chandrakup as a bare place-name (1).
  **The Karachi, Lahore, Peshawar, Multan and Islamabad counts match the ruled counts exactly.**
- **Twelve person-level conversions**, under the many-row rule of 22 September 2026 and the
  single-row rule of 18 September 2026: Guru Nanak (18 rows), Shiva (8), Guru Gorakhnath (2), Valmiki
  (3), Kali (3), Krishna (2), Rama (1), Durga (1), Hanuman (1), Narasimha (1), Lava (1), Varuna (1).
  **The Nanak, Shiva, Gorakhnath and Krishna counts match the ruled counts exactly.** The whole of the
  "deity case" that eight chunks escalated to Rauf is settled by these two rulings and is no longer
  open: a deity who is an archive row's principal figure takes that row's id, and a deity who heads
  many rows takes all of them.
- **What was NOT converted, and why.**
  - **`Noorani`** — the book prints the bare name once, in Raj Kumar's quoted speech, "Again we are
    climbing the mountains for Noorani" (p. 160, folio not stated), and indexes two headwords,
    `Noorani, 148` and `Noorani, Shah, 98` (p. 354, folio not stated). **Two archive rows answer to
    it and they are two different saints, not one saint heading two rows**:
    `shah-noorani-shrine-syed-bilawal-shah-noorani` (Syed Bilawal Shah Noorani, Lahoot Lamakan valley,
    Khuzdar District) and `shrine-of-pir-chhatal-shah-noorani` (Pir Chhatal Shah Noorani, Jhal Magsi
    District). **Neither the many-row rule nor the single-row rule reaches an ambiguity between two
    persons**, so no id is taken on the bare name. Evidence a human may weigh, offered as locator
    facts and not as a ruling: index locator 98 is also carried by `Lahoot, 89, 98`, `Khuzdar, 98` and
    `Lāhūt lā Makān (Nazamani), 98` (pp. 350, 351, folios not stated), and Lahoot and Khuzdar are the
    first row's location. **`shah-noorani-shrine-syed-bilawal-shah-noorani` has its own subsection
    above on separate, named evidence — "the abode of the saint Shah Noorani in the Khuzdar district
    in Balochistan" (p. 108, folio not stated) — and that mapping does not depend on this bare name.**
  - **`Shah Inayatullah (1655–1718)`** (p. 240, folio not stated) — the same structure. The
    candidates are `dargah-roza-sufi-shah-inayat-shaheed` (Sufi Shah Inayat Shaheed, Jhok Sharif,
    Sindh, `figure_died` 1718) and `shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri` (Shah Inayat Qadiri, Mozang Chungi,
    Lahore, `figure_died` c. 1728). **The book's printed death year, 1718, matches the first row
    exactly and not the second, and the book calls him a *Sindhi* poet, which also points to Jhok
    rather than Lahore — but the printed name "Shah Inayatullah" matches neither row's spelling, the
    book names no shrine, place or order for him, and these are two different men.** **No id
    assigned; a human should rule.** If the ruling goes to the first row, this material moves into the
    archive section under `dargah-roza-sufi-shah-inayat-shaheed`. Note that
    `shrine-of-shah-inayat-qadiri` already carries the id at place level on the Lahore line above,
    which is **not** the same thing as a person mapping and must not be read as one.
  - **Province- and region-level names** — `Sindh`, `Balochistan`, `Punjab`, `the Makran`, `Kutch`,
    `Gujarat`, `Rajasthan`, `Saurashtra`, `the United Provinces`. Each appears in many rows'
    `location_short` strings, and a literal join would attach most of the archive to this book. The
    Pass 1 notes refuse them consistently as "province-level and too broad for any row under any
    ruling", and the ruled toponym counts supplied to this run are all city-level. **Not converted;
    flagged here for Rauf as the one class of ruling-(a) hedge this document has left standing.**
  - **`darbar-malik-ahmad-ayaz` on the Lahore line.** Its `location_short` records "Shah Alam Market"
    and states "No city, district, tehsil or province is stated anywhere in the survey for the shrine
    itself", so it does not contain the string "Lahore" and a `location_short` join does not reach it.
    **The ruled count of 35 is reached without it**, which suggests the generated join agrees; but the
    survey text also says the mazar is "near Darbar Ali Hajveri Ganj Bakhsh (Data Darbar)", which is in
    Lahore. **A human should decide whether that row belongs on Lahore's line; adding it would make 36
    and break the asserted count.**
  - **`nagarparkar-jain-temples-nagarparkar-cultural-landscape` on the Tharparkar line.** Its
    `location_short` reads "Karoonjhar Mountains region, Nagarparkar, Sindh, Pakistan" and does not
    contain "Tharparkar". It is carried because the Pass 1 hedge named it explicitly as one of the
    three rows in that district. **A human should confirm the join.**
  - **`shrine-of-lakhi-shah-saddar` on the Sehwan line.** Its `location_short` reads "Laki (Lakhi),
    near Sehwan Sharif" — **near** the town, not in it. Carried because the Pass 1 hedge named it as a
    row that "would also answer to the name". **A human should confirm.**
  - **`Islamabad`** was not flagged as a toponym case by any Pass 1 chunk. It is added here because the
    toponym occurs at a cited page ("the Islamabad-based Jinnah Institute", p. 50, folio not stated)
    and ruling (a) is standing. **Strike it if that reading is thought too thin.**

### 5. Contradictions inside the book, kept side by side and not resolved

1. **The renovation's date.** "Around the end of the 1990s" (p. 227), "in the late 1990s" (p. 229),
   "at the end of the 1990s" (pp. 235–236) and **"in the early 1990s"** (p. 248), all folio not
   stated. The fourth disagrees with the other three.
2. **The removed liṅga / conical stone / globular stone — an ambiguous number of objects and four
   removal windows.** On p. 238 the author first distinguishes two objects: the large stone "located
   directly in the middle of the platform until the 1940s but must have been removed at some later
   time", and a liṅga "once planted a few meters away from the actual image, in front of the platform,
   which has in fact now been removed by the HSM" — then three sentences later appears to merge them:
   "This liṅga was removed by the HSM in the 1990s and thrown into the Sharan Kund". On p. 241 the
   merger is repeated for Abro's "sadā śivo": "This liṅga is most likely the same as that described by
   Hart, which was eventually removed by the HSM in the mid-1990s." On p. 242 a third window is given
   for "the globular stone that was so evident in Stein's 1943 photograph": "The stone must therefore
   have been removed from its oblong stone base sometime between 1943 and the early 1990s." (all folio
   not stated) **A terminological collision sits inside it: "globular stones" means the two faces on
   the eastern side throughout pp. 229–243, but at p. 242 "the globular stone" means the removed
   conical stone. Do not resolve this; quote whichever the entry cites and attribute it to its page.**
3. **`garbha`, glossed two ways one page apart** — "a small rift in the mountain wall called the
   garbha" (p. 200) against "the garbha, the tunnel underneath the sanctum" (p. 201), with "the tunnel
   underneath the mūrti" named separately on p. 200 (all folio not stated).
4. **The ticketing system's start date** — "in 2008, the Mandali introduced a new ticketing system
   requiring the purchase of an ochre-colored, plastic wristband called the bhag'vā paṭṭī" (p. 219)
   against "Before 2009, there had been another ticketing system in place" and a rearticulation
   "between 2009 and 2013" (p. 209), both folio not stated. The abolition date, 2013, is consistent.
5. **The Makran Coastal Highway's date** — "completed and in use by 2001" for the first part, "in
   2003 (Hasan 2009, 161)" for the whole road to Gabd (pp. 83, 85); "at the beginning of the new
   millennium" (pp. 30, 195, 319); "The construction of the MCH in 2001" (pp. 321–322); "around
   fifteen years later" than 1986 (p. 321), all folio not stated. **The book nowhere states that 2001
   is the highway's own completion year, and one chunk says so explicitly. Do not equate "after 2001"
   with "opened in 2001".**
6. **Which body part of Sati fell at Hinglaj** — head (Pīṭhanirṇaya, p. 55), navel (Caṇḍīmaṅgala,
   pp. 57, 230–231), womb (a pilgrim, p. 260), belly (a minority at the shrine, p. 57), all folio not
   stated. The author says the skull "is the prevailing one" (p. 231) but records all four.
7. **The Chandrakup coconut rule, inverted between two communities** — accepted when the coconut submerges
   (Abadhut, and Dayaram at p. 181) against accepted only when it "is thrown up again by the volcano"
   (Devipujak-Vagri pilgrims from Karachi, p. 264, note 16), both folio not stated. **These are two
   communities' rules, not a correction of one by the other; keep both and attribute each.**
8. **The number of mud-volcano fragments in the origin legends** — Hughes's "eighty-four pieces"
   against Hart's "eighteen pieces, and formed as many Koops", both on p. 214 (folio not stated).
9. **The number of mud volcanoes** — three seen during fieldwork; four in Hart; four in Snead;
   eighteen in the area per Hart 1840 (pp. 210, 263, folios not stated).
10. **The Todarmal–Bharimal journey** — 1508 CE (Chhabria) against 1558 CE (Ansari); **the book itself
    flags this** (p. 112, folio not stated).
11. **The dating of the first Sanskrit mention** — "between the ninth and fifteenth centuries"
    (p. 57) against "between the tenth and the fifteenth centuries" (pp. 96, 320), all folio not
    stated.
12. **The age of the Hinglaj cult** — fifth century (Tambs-Lyche via Tod, whose dating Tambs-Lyche
    himself calls too early); "between the fifth and seventh century" as "an accurate guess"; eighth
    century for Charani Deval; ninth century for worship at the cave (Kamphorst); "already active
    between the seventh and ninth centuries" in the author's own summary (pp. 71–74, 96–97, folios not
    stated). **The author closes with "Unfortunately, no definite proof is available" (p. 74).**
13. **Avar Mata's dates** — born "in the year 888 CE" against a Hun episode "dated to the eighth
    century by Samaur" (p. 72, folio not stated). The book reports both without reconciling them.
14. **Shah Abdul Latif's life dates** — 1690–1751 (pp. 90, 95) against 1689–1753 (pp. 108, 240),
    against the archive's `figure_died` 1752 (all folio not stated).
15. **Sehwan Sharif's district** — the book's "the Dadu district" (p. 107, folio not stated) against
    the archive's "Jamshoro District". **Neither is corrected here.**
16. **Masson's date** — "wrote an early description of the shrine in 1842" (p. 91) against "Masson's
    writing from 1844" (p. 94), the edition cited being Masson 1977 (folios not stated).
17. **The Hindi translation of `Marutīrtha Hiṃlāj`** — "the work's 1957 translation into Hindi"
    (p. 144) against "its Hindi translation, which was already available in 1964" (p. 145); compounded
    by every citation being to "(Abadhut 1964, …)", the work being called "the 1955 Bengali work"
    (p. 144) and elsewhere "[1957]" (p. 320), and the journey dated 1946 (p. 145), all folio not
    stated. **Five dates — 1946, 1955, 1957, 1964, and the novel's own 1957 — and the book reconciles
    none of them. Check the Works Cited (pp. 327–337) before any is used.**
18. **Goldsmid's year** — 1863 at pp. 220 and 225 against 1861 at p. 221, beside the author's own
    statement that he found no sources "from between the years 1861 and 1943" (p. 225), all folio not
    stated.
19. **Hart's year** — `Hart 1839` throughout against `Hart 1840, 145` in note 10 (p. 263, folio not
    stated). **The chunk containing the bibliography does not settle whether these are two
    publications**: **(not stated)**.
20. **The 1884 judgment resting on an 1891 census** — "the case Raj Kumar Lal v. Biseshwar Dayal,
    heard at the Calcutta High Court in 1884. The court held that a Kayastha man could not legally
    adopt his sister's son due to the declaration of the Kayasthas as Shudras in the 1891 census
    (Carroll 1978, 239)." (p. 60, folio not stated) **An 1884 judgment cannot rest on an 1891 census;
    both numerals are cleanly printed, so this is not a text-layer defect.** The census year at issue
    four times elsewhere in the same passage is **1901** (pp. 59–60, folio not stated), which makes
    1891 the odd figure. **Neither date should enter an entry until a human checks the printed page.**
21. **The Devī Bhāgavata Purāṇa's page ranges** — "section VII, chapter 30, pp. 53–102" followed by
    "section VII, chapter 38, pp. 5–30" (p. 54, folio not stated): the later chapter carries the lower
    range. **Cite as printed and do not reconcile.**
22. **The Eighteenth Amendment dated 2008** — "The Eighteenth Amendment, adopted in 2008, improved
    non-Muslim representation by adding four more seats for minorities in the Senate." (pp. 15–16,
    folios not stated). **Recorded exactly as printed and not corrected; a human should check it
    against the book's own note apparatus, since it is the kind of figure a reader will test.**
23. **Jareca's text, described two incompatible ways on one page** — he co-writes the mostly-Gujarati
    1905 `Hiṃgulā Purāṇ`, yet "Jareca's entire text is in Sanskrit and followed by a Hindi
    translation" (p. 61, folio not stated). The second sentence may refer to the undated Hindi
    `Brahmakṣatriyotpatti Evaṃ Māṁ Hiṃgulāj` instead.
24. **The Jasyasena narrative's opening chapter** — 77 (Jareca) against 79 (Shastri), which the book
    reports without resolving (p. 99, folio not stated).
25. **The sea route** — "the least frequented" in Wilford (p. 113) against "the easiest way to reach
    the shrine" in Abro (p. 114), against the author's informants saying the Gujarat coastal land
    route was most used (p. 115), all folio not stated.
26. **The 1959 film** — the author calls it "a fairly accurate adaption of the novel" (p. 127) while
    the devotee verdict he quotes says "it was not filmed at the proper place" (p. 125), both folio
    not stated. **Different axes — fidelity to the novel against fidelity to the place — and the book
    does not reconcile them.**
27. **The stated ban against the observed practice.** The HSM "officially banned animal sacrifice
    inside the valley in 2004" and "most of the devotees and visitors I spoke with seemed fully
    supportive of the ban" (p. 294), yet the same page reports a rooster sacrifice witnessed in 2010
    "and also in later years" and a goat offered by the Vagris "only the night before", and p. 296
    reports that "members of the HSM secretly continued sacrificing goats in the mountains". **This is
    not an error to resolve; it is the chapter's argument. An entry must not state flatly that
    sacrifice ceased at Hinglaj in 2004.**
28. **The HSM's account of its own power.** "the HSM does consider its regulations to be suggestions
    that cannot be strictly imposed" and "No one [of the HSM] sits there with a gun" (p. 287) sit
    against the volunteers' "authority to exclude visitors who misbehave" (p. 282) and two unposted
    rules "enforced by volunteers and the Balochistan police" (p. 284), all folio not stated. **An
    entry that quotes only one side will misrepresent the chapter.**
29. **One or several annual festivals?** "The establishment of annual festivals at Hinglaj" (plural)
    against "numerous recent adjustments and changes at the temple and its festival" (singular),
    eleven lines apart on p. 324 (folio not stated). **Four major festivals are counted at p. 197 and
    p. 277; the conclusion does not say.**
30. **Whether Hinglaj is the Vagris' clan goddess.** "Even though Hinglaj is not one of the
    Ghorava-Vagris' clan `Goddess`[text-layer?] [Goddesses], there are other families among the Vagris
    who worship Hinglaj as their `kul'devī` (Werth 1996, 350)." (p. 311, folio not stated) **The
    sentence both denies and affirms the relation depending on which Vagri family is meant; an entry
    must not shorten it to "the Vagris' clan goddess".**
31. **Dost Mohammad's tenure.** "The Zikris, in fact, were in charge of the shrine for many years,
    until in 1986 when the organization was taken over by the HSM" sits in the same sentence-pair as
    "Before the 1980s, Dost Mohammad was the main caretaker of Hinglaj" (p. 90, folio not stated).
    **Whether his caretaking ended before the 1980s or ran up to the 1986 handover is not stated.**
    Separately, his son Hussain was still guiding the Chaurasi tour "as a family tradition" in 2012,
    twenty-six years after the takeover (p. 265, note 33, folio not stated): **the family's guiding
    role and the family's charge of the shrine are evidently not the same thing, and a human should
    keep them apart.**
32. **Aghor and Hingol — a doubt the book itself answers, recorded so it is closed rather than
    carried.** Chunk 010 recorded stones taken "from the River Aghor" and, two pages later, "from the
    riverbed of the Hingol" (pp. 173, 175, 176, folios not stated). Note 18 resolves it: "Briggs
    writes that the river breaks through a gorge at some point, below which it is called Aghor. Above
    the gorge it is called Hingol (Briggs 1938, 106). Today the name Hingol is primarily used."
    (p. 264, folio not stated) **They are one watercourse under two names divided by a gorge; the
    doubt is closed.**
33. **Two dates for the alcohol rule** — "a rule that emerged around the beginning of the millennium
    when the crowds started to swell" (p. 281) against the fourth posted rule on the 2011 boards
    (p. 284), both folio not stated. **The book does not say whether the 2011 sign restated an
    existing rule or created one; on its face it restates. Do not print "alcohol banned in 2011".**
34. **"The temple committee" at p. 27 is not explicitly identified as the HSM.** The Chapter 6
    preview refers throughout to "the temple committee"; the HSM is named only at p. 30, three pages
    later (folios not stated). **The identification is very likely but the book does not state it
    there**: **(not stated)**.
35. **The banning body at p. 47 is called only "the temple organization"** and is not there named as
    the HSM (folio not stated): **(not stated)**.
36. **`bedī` against `vedī`** — `bedī` in the Abadhut passage (p. 134) against `vedī (Hin. a place for
    sacrifices)` four times in the Shastri passage (p. 142), both folio not stated. **Only the second
    is glossed. Whether this is the book's inconsistency, a difference between the two sources' usages,
    or a text-layer substitution cannot be decided from the text layer.**
37. **"spirit", "female ghost" and "jinn" used of one being within four sentences** (p. 141, folio not
    stated) — the last an Islamic term applied inside a Hindu pilgrim's possession episode. **Recorded
    as printed, not harmonised; it is unclear whether the shift is Shastri's, the author's or a
    translation artefact.**
38. **"formants" against "formats"** — "three 'formants' or 'moments'" and "none of these formants can
    be conceptualized separately" (pp. 196, 197) against "Lefebvre's format of imaginary of space" and
    "the other two formats" (p. 201), all folio not stated. **A whole-word substitution yielding a real
    English word, so it is not flagged inline; the book does not settle it.**
39. **The Times of India date in note 6** — "March 20, 2007" in the note text against the slug
    `2007-03-10` in the URL it prints (p. 193, folio not stated), and the Works Cited entry "Chatterjee,
    Mohua. 2007. 'Open Hinglaj to Indian Pilgrims.' Times of India, March 10." (p. 328, folio not
    stated). **Two of the three agree on 10 March; cite none until the printed page is checked.**
40. **The Narayanpur welfare body's name** — "the more recent Pakistani Hindu Seva Samaj" (p. 157)
    against "the Pakistan Hindu Seva Samaj" (p. 159), both folio not stated — and the scheduled-caste
    community, "the Meghvar" (p. 154) against "the Meghwarji—the Meghwarjis" (p. 161, inside quoted
    speech). **Pick one form per entry and say which.**
41. **The Hinglaj Sheva Mandali's own name, three ways** — body `Hinglāj Śevā Manḍalī (HSM)` with full
    diacritics (p. 30), endnote `Hinglaj Sheva Mandali` bare (p. 28), and the damaged `Hingaj Sheva
    Mandai (HSM)`[text-layer?] (p. 197), all folio not stated. **The book expands the abbreviation
    almost nowhere: the Conclusion's three body pages never expand it (p. 324, folio not stated), and
    the expansion appears in the index only, as "Hinglaj Sheva Mandali (HSM), 18, 37, 72–83, 91, 138,
    146, 152–163, 190–196, 204–207, 250, 258–277, 299–303" (p. 348, folio not stated).**
42. **Badar Abro cited by his given name as though it were his surname** — introduced as "Badar Abro"
    and cited "(Abro 1991, 62f.)", then "Badar's book", "Another photo from Badar's visit", "Stein and
    Badar both focused on the western part of the image" (pp. 241–244, folios not stated). **Left as
    printed; it will break a bibliography match.**
43. **Khan Baloch filed under two surnames** — `Khan Baloch, Nabi B. 1970` in the Works Cited (p. 332)
    against "Baloch, Nabi Bux Khan, 211, 214, 226" in the Index (p. 341), both folio not stated. **A
    name search will find only one.**
44. **Stein's photograph dated two ways** — "in Aurel Stein's 1943 photograph" (p. 233) and "the
    photograph Stein took of the image in 1943" (p. 237) against the figure caption "Aurel Stein's shot
    of the image from around 1943" (p. 239), all folio not stated. **"around 1943" is not the same claim
    as "1943"; do not harden the caption's hedge.**
45. **The Palvodar / Palvadar village, spelled two ways four pages apart** (pp. 307, 311, folios not
    stated), with the book's own warning at note 27: "The Vagris supplied me with the spelling of this
    and other names in this section. It is possible that they differ in other surroundings." (p. 317,
    folio not stated) **Any Vagri personal or place name carried into an entry must be flagged as the
    community's own orthography.**
46. **Ungakhera / Unghakhera Bherum Luk**, and `Kumbhodar` against `Kumbodhar` four lines apart on
    p. 120, and `Ramacandra` against `Ramachandra` against `Rama`, and `Nihararanjan` against
    `Niharanjan` against `Nihararanjan Chaudhari` (pp. 118–132, folios not stated). **All as printed;
    none harmonised.**
47. **The transliteration of the shrine's own name is not stable.** Within nineteen pages the book
    prints `Hinglaj`, `Hinglaj Devi`, `Hinglaj Mata`, `hinglāj mātā`, `Nani Hinglaj`, `Shree Mata`,
    `Hiṅgulā`, `Hiṃgulā`, `Hiṅglāja`, `Hiṅghalāja`, `Hiṃg'lāj`, `Hiṅglāj`, `Hiṃlāj`, `hiṅgula` and
    `Hinglāj Jī Yātrā` (pp. 98–116, folio not stated). **A human must decide which form an entry prints,
    and the archive's own row name, `Shaktipeeth Shri Hinglaj Mata Mandir`, matches none of them
    exactly.** The same instability affects the community names: `Brahmakshatriya`, `Brahmakshatria`,
    `Brahmakshatrias`, `Brahma Kshatriya [brahmakṣatriya]`, `Brahma Khatri [brahmakhatrī]`,
    `Brahma Khatris` (pp. 58–59, folios not stated); `Kapri` / `Kapadi` / `Kapadiya` / `kāpaḍiyā`
    (pp. 74–75, folios not stated); `Saraswat` against `Sarasvat Brahmins` in consecutive sentences
    (p. 81, folio not stated); `akhāṛā` against `akhārā` (pp. 307, 309, folios not stated);
    `Devipujaks` against `Devipujakas` (p. 309, folio not stated); `piṃḍ` against `piṇḍas` (pp. 231,
    242, folios not stated); `svayaṃbhū` in the author's voice against Stein's quoted `svayam-bhu`
    (p. 235, folio not stated), **which is inside a 1943 quotation and must not be normalised.**

### 6. Identification wrinkles inside the mapped rows

- **`chandragup-baba-chandragup`: the book spells the site `Chandrakup` with a *k* everywhere except
  once**, where it gives both forms and the etymologies: "The mud volcano Chandrakup (from Hin.
  caṃdrakūp 'the moon well') (sometimes shortened to 'Kup'), also called Chandragup (from Sin. chandra
  gūp 'the moon's mud')" (p. 210, folio not stated). **The archive's `Chandragup (Baba Chandragup)`
  matches the second form, so the identification is secure, but a later worker matching on string
  alone will miss every other occurrence.** The Sindhi quotation adds `Chandarkūp` (p. 264, folio not
  stated). **The archive row's `principal_figure`, "Shiva (associated)", is corroborated only by
  "worshipped as an embodiment of Shiva" (p. 203, folio not stated) and by current pilgrim explanation
  (p. 215, folio not stated), and the book itself says "There is no prevailing notion about which
  deity is actually worshipped at the rim of the volcano" (p. 214, folio not stated).**
- **`shrine-of-pir-mangho`: three spellings against the archive's one.** The book's `Magho Pir`
  (p. 78), `Mango Pir` (pp. 211, 263, 352) and Hart's `"Peer Moogha"` (p. 263), against the archive's
  `Pir Mangho (Haji Syed Sakhi Sultan)` — **name order reversed and one nasal different.** The book
  places it in "northern Karachi"; the row reads "Gadap Town, Karachi, Sindh". **No other Karachi row
  names a Magho or Mangho, so there is no competing candidate**, but the book's own sentence is hedged
  and the material is one geological comparison and one conjecture in an endnote. **`Mango Pir` is
  also a Karachi locality name, which is why the index headword alone would not carry the mapping.**
- **`sadh-belo-sadh-belo-island-temple`: `Sadh Bela` against `Sadh Belo`**, one vowel. **The location
  is decisive — "in the city of Sukkur on an island in the River Indus" (p. 138, folio not stated) —
  and no second Sukkur-island row exists.** The archive's other Sukkur rows, `gurdwara-dash-mesh-pita`
  and `gurdwara-pehli-patshahi-jind-pir-sukkur`, are in Sukkur town and not on the island, and are
  attached to this book only at place level.
- **`ramapir-temple-tando-allahyar`: two spellings and a dating the archive does not carry.** The
  book's `Thando Allah Yar` (three words, aspirated `Th-`) against `Tando Allahyar`, and `Ramdevji`
  against `Ramdev Pir (Ramapir)`. **The match rests on saint + main temple + town, all three present,
  so it is not a bare-name case — but "fourteenth-century" would be this book supplying a value the
  row's empty `figure_died` cell does not have, and a human should confirm before it enters the
  row.** The book separately attests a "local Ram Pir temple" in the Narayanpur neighbourhood of
  Karachi (p. 163, folio not stated) and an index headword `Ram Pir temple, 150` (p. 357, folio not
  stated); **neither is this row's site and neither has been folded in.** The index also has
  `Ramdev Shiv Santosh Mandali` as a **publisher's name** in a Karachi imprint (p. 331, folio not
  stated) — **a publisher is not a figure mention.**
- **`shri-swaminarayan-mandir-karachi`: the street match is exact, the rest is thin.** "the
  Swaminarayan temple on M. A. Jinnah Road" (p. 207, folio not stated) against the row's "M. A. Jinnah
  Road, Karachi, Sindh, Pakistan"; the book prints `M.A. Jinnah Road` without a space at p. 159. **A
  human should confirm no second Swaminarayan temple in Karachi is in play**, since the book gives no
  address at p. 180. **The `Swami Narayana organization` of p. 297 is a reform movement, not a
  building and not a named person**, and the index keeps it as a separate headword with a disjoint run
  (p. 360, folio not stated): **do not merge the two.** **Whether "(1781–1830)" may be carried into the
  row's empty `figure_died` cell is a human decision, since the book attaches those dates to "its
  founder" and never names him.**
- **`bhit-bhit-shah`: the evidence ranges from substantial to a bare title.** At pp. 90–96 and 108 the
  book gives a dated life, a named journey, a named work and a named section of it, and names his
  dargāh at Bhit Shah once. At p. 240 it gives a bare name and dates as another scholar's motivation;
  at pp. 327 and 332 it gives only two book titles, one of which ("Shah jo Risalo") **names no person
  at all**; at p. 351 it gives an index headword. **A human should confirm that the run intends the
  18 September ruling to reach a name appearing only inside another author's book title, rather than
  as the author of a cited work.** If not, those two bibliography lines move out of the row.
- **`lal-shahbaz-qalandar`: the index files him twice and the runs must not be merged.**
  `Lal Shahbaz Qalandar, 148` (p. 351) and `Qalandar, Lal Shahbaz, 97–98, 179` (p. 357), folio not
  stated, **with no cross-reference and no overlap.** Whether this is one entry split by an indexing
  error or two deliberate entries is **(not stated)**. Note also that `shamsabad` (Shams Ali
  Qalandar), `shrine-of-qalandar-baba-auliya` and `abul-faiz-qalander-ali-suharwardi` all contain the
  string "Qalandar", **so a string match on that word alone would fire four rows**; the full name here
  is unambiguous.
- **`shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir`: no name the book uses matches the row's name.** See §5.47.

### 7. Sites the book documents that have no archive row — candidates for new rows

Recorded here because the Pass 1 notes flagged them under Doubts and because two of them carry
material a Hinglaj entry would want. **None has been mapped.**
- **The Kali Mata temple inside the Hinglaj valley** — "the Kali and Hinglaj temple" (p. 165), "the
  Kali Mata temple, the main location for the bali" (p. 296), "the shrine of Kali Mata" (p. 284), "the
  Kali shrine located just some meters before the temple of Hinglaj" (p. 285), with its own pujārī and
  its own obligatory goat or rooster (pp. 306, 311, 312), all folio not stated. **This is a distinct
  shrine with its own priest, its own ritual and its own quarrel, sitting inside the Hinglaj complex,
  and it is the named location of the bali the whole of Chapter 6 is about.** The archive's three Kali
  rows (`kalat-kali-temple`, `kali-bari-mandir`, `kalka-cave-temple-asthan-of-kalka-devi`) were checked
  and refused on location. **A human should rule whether this becomes a new row or a sub-site of
  `shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir`.**
- **The Mata Bhawani temple at Makli, near Thatta** — given a full section and called "one of the most
  significant places of worship east of Karachi for Hinglaj pilgrims" (p. 205, folio not stated), with
  three historical names including "Ashapura (Briggs 1938)". **The archive has no Thatta or Makli row
  at all.**
- **The Melari (Meladi) Mata temple and the Momai Mata edifice inside the Hinglaj valley** (p. 271,
  folio not stated), and the **Ganesha temple at the paṛāv outside it** (pp. 279–281, folio not
  stated). **No archive row names any of them.**
- **The Nagnath akhāṛā and the Jagnath akhāṛā, Karachi** (pp. 127, 138, 159, 207, 292–294, folios not
  stated). **`jagannath-temple-sialkot` was considered and refused three times — different city,
  different province, different kind of institution — and the Nath rows `gorakhnath-goraknath-temple`
  and `tilla-jogian` were refused for the Nagnath akhāṛā, whose link to Gorakhnath the book never
  states.**
- **The Kali temple in Karachi where the Sanjesh interview took place** (p. 304, folio not stated) —
  **the book does not name it**: **(not stated)**.
- **The temple at Thatta the Charan and Udasin saṃnyāsīs held "as important as the main shrine in
  Balochistan"** (p. 71, folio not stated).
- **Mohammad Ali Jinnah's mazār**, the destination of an annual Hindu march (p. 273, folio not stated).
  **The archive's three Secular/Memorial rows were checked and none is his.**
- **The Liari temple, the Liari Devi temple and Lalu Jasraj's fireplace at Liari** (pp. 181, 209, folio
  not stated); **Astola Island and its ruined Kali temple** (pp. 259–260, folio not stated);
  **Chaurasi / Anil Kund, Til Kund, Kali Kund, Kir Kund, Brahma Kund, Sharan Kund and Akash Ganga**
  inside or near the Hinglaj valley (pp. 146–147, 180–181, 251–256, folios not stated) — **all
  unrepresented in `shrine_index.tsv`, checked name by name.**
- **Two attacked temples the book does not name.** "a Hindu temple in Larkana, a city in the north of
  Sindh" (March 2014) and "a temple in Hyderabad" (28 March 2014), both p. 17, folio not stated.
  **Assigning `swami-dharmdas-darbar-larkana-kennedy-market` or
  `guru-gurpat-mandir-db-80-sirey-ghat` to either incident would be a guess and was refused; the two
  rows take their ids at place level only.**

### 8. Locators, notes, timecodes and quoted paginations — four rules a mechanical reader will break

1. **No index or endnote locator has been converted into a `(p. N)` anywhere in this document.** Every
   index and endnote fact above is cited to the PDF page the entry is printed on, exactly as the Pass 1
   notes did. The index runs pp. 339–364 in PDF pages while its own locators reach 309 and also point
   into roman front matter (`Urdu language, xii`; `Sindh, xiiif`; `map of Pakistan, xiiif`), **and this
   book supplies no offset between the two**: **(not stated)**.
2. **Index locator grammar, as Pass 1 read it and not as the book states it** (the book prints no key):
   **(not stated)** — a bare number is a page; `N`+`f` is a figure on that page; `N`+`n`+`M` is note M
   on page N, **whose text is in a chapter endnote block and not on that page**; roman numerals are
   front matter.
3. **Endnote callouts and endnote texts must never be paired across a chapter boundary.** The book
   restarts note numbering at every chapter and prints each chapter's notes at the end of that chapter,
   so a callout and a note text with the same number are routinely different notes. The measured
   collisions: Chapter 1's callouts 1–7 against the Introduction's notes 1–8 printed on p. 28; Chapter
   2's callouts 1–21 against Chapter 1's notes 1–9 on p. 50; Chapter 3's callouts 1–19 against Chapter
   2's notes on pp. 98–102; Chapter 3's callouts 20–27 against notes printed before p. 150; Chapter 4's
   callouts 7–17 against a block after p. 187; Chapter 5's callouts 1–6 against numerals 1–6 on
   pp. 192–194 that belong to Chapter 4; Chapter 5's callouts 7–31 against notes printed pp. 263–265;
   and **Chapter 6's callouts 1 and 2 (pp. 267, 268) against Chapter 5's notes 1 and 2 on p. 263, which
   are demonstrably different notes** — Chapter 5's note 1 discusses "these three moments" and its note
   2 is Gopal Maharaj's Hindi on the temple roof. **No Chapter 6 note text may be taken from
   pp. 263–265.** Several notes that would materially strengthen facts above are unread: Chapter 5's
   note 2 (the Dawn interview's date) and note 3 (Jethamalani's thirteen paṛāv), and Chapter 6's notes
   16, 19 and 26.
4. **Three kinds of number in this book are not page references and must never be converted.** (i)
   Cited works' own paginations — `(Abadhut 1964, 209)`, `(Hart 1839, 91)`, `(Wilford 1851, 259)`,
   `(Jethamalani 1994, 12)`, `(Shastri 1977, 42)` and the rest; **`Abadhut 1964` alone runs 1–222.**
   (ii) **Film timecodes** — `Schaflechner 2011` at 11:00, 14:05, 17:00, 21:40, 23:00, 29:00, 29:30,
   33:00, 34:30, 40:40 and `Schaflechner 2013, 7:10` (pp. 171, 179, 181, 263, 312, folios not stated).
   (iii) A bibliography back-reference that is not this book's pagination at all: "Dawn. 2009 … (ch. 5,
   p. 36, n. 21)." (p. 329, folio not stated). **The "p. 36" cannot be a PDF page of this book and a
   human must check the printed page.** Two further back-references are inconsistent in form:
   "`The        News.      2014.`[text-layer?] … ch. 5, n. 23." (p. 336, folio not stated).
5. **Index headwords sharing a locator run prove nothing about either.** Measured pairs include
   `animal sacrifice` / `bali`; `Hiṃgulā Purāṇ` / `Hinglāj Jī Yātrā` / `Brahmakṣatriyotpatti Evaṃ Māṁ
   Hiṃgulāj`; `Chandrakup` / `Ashapura` (sharing 55 and 70); `Gorakhnath sect` / `Briggs, George W.`;
   `Gorakh Caritra` / `Gorakhpurāṇa`; `healing` / `eye disease, healing`; `Kali Kund` / `Kir Kund`;
   `Melari Mata` / `Momai Mata`; `sun, symbol of` / `moon, symbol of`; `Westphal, Heinz` /
   `Westphal-Hellbusch, Sigrid`; `tīrtha` / `tīrthasthān` / `tīrthayātrā`; `salvation market` /
   `pilgrimage-market` / `tour organizers`; `Lacan, Jacques` / `point de caption` / `quilting point`;
   `navel of Sati` / `womb of Sati`; and **`Swaminarayan temple, 167, 194` with `M. A. Jinnah Road,
   194`, where p. 194 is also carried by `Liari`, `Shastri, Devadatta`, `Wilford, F.`, `Samaur, Bhaṃvara
   S` and `N 25 Highway`** — a busy page, and the coincidence proves nothing (pp. 339–364, folios not
   stated). **The `pilgrimage:` sub-entry "route of, 92, 101–113, 105, 138, 146–149" and the main
   headword "92, 101–113, 106f, 146–149" disagree (`105, 138` against `106f`); do not merge them
   silently.** **One locator run cannot be right as printed: "Hingol River, 55, 64, 75–78,
   `104–195`[text-layer?], 133, 176, 203–205, 248" (p. 348, folio not stated) is out of ascending order
   and no reading is supplied. Do not cite that range.** **`Lalu Jasraj` is indexed under three
   headwords with three disjoint runs** — `Jasraj, Lalu, 67, 71, 168, 196`, `Kapri, Lalu Jasraj, 69`
   and `Lalu Jasraj, 111` (pp. 349, 350, 351, folios not stated) — **and the three must not be
   concatenated.** **`Omara, 78` and `Ormara, 245` are two headwords one letter apart with disjoint
   runs; a human must decide whether they are one place.** **`Nanak, 73` against `Nanak, Guru, 242,
   243`, and `Lal Shahbaz Qalandar, 148` against `Qalandar, Lal Shahbaz, 97–98, 179`, are the same
   problem.**

### 9. People the book deliberately leaves unnamed, who must stay that way

"aguā 1" and "aguā 2", whose city is given only as "a major Pakistani city" (pp. 183–185, folio not
stated); the foreman responsible for construction in the valley (p. 251); the HSM worker interviewed
in 2013 (p. 264, note 19); the HSM associate quoted on class (p. 324); the pilgrim quoted at length on
the fee (p. 290); the Sindhi Hindu of the Bhanushali jāti (p. 292); the president of the Pakistan Hindu
Seva Trust (p. 294); the female bystander who protested the rooster sacrifice (p. 294); the Meghwar
interviewee (p. 303); the Kali Mata pujārī (p. 304); the police officer at Chandrakup (p. 291); the
HSM's donors after 2013 (p. 292); the Kali pujārī who stopped sacrificing goats (p. 312); the "Meghwar
social worker" colleague (p. 308); the Minority Minister of Sindh (p. 291); the Islamabad Jinnah
Institute official (p. 50); the Gujarati businessman who erected the Ashapura statue (p. 220); the
donor named on the surviving inscription, whose name is visible but is not printed (p. 227); the
descendant of Dost Mohammad who guided the 2010 group (p. 181); the Thatta priest (p. 171); the Delhi
temple's guru (p. 170); the Sindhi television station (p. 181); "one pilgrim" (p. 260); "as one author
on Hindu traditions in Pakistan" (p. 268); the holy man whose footprints are engraved (p. 227); the
mahaṃt of Sadh Bela (p. 138); Aghori Baba's personal name (p. 142); the two Hindu families in the
Sehwan henna ceremony (p. 108); the November 2016 Lahoot bombers (p. 108); the injured pilgrim of 2012
(p. 312); and the Prophet's family member the Zikris identify with the grave (p. 325). All folio not
stated; all **(not stated)**. **Do not attempt to identify any of them.** Two further figures are
quoted but never introduced in the chunk that quotes them: `Dewani` (p. 316, note 10; p. 317, note 21)
and the "one official" who explained the Khari stop's removal (p. 204).

### 10. Gaps the book leaves open, so no one fills them later

For **`shaktipeeth-shri-hinglaj-mata-mandir`**: its district, tehsil, road address or coordinates; any
foundation date, architect or patron for the temple, the enclosure, the platform, the tunnel masonry
or the outdoor yajña kuṃḍ; the date of the tiling of the oblong stone; the date the eyes and noses
were attached to the two round stones; the date of either inscription and the donor's name; the exact
age of the enclosure, the mud hill and the tunnel; the HSM's officers, membership and legal form
beyond "Trust"; Vasrup Chand Tulsi's dates and anything else about him; Gopal Maharaj's dates, caste,
lineage, predecessor or appointment beyond 2008; Dost Mohammad's given name, office title, birth year
and exact year of death, and Hussain's dates; any sajjāda nashīn, mutawalli, waqf, Auqaf, endowment,
land grant or revenue total — **the book names no Auqaf or waqf involvement of any kind, only the
Balochistan provincial government as funder and private Hindu donors**; any absolute annual visitor
count; any calendar date, in any era, for the phālgun/caitra melā, or its duration, or the old and new
dates the Hingol bridge changed; the names and dates of the three unnamed annual festivals; which ʿīd
the autumn "`cīd` celebrations" are; the month of the 2004 sacrifice ban, the body that passed it and
its terms; the "functional yet ultimately contingent events" that prompted it; who introduced the
ear-cutting substitute and when; the price of the bhag'vā paṭṭī; the fate of the 100-rupee police
levy after 2013; any named politician or sum behind the bhanḍārā donations; the completion date of the
Makran Coastal Highway, the Hingol bridge and the dam proposal; which three holy places WAPDA offered
to relocate; who made the spackle-filled sun and moon; the year of the child's drowning at Sharan Kund
and of the mother-and-four-children drowning at Til Kund; the year the Melari Mata booth was begun and
the year of the Momai Mata vision; the name and location of the Kutch Ashapura temple; the text, date
and location of any Thatta inscription; and any translation of the Sindhi of notes 17 and 24 or the
Hindi/Urdu of notes 2, 8, 28, 29, 31, 32 and 42. For **`chandragup-baba-chandragup`**: any deity,
temple, image, custodian, priest, district or date of its own. For **`bhit-bhit-shah`**: his silsila,
tomb description, ʿurs, custodian, and what in his work concerns Hinglaj. For **`lal-shahbaz-qalandar`**:
his dates, silsila, ʿurs date, tomb description and the names of the two Hindu families. For
**`shah-noorani-shrine-syed-bilawal-shah-noorani`**: his death date, silsila, tomb and any ʿurs. For
**`sadh-belo-sadh-belo-island-temple`**: the mahaṃt's name, dates and lineage, and any date for the
visit. For **`ramapir-temple-tando-allahyar`**: Ramdevji's death year, silsila or sampradaya, and any
description of the temple. For **`shri-swaminarayan-mandir-karachi`**: its founder's name, its
foundation date, its custodian and any ritual. For **`shrine-of-pir-mangho`**: the saint's name, dates,
silsila, custodian and any rite. All **(not stated)**.

Three further gaps that are not row-specific but that a reader will look for: the book's own present
is never fixed to a year — "the past decade alone" (p. 325), "at the time of writing" twice for visa
rules (p. 169) and "the past thirty years" (p. 320) are all undated, and **the only fixed date in the
back matter is the link-access date "June 1, 2017" (p. 337, folio not stated) with the latest Works
Cited item dated 2017**; the Contents page prints **no page numbers at all** (p. 6, folio not stated),
which removes the usual independent cross-check on where chapters begin; and the Internet Sources
block lists chapters 1, 2 and 6 and the Introduction and **skips chapters 3, 4 and 5** (pp. 337–338,
folio not stated), though the Works Cited elsewhere back-references "ch. 5" — **the book does not say
whether those chapters had no web sources or whether the block is incomplete**: **(not stated)**.
Finally, the author's own name is printed two ways on facing pages: `JÜRGEN SCHAFLECHNER` on the title
page (p. 3) and `Schaflechner, Jurgen, 1981– author` without the umlaut in the Library of Congress
block (p. 4), both folio not stated — **most likely the LC record's own diacritic-stripping, since the
same page renders every other accented character cleanly, but it is not certain from the text layer.
The birth year 1981 is the LC heading's, not the author's own voice.**
